D2
FL_PyTorch as a GPU-Accelerated Application

Machine learning applications today predominantly rely on GPUs or CPUs. Recently, there has been growing interest in using FPGA devices. Unlike CPUs and GPUs with fixed instruction sets, FPGAs consist of programmable logic blocks that can be dynamically interconnected to create custom hardware circuits for specialized tasks. FPGAs offer an interesting option and unique capabilities for tighter algorithms and computational hardware integration.

Historically, between 2003 and 2010, in the absence of general-purpose GPU computing support, researchers relied on graphics APIs to encode computations within the framework of rasterized image processing. Since 2010, direct programming interfaces for GPU computing have become widely available,
4
4

4

Some GPU Compute API: AMD ROCm, CUDA, OpenCL, Apple Metal, Vulkan Compute.
enabling explicit access to GPU acceleration. For Deep Learning training, NVIDIA GPUs remain the dominant choice, though other vendors are beginning to catch up
5
5

5

Example is the
AMD ROCm
platform which is integrated into TensorFlow, PyTorch, JAX.
. Low-level programming of NVIDIA GPUs is done through CUDA
6
6

6

Compute Unified Device Architecture (CUDA):
https://developer.nvidia.com/cuda-toolkit
. CUDA is not just a programming language but a comprehensive platform and ecosystem that extends several programming languages, both syntactically and semantically, to facilitate efficient GPU programming.

Because

FL_PyTorch
uses
GPU
s indirectly through

PyTorch
, it can be worthwhile to observe the larger picture of how

FL_PyTorch
fits into the overall computational stack once configured to use an NVIDIA GPU. We illustrate this in Figure
2.8
.

Figure 2.8
:
NVIDIA compute ecosystem and the place of
FL_PyTorch
in it if using GPU compute.

The

FL_PyTorch
is distributed under the Apache License, Version 2.0 (January 2004) and is publicly available at the following link:

https://github.com/burlachenkok/flpytorch

Chapter 3
EF21-W: Error Feedback Reloaded

The goals and summaries of this chapter are outlined in Table
1.1
and Section
1.4.2
.

3.1
Introduction

Due to their ability to harness the computational capabilities of modern devices and their capacity to extract value from the enormous data generated by organizations, individuals, and various digital devices and sensors, Machine Learning (ML) methods
(
Bishop 2007
;
Shalev-Shwartz and Ben-David 2014
)
have become indispensable in numerous practical applications
(
Krizhevsky et al. 2012
;
Lin et al. 2022
;
Vaswani et al. 2017
;
Onay and Öztürk 2018
;
Poplin et al. 2017
;
Gavrilut et al. 2009
;
Sun et al. 2017
)
.

The necessity to handle large datasets has driven application entities to store and process their data in powerful computing centers
(
Yang et al. 2019b
;
Dean et al. 2012
;
Verbraeken et al. 2021
)
via
distributed
training algorithms. Besides this industry-standard centralized approach, decentralized forms of distributed learning are becoming increasingly popular. For example, Federated Learning (FL) facilitates a collaborative learning process in which various clients, such as hospitals or owners of edge devices, collectively train a model on their devices while retaining their data locally, without uploading it to a centralized location
(
Konečný et al. 2016b
;
Konečný et al. 2016a
;
McMahan et al. 2017
;
Li et al. 2020c
;
Kairouz et al. 2021
;
Wang et al. 2021
)
.

Distributed training problems are typically formulated as optimization problems of the form

\min\limits_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\{f(x)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\dfrac{1}{n}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}f_{i}(x)\right\}\mathchar 59\relax

(3.1)

where
n
is the number of clients/workers/nodes, vector
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
represents the
d
trainable parameters, and
f_{i}(x)
is the loss of the model parameterized by
x
on the training data stored on client
i\in[n]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\{1\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\dots\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}n\}
. One of the key issues in distributed training in general, and FL in particular, is the
communication bottleneck

(
Konečný et al. 2016b
;
Kairouz et al. 2021
)
. The overall efficiency of a distributed algorithm for solving (
3.1
) can be characterized by multiplying the number of communication rounds needed to find a solution of acceptable accuracy by the cost of each communication round:

\displaystyle\mathsf{Communication\ Complexity}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{\#\ Communication\ Rounds}

(3.2)

\displaystyle\times\mathsf{Cost\ per\ Round}.

This simple formula clarifies the rationale behind two orthogonal approaches to alleviating the communication bottleneck. i) The first approach aims to minimize the first factor in (
3.2
). This is done by carefully deciding on
what work should be done
on the clients in each communication round in order for it to reduce the total number of communication rounds needed and includes methods based on local training
(
Stich 2019
;
Lin et al. 2020
;
Mishchenko et al. 2022
;
Condat et al. 2023
;
Li et al. 2020a
)
and momentum
(
Nesterov 1983
;
Nesterov 2004
;
d’Aspremont et al. 2021
)
. Methods in this class communicate dense
d
-dimensional vectors. ii) The second approach aims to minimize the second factor in (
3.2
). Methods in this category
compress the information
(typically
d
-dimensional vectors) transmitted between the clients and the server
(
Alistarh et al. 2017
;
Khirirat et al. 2018b
;
Bernstein et al. 2018
;
Safaryan et al. 2020
)
.

3.1.1
Communication compression

Vector compression can be achieved through the application of a compression operator. Below, we outline two primary classes of these operators: unbiased (with conically bounded variance) and contractive.

Definition 6

(Compressors)
.

A randomized mapping
\mathcal{C}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
is called i) an
unbiased compressor
if for some
\omega>0
it satisfies

\mathbb{E}\left[\mathcal{C}(x)\right]=x\mathchar 59\relax\quad\mathbb{E}\left[\|\mathcal{C}(x)-x\|^{2}\right]\leq\omega\|x\|^{2}\mathchar 59\relax\quad\forall x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}

(3.3)

and ii) a
contractive compressor
if for some
\alpha\in(0\mathchar 59\relax 1]
it satisfies

\mathbb{E}\left[\|\mathcal{C}(x)-x\|^{2}\right]\leq(1-\alpha)\|x\|^{2}\mathchar 59\relax\quad\forall x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}.

(3.4)

It is well known that whenever a compressor
\mathcal{C}(x)
satisfies (
3.3
), then the scaled compressor
\mathcal{C}(x)/(\omega+1)
satisfies (
3.4
) with
\alpha=(\omega+1)^{-1}
. In this sense, the class of contractive compressors includes all unbiased compressors as well. However, it is also strictly larger. For example, the
TopK
compressor, which retains the
K
largest elements in the absolute value of the vector it is applied to and replaces the rest by zeros, and happens to be very powerful in practice
(
Alistarh et al. 2018
)
, satisfies (
3.4
) with
\alpha=\nicefrac{{K}}{{d}}
, but does not satisfy (
3.3
). From now on, we write
\mathbb{C}(\alpha)
to denote the class of compressors satisfying (
3.4
).

It will be convenient to define the following functions of the contraction parameter

\begin{split}\theta&=\theta(\alpha)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}1-\sqrt{1-\alpha}\mathchar 59\relax\\
\beta&=\beta(\alpha)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\dfrac{1-\alpha}{1-\sqrt{1-\alpha}}\mathchar 59\relax\\
\xi&=\xi(\alpha)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\sqrt{\dfrac{\beta(\alpha)}{\theta(\alpha)}}=\dfrac{1+\sqrt{1-\alpha}}{\alpha}-1.\end{split}

(3.5)

Note that

0\leq\xi(\alpha)<\dfrac{2}{\alpha}-1.

The behavior of distributed algorithms utilizing unbiased compressors for solving (
3.1
) is relatively well-understood from a theoretical standpoint
(
Khirirat et al. 2018b
;
Mishchenko et al. 2019
;
Li et al. 2020b
;
Gorbunov et al. 2021
;
Tyurin and Richtárik 2023
)
. By now, the community possesses a robust theoretical understanding of the advantages such methods can offer and the mechanisms behind their efficacy
(
Gorbunov et al. 2020b
;
Khaled et al. 2023
;
Tyurin and Richtárik 2023
)
. However, it is well known that the class of contractive compressors includes some practically very powerful operators, such as the greedy sparsifier
TopK

(
Stich et al. 2018
;
Alistarh et al. 2018
)
and the low-rank approximator
RankK

(
Vogels et al. 2019
;
Safaryan et al. 2022
)
, which are biased, and hence their behavior is not explainable by the above developments. These compressors have demonstrated surprisingly effective performance in practice
(
Seide et al. 2014
;
Alistarh et al. 2018
)
, even when compared to the best results we can get with unbiased compressors
(
Szlendak et al. 2022
)
, and are indispensable on difficult tasks such as the fine-tuning of foundation models in a geographically distributed manner over slow networks
(
Wang et al. 2023b
)
.

However, our theoretical understanding of algorithms based on contractive compressors in general, and these powerful biased compressors in particular, is very weak. Indeed, while the SOTA theory involving unbiased compressors offers significant and often several-degrees-of-magnitude improvements over the baseline methods that do not use compression
(
Mishchenko et al. 2019
;
Horváth et al. 2023
;
Li et al. 2020b
;
Gorbunov et al. 2020b
;
Gorbunov et al. 2021
;
Tyurin and Richtárik 2023
)
, the best theory we currently have for methods that can provably work with contractive compressors, i.e., the theory behind the error feedback method called
EF21
developed by
Richtárik et al. 2021a
(see Algorithm
2
) and its variants
(
Fatkhullin et al. 2021
;
Condat et al. 2022
;
Fatkhullin et al. 2023
)
, merely matches the communication complexity of the underlying methods that do not use any compression
(
Szlendak et al. 2022
)
.

To the best of our knowledge, the only exception is the recent work of
Richtárik et al. 2023
, which demonstrates that in the
rare features
regime, the
EF21
method
(
Richtárik et al. 2021a
)
can be substantially improved in theory for this restricted class of optimization problems. However,
Richtárik et al. 2023
do not establish any improvements over the current best theoretical results for vanilla
EF21

(
Richtárik et al. 2021a
)
in the general smooth non-convex regime outlined in Section
3.1.2
, which is the focus of our investigation.

1:

Input:
initial model
x^{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
; initial gradient estimates
g_{1}^{0}\mathchar 59\relax g_{2}^{0}\mathchar 59\relax\dots\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}g_{n}^{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
stored at the server and the clients; step size
\gamma>0
; number of iterations
T>0

2:

Initialize:

g^{0}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}g_{i}^{0}
on the server

3:

for

t=0\mathchar 59\relax 1\mathchar 59\relax 2\mathchar 59\relax\dots\mathchar 59\relax T-1

do

4:

Server computes
x^{t+1}=x^{t}-\gamma g^{t}
and broadcasts
x^{t+1}
to all
n
clients

5:

for

i=1\mathchar 59\relax\dots\mathchar 59\relax n

on the clients in parallel

do

6:

Compute
u_{i}^{t}=\mathcal{C}_{i}^{t}(\nabla f_{i}(x^{t+1})-g_{i}^{t})
and update
g_{i}^{t+1}=g_{i}^{t}+u_{i}^{t}

7:

Send the compressed message
u_{i}^{t}
to the server

8:

end

for

9:

Server updates
g_{i}^{t+1}=g_{i}^{t}+u_{i}^{t}
for all
i\in[n]
, and computes
g^{t+1}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}g_{i}^{t+1}

10:

end

for

11:

Output:
Point
\hat{x}^{T}
chosen from the set
\{x^{0}\mathchar 59\relax\dots\mathchar 59\relax x^{T-1}\}
uniformly at random

Algorithm 2

EF21
: Error Feedback 2021 from
Richtárik et al. 2021a

[Existing]
.

3.1.2
Assumptions

We adopt the same very weak assumptions as those used by
Richtárik et al. 2021a
in their analysis of
EF21
.

Assumption 1
.

The function
f
is
L
-smooth, i.e., there exists
L>0
such that

\left\|\nabla f(x)-\nabla f(y)\right\|\leq L\left\|x-y\right\|\mathchar 59\relax\quad\forall x\mathchar 59\relax y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}.

(3.6)

Assumption 2
.

The functions
f_{i}
are
L_{i}
-smooth, i.e., for all
i\in[n]
there exists
L_{i}>0
such that

\left\|\nabla f_{i}(x)-\nabla f_{i}(y)\right\|\leq L_{i}\left\|x-y\right\|\mathchar 59\relax\quad\forall x\mathchar 59\relax y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}.

(3.7)

Note that if (
3.7
) holds, then (
3.6
) holds, and

L\leq{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}L_{\rm AM}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\dfrac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}L_{i}}.

So, Assumption
1
does
not
further limit the class of functions already covered by Assumption
2
. Indeed, it merely provides a new parameter
L
better characterizing the smoothness of
f
than the estimate
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}L_{\rm AM}}
obtainable from Assumption
2
could.

Since our goal in (
3.1
) is to minimize
f
, the below assumption is necessary for the problem to be meaningful.

Assumption 3
.

There exists
f^{\ast}\in\mathbb{R}
such that
\inf f\geq f^{\ast}
.

3.1.3
Contributions

1.

In our work, we improve the current SOTA theoretical communication complexity guarantees for distributed algorithms that work with contractive compressors in general, and empirically powerful biased compressors such as
TopK
and
RankK
in particular
(
Richtárik et al. 2021a
;
Fatkhullin et al. 2021
)
. In particular, under Assumptions
1
,
2
,
3
, the best known guarantees were obtained by
Richtárik et al. 2021a
for the
EF21
method: to find a (random) vector
\hat{x}^{T}
satisfying

\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\nabla f(\hat{x}^{T})\right\|^{2}\right]\leq\varepsilon\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}

Algorithm
2
requires

T=\mathcal{O}\left({\left(L+{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}L_{\rm QM}}\xi(\alpha)\right)}{\varepsilon}^{-1}\right)

iterations, where

\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}L_{\rm QM}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\sqrt{\dfrac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}L_{i}^{2}}
is the
Quadratic Mean
of the smoothness constants
L_{1}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\dots\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}L_{n}
. Our main finding is an improvement of this result to

T=\mathcal{O}\left({\left(L+{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}L_{\rm AM}}\xi(\alpha)\right)}{\varepsilon}^{-1}\right)\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}

(3.8)

where
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}L_{\rm AM}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\dfrac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}L_{i}
is the
Arithmetic Mean
of the smoothness constants
L_{1}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\dots\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}L_{n}
.

2.

We obtain this improvement in
three different ways:

i)

by
client cloning
(see Sections
3.2.1
and
3.2.2
and Theorem
7
),

ii)

by proposing a new
smootness-weighted
variant of
EF21
which we call
EF21-W
(see Section
3.2.3
and Theorem
8
),

iii)

and by a new
smoothness-weighted
analysis of classical
EF21
(see Section
3.2.4
and Theorem
9
).

3.

We obtain refined linear convergence results in cases under the Polyak-Łojasiewicz condition.

4.

Further, our analysis technique extends to many variants of
EF21
, including
EF21-SGD
which uses stochastic gradients instead of gradients (Section
E3
), and
EF21-PP
which enables partial participation of clients (Section
G3
).

5.

Our analysis also improves upon the results of
Richtárik et al. 2023
who study
EF21
in the
rare features
regime (Section
H3
).

6.

Finally, we validate our theory with suitable computational experiments (Sections
3.3
,
I3
and
J3
).

3.2
EF21 Reloaded: Our Discovery Story

We now take the reader along on a ride of our discovery process.

3.2.1
Step 1: Cloning the client with the worse smoothness constant

The starting point of our journey is a simple observation described in the following example.

Example 3
.

Let
n=4
and

f(x)=\dfrac{1}{4}(f_{1}(x)+f_{2}(x)+f_{3}(x)+f_{4}(x)).

Assume the smoothness constants
L_{1}\mathchar 59\relax L_{2}\mathchar 59\relax L_{3}
of
f_{1}\mathchar 59\relax f_{2}\mathchar 59\relax f_{3}
are equal to 1, and
L_{4}
is equal to
100
. In this case,
EF21
needs to run for

T_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\mathcal{O}\left({\left(L+{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}L_{\rm QM}}\xi(\alpha)\right)}{\varepsilon}^{-1}\right)=\mathcal{O}\left({\left(L+\sqrt{2501.5}\xi(\alpha)\right)}{\varepsilon}^{-1}\right)

iterations. Now, envision the existence of an additional machine capable of downloading the data from the fourth “problematic” machine. By rescaling local loss functions, we maintain the overall loss function as:

\displaystyle f(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\dfrac{1}{4}(f_{1}(x)+f_{2}(x)+f_{3}(x)+f_{4}(x))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\dfrac{1}{5}\left(\dfrac{5}{4}f_{1}(x)+\dfrac{5}{4}f_{2}(x)+\dfrac{5}{4}f_{3}(x)+\dfrac{5}{8}f_{4}(x)+\dfrac{5}{8}f_{4}(x)\right)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\tilde{f}(x).

Rescaling of the functions modifies the smoothness constants to
\hat{L}_{i}=\dfrac{5}{4}L_{i}
for
i=1\mathchar 59\relax 2\mathchar 59\relax 3
, and
\hat{L}_{i}=\dfrac{5}{8}L_{4}
for
i=4\mathchar 59\relax 5
.
EF21
, launched on this setting of five nodes, requires

T_{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\mathcal{O}\left({\left(L+{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\tilde{L}_{\rm QM}}\xi(\alpha)\right)}{\varepsilon}^{-1}\right)\approx\mathcal{O}\left({\left(L+\sqrt{1564}\xi(\alpha)\right)}{\varepsilon}^{-1}\right)

iterations, where
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\tilde{L}_{\rm QM}}
is the quadratic mean of the new smoothness constants
\hat{L}_{1}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\dots\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\hat{L}_{5}
.

This simple observation highlights that the addition of just one more client significantly enhances the convergence rate. Indeed,
EF21
requires approximately
\nicefrac{{\xi(\alpha)}}{{\varepsilon}}\left(\sqrt{2501.5}-\sqrt{1564}\right)\approx 10\,\nicefrac{{\xi(\alpha)}}{{\varepsilon}}
fewer iterations. We will generalize this client cloning idea in the next section.

3.2.2
Step 2: Generalizing the cloning idea

We will now take the above motivating example further, allowing each client
i
to be cloned arbitrarily many (
N_{i}
) times. Let us see where this gets us. For each
i\in[n]
, let
N_{i}
denote a positive integer. We define
N\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}N_{i}
(the total number of clients after cloning), and observe that
f
can be equivalently written as

\displaystyle f(x)

\displaystyle\overset{\eqref{ch3:eq:main_problem}}{=}

\displaystyle\dfrac{1}{n}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}f_{i}(x)

(3.9)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\dfrac{1}{n}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}\sum\limits_{j=1}^{N_{i}}\dfrac{1}{N_{i}}f_{i}(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\dfrac{1}{N}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}\sum\limits_{j=1}^{N_{i}}\dfrac{N}{nN_{i}}f_{i}(x)=\dfrac{1}{N}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}\sum\limits_{j=1}^{N_{i}}f_{ij}(x)\mathchar 59\relax

where

f_{ij}(x)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\dfrac{N}{nN_{i}}f_{i}(x)
for all
i\in[n]
and
j\in[N_{i}].

Notice that we scaled the functions as before and that
f_{ij}
is
L_{ij}
-smooth, where
L_{ij}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\dfrac{N}{nN_{i}}L_{i}
.

Analysis of the convergence rate.
The performance of
EF21
, when applied to the Problem (
3.9
) involving
N
clients, depends on the quadratic mean of the new smoothness constants:

\displaystyle M(N_{1}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\dots\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}N_{n})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\sqrt{\dfrac{1}{N}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}\sum\limits_{j=1}^{N_{i}}L_{ij}^{2}}=\sqrt{\dfrac{1}{N}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}{N_{i}}\cdot\left(\dfrac{N}{nN_{i}}L_{i}\right)^{2}}

(3.10)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sqrt{\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}\dfrac{N}{n^{2}N_{i}}L_{i}^{2}}=\dfrac{1}{n}\sqrt{\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}\dfrac{L_{i}^{2}}{N_{i}/N}}.

Note that if
N_{i}=1
for all
i\in[n]
, then
M(1\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\dots\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}1)={\color[rgb]{1,0,0}L_{\rm QM}}
.

Optimal choice of cloning frequencies.
Our goal is to find integer values
N_{1}\in\mathbb{N}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\dots\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}N_{n}\in\mathbb{N}
minimizing the function
M(N_{1}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\dots\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}M_{n})
defined in (
3.10
). While we do not have a closed-form formula for the global minimizer, we can explicitly find a solution that is at most
\sqrt{2}
times worse than the optimal one in terms of the objective value. In particular, if we let
N^{\star}_{i}=\left\lceil\nicefrac{{L_{i}}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}L_{\rm AM}}}}\right\rceil
for all
i\in[n]
, then

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}L_{\rm AM}}\leq\min\limits_{N_{1}\in\mathbb{N}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\dots\mathchar 59\relax N_{n}\in\mathbb{N}}M(N_{1}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\dots\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}N_{n})\leq M(N^{\star}_{1}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\dots\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}N^{\star}_{n})\leq\sqrt{2}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}L_{\rm AM}}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}

and moreover,
n\leq N^{\star}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\sum_{i}N^{\star}_{i}\leq 2n
. That is, we need at most double the number of clients in our client cloning construction. See Lemma
2
in the Appendix for details.

By applying
EF21
theory from
(
Richtárik et al. 2021a
)
to Problem (
3.9
) involving
N^{\star}
clients, we obtain the advertised improvement from
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}L_{\rm QM}}
to
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}L_{\rm AM}}
.

Theorem 7

(
Convergence of
EF21
applied to Problem (
3.9
) with
N^{\star}
machines
)
.

Consider Algorithm
2
(
EF21
) applied to the “cloning reformulation”
(
3.9
)
of the distributed optimization Problem
(
3.1
)
, where
N^{\star}_{i}=\left\lceil\nicefrac{{L_{i}}}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}L_{\rm AM}}}}\right\rceil
for all
i\in[n]
. Let Assumptions
1
,
2
,
3
hold, assume that

\mathcal{C}_{ij}^{t}\in\mathbb{C}(\alpha)
for all
i\in[n]
,
j\in[N_{i}]
and
t\geq 0
, set

G^{t}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\dfrac{1}{N}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{N}\sum\limits_{j=1}^{N_{i}}\left\|g_{ij}^{t}-\nabla f_{ij}(x^{t})\right\|^{2}\mathchar 59\relax

and let the step size satisfy

0<\gamma\leq\dfrac{1}{L+\sqrt{2}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}L_{\rm AM}}\xi(\alpha)}.

If for
T\geq 1
we define
\hat{x}^{T}
as an element of the set
\{x^{0}\mathchar 59\relax x^{1}\mathchar 59\relax\dots\mathchar 59\relax x^{T-1}\}
chosen uniformly at random, then

\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\nabla f(\hat{x}^{T})\right\|^{2}\right]\leq\dfrac{2(f(x^{0})-f^{\ast})}{\gamma T}+\dfrac{G^{0}}{\theta(\alpha)T}.

When we choose the largest allowed step size and
g_{ij}^{0}=\nabla f_{ij}(x^{0})
for all
i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j
, this leads to the complexity (
3.8
); that is, by cloning client machines, we can replace
\color[rgb]{1,0,0}L_{\rm QM}
in the standard rate with
\sqrt{2}\color[rgb]{0,0,1}L_{\rm AM}
. A similar result can be obtained even if we do not ignore the integrality constraint, but we do not include it for brevity reasons. However, it is important to note that the cloning approach has several straightforward shortcomings, which we will address in the next section.
1
1

1

In our work, we address an optimization problem of the form

\min_{\begin{subarray}{c}w_{j}\geq 0\ \forall j\in[n];\sum_{i=1}^{n}w_{i}=1\end{subarray}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\dfrac{a_{i}^{2}}{w_{i}}\mathchar 59\relax

where
a_{i}
represent certain constants. This formulation bears a resemblance to the meta-problem in the importance sampling strategy discussed in
(
Zhao and Zhang 2015
)
. Despite the apparent similarities in the abstract formulation, our approach and the one in the referenced work diverge significantly in both motivation and implementation. While
Zhao and Zhang 2015
applies importance sampling to reduce the variance of a stochastic gradient estimator by adjusting sampling probabilities, our method involves adjusting client cloning weights without sampling. Furthermore, our gradient estimator is biased, unlike the unbiased estimator in the referenced paper, and we aim to minimize the quadratic mean of the smoothness constants, which is inherently different from the objectives in
Zhao and Zhang 2015
. Although both approaches can be expressed through a similar mathematical framework, they are employed in vastly different contexts, and any parallelism may be coincidental rather than indicative of a direct connection.

3.2.3
Step 3: From client cloning to update weighting

It is evident that employing client cloning improves the convergence rate. Nevertheless, there are obvious drawbacks associated with this approach. Firstly, it necessitates a larger number of computational devices, rendering its implementation less appealing from a resource allocation perspective. Secondly, the utilization of
EF21
with cloned machines results in a departure from the principles of Federated Learning, as it inherently compromises user privacy – transferring data from one device to another is prohibited in FL.

However, a simpler approach to implementing the cloning idea emerges when we assume the compressors used to be
deterministic
. To illustrate this, let us initially examine how we would typically implement
EF21
with cloned machines:

\displaystyle x^{t+1}=x^{t}-\gamma\dfrac{1}{N}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}\sum\limits_{j=1}^{N_{i}}g_{ij}^{t}\mathchar 59\relax

(3.11)

\displaystyle g_{ij}^{t+1}=g_{ij}^{t}+\mathcal{C}_{ij}^{t}(\nabla f_{ij}(x^{t+1})-g_{ij}^{t})\mathchar 59\relax\quad i\in[n]\mathchar 59\relax\quad j\in[N_{i}].

(3.12)

We will now rewrite the same method in a different way. Assume we choose
g_{ij}^{0}=g_{i}^{0}
for all
j\in[N_{i}]
. We show by induction that
g_{ij}^{t}
is the same for all
j\in[N_{i}]
. We have just seen that this holds for
t=0
. Assume this holds for some
t
. Then since
\nabla f_{ij}(x^{t+1})=\dfrac{N}{nN_{i}}\nabla f_{i}(x^{t+1})
for all
j\in[N_{i}]
combined with the induction hypothesis, (
3.12
) and the determinism of
\mathcal{C}_{ij}^{t}
, we see that
g_{ij}^{t+1}
is the same for all
j\in[N_{i}]
. Let us define
g_{i}^{t}\equiv g_{ij}^{t}
for all
t
. This is a valid definition since we have shown that
g_{ij}^{t}
does not depend on
j
. Because of all of the above, iterations (
3.11
), (
3.12
) can be equivalently written in the form

\displaystyle x^{t+1}=x^{t}-\gamma\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}w_{i}}g_{i}^{t}\mathchar 59\relax

(3.13)

\displaystyle g_{i}^{t+1}=g_{i}^{t}+\mathcal{C}_{i}^{t}\left(\dfrac{1}{n{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}w_{i}}}\nabla f_{i}(x^{t+1})-g_{i}^{t}\right)\mathchar 59\relax\quad i\in[n]\mathchar 59\relax

(3.14)

where
{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}w_{i}}=\dfrac{L_{i}}{\sum_{j}L_{j}}
.

This transformation effectively enables us to operate the method on the original
n
clients, eliminating the need for
N
clients! This refinement has led to the creation of a new algorithm that outperforms
EF21
in terms of convergence rate, which we call
EF21-W
(Algorithm
3
).

1:

Input:
initial model parameters
x^{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
; initial gradient estimates
g_{1}^{0}\mathchar 59\relax g_{2}^{0}\mathchar 59\relax\dots\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}g_{n}^{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
stored at the server and the clients; weights
{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}w_{i}}=\nicefrac{{L_{i}}}{{\sum_{j}L_{j}}}
; step size
\gamma>0
; number of iterations
T>0

2:

Initialize:

g^{0}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}w_{i}}g_{i}^{0}
on the server

3:

for

t=0\mathchar 59\relax 1\mathchar 59\relax 2\mathchar 59\relax\dots\mathchar 59\relax T-1

do

4:

Server computes
x^{t+1}=x^{t}-\gamma g^{t}
and broadcasts
x^{t+1}
to all
n
clients

5:

for

i=1\mathchar 59\relax\dots\mathchar 59\relax n

on the clients in parallel

do

6:

Compute
u_{i}^{t}=\mathcal{C}_{i}^{t}(\dfrac{1}{n{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}w_{i}}}\nabla f_{i}(x^{t+1})-g_{i}^{t})
and update
g_{i}^{t+1}=g_{i}^{t}+u_{i}^{t}

7:

Send the compressed message
u_{i}^{t}
to the server

8:

end

for

9:

Server updates
g_{i}^{t+1}=g_{i}^{t}+u_{i}^{t}
for all
i\in[n]
, and computes
g^{t+1}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}w_{i}}g_{i}^{t+1}

10:

end

for

11:

Output:
Point
\hat{x}^{T}
chosen from the set
\{x^{0}\mathchar 59\relax\dots\mathchar 59\relax x^{T-1}\}
uniformly at random

Algorithm 3

EF21-W
: Weighted Error Feedback 2021.

While we relied on assuming that the compressors are
deterministic
in order to motivate the transition from
N
to
n
clients, it turns out that
EF21-W
converges without the need to invoke this assumption.

Theorem 8

(
Theory for
EF21-W
)
.

Consider Algorithm
3
(
EF21-W
) applied to the distributed optimization Problem
(
3.1
)
.
Let Assumptions
1
,
2
,
3
hold, assume that
\mathcal{C}_{i}^{t}\in\mathbb{C}(\alpha)
for all
i\in[n]
and
t\geq 0
, set

G^{t}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}w_{i}}\left\|g_{i}^{t}-\dfrac{1}{n\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}w_{i}}\nabla f_{i}(x^{t})\right\|^{2}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}

where
{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}w_{i}}=\dfrac{L_{i}}{\sum_{j}L_{j}}
for all
i\in[n]
, and let the step size satisfy

0<\gamma\leq\dfrac{1}{L+{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}L_{\rm AM}}\xi(\alpha)}.

If for
T>1
we define
\hat{x}^{T}
as an element of the set
\{x^{0}\mathchar 59\relax x^{1}\mathchar 59\relax\dots\mathchar 59\relax x^{T-1}\}
chosen uniformly at random, then

\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\nabla f(\hat{x}^{T})\right\|^{2}\right]\leq\dfrac{2(f(x^{0})-f^{\ast})}{\gamma T}+\dfrac{G^{0}}{\theta(\alpha)T}.

(3.15)

3.2.4
Step 4: From weights in the algorithm to weights in the analysis

In the preceding section, we introduced a novel algorithm:
EF21-W
. While it bears some resemblance to the vanilla
EF21
algorithm
(
Richtárik et al. 2021a
)
(we recover it for uniform weights), the reliance on particular non-uniform weights enables it to achieve a faster convergence rate. However, this is not the end of the story as another insight reveals yet another surprise.

Let us consider the scenario when the compressors in Algorithm
3
are
positively homogeneous
2
2

2

A compressor
\mathcal{C}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
is positively homogeneous if
\mathcal{C}(tx)=t\mathcal{C}(x)
for all
t>0
and
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
. Introducing the new variable
h_{i}^{t}=n{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}w_{i}}g_{i}^{t}
, we can reformulate the gradient update in Algorithm
3
to

\displaystyle h_{i}^{t+1}

\displaystyle={n{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}w_{i}}}g_{i}^{t+1}\overset{\eqref{ch3:eq:AlgStep2X}}{=}{n{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}w_{i}}}\left[g_{i}^{t}+\mathcal{C}_{i}^{t}\left(\dfrac{\nabla f_{i}(x^{t+1})}{n{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}w_{i}}}-g_{i}^{t}\right)\right]=h_{i}^{t}+\mathcal{C}_{i}^{t}(\nabla f_{i}(x^{t})-h_{i}^{t})\mathchar 59\relax

indicating that
h_{i}^{t}
adheres to the update rule of the vanilla
EF21
method! Furthermore, the iterates
x^{t}
also follow the same rule as
EF21
:

x^{t+1}\overset{\eqref{ch3:eq:AlgStep1X}}{=}x^{t}-\gamma\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}w_{i}}g^{t}=x^{t}-\gamma\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}w_{i}}\dfrac{1}{n{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}w_{i}}}h_{i}^{t}=x^{t}-\gamma\dfrac{1}{n}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}h_{i}^{t}.

So, what does this mean?

One interpretation suggests that for positively homogeneous contractive compressors, the vanilla
EF21
algorithm is equivalent to
EF21-W
, and hence inherits its faster convergence rate that depends on
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}L_{\rm AM}}
rather than on
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}L_{\rm QM}}
. However, it turns out that we can establish the same result without having to resort to positive homogeneity altogether. For example, the "natural compression" quantizer, which rounds the components of an input vector from

\mathbb{R}^{d}
elementwise to one of the two nearest powers of two is not positively homogeneous
(
Horváth et al. 2019
)
.

Theorem 9

(
New theory for
EF21
).

Consider Algorithm
2
(
EF21
) applied to the distributed optimization Problem
(
3.1
)
. Let Assumptions
1
,
2
,
3
hold, assume that
\mathcal{C}_{i}^{t}\in\mathbb{C}(\alpha)
for all
i\in[n]
and
t\geq 0
, set

G^{t}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\dfrac{1}{n}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}\dfrac{1}{n{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}w_{i}}}\left\|g_{i}^{t}-\nabla f_{i}(x^{t})\right\|^{2}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}

where
{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}w_{i}}=\dfrac{L_{i}}{\sum_{j}L_{j}}
for all
i\in[n]
, and let the step size satisfy

0<\gamma\leq\dfrac{1}{L+{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}L_{\rm AM}}\xi(\alpha)}.

If for
T>1
we define
\hat{x}^{T}
as an element of the set
\{x^{0}\mathchar 59\relax x^{1}\mathchar 59\relax\dots\mathchar 59\relax x^{T-1}\}
chosen uniformly at random, then

\mathbb{E}\left[\|\nabla f(\hat{x}^{T})\|^{2}\right]\leq\dfrac{2(f(x^{0})-f^{\ast})}{\gamma T}+\dfrac{G^{0}}{\theta(\alpha)T}.

(3.16)

This last result effectively pushes the weights from the algorithm in
EF21-W
to the proof, which enabled us to show that the original
EF21
method also enjoys the same improvement: from
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}L_{\rm QM}}
to
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}L_{\rm AM}}
.

3.3
Experiments

3.3.1
Non-convex logistic regression on benchmark datasets

In our first experiment, we employed a
logistic regression
model with a non-convex regularizer, i.e.,

f_{i}(x)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\dfrac{1}{n_{i}}\sum\limits_{j=1}^{n_{i}}\log\left(1+\exp({-y_{ij}\cdot a_{ij}^{\top}x})\right)+\lambda\sum\limits_{j=1}^{d}\dfrac{x_{j}^{2}}{x_{j}^{2}+1}\mathchar 59\relax

where
(a_{ij}\mathchar 59\relax y_{ij})\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\{-1\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}1\}
represents the
j
-th data point out from a set of
n_{i}
data points stored at client
i
, and
\lambda>0
denotes a regularization coefficient. We utilized six datasets from
LIBSVM

(
Chang and Lin 2011a
)
. The dataset shuffling strategy, detailed in Appendix
I3.5
, was employed to emulate heterogeneous data distribution. Each client was assigned the same number of data points. Figure
3.1
provides a comparison between
EF21
employing the original step size
(
Richtárik et al. 2021a
)
and
EF21-W
with the better step size. The initial gradient estimators were chosen as
g_{i}^{0}=\nabla f_{i}(x^{0})
for all
i\in[n]
. As evidenced empirically, the
EF21-W
algorithm emerges as a practical choice when utilized in situations characterized by high variance in smoothness constants. As evident from the plots, the algorithm employing the new step size exhibits superior performance compared to its predecessor.

Next, we conducted a comparative analysis of the performance of
EF21-W-PP
and
EF21-W-SGD
, as elucidated in the appendix, compared to their non-weighted counterparts. In the
EF21-PP
/
EF21-W-PP
algorithms, each client participated independently in each round with probability
p_{i}=0.5
. Moreover, in the case of
EF21-SGD
/
EF21-W-SGD
algorithms, a single data point was stochastically sampled from a uniform distribution at each client during each iteration of the algorithm. As observed in Figure
3.2
, the algorithms employing the new learning rates demonstrate faster convergence. Notably, Figure
3.2
(c) depicts more pronounced oscillations with updated step sizes, as the new analysis permits larger step sizes, which can induce oscillations in stochastic methods.

3.3.2
Non-convex linear model on synthetic datasets

In our second set of experiments, we trained a linear regression model with a non-convex regularizer. The function
f_{i}
for the linear regression problem is defined as follows:

f_{i}(x)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\dfrac{1}{n_{i}}\left\|\mathbf{A}_{i}x-{b_{i}}\right\|^{2}+\lambda\sum_{j=1}^{d}\dfrac{x_{j}^{2}}{x_{j}^{2}+1}.

Here,
\mathbf{A}_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{n_{i}\times d}
and
b_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{n_{i}}
represent the feature matrix and labels stored on client
i
encompassing
n_{i}
data points. The data employed in four experiments, as illustrated in Figure
3.3
, was generated in such a manner that the smoothness constant
L
remained fixed, while
L_{i}
varied so that the difference between two crucial to analysis terms
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}L_{\rm QM}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}L_{\rm AM}}
changed from a relatively large value to negligible. As evident from Figure
3.3
, the performance of
EF21-W
consistently matches or surpasses that of the original
EF21
, particularly in scenarios characterized by significant variations in the smoothness constants. For additional details and supplementary experiments, we refer the reader to Sections
I3
and
J3
.

.

3.4
Conclusions

In this work, we present significant improvements to the
EF21
algorithm and its extensions, which are distributed optimization algorithms that utilize contractive compressors, achieving state-of-the-art theoretical communication complexity guarantees. We demonstrate both theoretically and empirically that our improvements are fundamental. These enhancements apply not only to
EF21
, but also to its variants addressing stochastic gradients (
EF21-W-SGD
, Section
E3
), partial client participation (
EF21-W-PP
, Section
G3
), and
EF21
in the
rare features
regime (Section
H3
). We share our discovery process with the reader, hoping it will be helpful to researchers encountering challenges at any stage of their research journey. We believe that the empirical success of
EF21
stems from a complex underlying factor and that further discoveries in this area are needed.

Appendices to Chapter
3

A3
Basic Results and Lemmas

In this section, we offer a few results that serve as essential prerequisites for establishing the main findings.

A3.1
Optimal client cloning frequencies

Lemma 1

(Optimal weights)
.

Let
a_{i}>0
for
i\in[n]
. Then

\min\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}w_{1}>0\mathchar 59\relax\dots\mathchar 59\relax w_{n}>0\\
\sum_{i=1}^{n}w_{i}=1\end{subarray}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\dfrac{a_{i}^{2}}{w_{i}}=\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\right)^{2}\mathchar 59\relax

(3.17)

which is achieved when
w^{\ast}_{i}=\dfrac{a_{i}}{\sum_{j}a_{j}}
. This means that

\min\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}w_{1}>0\mathchar 59\relax\dots\mathchar 59\relax w_{n}>0\\
\sum_{i=1}^{n}w_{i}=1\end{subarray}}\dfrac{1}{n}\sqrt{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\dfrac{a_{i}^{2}}{w_{i}}}=\dfrac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}.

(3.18)

We now show that the cloning frequencies given by
N^{\star}_{i}=\left\lceil\dfrac{L_{i}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}L_{\rm AM}}}\right\rceil
form a
\sqrt{2}
-approximation for the optimization problem of finding the optimal integer client frequencies.

Lemma 2

(
\sqrt{2}
-approximation)
.

If we let
N^{\star}_{i}=\left\lceil\dfrac{L_{i}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}L_{\rm AM}}}\right\rceil
for all
i\in[n]
, then

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}L_{\rm AM}}\leq\min_{N_{1}\in\mathbb{N}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\dots\mathchar 59\relax N_{n}\in\mathbb{N}}M(N_{1}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\dots\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}N_{n})\leq M(N^{\star}_{1}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\dots\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}N^{\star}_{n})\leq\sqrt{2}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}L_{\rm AM}}.

Proof.

Recall that

M(N_{1}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\dots\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}N_{n})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\dfrac{1}{n}\sqrt{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\dfrac{L_{i}^{2}}{N_{i}/N}}.

The first inequality in the claim follows by relaxing the integrality constraints, which gives us the bound

\min\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}w_{1}>0\mathchar 59\relax\dots\mathchar 59\relax w_{n}>0\\
\sum_{i=1}^{n}w_{i}=1\end{subarray}}\dfrac{1}{n}\sqrt{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\dfrac{L_{i}^{2}}{w_{i}}}\leq\min_{N_{1}\in\mathbb{N}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\dots\mathchar 59\relax N_{n}\in\mathbb{N}}\dfrac{1}{n}\sqrt{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\dfrac{L_{i}^{2}}{N_{i}/N}}\mathchar 59\relax

and subsequently applying Lemma
1
.

Next, we argue that the quantity
N^{\star}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\sum_{i}N^{\star}_{i}
is at most
2n
. Indeed,

N^{\star}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}N^{\star}_{i}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left\lceil\dfrac{L_{i}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}L_{\rm AM}}}\right\rceil\leq\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(\dfrac{L_{i}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}L_{\rm AM}}}+1\right)=2n.

(3.19)

We will now use this to bound
M(N^{\star}_{1}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\dots\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}N^{\star}_{n})
from above:

\displaystyle M(N^{\star}_{1}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\dots\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}N^{\star}_{n})=\dfrac{1}{n}\sqrt{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\dfrac{L_{i}^{2}}{N^{\star}_{i}/N^{\star}}}\overset{\eqref{ch3:eq:98y08fd=-=f-d}}{\leq}\dfrac{\sqrt{2}}{\sqrt{n}}\sqrt{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\dfrac{L_{i}^{2}}{N^{\star}_{i}}}=\dfrac{\sqrt{2}}{\sqrt{n}}\sqrt{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\dfrac{\dfrac{L_{i}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}L_{\rm AM}}}}{N^{\star}_{i}}L_{i}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}L_{\rm AM}}}.

Since
\dfrac{\dfrac{L_{i}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}L_{\rm AM}}}}{N^{\star}_{i}}\leq 1
for all
i\in[n]
, as stated in Lemma
2
, the proof proceeds as follows:

\displaystyle M(N^{\star}_{1}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\dots\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}N^{\star}_{n})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\dfrac{\sqrt{2}}{\sqrt{n}}\sqrt{\sum_{i=1}^{n}L_{i}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}L_{\rm AM}}}=\dfrac{\sqrt{2}}{\sqrt{n}}\sqrt{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}L_{\rm AM}}}\sqrt{\sum_{i=1}^{n}L_{i}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sqrt{2}\sqrt{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}L_{\rm AM}}}\sqrt{\dfrac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}L_{i}}=\sqrt{2}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}L_{\rm AM}}.

∎

A3.2
Descent lemma

Lemma 3

(
Li et al. 2021a
)
.

Let Assumption
1
hold and
x^{t+1}=x^{t}-\gamma g^{t}
, where
g^{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
is any vector, and
\gamma>0
is any scalar. Then, we have

\displaystyle f(x^{t+1})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle f(x^{t})-\dfrac{\gamma}{2}\|\nabla f(x^{t})\|^{2}

(3.20)

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle\left(\dfrac{1}{2\gamma}-\dfrac{L}{2}\right)\|x^{t+1}-x^{t}\|^{2}+\dfrac{\gamma}{2}\|g^{t}-\nabla f(x^{t})\|^{2}.

A3.3
Young’s inequality

Lemma 4

(Young’s inequality)
.

For any
a\mathchar 59\relax b\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and any positive scalar
s>0
it holds that

\|a+b\|^{2}\leq(1+s)\|a\|^{2}+(1+s^{-1})\|b\|^{2}.

(3.21)

A3.4
2
-Suboptimal but simple step size rule

Lemma 5

(Lemma 5,
Richtárik et al. 2021a
)
.

Let
a\mathchar 59\relax b>0
. If

0<\gamma\leq\dfrac{1}{\sqrt{a}+b}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}

then

a\gamma^{2}+b\gamma\leq 1.

Moreover, the bound is tight up to the factor of 2 since

\dfrac{1}{\sqrt{a}+b}\leq\min\left\{\dfrac{1}{\sqrt{a}}\mathchar 59\relax\dfrac{1}{b}\right\}\leq\dfrac{2}{\sqrt{a}+b}.

A3.5
Optimal coefficient in Young’s inequality

Lemma 6

(Lemma 3,
Richtárik et al. 2021a
)
.

Let
0<\alpha\leq 1
and for
s>0
, let
\theta(\alpha\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}s)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}1-(1-\alpha)(1+s)
and
\beta(\alpha\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}s)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}(1-\alpha)\left(1+s^{-1}\right)
. Then, the solution of the optimization problem

\min\limits_{s}\left\{\dfrac{\beta(\alpha\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}s)}{\theta(\alpha\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}s)}:0<s<\dfrac{\alpha}{1-\alpha}\right\}

(3.22)

is given by

s^{\ast}=\dfrac{1}{\sqrt{1-\alpha}}-1.

Furthermore,

\theta(\alpha\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}s^{\ast})=1-\sqrt{1-\alpha}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}

and

\beta(\alpha\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}s^{\ast})=\dfrac{1-\alpha}{1-\sqrt{1-\alpha}}.

B3
Cloning Reformulation for Polyak-Łojasiewicz Functions

For completeness, we also provide a series of convergence results under Polyak-Łojasiewicz condition. We commence our exposition with the subsequent definition.

Assumption 4

(Polyak-Łojasiewicz)
.

There exists a positive scalar
\mu>0
such that for all points
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, the following inequality is satisfied:

f(x)-f(x^{\ast})\leq\dfrac{1}{2\mu}\|\nabla f(x)\|^{2}\mathchar 59\relax

(3.23)

where
x^{\ast}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\argmin f(x)
.

Theorem 10
.

Let Assumptions
1
,
2
, and
4
hold. Assume that
\mathcal{C}_{i}^{t}\in\mathbb{C}(\alpha)
for all
i\in[n]
and
t\geq 0
. Consider Algorithm
2
(
EF21
) applied to the “cloning” reformulation
(
3.9
)
of the distributed optimization Problem
(
3.1
)
, where
N_{i}^{\ast}=\lceil\dfrac{L_{i}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}L_{\rm AM}}}\rceil
for all
i\in[n]
. Let the step size be set as

0\leq\gamma\leq\min\left\{\left(L+\sqrt{2}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}L_{\rm AM}}\sqrt{\dfrac{2\beta}{\theta}}\right)^{-1}\mathchar 59\relax\dfrac{\theta}{2\mu}\right\}\mathchar 59\relax

where
\theta=1-\sqrt{1-\alpha}
and
\beta=\dfrac{1-\alpha}{1-\sqrt{1-\alpha}}
.
Let

\Psi^{t}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}f(x^{t})-f(x^{\ast})+\dfrac{\gamma}{\theta}G^{t}.

Then, for any
T\geq 0
, we have

\mathbb{E}\left[\Psi^{T}\right]\leq(1-\gamma\mu)^{T}\Psi^{0}.

Proof.

This theorem is a corollary of Theorem 2 in
Richtárik et al. 2021a
and Lemma
2
.
∎

C3
Proof of Theorem
8
(Theory for EF21-W)

In this section, we present a proof for Theorem
8
. To start this proof, we establish a corresponding contraction Lemma. We define the following quantities:

G_{i}^{t}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\left\|g_{i}^{t}-\dfrac{\nabla f_{i}(x^{t})}{nw_{i}}\right\|^{2};\qquad G^{t}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}w_{i}G_{i}^{t}\mathchar 59\relax

(3.24)

where the weights
w_{i}
are defined as specified in Algorithm
3
, that is,

w_{i}=\dfrac{L_{i}}{\sum_{j=1}^{n}L_{j}}.

(3.25)

C3.1
A lemma

With these definitions in place, we are now prepared to proceed to the Lemma.

Lemma 7
.

Let
\mathcal{C}_{i}^{t}\in\mathbb{C}(\alpha)
for all
i\in[n]
and
t\geq 0
. Let
W^{t}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\{g_{1}^{t}\mathchar 59\relax g_{2}^{t}\mathchar 59\relax\dots\mathchar 59\relax g_{n}^{t}\mathchar 59\relax x^{t}\mathchar 59\relax x^{t+1}\}
. Then, for iterates of Algorithm
3
we have

\mathbb{E}\left[G_{i}^{t+1}\;|\;W^{t}\right]\leq(1-\theta(\alpha\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}s))G_{i}^{t}+\beta(\alpha\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}s)\dfrac{1}{n^{2}w_{i}^{2}}\left\|\nabla f_{i}(x^{t+1})-\nabla f_{i}(x^{t})\right\|^{2}\mathchar 59\relax

(3.26)

and

\mathbb{E}\left[G^{t+1}\right]{\leq}\left(1-\theta(\alpha\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}s)\right)\mathbb{E}\left[G^{t}\right]+\beta(\alpha\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}s){\color[rgb]{0,0,1}L_{\rm AM}^{2}}\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|x^{t+1}-x^{t}\right\|^{2}\right]\mathchar 59\relax

(3.27)

where
s>0
is an arbitrary positive scalar, and

\theta(\alpha\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}s)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}1-(1-\alpha)(1+s)\mathchar 59\relax\qquad\text{and}\qquad\beta(\alpha\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}s)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}(1-\alpha)\left(1+s^{-1}\right).

(3.28)

Proof.

The proof is straightforward and bears resemblance to a similar proof found in a prior work
(
Richtárik et al. 2021a
)
.

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[G_{i}^{t+1}\;|\;W^{t}\right]

\displaystyle\overset{\eqref{ch3:eq:weighted_def_grad_distortion}}{=}

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|g_{i}^{t+1}-\dfrac{\nabla f_{i}(x^{t+1})}{nw_{i}}\right\|^{2}\;|\;W^{t}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|g_{i}^{t}+\mathcal{C}_{i}^{t}\left(\dfrac{\nabla f_{i}(x^{t+1})}{nw_{i}}-g_{i}^{t}\right)-\dfrac{\nabla f_{i}(x^{t+1})}{nw_{i}}\right\|^{2}\;|\;W^{t}\right]

\displaystyle\overset{\eqref{ch3:eq:compressor_contraction}}{\leq}

\displaystyle(1-\alpha)\left\|\dfrac{\nabla f_{i}(x^{t+1})}{nw_{i}}-g_{i}^{t}\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(1-\alpha)\left\|\dfrac{\nabla f_{i}(x^{t+1})}{nw_{i}}-g_{i}^{t}+\dfrac{\nabla f_{i}(x^{t})}{nw_{i}}-\dfrac{\nabla f_{i}(x^{t})}{nw_{i}}\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\overset{\eqref{ch3:eq:young}}{\leq}

\displaystyle(1-\alpha)(1+s)\left\|\dfrac{\nabla f_{i}(x^{t})}{nw_{i}}-g_{i}^{t}\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad+(1-\alpha)\left(1+s^{-1}\right)\dfrac{1}{n^{2}w_{i}^{2}}\left\|\nabla f_{i}(x^{t+1})-\nabla f_{i}(x^{t})\right\|^{2}\mathchar 59\relax

with the final inequality holding for any positive scalar
s>0
. Consequently, we have successfully established the first part of the Lemma.

By employing (
3.24
) and the preceding inequality, we can derive the subsequent bound for the conditional expectation of
G^{t+1}
:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[G^{t+1}\mid W^{t}\right]

\displaystyle\overset{\eqref{ch3:eq:weighted_def_grad_distortion}}{=}

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{i=1}^{n}w_{i}G_{i}^{t+1}\mid W^{t}\right]

(3.29)

\displaystyle\overset{\eqref{ch3:eq:weighted_def_grad_distortion}}{=}

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}w_{i}\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|g_{i}^{t+1}-\dfrac{\nabla f_{i}(x^{t+1})}{nw_{i}}\right\|^{2}\mid W^{t}\right]

\displaystyle\overset{\eqref{ch3:eq:weighted_ind_grad_dist_evolution}}{\leq}

\displaystyle\left(1-\theta(\alpha\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}s)\right)\sum_{i=1}^{n}w_{i}\left\|g_{i}^{t}-\dfrac{\nabla f_{i}(x^{t})}{nw_{i}}\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\quad+\beta(\alpha\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}s)\sum_{i=1}^{n}\dfrac{w_{i}}{w_{i}^{2}n^{2}}\left\|\nabla f_{i}(x^{t+1})-\nabla f_{i}(x^{t})\right\|^{2}.

Applying Assumption
2
and (
3.25
), we further proceed to:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[G^{t+1}\mid W^{t}\right]

\displaystyle\overset{\eqref{ch3:eq:weighted_aux_1}}{\leq}

\displaystyle\left(1-\theta(\alpha\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}s)\right)\sum_{i=1}^{n}w_{i}\left\|g_{i}^{t}-\dfrac{\nabla f_{i}(x^{t})}{nw_{i}}\right\|^{2}

(3.30)

\displaystyle\qquad+\beta(\alpha\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}s)\sum_{i=1}^{n}\dfrac{w_{i}}{w_{i}^{2}n^{2}}\left\|\nabla f_{i}(x^{t+1})-\nabla f_{i}(x^{t})\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\overset{\eqref{ch3:eq:weighted_def_grad_distortion}}{=}

\displaystyle\left(1-\theta(\alpha\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}s)\right)G^{t}+\beta(\alpha\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}s)\sum_{i=1}^{n}\dfrac{1}{w_{i}n^{2}}\left\|\nabla f_{i}(x^{t+1})-\nabla f_{i}(x^{t})\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\overset{\eqref{ch3:eq:L_i}}{\leq}

\displaystyle\left(1-\theta(\alpha\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}s)\right)G^{t}+\beta(\alpha\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}s)\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\dfrac{L_{i}^{2}}{w_{i}n^{2}}\right)\left\|x^{t+1}-x^{t}\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\overset{\eqref{ch3:eq:weight_definition}}{=}

\displaystyle\left(1-\theta(\alpha\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}s)\right)G^{t}+\beta(\alpha\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}s)\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\dfrac{L_{i}^{2}}{\dfrac{L_{i}}{\left(\sum_{j=1}^{n}L_{j}\right)}n^{2}}\right)\left\|x^{t+1}-x^{t}\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(1-\theta(\alpha\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}s)\right)G^{t}+\beta(\alpha\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}s)\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\dfrac{L_{i}\sum_{j=1}^{n}L_{j}}{n^{2}}\right)\left\|x^{t+1}-x^{t}\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(1-\theta(\alpha\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}s)\right)G^{t}+\beta(\alpha\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}s){\color[rgb]{0,0,1}L_{\rm AM}^{2}}\left\|x^{t+1}-x^{t}\right\|^{2}.

Using the tower property, we get

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[G^{t+1}\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbb{E}\left[G^{t+1}\mid W^{t}\right]\right]

\displaystyle\overset{\eqref{ch3:eq:weighted_aux_2}}{\leq}

\displaystyle\left(1-\theta(\alpha\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}s)\right)\mathbb{E}\left[G^{t}\right]

\displaystyle\qquad+\beta(\alpha\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}s){\color[rgb]{0,0,1}L_{\rm AM}^{2}}\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|x^{t+1}-x^{t}\right\|^{2}\right]\mathchar 59\relax

and this finalizes the proof.
∎

C3.2
Main result

We are now prepared to establish the proof for Theorem
8
.

Proof.

Note that, according to (
3.13
), the gradient estimate for Algorithm
3
gets the following form:

g^{t}=\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}w_{i}g_{i}^{t}.

(3.31)

Using Lemma
3
and Jensen’s inequality applied to the function
x\mapsto\left\|x\right\|^{2}
(since
\sum_{i=1}^{n}w_{i}=1
), we obtain the following bound:

\displaystyle f(x^{t+1})

\displaystyle\overset{\eqref{ch3:eq:descent_lemma}}{\leq}

\displaystyle f(x^{t})-\dfrac{\gamma}{2}\left\|\nabla f(x^{t})\right\|^{2}-\left(\dfrac{1}{2\gamma}-\dfrac{L}{2}\right)\left\|x^{t+1}-x^{t}\right\|^{2}

(3.32)

\displaystyle\qquad+\dfrac{\gamma}{2}\left\|g^{t}-\sum_{i=1}^{n}\dfrac{\nabla f_{i}(x^{t})}{n}\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\overset{\eqref{ch3:eq:weighted_grad_estimate_def}}{=}

\displaystyle f(x^{t})-\dfrac{\gamma}{2}\left\|\nabla f(x^{t})\right\|^{2}-\left(\dfrac{1}{2\gamma}-\dfrac{L}{2}\right)\left\|x^{t+1}-x^{t}\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad+\dfrac{\gamma}{2}\left\|\sum_{i=1}^{n}w_{i}\left(g_{i}^{t}-\dfrac{\nabla f_{i}(x^{t})}{nw_{i}}\right)\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle f(x^{t})-\dfrac{\gamma}{2}\left\|\nabla f(x^{t})\right\|^{2}-\left(\dfrac{1}{2\gamma}-\dfrac{L}{2}\right)\left\|x^{t+1}-x^{t}\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad+\dfrac{\gamma}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{n}w_{i}\left\|g_{i}^{t}-\dfrac{\nabla f_{i}(x^{t})}{nw_{i}}\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\overset{\eqref{ch3:eq:weighted_def_grad_distortion}}{=}

\displaystyle f(x^{t})-\dfrac{\gamma}{2}\left\|\nabla f(x^{t})\right\|^{2}-\left(\dfrac{1}{2\gamma}-\dfrac{L}{2}\right)\left\|x^{t+1}-x^{t}\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad+\dfrac{\gamma}{2}G^{t}.

Subtracting
f^{\ast}
from both sides and taking expectation, we get

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[f(x^{t+1})-f^{\ast}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\left[f(x^{t})-f^{\ast}\right]-\dfrac{\gamma}{2}\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\nabla f(x^{t})\right\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\qquad-\left(\dfrac{1}{2\gamma}-\dfrac{L}{2}\right)\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|x^{t+1}-x^{t}\right\|^{2}\right]+\dfrac{\gamma}{2}\mathbb{E}\left[G^{t}\right].

(3.33)

Let

\displaystyle\delta^{t}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\mathbb{E}\left[f(x^{t})-f^{\ast}\right]\mathchar 59\relax

\displaystyle s^{t}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\mathbb{E}\left[G^{t}\right]\mathchar 59\relax

\displaystyle r^{t}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|x^{t+1}-x^{t}\right\|^{2}\right].

The last Equation (
3.33
) can be re-expressed using the new notation.

Subsequently, by adding (
3.27
) with a
\dfrac{\gamma}{2\theta(\alpha\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}s)}
multiplier, we obtain

\displaystyle\delta^{t+1}+\dfrac{\gamma}{2\theta(\alpha\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}s)}s^{t+1}

\displaystyle\overset{\eqref{ch3:eq:weighted_function_descent}}{\leq}

\displaystyle\delta^{t}-\dfrac{\gamma}{2}\left\|\nabla f(x^{t})\right\|^{2}-\left(\dfrac{1}{2\gamma}-\dfrac{L}{2}\right)r^{t}+\dfrac{\gamma}{2}s^{t}

\displaystyle\qquad+\dfrac{\gamma}{2\theta(\alpha\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}s)}s^{t+1}

\displaystyle\overset{\eqref{ch3:eq:weighted_full_grad_dist_evolution}}{\leq}

\displaystyle\delta^{t}-\dfrac{\gamma}{2}\left\|\nabla f(x^{t})\right\|^{2}-\left(\dfrac{1}{2\gamma}-\dfrac{L}{2}\right)r^{t}+\dfrac{\gamma}{2}s^{t}

\displaystyle\qquad+\dfrac{\gamma}{2\theta(\alpha\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}s)}\left(\beta(\alpha\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}s){\color[rgb]{0,0,1}L_{\rm AM}^{2}}r^{t}+(1-\theta(\alpha\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}s))s^{t}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\delta^{t}+\dfrac{\gamma}{2\theta(\alpha\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}s)}s^{t}-\dfrac{\gamma}{2}\left\|\nabla f(x^{t})\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad-\left(\dfrac{1}{2\gamma}-\dfrac{L}{2}-\dfrac{\gamma}{2\theta(\alpha\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}s)}\beta(\alpha\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}s){\color[rgb]{0,0,1}L_{\rm AM}^{2}}\right)r^{t}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\delta^{t}+\dfrac{\gamma}{2\theta(\alpha\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}s)}s^{t}-\dfrac{\gamma}{2}\left\|\nabla f(x^{t})\right\|^{2}.

The last inequality is a result of the bound

\gamma^{2}\dfrac{\beta(\alpha\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}s){\color[rgb]{0,0,1}L_{\rm AM}^{2}}}{\theta(\alpha\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}s)}+L\gamma\leq 1\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}

which is satisfied for the step size

\gamma\leq\dfrac{1}{L+{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}L_{\rm AM}}\xi(\alpha\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}s)}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}

where
\xi(\alpha\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}s)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\sqrt{\dfrac{\beta(\alpha\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}s)}{\theta(\alpha\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}s)}}
.
Maximizing the step size bound over the choice of
s
using Lemma
6
, we obtain the final step size.
By summing up inequalities for
t=0\mathchar 59\relax\ldots\mathchar 59\relax T-1\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}
we get

0\leq\delta^{T}+\dfrac{\gamma}{2\theta}s^{T}\leq\delta^{0}+\dfrac{\gamma}{2\theta}s^{0}-\dfrac{\gamma}{2}\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\nabla f(x^{t})\right\|^{2}\right].

Multiplying both sides by
\dfrac{2}{\gamma T}
, after rearranging we get

\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}\dfrac{1}{T}\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\nabla f(x^{t})\right\|^{2}\right]\leq\dfrac{2\delta^{0}}{\gamma T}+\dfrac{s^{0}}{\theta T}.

It remains to notice that the left-hand side can be interpreted as

\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\nabla f(\hat{x}^{T})\right\|^{2}\right]\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}

where
\hat{x}^{T}
is chosen from
\{x^{0}\mathchar 59\relax x^{1}\mathchar 59\relax\ldots\mathchar 59\relax x^{T-1}\}
uniformly at random.
∎

C3.3
Main result for Polyak-Łojasiewicz functions

The main result is presented next.

Theorem 11
.

Let Assumptions
1
,
2
, and
4
hold. Assume that
\mathcal{C}_{i}^{t}\in\mathbb{C}(\alpha)
for all
i\in[n]
and
t\geq 0
. Let the step size in Algorithm
3
be set as

0<\gamma\leq\min\left\{\dfrac{1}{L+\sqrt{2}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}L_{\rm AM}}\xi(\alpha)}\mathchar 59\relax\dfrac{\theta(\alpha)}{2\mu}\right\}.

Let

\Psi^{t}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}f(x^{t})-f(x^{\ast})+\dfrac{\gamma}{\theta}G^{t}.

Then, for any
T>0
the following inequality holds:

\mathbb{E}\left[\Psi^{T}\right]\leq(1-\gamma\mu)^{T}\Psi^{0}.

(3.34)

Proof.

We proceed as in the previous proof, starting from the descent Lemma
3
with the same vector but using the PL inequality (
3.23
) and subtracting
f(x^{\star})
from both sides:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[f(x^{t+1})-f(x^{\star})\right]

\displaystyle\overset{\eqref{ch3:eq:descent_lemma}}{\leq}

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[f(x^{t})-f(x^{\star})\right]-\dfrac{\gamma}{2}\left\|\nabla f(x^{t})\right\|^{2}

(3.35)

\displaystyle\qquad-\left(\dfrac{1}{2\gamma}-\dfrac{L}{2}\right)\left\|x^{t+1}-x^{t}\right\|^{2}+\dfrac{\gamma}{2}G^{t}

\displaystyle\overset{\eqref{ch3:eq:PL}}{\leq}

\displaystyle(1-\gamma\mu)\mathbb{E}\left[f(x^{t})-f(x^{\star})\right]

\displaystyle\qquad-\left(\dfrac{1}{2\gamma}-\dfrac{L}{2}\right)\left\|x^{t+1}-x^{t}\right\|^{2}+\dfrac{\gamma}{2}G^{t}.

Let

\displaystyle\delta^{t}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\mathbb{E}\left[f(x^{t})-f(x^{\star})\right]\mathchar 59\relax

\displaystyle s^{t}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\mathbb{E}\left[G^{t}\right]\mathchar 59\relax

\displaystyle r^{t}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|x^{t+1}-x^{t}\right\|^{2}\right].

Thus, after adding
\dfrac{\gamma}{\theta}s^{t+1}
to both sides of inequality (
3.44
), it can be rewritten as follows:

\displaystyle\delta^{t+1}+\dfrac{\gamma}{\theta}s^{t+1}

\displaystyle\overset{\eqref{ch3:eq:weighted_aux_4}}{\leq}

\displaystyle(1-\gamma\mu)\delta^{t}-\left(\dfrac{1}{2\gamma}-\dfrac{L}{2}\right)r^{t}+\dfrac{\gamma}{2}s^{t}

\displaystyle\qquad+\dfrac{\gamma}{\theta}s^{t+1}

\displaystyle\overset{\eqref{ch3:eq:weighted_full_grad_dist_evolution}}{\leq}

\displaystyle(1-\gamma\mu)\delta^{t}-\left(\dfrac{1}{2\gamma}-\dfrac{L}{2}\right)r^{t}+\dfrac{\gamma}{2}s^{t}

\displaystyle\qquad+\dfrac{\gamma}{\theta}\left((1-\theta)s^{t}+\beta\left(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}L_{i}\right)^{2}r^{t}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(1-\gamma\mu)\delta^{t}+\dfrac{\gamma}{\theta}\left(1-\dfrac{\theta}{2}\right)s^{t}-\left(\dfrac{1}{2\gamma}-\dfrac{L}{2}-\dfrac{\beta{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}L_{\rm AM}^{2}}\gamma}{\theta}\right)r^{t}\mathchar 59\relax

where
\theta
and
\beta
are set as in Lemma
6
.
Note that our extra assumption on the step size implies that
1-\dfrac{\theta}{2}\leq 1-\gamma\mu
and

\dfrac{1}{2\gamma}-\dfrac{L}{2}-\dfrac{\beta{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}L_{\rm AM}^{2}}\gamma}{\theta}\geq 0.

The last inequality follows from the bound

\gamma^{2}\dfrac{2\beta{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}L_{\rm AM}^{2}}}{\theta}+\gamma L\leq 1.

Thus,

\displaystyle\delta^{t+1}+\dfrac{\gamma}{\theta}s^{t+1}\leq(1-\gamma\mu)\left(\delta^{t}+\dfrac{\gamma}{\theta}s^{t}\right).

It remains to unroll the recurrence.
∎

D3
Proof of Theorem
9
(Improved Theory for EF21)

We commence by redefining gradient distortion as follows:

G^{t}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\dfrac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\dfrac{1}{w_{i}}\|\nabla f_{i}(x^{t})-g^{t}_{i}\|^{2}.

(3.36)

We recall that the gradient update step for standard
EF21
(Algorithm
2
) takes the following form:

\displaystyle g_{i}^{t+1}=g_{i}^{t}+\mathcal{C}_{i}^{t}(\nabla f_{i}(x^{t+1})-g_{i}^{t})\mathchar 59\relax

(3.37)

\displaystyle g^{t+1}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}g_{i}^{t+1}.

(3.38)

D3.1
Two lemmas

Once more, we start our proof with the contraction Lemma.

Lemma 8
.

Let
\mathcal{C}_{i}^{t}\in\mathbb{C}(\alpha)
for all
i\in[n]
and
t\geq 0
.

Define

W^{t}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\{g_{1}^{t}\mathchar 59\relax g_{2}^{t}\mathchar 59\relax\dots\mathchar 59\relax g_{n}^{t}\mathchar 59\relax x^{t}\mathchar 59\relax x^{t+1}\}.

Let Assumption
2
hold. Then

\mathbb{E}\left[G^{t+1}\;|\;W^{t}\right]\leq(1-\theta(\alpha\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}s))G^{t}+\beta(\alpha\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}s){\color[rgb]{0,0,1}L_{\rm AM}^{2}}\|x^{t+1}-x^{t}\|^{2}\mathchar 59\relax

(3.39)

where for any
s>0

\displaystyle\theta(\alpha\mathchar 59\relax s)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}1-(1-\alpha)(1+s)\mathchar 59\relax

\displaystyle\beta(\alpha\mathchar 59\relax s)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}(1-\alpha)(1+s^{-1}).

Proof.

The proof of this Lemma starts as the similar Lemma in the standard analysis of
EF21
:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[G^{t+1}\;|\;W^{t}\right]

\displaystyle\overset{\eqref{ch3:eq:new_def_distortion}}{=}

\displaystyle\dfrac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\dfrac{1}{w_{i}}\mathbb{E}\left[\|\nabla f_{i}(x^{t+1})-g^{t+1}_{i}\|^{2}\;|\;W^{t}\right]

\displaystyle\overset{\eqref{ch3:eq:standard_ind_grad_upd}}{=}

\displaystyle\dfrac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\dfrac{1}{w_{i}}\mathbb{E}\left[\|g_{i}^{t}+\mathcal{C}_{i}^{t}(\nabla f_{i}(x^{t+1})-g_{i}^{t})-\nabla f_{i}(x^{t+1})\|^{2}\;|\;W^{t}\right]

\displaystyle\overset{\eqref{ch3:eq:compressor_contraction}}{\leq}

\displaystyle\dfrac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\dfrac{1-\alpha}{w_{i}}\|\nabla f_{i}(x^{t+1})-g_{i}^{t})\|^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\dfrac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\dfrac{1-\alpha}{w_{i}}\|\nabla f_{i}(x^{t+1})-\nabla f_{i}(x^{t})+\nabla f_{i}(x^{t})-g_{i}^{t})\|^{2}

\displaystyle\overset{\eqref{ch3:eq:young}}{\leq}

\displaystyle\dfrac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\dfrac{1-\alpha}{w_{i}}\Big((1+s^{-1})\|\nabla f_{i}(x^{t+1})-\nabla f_{i}(x^{t}))\|^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad+(1+s)\|g_{i}^{t}-\nabla f_{i}(x^{t})\|^{2}\Big)

For all
s>0
we proceed with the proof as follows:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[G^{t+1}\;|\;W^{t}\right]

\displaystyle\overset{\eqref{ch3:eq:aux1}}{\leq}

\displaystyle\dfrac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\dfrac{1-\alpha}{w_{i}}\Big((1+s^{-1})\|\nabla f_{i}(x^{t+1})-\nabla f_{i}(x^{t}))\|^{2}

(3.41)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad+(1+s)\|g_{i}^{t}-\nabla f_{i}(x^{t})\|^{2}\Big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(1-\theta(\alpha\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}s))\dfrac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\dfrac{1}{w_{i}}\|g_{i}^{t}-\nabla f_{i}(x^{t})\|^{2}

\displaystyle+\dfrac{\beta(\alpha\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}s)}{n^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\dfrac{1}{w_{i}}\|\nabla f_{i}(x^{t+1})-\nabla f_{i}(x^{t}))\|^{2}

\displaystyle\overset{\eqref{ch3:eq:new_def_distortion}}{=}

\displaystyle(1-\theta(\alpha\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}s))G^{t}

\displaystyle+\dfrac{\beta(\alpha\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}s)}{n^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\dfrac{1}{w_{i}}\|\nabla f_{i}(x^{t+1})-\nabla f_{i}(x^{t})\|^{2}

\displaystyle\overset{\eqref{ch3:eq:L_i}}{\leq}

\displaystyle(1-\theta(\alpha\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}s))G^{t}

\displaystyle+\dfrac{\beta(\alpha\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}s)}{n^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\dfrac{L_{i}^{2}}{w_{i}}\|x^{t+1}-x^{t}\|^{2}.

Note that this is the exact place where the current analysis differs from the standard one. It fully coincides with it when
w_{i}=\dfrac{1}{n}
, i.e., when we assign the same weight for each individual gradient distortion
\|g_{i}^{t}-\nabla f_{i}(x^{t})\|^{2}
. However, applying weights according to “importance” of each function, we proceed as follows:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[G^{t+1}\;|\;W^{t}\right]

\displaystyle\overset{\eqref{ch3:eq:aux2}}{\leq}

\displaystyle(1-\theta(\alpha\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}s))G^{t}+\dfrac{\beta(\alpha\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}s)}{n^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\dfrac{L_{i}^{2}}{w_{i}}\|x^{t+1}-x^{t}\|^{2}

\displaystyle\overset{\eqref{ch3:eq:weight_definition}}{=}

\displaystyle(1-\theta(\alpha\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}s))G^{t}+\dfrac{\beta(\alpha\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}s)}{n^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\dfrac{L_{i}^{2}}{L_{i}}\left(\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}L_{i}\right)\|x^{t+1}-x^{t}\|^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(1-\theta(\alpha\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}s))G^{t}+\dfrac{\beta(\alpha\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}s)}{n^{2}}\sum_{j}L_{j}\left(\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}L_{i}\right)\|x^{t+1}-x^{t}\|^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(1-\theta(\alpha\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}s))G^{t}+\beta(\alpha\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}s){\color[rgb]{0,0,1}L_{\rm AM}^{2}}\|x^{t+1}-x^{t}\|^{2}\mathchar 59\relax

what finishes the proof.
∎

To prove the main convergence theorem, we also need the following Lemma.

Lemma 9
.

For the variable
g^{t}
from Algorithm
2
, the following inequality holds:

\|g^{t}-\nabla f(x^{t})\|^{2}\leq G^{t}.

(3.42)

Proof.

The proof is straightforward:

\displaystyle\|g^{t}-\nabla f(x^{t})\|^{2}

\displaystyle\overset{\eqref{ch3:eq:standard_average_grad}}{=}

\displaystyle\left\|\sum_{i=1}^{n}\dfrac{1}{n}\left(g_{i}^{t}-\nabla f_{i}(x^{t})\right)\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\|\sum_{i=1}^{n}w_{i}\dfrac{1}{w_{i}n}\left(g_{i}^{t}-\nabla f_{i}(x^{t})\right)\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}w_{i}\left\|\dfrac{1}{w_{i}n}\left(g_{i}^{t}-\nabla f_{i}(x^{t})\right)\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}\dfrac{1}{w_{i}n^{2}}\|g^{t}-\nabla f_{i}(x^{t})\|^{2}\quad\overset{\eqref{ch3:eq:new_def_distortion}}{=}\quad G^{t}\mathchar 59\relax

where the only inequality in this series of equations is derived using Jensen’s inequality.
∎

D3.2
Main result

We are now equipped with all the necessary tools to establish the convergence theorem.

Proof.

Let us define the Lyapunov function

\Phi^{t}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}f(x^{t})-f^{\ast}+\dfrac{\gamma}{2\theta(\alpha\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}s)}G^{t}.

Let us also define
W^{t}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\{g_{1}^{t}\mathchar 59\relax g_{2}^{t}\mathchar 59\relax\dots\mathchar 59\relax g_{n}^{t}\mathchar 59\relax x^{t}\mathchar 59\relax x^{t+1}\}
. We start as follows:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\Phi^{t+1}\;|\;W^{t}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[f(x^{t+1})-f^{\ast}\;|\;W^{t}\right]+\dfrac{\gamma}{2\theta(\alpha\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}s)}\mathbb{E}\left[G^{t+1}\;|\;W^{t}\right]

\displaystyle\overset{\eqref{ch3:eq:descent_lemma}}{\leq}

\displaystyle f(x^{t})-f^{\ast}-\dfrac{\gamma}{2}\|\nabla f(x^{t})\|^{2}-\left(\dfrac{1}{2\gamma}-\dfrac{L}{2}\right)\|x^{t+1}-x^{t}\|^{2}+

\displaystyle\qquad\dfrac{\gamma}{2}\|g^{t}-\nabla f(x^{t})\|^{2}+\dfrac{\gamma}{2\theta(\alpha\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}s)}\mathbb{E}\left[G^{t+1}\;|\;W^{t}\right]

\displaystyle\overset{\eqref{ch3:eq:distortion_connection}}{\leq}

\displaystyle f(x^{t})-f^{\ast}-\dfrac{\gamma}{2}\|\nabla f(x^{t})\|^{2}-\left(\dfrac{1}{2\gamma}-\dfrac{L}{2}\right)\|x^{t+1}-x^{t}\|^{2}+\dfrac{\gamma}{2}G^{t}

\displaystyle\qquad+\dfrac{\gamma}{2\theta(\alpha\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}s)}\mathbb{E}\left[G^{t+1}\;|\;W^{t}\right]

\displaystyle\overset{\eqref{ch3:eq:grad_distortion_evolution}}{\leq}

\displaystyle f(x^{t})-f^{\ast}-\dfrac{\gamma}{2}\|\nabla f(x^{t})\|^{2}-\left(\dfrac{1}{2\gamma}-\dfrac{L}{2}\right)\|x^{t+1}-x^{t}\|^{2}+\dfrac{\gamma}{2}G^{t}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle f(x^{t})-f^{\ast}+\dfrac{\gamma}{2\theta(\alpha\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}s)}G^{t}-\dfrac{\gamma}{2}\|\nabla f(x^{t})\|^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad-\underbrace{\left(\dfrac{1}{2\gamma}-\dfrac{L}{2}-\dfrac{\gamma\beta(\alpha\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}s)}{2\theta(\alpha\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}s)}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}L_{\rm AM}^{2}}\right)}_{\geq 0}\|x^{t+1}-x^{t}\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle f(x^{t})-f^{\ast}+\dfrac{\gamma}{2\theta(\alpha\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}s)}G^{t}-\dfrac{\gamma}{2}\|\nabla f(x^{t})\|^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Phi^{t}-\dfrac{\gamma}{2}\|\nabla f(x^{t})\|^{2}.

The inequality in the last but one line is valid if

\gamma\leq\dfrac{1}{L+{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}L_{\rm AM}}\sqrt{\dfrac{\beta(\alpha\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}s)}{\theta(\alpha\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}s)}}}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}

according to Lemma
5
. By optimizing the step size bound through the selection of
s
in accordance with Lemma
6
, we derive the final step size and establish the optimal value for
\theta
in defining the Lyapunov function. Applying the tower property and unrolling the recurrence, we finish the proof.
∎

D3.3
Main result for Polyak-Łojasiewicz functions

For completeness, we also provide a convergence result under Polyak-Łojasiewicz condition (Assumption
4
). The main result is presented next.

Theorem 12
.

Let Assumptions
1
,
2
, and
4
hold. Assume that
\mathcal{C}_{i}^{t}\in\mathbb{C}(\alpha)
for all
i\in[n]
and
t\geq 0
. Let the step size in Algorithm
3
be set as

0<\gamma\leq\min\left\{\dfrac{1}{L+\sqrt{2}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}L_{\rm AM}}\xi(\alpha)}\mathchar 59\relax\dfrac{\theta(\alpha\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}s)}{2\mu}\right\}.

Let

\Psi^{t}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}f(x^{t})-f(x^{\ast})+\dfrac{\gamma}{\theta(\alpha\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}s)}G^{t}.

Then, for any
T>0
the following inequality holds:

\mathbb{E}\left[\Psi^{T}\right]\leq(1-\gamma\mu)^{T}\Psi^{0}.

(3.43)

Proof.

We proceed as in the previous proof, starting from the descent Lemma with the same vector but using the PL inequality (
3.23
) and subtracting
f(x^{\star})
from both sides:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[f(x^{t+1})-f(x^{\star})\right]

\displaystyle\overset{\eqref{ch3:eq:descent_lemma}}{\leq}

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[f(x^{t})-f(x^{\star})\right]-\dfrac{\gamma}{2}\left\|\nabla f(x^{t})\right\|^{2}

(3.44)

\displaystyle-\left(\dfrac{1}{2\gamma}-\dfrac{L}{2}\right)\left\|x^{t+1}-x^{t}\right\|^{2}+\dfrac{\gamma}{2}G^{t}

\displaystyle\overset{\eqref{ch3:eq:PL}}{\leq}

\displaystyle(1-\gamma\mu)\mathbb{E}\left[f(x^{t})-f(x^{\star})\right]

\displaystyle-\left(\dfrac{1}{2\gamma}-\dfrac{L}{2}\right)\left\|x^{t+1}-x^{t}\right\|^{2}+\dfrac{\gamma}{2}G^{t}.

Let

\displaystyle\delta^{t}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\mathbb{E}\left[f(x^{t})-f(x^{\star})\right]\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}

\displaystyle s^{t}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\mathbb{E}\left[G^{t}\right]\mathchar 59\relax

\displaystyle r^{t}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|x^{t+1}-x^{t}\right\|^{2}\right].

Thus,

\displaystyle\delta^{t+1}+\dfrac{\gamma}{\theta(\alpha\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}s)}s^{t+1}

\displaystyle\overset{\eqref{ch3:eq:weighted_aux_4}}{\leq}

\displaystyle(1-\gamma\mu)\delta^{t}-\left(\dfrac{1}{2\gamma}-\dfrac{L}{2}\right)r^{t}+\dfrac{\gamma}{2}s^{t}+\dfrac{\gamma}{\theta(\alpha\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}s)}s^{t+1}

\displaystyle\overset{\eqref{ch3:eq:grad_distortion_evolution}}{\leq}

\displaystyle(1-\gamma\mu)\delta^{t}-\left(\dfrac{1}{2\gamma}-\dfrac{L}{2}\right)r^{t}+\dfrac{\gamma}{2}s^{t}

\displaystyle\qquad+\dfrac{\gamma}{\theta(\alpha\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}s)}\left((1-\theta(\alpha\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}s))s^{t}+\beta\left(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}L_{i}\right)^{2}r^{t}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(1-\gamma\mu)\delta^{t}+\dfrac{\gamma}{\theta(\alpha\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}s)}\left(1-\dfrac{\theta(\alpha\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}s)}{2}\right)s^{t}

\displaystyle\qquad-\left(\dfrac{1}{2\gamma}-\dfrac{L}{2}-\dfrac{\beta{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}L_{\rm AM}^{2}}\gamma}{\theta(\alpha\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}s)}\right)r^{t}.

Note that our extra assumption on the step size implies that

1-\dfrac{\theta(\alpha\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}s)}{2}\leq 1-\gamma\mu

and

\dfrac{1}{2\gamma}-\dfrac{L}{2}-\dfrac{\beta{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}L_{\rm AM}^{2}}\gamma}{\theta(\alpha\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}s)}\geq 0.

The last inequality follows from the bound

\gamma^{2}\dfrac{2\beta{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}L_{\rm AM}^{2}}}{\theta(\alpha\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}s)}+\gamma L\leq 1.

Thus,

\displaystyle\delta^{t+1}+\dfrac{\gamma}{\theta(\alpha\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}s)}s^{t+1}\leq(1-\gamma\mu)\left(\delta^{t}+\dfrac{\gamma}{\theta(\alpha\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}s)}s^{t}\right).

It remains to unroll the recurrence which finishes the proof.
∎

E3
EF21-W-SGD: Weighted Error Feedback 2021 with Stochastic Subsampled Gradients

The
EF21-W
algorithm assumes that all clients can compute the exact gradient in each round. In some scenarios, the exact gradients may be unavailable or too costly to compute, and only approximate gradient estimators can be obtained. In this section, we present the convergence result for
EF21-W
in the setting where the gradient computation on the clients,
\nabla f_{i}(x^{t+1})
, is replaced by a specific stochastic gradient estimator. For a variation of
EF21-W-SGD
which is working under a more general setting please see Appendix
F3
.

E3.1
Algorithm

In this section, we extend
EF21-W
to handle stochastic gradients, and we call the resulting algorithm
EF21-W-SGD
(Algorithm
4
). Our analysis of this extension follows a similar approach as the one used by
Fatkhullin et al. 2021
for studying the stochastic gradient version of the vanilla
EF21
algorithm, which they called
EF21-SGD
. Analysis of
EF21-W-SGD
has two important differences with vanilla
EF21-SGD
:

1.

Vanilla
EF21-SGD
provides maximum theoretically possible

\gamma=\left(L+{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}L_{\rm QM}}\sqrt{\dfrac{\beta_{1}}{\theta}}\right)^{-1}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}

where
EF21-W-SGD
has

\gamma=\left(L+{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}L_{\rm AM}}\sqrt{\dfrac{\beta_{1}}{\theta}}\right)^{-1}.

2.

Vanilla
EF21-SGD
and
EF21-W-SGD
formally differs in a way how it reports iterate
x^{T}
which minimizes
\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\nabla f(x^{T})\right\|^{2}\right]
due to a slightly different definition of
\widetilde{A}
. The
EF21-W-SGD
(Algorithm
4
) requires output iterate
\hat{x}^{T}
randomly according to the probability mass function described by (
3.49
).

1:

Input:
initial model
x^{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
; initial gradient estimates
g_{1}^{0}\mathchar 59\relax g_{2}^{0}\mathchar 59\relax\dots\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}g_{n}^{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
stored at the server and the clients; step size
\gamma>0
; number of iterations
T>0
; weights
{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}w_{i}}=\dfrac{L_{i}}{\sum_{j}L_{j}}\mathchar 59\relax i\in[n]

2:

Initialize:

g^{0}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}w_{i}}g_{i}^{0}
on the server

3:

for

t=0\mathchar 59\relax 1\mathchar 59\relax 2\mathchar 59\relax\dots\mathchar 59\relax T-1

do

4:

Server computes
x^{t+1}=x^{t}-\gamma g^{t}
and broadcasts
x^{t+1}
to all
n
clients

5:

for

i=1\mathchar 59\relax\dots\mathchar 59\relax n

on the clients in parallel

do

6:

Compute a stochastic estimator
\hat{g_{i}}(x^{t+1})
of the gradient
\nabla f_{i}(x^{t+1})

7:

Compute
u_{i}^{t}=\mathcal{C}_{i}^{t}\left(\dfrac{1}{n{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}w_{i}}}\hat{g_{i}}(x^{t+1})-g_{i}^{t}\right)
and update
g_{i}^{t+1}=g_{i}^{t}+u_{i}^{t}

8:

Send the compressed message
u_{i}^{t}
to the server

9:

end

for

10:

Server updates
g_{i}^{t+1}=g_{i}^{t}+u_{i}^{t}
for all
i\in[n]
, and computes
g^{t+1}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}w_{i}}g_{i}^{t+1}

11:

end

for

12:

Output:
Point
\hat{x}^{T}
chosen from the set
\{x^{0}\mathchar 59\relax\dots\mathchar 59\relax x^{T-1}\}
randomly according to the law (
3.49
)

Algorithm 4

EF21-W-SGD
: Weighted Error Feedback 2021 with Stochastic Gradients.

Assumption 5

(General assumption for stochastic gradient estimators)
.

We assume that for all
i\in[n]
there exist parameters
A_{i}\mathchar 59\relax C_{i}\geq 0
,
B_{i}\geq 1
such that

\mathbb{E}\left[\|\nabla f_{\xi_{ij}^{t}}(x)\|^{2}\right]\leq 2A_{i}\left(f_{i}(x)-f_{i}^{\inf}\right)+B_{i}\|\nabla f_{i}(x)\|^{2}+C_{i}\mathchar 59\relax

(3.45)

holds for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
where
3
3

3

When
A_{i}=0
one can ignore the first term in the right-hand side of (
3.45
), i.e., assumption
\inf_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}f_{i}(x)>-\infty
is not required in this case.

f_{i}^{\inf}=\inf_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}f_{i}(x)>-\infty
.

We study
EF21-W-SGD
under the same assumption as was used for analyzing
Vanilla EF21-SGD
, which we denote as Assumption
5
. To the best of our knowledge, this assumption, which was originally presented as Assumption 2 by
Khaled and Richtárik 2023
, is the most general assumption for a stochastic gradient estimator in a non-convex setting.

Next, to be aligned with original
Vanilla EF21-SGD

(
Fatkhullin et al. 2021
)
we have considered a specific form of gradient estimator. This specific form of gradient estimator from
Vanilla EF21-SGD
is presented in Section 4.1.2. of
Fatkhullin et al. 2021
where the stochastic gradient
\hat{g_{i}}
has been computed as follows:

\displaystyle\hat{g_{i}}(x^{t+1})=\dfrac{1}{\tau_{i}}\sum_{j=1}^{\tau_{i}}\nabla f_{\xi_{ij}^{t}}(x^{t+1})\mathchar 59\relax

Here
\tau_{i}
is a minibatch size of sampled datapoint indexed by
\xi_{ij}^{t}
of client
i
in iteration
t
. And
\xi_{ij}^{t}
are independent random variables. For a version of
EF21-W-SGD
which is working under a more general setting please see Appendix
F3
.

E3.2
A lemma

The contraction Lemma in this case gets the following form:

Lemma 10
.

Let
\mathcal{C}_{i}^{t}\in\mathbb{C}(\alpha)
for all
i\in[n]
and
t\geq 0
. Define

G_{i}^{t}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\left\|g_{i}^{t}-\dfrac{\nabla f_{i}(x^{t})}{nw_{i}}\right\|^{2}\mathchar 59\relax\qquad G^{t}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}w_{i}G_{i}^{t}.

Let Assumptions
2
and
5
hold. Then, for any
s\mathchar 59\relax\nu>0
we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[G^{t+1}\right]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle(1-\hat{\theta})\mathbb{E}\left[G^{t}\right]

(3.46)

\displaystyle+\hat{\beta_{1}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}L_{\rm AM}^{2}}\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|x^{t+1}-x^{t}\right\|^{2}\right]+{\widetilde{A}{\hat{\beta_{2}}}}\mathbb{E}\left[f(x^{t+1})-f^{\inf}\right]

\displaystyle+{\widetilde{C}{\hat{\beta_{2}}}}\mathchar 59\relax

where

\displaystyle w_{i}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\dfrac{L_{i}}{\sum_{j}L_{j}}\mathchar 59\relax

\displaystyle\hat{\theta}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}

\displaystyle 1-\left(1-\alpha\right)(1+s)(1+\nu)\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}

\displaystyle\hat{\beta_{1}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}

\displaystyle 2(1-\alpha)\left(1+s\right)\left(s+\nu^{-1}\right)\mathchar 59\relax

\displaystyle\hat{\beta_{2}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}

\displaystyle 2(1-\alpha)(1+s)(1+\nu^{-1})+(1+s^{-1})\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}

\displaystyle\widetilde{A}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\max_{i=1\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\ldots\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}n}\left(\dfrac{2(A_{i}+L_{i}(B_{i}-1))}{\tau_{i}}\dfrac{1}{nw_{i}}\right)\mathchar 59\relax

\displaystyle\widetilde{C}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\max_{i=1\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\ldots\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}n}\left(\dfrac{C_{i}}{\tau_{i}}\dfrac{1}{nw_{i}}\right).

Proof.

Define
W^{t}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\{g_{1}^{t}\mathchar 59\relax\dots\mathchar 59\relax g_{n}^{t}\mathchar 59\relax x^{t}\mathchar 59\relax x^{t+1}\}
. The proof starts as follows:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[G_{i}^{t+1}\;|\;W^{t}\right]

\displaystyle\overset{\eqref{ch3:eq:weighted_def_grad_distortion}}{=}

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|g_{i}^{t+1}-\dfrac{\nabla f_{i}(x^{t+1})}{nw_{i}}\right\|^{2}\;|\;W^{t}\right]

\displaystyle\overset{\text{line}~\ref{ch3:line:weighted_ef21_sgd_grad_update}}{=}

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|g_{i}^{t}+\mathcal{C}_{i}^{t}\left(\dfrac{\hat{g_{i}}(x^{t+1})}{nw_{i}}-g_{i}^{t}\right)-\dfrac{\nabla f_{i}(x^{t+1})}{nw_{i}}\right\|^{2}\;|\;W^{t}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big[\left\|\mathcal{C}_{i}^{t}\left(\dfrac{\hat{g_{i}}(x^{t+1})}{nw_{i}}-g_{i}^{t}\right)-\left(\dfrac{\hat{g_{i}}(x^{t+1})}{nw_{i}}-g_{i}^{t}\right)\right.
