\left\|g_{i}^{t+1}-\nabla f_{i}(x^{t+1})\right\|^{2}\leq(1-\theta(\alpha))\left\|g_{i}^{t}-\nabla f_{i}(x^{t})\right\|^{2}+\beta(\alpha)\|\nabla f_{i}(x^{t+1})-\nabla f_{i}(x^{t})\|^{2}\mathchar 59\relax

(3.82)

where

\alpha=\min\left\{\min\limits_{i\in[n]}\dfrac{K_{i}}{|\mathcal{J}_{i}|}\mathchar 59\relax 1\right\}.

Lemma 18
.

Under Assumption
2
, iterates of Algorithm
7
satisfies

G^{t+1}\leq(1-\theta(\alpha))G^{t}+\beta(\alpha)\dfrac{c}{n}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}L_{\rm AM}^{2}}\|x-y\|^{2}.

(3.83)

Proof.

The proof is a combination of Lemmas
16
and
17
:

\displaystyle G^{t+1}

\displaystyle\overset{\eqref{ch3:eq:new_def_distortion}}{=}

\displaystyle\dfrac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\dfrac{1}{w_{i}}\left\|g_{i}^{t+1}-\nabla f_{i}(x^{t+1})\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\overset{\eqref{ch3:eq:sparse_grad_est_evolution}}{\leq}

\displaystyle\dfrac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\dfrac{1}{w_{i}}\left[(1-\theta(\alpha))\|g_{i}^{t}-\nabla f_{i}(x^{t})\|^{2}+\beta(\alpha)\|\nabla f_{i}(x^{t+1})-\nabla f_{i}(x^{t})\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(1-\theta(\alpha))G^{t}+\dfrac{\beta(\alpha)}{n^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\dfrac{1}{w_{i}}\|\nabla f_{i}(x^{t+1})-\nabla f_{i}(x^{t})\|^{2}

\displaystyle\overset{\eqref{ch3:eq:weight_definition}}{=}

\displaystyle(1-\theta(\alpha))G^{t}+\dfrac{\beta(\alpha)}{n^{2}}\left(\sum_{j=1}^{n}L_{j}\right)\sum_{i=1}^{n}\dfrac{1}{L_{i}}\|\nabla f_{i}(x^{t+1})-\nabla f_{i}(x^{t})\|^{2}

\displaystyle\overset{\eqref{ch3:eq:sparse_L_+}}{\leq}

\displaystyle(1-\theta(\alpha))G^{t}+\dfrac{\beta(\alpha)}{n^{2}}\dfrac{c}{n}\left(\sum_{j=1}^{n}L_{j}\right)^{2}\|x-y\|^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(1-\theta(\alpha))G^{t}+\beta(\alpha)\dfrac{c}{n}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}L_{\rm AM}^{2}}\|x-y\|^{2}.

∎

H3.4
Main result

And now we are ready to formulate the main result.

Theorem 16
.

Let Assumptions
1
,
2
and
3
hold. Let
g_{i}^{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}_{i}
for all
i\in[n]
,

\alpha=\min\left\{\min\limits_{i\in[n]}\dfrac{K_{i}}{|\mathcal{J}_{i}|}\mathchar 59\relax 1\right\}\mathchar 59\relax\qquad 0<\gamma\leq\dfrac{1}{L+\dfrac{c}{n}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}L_{\rm AM}}\xi(\alpha)}.

Under these conditions, the iterates of Algorithm
7
satisfy

\dfrac{1}{T}\sum\limits_{t=0}^{T-1}\|\nabla f(x^{t})\|^{2}\leq\dfrac{2(f(x^{0})-f^{\ast})}{\gamma T}+\dfrac{c}{n}\dfrac{G^{0}}{\theta(\alpha)T}.

(3.84)

Proof.

Let us define the Lyapunov function:

\Psi^{t}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}f(x^{t})-f^{\ast}+\dfrac{\gamma c}{2\theta n}G^{t}.

(3.85)

We start the proof as follows:

\displaystyle\Psi^{t+1}

\displaystyle\overset{\eqref{ch3:eq:sparse_Lyapunov}}{=}

\displaystyle f(x^{t+1})-f^{\ast}+\dfrac{\gamma c}{2\theta n}G^{t+1}

\displaystyle\overset{\eqref{ch3:eq:descent_lemma}}{\leq}

\displaystyle f(x^{t})-f^{\ast}-\dfrac{\gamma}{2}\|\nabla f(x^{t})\|^{2}-\left(\dfrac{1}{2\gamma}-\dfrac{L}{2}\right)\|x^{t+1}-x^{t}\|^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad+\dfrac{\gamma}{2}\|g^{t}-\nabla f(x^{t})\|^{2}+\dfrac{\gamma c}{2\theta n}G^{t+1}

\displaystyle\overset{\eqref{ch3:eq:grad_est_distortion}}{\leq}

\displaystyle f(x^{t})-f^{\ast}-\dfrac{\gamma}{2}\|\nabla f(x^{t})\|^{2}-\left(\dfrac{1}{2\gamma}-\dfrac{L}{2}\right)\|x^{t+1}-x^{t}\|^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad+\dfrac{\gamma}{2}\dfrac{c}{n}G^{t}+\dfrac{\gamma c}{2\theta n}G^{t+1}

\displaystyle\overset{\eqref{ch3:eq:sparse_grad_est_evolution}}{\leq}

\displaystyle f(x^{t})-f^{\ast}-\dfrac{\gamma}{2}\|\nabla f(x^{t})\|^{2}-\left(\dfrac{1}{2\gamma}-\dfrac{L}{2}\right)\|x^{t+1}-x^{t}\|^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad+\dfrac{\gamma}{2}\dfrac{c}{n}G^{t}+\dfrac{\gamma c}{2\theta n}\left((1-\theta)G^{t}+\beta\dfrac{c}{n}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}L_{\rm AM}^{2}}\|x^{t+1}-x^{t}\|^{2}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle f(x^{t})-f^{\ast}+\dfrac{\gamma c}{2\theta n}G^{t}-\dfrac{\gamma}{2}\|\nabla f(x^{t})\|^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad-\left(\dfrac{1}{2\gamma}-\dfrac{L}{2}-\dfrac{\gamma}{2}\dfrac{\beta}{\theta}\dfrac{c^{2}}{n^{2}}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}L_{\rm AM}^{2}}\right)\|x^{t+1}-x^{t}\|^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Psi^{t}-\dfrac{\gamma}{2}\|\nabla f(x^{t})\|^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad-\underbrace{\left(\dfrac{1}{2\gamma}-\dfrac{L}{2}-\dfrac{\gamma}{2}\dfrac{\beta}{\theta}\dfrac{c^{2}}{n^{2}}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}L_{\rm AM}^{2}}\right)}_{\geq 0}\|x^{t+1}-x^{t}\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\Psi^{t}-\dfrac{\gamma}{2}\|\nabla f(x^{t})\|^{2}.

Unrolling the inequality above, we get

\displaystyle 0\leq\Psi^{T}\leq\Psi^{T-1}-\dfrac{\gamma}{2}\|\nabla f(x^{T-1})\|^{2}\leq\Psi^{0}-\dfrac{\gamma}{2}\sum\limits_{t=0}^{T-1}\|\nabla f(x^{t})\|^{2}\mathchar 59\relax

from what the main result follows.
∎

I3
Experiments: Further Details

(a)

Figure 3.4
:
The factor
\xi=\sqrt{{\beta}/{\theta}}
as a function of optimization variable dimension
d
for several
TopK
compressors. The behavior is independent of properties of
\{f_{1}(x)\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\dots\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}f_{n}(x)\}
and
f(x)
.

I3.1
Computing and software environment

We used the Python software suite

FL_PyTorch

(
Burlachenko et al. 2021
)
to simulate the distributed environment for training. We carried out experiments on a compute node with Ubuntu 18.04 LTS,
256
GBytes of DRAM DDR4 memory at
2.9
GHz, and
48
cores (
2
sockets with
24
cores per socket) of Intel(R) Xeon(R) Gold 6246 CPU at
3.3
GHz. We used double-precision arithmetic (FP64) during computing gradient oracles. All our computations were carried on CPU.

I3.2
Comments on the improvement

The standard
EF21
analysis
(
Richtárik et al. 2021a
)
allows to utilize
EF21
with maximum allowable step size
\gamma
equal to:

\displaystyle\gamma=\left(L+{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}L_{\rm QM}}\sqrt{\dfrac{\beta(\alpha)}{\theta(\alpha)}}\right)^{-1}\mathchar 59\relax\qquad\theta(\alpha)=1-\sqrt{1-\alpha}\mathchar 59\relax\qquad\beta(\alpha)=\dfrac{1-\alpha}{1-\sqrt{1-\alpha}}.

Our analysis allows us to replace the quantity
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}L_{\rm QM}}
with
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}L_{\rm AM}}
. This improvement has an important consequence. The replaced quantity affects the step size by a factor of
\xi(\alpha)=\sqrt{\dfrac{\beta(\alpha)}{\theta(\alpha)}}
. This factor can be arbitrarily large as
d
increases, as shown in Figure
3.4
. If
d
is increasing and the parameter
k
of
TopK
compressor is fixed, then even a small improvement in the constant term can have a significant impact in an absolute sense on the computed step size if
\xi(\alpha)\gg L
.

I3.3
When improved analysis leads to more aggressive steps

The quantity
\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}L_{\rm QM}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\sqrt{\dfrac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}L_{i}^{2}}
plays essential role in
EF21
analysis. As we saw with special consideration this quantity for
EF21
and its extensions is improvable. The improved analysis allows us to replace it with
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}L_{\rm AM}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}{\dfrac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}L_{i}}.
Clearly, by the arithmetic-quadratic mean inequality,

L_{\rm var}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}L_{\rm QM}^{2}}-{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}L_{\rm AM}^{2}}\geq 0.

The difference
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}L_{\rm QM}}-{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}L_{\rm AM}}
can be expressed as follows:

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}L_{\rm QM}}-{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}L_{\rm AM}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}L_{\rm QM}}-{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}L_{\rm AM}})\left(\dfrac{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}L_{\rm QM}}+{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}L_{\rm AM}}}{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}L_{\rm QM}}+{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}L_{\rm AM}}}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\dfrac{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}L_{\rm QM}^{2}}-{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}L_{\rm AM}^{2}}}{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}L_{\rm QM}}+{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}L_{\rm AM}}}\quad=\quad\dfrac{1}{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}L_{\rm QM}}+{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}L_{\rm AM}}}\cdot{\dfrac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(L_{i}-\dfrac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}L_{i}\right)^{2}}.

The coefficient
\dfrac{1}{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}L_{\rm QM}}+{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}L_{\rm AM}}}
in the last equation can be bounded from below and above as follows:

\dfrac{1}{2{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}L_{\rm QM}}}=\dfrac{1}{2\cdot\max({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}L_{\rm QM}}\mathchar 59\relax{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}L_{\rm AM}})}\leq\dfrac{1}{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}L_{\rm QM}}+{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}L_{\rm AM}}}\leq\dfrac{1}{2\cdot\min({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}L_{\rm QM}}\mathchar 59\relax{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}L_{\rm AM}})}\leq\dfrac{1}{2{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}L_{\rm AM}}}.

As a consequence, the difference
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}L_{\rm QM}}-{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}L_{\rm AM}}
is bound above by the estimated variance of
L_{i}
divided by the mean of
L_{i}
, also known as
Index of Dispersion
in statistics. From this consideration, we can more easily observe that
EF21-W
can have an arbitrarily better step size than vanilla
EF21
if the variance of
L_{i}
is increasing faster than the mean of
L_{i}
.

I3.4
Dataset generation for synthetic experiment

First, we assume that the user provides two parameters:
\mu\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
and
L\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
. These parameters define the construction of strongly convex function
f_{i}(x)
, which are modified by meta-parameters
q\in[-1\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}1]
and
z>0
, described next.

1.

Each client initially has

f_{i}(x)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\dfrac{1}{n_{i}}\left\|\mathbf{A}_{i}x-{b_{i}}\right\|^{2}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}

where
\mathbf{A}_{i}
is initialized in such way that
f_{i}
is
L_{i}
smooth and
\mu_{f_{i}}
strongly convex. Parameters are defined in the following way:

L_{i}=\dfrac{i}{n}\cdot(L-\mu)+\mu\mathchar 59\relax\qquad\mu_{f_{i}}=\mu.

2.

The scalar value
q\in[-1\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}+1]
informally plays the role of meta parameter to change the distribution of
L_{i}
and make values of
L_{i}
close to one of the following: (i)
\mu
; (ii)
L
; (iii)
(L+\mu)/2
. The exact modification of
L_{i}
depends on the sign of meta parameter
q
.

•

Case
q\in[0\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}1]
. In this case for first
n/2
(i.e.,
i\in[0\mathchar 59\relax n/2]
) compute the value
L_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}q}=\mathrm{lerp}(L_{i}\mathchar 59\relax\mu\mathchar 59\relax q)
, where
\mathrm{lerp}(a\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}b\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}t):\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times[0\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}1]\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
is standard linear interpolation

\mathrm{lerp}(a\mathchar 59\relax b\mathchar 59\relax t)=a(1-t)+bt.

The last
n/2
(
i\in[n/2+1\mathchar 59\relax n]
) compute the value
L_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}q}=\mathrm{lerp}(L_{i}\mathchar 59\relax L\mathchar 59\relax q)
. For example, if
q=0
then
L_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}q}=L_{i}\mathchar 59\relax\forall i\in[n]
, and if
q=1
then
L_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}q}=\mu
for first
{n}/{2}
clients and
L_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}q}=L
for last
{n}/{2}
clients.

•

Case
q\in[-1\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}0]
. In this for all
n
clients the new value
L_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}q}=\mathrm{lerp}(L_{i}\mathchar 59\relax({L+\mu})/{2}\mathchar 59\relax-q)
. In this case for example if
q=0
then
L_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}q}=L_{i}
and if
q=-1
then
L_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}q}=({L+\mu})/{2}
.

The process firstly fills the
\mathbf{A}_{i}
in such form that
L_{i}
forms a uniform spectrum in
[\mu\mathchar 59\relax L]
with the center of this spectrum equal to
a=\dfrac{L+\mu}{2}
. And then as
q\to 1
, the variance of
L_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}q}
is increasing.

3.

We use these new values
L_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}q}
for all
i\in[n]
clients as a final target
L_{i}^{\mathrm{new}}
values. Due to numerical issues, we found that it is worthwhile for the first and last client to add extra scaling. First client scales
L_{1\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}q}
by
factor
1/z
, and last
n
-th client scales
L_{n\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}q}
by factor
z
. Here
z\geq 0
is an additional meta-parameter.

4.

Next obtained values are used to generate
\mathbf{A}_{i}
in such way that
\nabla^{2}f_{i}(x)
has uniform spectrum in
[\mu_{f_{i}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}q\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}z}\mathchar 59\relax L_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}q\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}z}]
.

5.

As a last step the objective function
f(x)
is scaled in such a way that it is
L
smooth with constant value
L
. The
b_{i}
for each client is initialized as
b_{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\mathbf{A}_{i}\cdot x_{\mathrm{solution}}
, where
x_{\mathrm{solution}}
is fixed solution.

I3.5
Dataset shuffling strategy for LIBSVM dataset

Our dataset shuffling strategy heuristically splits data points so that
L_{\rm var}
is maximized. It consists of the following steps:

1.

Sort data points from the whole dataset according to
L
constants.
Sort all data points according to the smoothness constants of the loss function for each single data point.

2.

Assign a single data point to each client
Assume that there are total
m
data points in the datasets, and the total number of clients is
n
. At the beginning each client
i
holds a single data point
\lfloor(i-1+1/2)\cdot\dfrac{m}{n}\rfloor
.

3.

Data assignment.
First, initialize set
F=\{\}
. Next, iterate through all points except those assigned from the previous step. For each point we find the best client
i^{\prime}\in[n]\backslash F
to assign the point in a way that assignment of point to client
i^{\prime}
maximizes
L_{\rm var}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}L_{\rm QM}^{2}}-{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}L_{\rm AM}^{2}}
. Once the client already has
\lceil\dfrac{m}{n}\rceil
data points assigned to it, the client is added to the set
F
.

The set
F
in the last step guarantees each client will have
\dfrac{m}{n}
data points. In general, this is a heuristic greedy strategy that approximately maximizes
L_{\rm var}
under the constraint that each client has the same amount of data points equal to
\lfloor\dfrac{m}{n}\rfloor
. Due to its heuristic nature, the Algorithm does not provide deep guarantees, but it was good enough for our experiments.

J3
Additional Experiments

In this section, we present additional experiments for comparison
EF21-W
,
EF21-W-PP
,
EF21-W-SGD
with their vanilla versions. We applied these algorithms in a series of synthetically generated convex and non-convex optimization problems and for training
logistic regression
with non-convex regularized with using several
LIBSVM
datasets
(
Chang and Lin 2011a
)
. While carrying out additional experiments we will use three quantities. These quantities have already been mentioned in the main part, but we will repeat them here:

\begin{split}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}L_{\rm QM}}&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\sqrt{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}L_{i}^{2}}}\mathchar 59\relax\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}L_{\rm AM}}&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}L_{i}}\mathchar 59\relax\\
L_{\rm var}&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}L_{\rm QM}^{2}}-{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}L_{\rm AM}^{2}}=\dfrac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(L_{i}-\dfrac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}L_{i}\right)^{2}.\end{split}

The relationship between these quantities was discussed in Appendix
I3.3
. In our experiments, we used
TopK
compressor. The
TopK
compressor returns sparse vectors filled with zeros, except
K
positions, which correspond to
K
maximum values in absolute value and which are unchanged by the compressor. Even if this compressor breaks ties arbitrarily, it is possible to show that
\alpha=\nicefrac{{K}}{{d}}
.
The compressor parameter
\alpha
is defined without considering properties of
f_{i}
. The quantities
\beta
,
\theta
,
\dfrac{\beta}{\theta}
are derived from
\alpha
, and they do not depend on
L_{i}
.

Convex case with synthetic datasets.

We aim to solve optimization Problem (
3.1
) in the case when the functions
f_{1}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\dots\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}f_{n}
are strongly convex. In particular, we work with

\displaystyle f_{i}(x)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\dfrac{1}{n_{i}}\left\|\mathbf{A}_{i}x-{b_{i}}\right\|^{2}+\dfrac{\lambda}{2}\|x\|^{2}\mathchar 59\relax

(3.86)

where
\lambda=0.01
. It can be shown that
L_{i}=\dfrac{2}{n_{i}}\lambda_{\max}({\mathbf{A}_{i}}^{\top}\mathbf{A}_{i})+\lambda
. The result of experiments for training linear regression model with a convex regularized is presented in Figure
3.5
. The total number of rounds for simulation is
r=10\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}000
. Instances of optimization problems were generated for values
L=50
,
\mu=1
with several values of
q\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}z
with using the dataset generation schema described in Appendix
I3.4
. The summary of derived quantities is presented in Table
3.1
. We present several optimization problems to demonstrate the possible different relationships between
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}L_{\rm QM}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}L_{\rm AM}}
. As we see from experiments, the
EF21-W
is superior as the variance of
L_{i}
tends to increase. The plots in Figure
3.5
(a–d) correspond to decreasing variance of
L_{i}
. As we see, as the variance of
L_{i}
decreases, the difference between
EF21-W
and
EF21
also tends to decrease. Finally,
EF21-W
is always at least as best as
EF21
.

Non-convex
logistic regression
on benchmark datasets.

We aim to solve optimization Problem (
3.1
) in the case when the functions
f_{1}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\dots\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}f_{n}
are non-convex. In particular, we work with
logistic regression
with a non-convex robustification regularization term:

\displaystyle f_{i}(x)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\dfrac{1}{n_{i}}\sum_{j=1}^{n_{i}}\log\left(1+\exp({-y_{ij}\cdot a_{ij}^{\top}x})\right)+\lambda\cdot\sum_{j=1}^{d}\dfrac{x_{j}^{2}}{x_{j}^{2}+1}\mathchar 59\relax

(3.88)

where
(a_{ij}\mathchar 59\relax y_{ij})\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\{-1\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}1\}
.

We used several
LIBSVM
datasets
(
Chang and Lin 2011a
)
for our benchmarking purposes. The results are presented in Figures
3.7
,
3.8
. The important quantities for these instances of optimization problems are summarized in Table
3.3
. From Figure
3.7
(a, b, c), we can observe that for these datasets, the
EF21-W
is better, and this effect is observable in practice. From these examples, we can observe that
12.5
K rounds of
EF21-W
corresponds to only
10
K rounds of
EF21
. This improvement is essential for Federated Learning, in which both communication rounds and communication information during a round represent the main bottlenecks and are the subject of optimization. Figure
3.7
(d, e, f) demonstrate that sometimes the
EF21-W
can have practical behavior close to
EF21
, even if there is an improvement in step size (For exact values of step size see Table
3.3
). The experiment on
AUSTRALIAN
datasets is presented in Figure
3.8
. This example demonstrates that in this
LIBSVM
benchmark datasets, the relative improvement in the number of rounds for
EF21-W
compared to
EF21
is considerable. For example
40
K rounds of
EF21
corresponds to
5
K rounds of
EF21-W
in terms of attainable
\|\nabla f(x^{t})\|^{2}
.

Non-convex
logistic regression
with non-homogeneous compressor.

In this supplementary experiment, we used
AUSTRALIAN
dataset
(
Chang and Lin 2011a
)
to train a
logistic regression
model with a non-convex sparsity-enhanced regularization term, as defined in Equation (
3.88
). We employed the non-homogeneous
Natural
compressor
(
Horváth et al. 2019
)
, an unbiased compressor satisfying Definition
3.3
with
w=1/8
. This compressor randomly rounds the exponential part and discards the mantissa when using the IEEE 754 Standard
(
Markstein 2008
)
floating-point representation. In FP32 format, only 9 bits per scalar are transmitted to the master, while the remaining 23 mantissa bits are entirely dropped. Figure
3.9
presents the experimental results. We fine-tuned the theoretical step size by multiplying it by a constant and observed that
EF21-W
consistently outperforms
EF21
across all considered step size multipliers.
EF21-W
effectively leverages unbiased non-homogeneous compressors, extending its advantages beyond applications to homogeneous compressors. Finally, the theoretically increased step size in
EF21-W
in this experiment does not fully reflect the potential for significantly larger practical step size increases such as a
\times 40
multiplier, which remains an open question for future research.

compressor

. The objective function is constituted of

). Regularization term

, with

. Multipliers of theoretical step size. Full participation. Computation format single precision (FP32). Dataset:
AUSTRALIAN
.

J3.3
Additional experiments for EF21-W-SGD

The standard
EF21-SGD
with the analysis described in Corollary 4
(
Fatkhullin et al. 2021
)
allows performing the optimization procedure with maximum allowable step size up to the factor of
2
equal to:

\gamma_{\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}\tiny\sf EF21-SGD} }}=\left(L+\sqrt{\dfrac{\hat{\beta_{1}}}{\hat{\theta}}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}L_{\rm QM}}}}\right)^{-1}.

In last expression quantities
\hat{\theta}=1-(1-\alpha)(1+s)(1+\nu)
, and
\hat{\beta_{1}}=2(1-\alpha)\left(1+s\right)\left(s+\nu^{-1}\right)
. Improved analysis for
EF21-W-SGD
allows to apply step size:

\gamma_{\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}\tiny\sf EF21-W-SGD} }}=\left(L+\sqrt{\dfrac{\hat{\beta_{1}}}{\hat{\theta}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}L_{\rm AM}}}}\right)^{-1}.

Therefore in terms of step size

\gamma_{\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}\tiny\sf EF21-W-SGD} }}\geq\gamma_{\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}\tiny\sf EF21-SGD} }}

and
EF21-W-SGD
exhibits a more aggressive step size.

We conducted distributed training of a
logistic regression
model on
W1A
,
W2A
,
W3A
,
PHISHING
,
AUSTRALIAN
datasets with non-convex regularization. For all datasets, we consider the optimization Problem (
3.1
),
where

\displaystyle f_{i}(x)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\dfrac{1}{n_{i}}\sum_{j=1}^{n_{i}}\log\left(1+\exp({-y_{ij}\cdot a_{ij}^{\top}x})\right)+\lambda\sum_{j=1}^{d}\dfrac{x_{j}^{2}}{x_{j}^{2}+1}\mathchar 59\relax

(3.91)

and
(a_{ij}\mathchar 59\relax y_{ij})\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\{-1\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}1\}
.

The initial gradient estimators are set to
g_{i}^{0}=\nabla f_{i}(x^{0})
for all
i\in[n]
. For comparison of
EF21-SGD
and
EF21-W-SGD
, we used the largest step size allowed by theory. The dataset shuffling strategy repeats the strategy that we have used for
EF21-W-PP
and
EF21-W
and it is described in Appendix
I3.5
. The algorithms
EF21-SGD
and
/EF21-W-SGD
employed an unbiased gradient estimator, which was estimated by sampling a single training point uniformly at random and independently at each client.

K3
Reproducibility

To ensure reproducibility, we use the following

FL_PyTorch

(
Burlachenko et al. 2021
)
simulator features: (i) random seeds were fixed for data synthesis; (ii) random seeds were fixed for the runtime pseudo-random generators involved in
EF21-PP
and
EF21-SGD
across clients and the server; (iii) the thread pool size was turned off to avoid the non-deterministic order of client updates in the server. The source code for the experiments, along with a description for reproducing the experiment, can be downloaded from:

https://openreview.net/forum?id=Ch7WqGcGmb

Chapter 4
DCGD/PermK/AES: Classical Cryptography in FL

The goals and summaries of this chapter are outlined in Table
1.1
and Section
1.4.3
.

4.1
Introduction

Effective machine learning models necessitate vast training data from diverse sources
(
Zhang et al. 2020
)
. However, such data, often dispersed across various entities, faces sharing restrictions due to privacy concerns
(
Jiang et al. 2021
;
Liu et al. 2022a
)
. Federated Learning (FL) provides a viable solution, enabling collaborative global model training without exposing sensitive information
(
McMahan et al. 2017
;
Kairouz et al. 2021
)
. FL algorithms bifurcate into
cross-device
FL, involving big amount of devices, and
cross-silo
FL, typically engaging several distinct organizations
(
Liu et al. 2022a
)
. In both cases, preserving the privacy of clients’ datasets is significant. FL strives to ensure confidentiality by retaining private data on the client side, yet it falls short of providing strong privacy assurance if relying only on that. Model parameters and derived quantities from it, transmitted from clients to a server, may embed sensitive data (see Appendix
E4.1
). Detailed information about privacy methods for FL is presented in Appendix
D4
. We briefly discuss two of them.

Differential Privacy (
DP
) is an approach to assess the privacy of a specific Algorithm provided by
Dwork et al. 2006
;
Dwork et al. 2016
.
DP
algorithms ensure that, for any set of training examples, no attacker, no matter how powerful, can learn much more information about a single training example than they could if this example had been excluded from the training data. Despite offering privacy protection for individual users, integrating
DP
with FL could amplify communication overhead, and diminish accuracy. When striving for strong privacy guarantees in scenarios where the user count is small (which is possible in
cross-silo
) the noise impact from
DP
techniques makes training challenging. Understanding the convergence of
SGD
with
DP
is an active research topic
(
Chen et al. 2020
)
.

While
DP
statistically hides the training data, Homomorphic Encryption (
HE
) provides means to perform computation operations under encrypted numbers from
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
,
\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
\mathbb{B}^{d}
without decryption, protecting input and output from execution part. Therefore
HE
can be used for aggregating encrypted gradients
(
Liu et al. 2018
;
Phong et al. 2018
)
in a server that clients do not trust, given that the master has a public key which is required for execution of arithmetic operations.

Classical Cryptography in FL.

Classical Cryptography operates on the binary representation of data. It does not preserve even linear relations in a homomorphic way. The fact of exhibiting poor algebraic properties is an underlying reason why using Classical Cryptography was considered challenging by previous research papers
(
Kaissis et al. 2020
;
Lauter 2022
;
Jain and Cherukuri 2023
;
Pan et al. 2024
)
. In these works, authors stated that Advanced Encryption Standard (
AES
)
(
Daemen and Rijmen 2001
)
is not suitable for FL or is too challenging.

Communication compression in FL.

Numerous compression methods such as quantization
(
Wen et al. 2017
;
Safaryan and Richtárik 2021
)
, sparsification
(
Wangni et al. 2018
;
Alistarh et al. 2018
)
, and dithering
(
Alistarh et al. 2017
;
Horváth et al. 2019
)
have been explored to mitigate communication cost during FL training. However, these techniques necessitate secure server-side aggregation, and there are no guarantees that communication reduction techniques and techniques aimed to preserve privacy or security are combinable.

4.1.1
Problem formulation

In this work, we develop a practical communication efficient privacy and secure aware framework for FL training.

Requirements for optimization objective.

From the perspective of Machine Learning (ML), our objective is to select a function from a parameterized function class
\mathcal{F}
indexed by
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, by solving the following optimization problem:

\min\limits_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\{f(x)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\dfrac{1}{n}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}f_{i}(x)\right\}\mathchar 59\relax

(4.1)

Here,
n\in\mathbb{N}
represents the number of clients, and
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
denotes the
d
parameters or weights of a model
\hat{F}(\cdot;x)\in\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{Y}
that need to be learned across all
n
clients. The function
f_{i}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
provides the score criteria for using the model
\hat{F}(\cdot;x)
on the client’s
i
data. In the context of FL the functions
f_{i}
typically represented as:

f_{i}(x)=\dfrac{w_{i}}{n_{i}}\sum\limits_{j=1}^{n_{i}}\left(\mathcal{L}_{ij}(b_{ij}\mathchar 59\relax\hat{F}(a_{ij};x))+R_{i}(x)\right)\mathchar 59\relax

(4.2)

Here,
n_{i}\in\mathbb{R}
denotes the number of data points at client
i\in[n]
, and
(a_{ij}\mathchar 59\relax b_{ij})\in\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}
represent the input-output pairs at client
i
. The function
\mathcal{L}_{ij}(y_{\mathrm{real}}\mathchar 59\relax y_{\mathrm{pred}}):\mathcal{Y}\times\mathcal{Y}\to\mathbb{R}
is a loss function that scores prediction,
R_{i}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
is the regularization function used for parameter
x
at client
i
. The weight
w_{i}\in\mathbb{R}
encodes knowledge about the role of client
i
.
For example: (i)
w_{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}{\left(n_{i}\cdot n\right)}/{\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}n_{i}\right)}
corresponds to a case when
all data point
s are equally important; (ii)
w_{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}1
corresponds to a case when
all devices
are equally important. We aim to solve Problem (
4.1
) under the next requirements:

4.1.2
Assumptions

Assumptions for applying our framework.

i)
f(x)
is differentiable in training variable
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

ii) Clients trust each other through the established key.

iii)
\mathrm{dom}(f)=\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

Security and system requirements for the FL training procedure.

(A)

Clients never transfer the training data to the master.

(B)

Clients do not trust communication devices.

(C)

System should detect attempts of message tampering by adversaries.

(D)

Clients do not trust server.

(E)

Preventing competitor worker interference.

(F)

Limit memory traffic from clients to the master.

(G)

System should allow overlapping communication and computing in clients because separate physical devices implement it.

4.1.3
Real-world scenarios for applying our framework.

The potential benefits of our framework can be demonstrated through at least three possible scenarios.

(a) An individual using multiple
IoT
devices wants to train an ML model via third-party servers. In practice, engineers at these companies often have unrestricted access to server software, which raises privacy concerns. Individuals may seek guarantees that third-party providers only offer computation and storage services without being able to access the transmitted data.

(b) In cross-device settings, multiple mutually trusted
IoT
clients collaborate on FL training. However, a central coordinator may still be needed. For instance, if a client is temporarily unavailable, updates must be buffered. Additionally, applied optimization steps should be stored to reconstruct the training trajectory for statistical analysis, but
IoT
devices may lack sufficient storage. Another reason for requiring a coordinator is to facilitate training restarts in case of system failures.

(c) We aim to train an FL model without a physical master in scenarios where the communication topology naturally supports broadcasting. However, communication must remain secure against tampering and eavesdropping. Our framework can be instantiated in this setting (see Appendix
J4
).

4.1.4
Contributions

We discovered that recently proposed permutated correlated compressors
(
Szlendak et al. 2022
)

PermK
exhibit properties essential for using
Classical Cryptography in FL
while preserving the ability of
Communication Compression
. In our work, we introduce a framework that provides privacy and secure preserving training process to FL applications in which previously
HE
methods have been used. Summary of our contributions:

1.

We addressed the challenge highlighted in
(
Kaissis et al. 2020
;
Lauter 2022
;
Jain and Cherukuri 2023
;
Pan et al. 2024
)
, which states that using
AES
in FL is challenging, by constructing an FL system based on
AES
.

2.

We demonstrated the operational advantages of the proposed privacy and secure aware optimization Algorithm
10
over
HE
in the setting in which
HE
is typically applied in FL.

3.

We demonstrated the framework’s ability to train a
ResNet-18

(
He et al. 2016
)
DL model on
CIFAR-10
and discussed its potential benefits for DL more broadly in Appendix
I4
.

4.

We demonstrated a possibility of computation communication overlap and handling compute heterogeneity in Appendix
H4.2
. Deployment flexibility in communication topologies is discussed in Appendix
J4
.

To support readers with various backgrounds we provide:

i) Glossary in Appendix
A4
.

ii) Details about
AES
in Appendix
F4
.

iii) Overview of privacy mechanisms in FL in Appendix
D4
, discussion about the difference between privacy and security in Appendix
E4.2
.

iv) Overview of
CKKS
in Appendix
G4
.

4.2
Framework of Security Aware FL with Permuted Compressors

The Distributed Compressed Gradient Descent
(
DCGD (Baseline)
)
(
Khirirat et al. 2018a
)
, presented as (Algorithm
8
,
Option B
), enables the use of independent unbiased compressors
\mathcal{C}_{i}
if
\mathbb{E}\left[\|\mathcal{C}_{i}(x)-x\|^{2}\right]\leq w\|x\|^{2}
. If
w\neq 0
this algorithm does not induce variance for a
\mu
-strongly convex objective
f
only in a specific (
overparameterized
) mode:
\nabla f_{i}(x)=0\mathchar 59\relax\forall i\in[n]
. If
\mathcal{C}_{i}(\nabla f_{i}(x))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\nabla f_{i}(x)
, it reconstitutes distributed Gradient Descent (
GD
). One line of research involves removing variance from the use of compressors. For instance,
MARINA

(
Gorbunov et al. 2021
)
and
COFIG/FRECON

(
Zhao et al. 2024b
)
do not induce variance when using unbiased independent compressors, while
EF21

(
Richtárik et al. 2021b
)
avoids inducing variance from any independent contractive compressors.

In all these algorithms, the logic in the master starts to include extra state updates based on obtaining messages from clients. But this is what we aim to avoid. Thus, the development of our framework was based on stateless
DCGD (Baseline)
. For plain
DCGD (Baseline)
we have two issues: (a) In Line 6, the algorithm sends sparsified information about
\nabla f_{i}(x)
to the master, which is insecure; (b) In Line 8, the master computes the average of
g_{i}^{k}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, but the master should not obtain
g_{i}^{k}=\nabla f_{i}(x^{k})
or quantities that are subject to reconstruction attacks (see Appendix
E4.1
). One way to address these challenges is through
HE
schemes. The current practical state-of-the-art
HE
scheme that operates on elements of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
is
CKKS
, designed by
Cheon et al. 2017
.
CKKS
is the most widely used scheme for ML applications and is considered a highly practical choice
(
Lauter et al. 2022
)
. As we demonstrate in our experiments,
CKKS
introduces an approximation error due to its inherently lossy design. In our experiments, we used
CKKS
as a black-box with an appropriate configuration. For more background and details on
CKKS
, see Appendix
G4
, and for a broader overview of
HE
, see Appendix
D4
.

DCGD
with
Naive Usage of AES (A)
and
Baseline (B)
.

1:

Input and Initialization:
Dimension of opt. problem
d>0
, number of clients
n>0
.

2:

Uniformly at random generate permutation
z
of sequence
[d]=\{1\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}2\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\dots\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}d\}

3:

Split z into
n
buckets, where each bucket has a size at least
B=\left\lfloor\nicefrac{{d}}{{n}}\right\rfloor

4:

Each bucket
b_{i}
is initialized with
\{z_{(i\cdot B)-B+1}\mathchar 59\relax\dots\mathchar 59\relax z_{(i\cdot B)}\}\mathchar 59\relax 1\leq i\leq n

5:

Compute the residual
t=d-n\cdot\left\lfloor\nicefrac{{d}}{{n}}\right\rfloor

6:

Sample without replacement
t
clients from
n
as a set
S
,
|S|=t

7:

Scan the set
S=\{s_{1}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\dots\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}s_{k}\mathchar 59\relax\dots\mathchar 59\relax s_{t}\}
and update
b_{s_{k}}=b_{s_{k}}\cup z_{d-t+k}

\mathcal{C}=(\mathcal{C}_{1}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\dots\mathchar 59\relax\mathcal{C}_{n})
:
[\mathcal{C}_{i}(x)]_{j}=n\cdot x_{j}\cdot I(j\in b_{i})

) (

).

Input and Initialization:
learning rate

, start iterate
x^{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}sk}
. All clients and master negotiate a
{seed}
for pseudo-random number generator (
PRG
).

for

3:

for

all workers
i\in\{1\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}2\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\dots\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}n\}
in parallel

do

4:

Generate permutation compressor
\mathcal{C}_{i}^{k}(\cdot;{seed})
for round
k
at worker
i
with Algorithm
9
using the known
seed
.

Compute and compress local gradient
g_{i}^{k}=\mathcal{C}_{i}^{k}(\nabla f_{i}(x^{k}))

6:

Represent
g_{i}^{k}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
in sparse form.

to the master.

{G}^{k}=concat(m_{1}^{k}\mathchar 59\relax\dots\mathchar 59\relax m_{n}^{k})

{G}^{k}
to all workers

for

all workers

) for all compressors
\mathcal{C}_{1}^{k}\mathchar 59\relax\dots\mathchar 59\relax\mathcal{C}_{n}^{k}
with Algorithm
9
using the known

.

Obtain part of

corresponds to

, and

Algorithm 10

DCGD/PermK/AES
,
d\geq n
.

The clients need to send in parallel
n

encrypted
messages to the master. Since clients trust each other due to Assumption (ii) from Section
4.1.1
, symmetric key encryption is a natural choice. In the world of symmetric ciphers, we have decided to use a block cipher, specifically the industry-standard
AES

(
Daemen and Rijmen 1999
)
, instead of stream ciphers like
SALSA

(
Bernstein 2008
)
. The choice was made due to the advantages of block ciphers, as:

i) Hardware support within CPU
1
1

1

AES is supported by Intel x86 Westmere, AMD x86 Bulldozer, ARM Cortex-A53.
.

ii) The provision of multiple security levels (
128\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}192\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}256
bits).

iii) The necessary flexibility to work with available parts of the vector
G^{k}
in coming Algorithm
10
.

The
AES
block-cipher provides security only for a single 16-byte block. The
Modes of Operation
provide a way to use
AES
for more than one block.
Message Authentication Code
(
MAC
) provides guarantees from tampering. In our work, we used
AES/EAX
mode of operation. For details about
AES
see Appendix
F4
.

The naive way to use
AES
is to use Algorithm
8
,
(Option A)
. It solves problems with an untrusted server and channels. Without knowing
sk
, the untrusted party cannot join the training procedure. Due to the use of
MAC
(Lines 5, 13), the training process verifies the integrity and protects against malicious attacks. To provide semantic security against a chosen-plaintext attack (
CPA
) and have the ability to use
sk
in a distributed way, we employ random
nonce
based selection. It eliminates the need for client coordination in a
nonce
selection if
nonce
is big enough such as
128
bits. Details about the role of
nonce
in block ciphers are presented in Appendix
F4
.

With this Algorithm
8

Option-A
, solves (A)–(E) from Section
4.1.1
. However, this strategy has two big downsides: (i) Computation at Lines 13 and 14 is repeated by all clients with complexity per client
\mathcal{O}(dn)
; (ii) Amount of information from the master in Line 9 is
\mathcal{O}(dn)
, not
\mathcal{O}(d)
.

We pay this price due to the use of
AES
, which does not allow us to perform aggregation in the server. From one point of view, the natural way is to perform averaging for vectors in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
in the master (Line 10), but on the other hand, we perform bit-wise operations (Line 5) inside
AES
cipher. There is no way to connect it from the first point of view, but we have discovered how to do it!

The
PermK
correlated compressor from work
(
Szlendak et al. 2022
)
possesses a compelling property that has yet to be recognized. The correlated compressors operate interdependently for clients and the schema for
PermK
was outlined in Algorithm
9
. Let
\mathcal{C}_{1}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\dots\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\mathcal{C}_{n}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
be randomized
PermK
compressors, then

\mathbb{E}\left[\dfrac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathcal{C}_{i}(v_{i})\right]=\dfrac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}v_{i}\mathchar 59\relax\forall v_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}.

If
d\geq n
, then
\dfrac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathcal{C}_{i}(v_{i})
fulfills the variance bound:

\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\dfrac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathcal{C}_{i}(v_{i})-\dfrac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}v_{i}\right\|^{2}\right]\leq\dfrac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left\|v_{i}\right\|^{2}-\left\|\dfrac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}v_{i}\right\|^{2}

.

This compressor is used in our
DCGD/PermK
Algorithm
10
. Its unbiasedness property ensures the absence of systematic errors and the variance bound guarantees that if we are in an overparameterized setting and
x^{k}\to x^{*}
, then the estimator’s variance will decay to zero, which should remove any oscillation behavior near the solution. The crucial property of
DCGD/PermK
is that the aggregation of compressed gradients can be replaced by concatenation, which is only an algebraic monoid for
(\mathbb{R}^{*}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{concat})
, and not the algebraic group. Scaling an encrypted vector by
\nicefrac{{1}}{{n}}
is unfeasible in the master, but this can be delegated to the iterate update Algorithm
10
, Line 17. We can use non-secure pseudo-random generators as
(
Matsumoto and Nishimura 1998
)
for sampling indices because they are independent of the input.

4.3
The Resilience for Attacks

In chosen-plaintext-attack (
CPA
), an attacker may adaptively ask for the encryption
(e_{1}\mathchar 59\relax\dots)
of arbitrary messages
(m_{1}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\dots)
to obtain the ability to correctly guess from the set of two encrypted cipher-texts
\{E_{1}\mathchar 59\relax E_{2}\}
which encryption belongs to which message in the set
\{M_{1}\mathchar 59\relax M_{2}\}
, given that attacker knows the set
\{M_{1}\mathchar 59\relax M_{2}\}
.
CKKS
and
AES
in
EAX
mode of operation
(
Bellare et al. 2004
)
are
CPA
-secure.

In a chosen-ciphertext attack (
CCA
), the attacker has unlimited access to a decryption oracle and aims to achieve the same goal as in
CPA
. No
HE
schemes can achieve
CCA
security
(
Fauzi et al. 2022
)
. However, it has been proven that
AES
/
EAX
is secure against
CCA

(
Bellare et al. 2004
)
.

Algorithms are often executed on general-purpose microprocessors, which process a stream of instructions during execution. However, one subtle aspect lies in the fact that the energy consumption and latency for different operations are different
(
Horowitz 2014
)
. This discrepancy leads to the possibility of side-channel attacks, where the attacker can use information about measurable physical information to detect when, how much, and the type of operations have been executed. To have this ability the executable operations should be some partially invertible function of the secret key or plaintext. The
AES
block cipher and
Operation Modes
perform the same stream of operation types (see Appendix
F4
). However, in
CKKS
polynomial multiplication, encoding, decoding, and randomization can lead to a trace to plain message
(
Aydin et al. 2022
)
using side-channel attacks.

Since the standardization of
AES
, significant efforts have been made to protect it from side-channel attacks
(
Rahaman and Hossain 2008
;
Gross et al. 2017
)
. However, protecting
HE
libraries from such attacks remains an open challenge. For instance,
(
Aydin et al. 2022
)
highlights that the
HE
implementation in the reference

SEAL
library
(
Chen et al. 2017
)
, as well as in derived libraries like

TenSEAL

(
Benaissa et al. 2021
)
, needs to be revised to improve protection against side-channel attacks. The work by
Aydin et al. 2022
demonstrates how an asymmetry in ciphertext generation can be exploited, reducing the security level of the Brakerski/Fan-Vercauteren (
BFV
)
(
Fan and Vercauteren 2012
)
Ring Learning With Error (
RLWE
) scheme from
2^{128}
to
2^{4.4}
(see Appendix
G4.1
). As a result, the protection against side-channel attacks for
AES
can be considered more developed.

4.4
Experiments

We conducted an experimental comparison of several optimization algorithms with different compression and security methods. In Table
4.6
, presented in Appendix
B4
, we summarize their qualitative aspects. Since there is a chain of modifications to
DCGD
baseline, this summary can help trace the steps in our algorithm design.

4.4.1
Synthetic experiments

The ultimate goal of the forthcoming chain of the algorithm design process is to illustrate the advantages of using the block cipher
AES
(see Appendix
F4
) over
CKKS
(see Appendix
G4
). The experiments were conducted in a

FL_PyTorch
simulator
(
Burlachenko et al. 2021
)
. For details on the computing environment see Appendix
E4.1
. We configured the
CKKS
to offer security guarantees as
AES
with
128
bit (
16
bytes) key. In this case, the size of public and private keys for
CKKS
has a lower bound
420\,000
bytes (see Appendix
G4
for underlying reason). During usage of
CKKS
, the public key should be reported somehow to the master once. As we will see while using
DCGD/PermK/AES
no key at all should be reported to the master for the master to operate. Therefore, the key size can be a problem for
CKKS
already when the volume of communicated information is far smaller than
0.42\cdot 10^{6}
Bytes. In our experiments, we neglect the overhead from
key agreement
.

Optimization problem and experimental setup.

In our synthetically controlled experiments, we consider a specific smooth convex optimization problem which is obtained from Equation (
4.1
) via

f_{i}(x)=\dfrac{1}{n_{i}}\|\mathbf{A}_{i}x-b_{i}\|^{2}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{where\,}\mathbf{A}_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{n_{i}\times d}\mathchar 59\relax b_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}.

Case 1: Distributed GD with and without AES/CKKS.

We conducted experiments with
d=1000
,
n=50
, and
n_{i}=12
. In our designed experimental setup, we filled the Hessian
\nabla^{2}f(x)
such that its nonzero eigenvalues lie uniformly in
[1.0\mathchar 59\relax 10.0]
, therefore
L_{f}=10
. We used the maximum theoretical constant step size
\gamma=1/L_{f}
for
GD
. Figure
4.1
(a) shows the impact of IEEE-754 FP16, FP32, and FP64 formats. It shows that
GD/AES
does not hurt float arithmetic. However,
GD/AES
increases traffic between the master and clients by a factor of
n
as we see from Figure
4.1
(b) and increases wall clock time by a factor of 1.7 as in Figure
4.1
(c). Next, Figure
4.2
compares
GD
with
AES
and
CKKS
. According to Figure
4.2
(a),
GD/CKKS
and FP64 arithmetic, it is possible to obtain
|\nabla f(x^{k})|^{2}\approx 10^{-9}
, when
GD/AES
attains
|\nabla f(x^{k})|^{2}\approx 10^{-23}
. From Figure
4.2
(b, c) we see that
GD/CKKS
increases load from master slightly compared to
GD/AES
, but increases the load from clients by
\times 10^{4}
. As per Figure
4.2
(d),
CKKS
is approximately
\times 3
slower compared to
AES
. Using
GD/AES
is reasonable only if
d
and
n
are small.

. No compression. Theoretical step sizes.

Case 2: DCGD with AES/CKKS.

In this experiment, we employ
DCGD
with
RandK
sparsification compressor to analyze the possibility of gradient sparsification while preserving the client’s privacy from the master. To compress
\nabla f_{i}(x)
each client creates a set
S_{i}\subset\{1\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}2\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\dots\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}d\}
of size
K
chosen uniformly at random, and compute
C(\nabla f_{i}(x))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\dfrac{d}{K}\sum_{j\in S_{i}}[\nabla f_{i}(x)]_{j}\cdot e_{j}
, where
e_{j}
are unit vectors of standard basis of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Results are presented in Figure
4.3
, and Figure
4.4
. Figure
4.3
(a) and Figure
4.4
(a) demonstrates that using
AES
does not lead to numerical issues, whereas
CKKS
for FP64 does. In
GD
, the master broadcasts a vector from
\mathbb{R}^{d}
in each round. For
DCGD/RandK/AES
, if clients can reconstruct sparsified indices, then the master has to broadcast
d=K\cdot n=\dfrac{1}{5}\cdot n
encrypted scalars and
32n
bytes from employing
nonce
, and
mac
for privacy and integrity. This process reduces the number of bits transmitted from the master to the clients by a factor
\times 5
, compared to standard
GD/AES
, as depicted in Figure
4.3
(b) and Figure
4.1
(b). Figure
4.4
(c) shows that
CKKS
does not leverage the sparsity. For
HE
schemas, encoding of any two vectors (for example, sparse and dense) should be indistinguishable due to semantic security requirements. However, from a computational perspective, ignoring sparsity is sometimes highly impractical, and this gap presents an open research question for
HE
. Figure
4.4
(b) highlights that
AES
adapts to any bit representation of scalars. In contrast,

TenSEAL

(
Benaissa et al. 2021
)
implementation of
CKKS
does not exhibit this property.

,

,

. Compressors:

[

]. Theoretical step sizes.

[

]. Theoretical step sizes.

Case 3: DCGD with PermK.

Both
GD/AES
and
DCGD/AES
require a significant amount of data to be sent from the master to the clients. In the case of using correlated compressors as
PermK
, all clients do not intersect in supports of sparsified gradients by design. The encryption of the global direction can be obtained by concatenating encrypted messages from clients. When using
PermK
, clients do not need to perform any aggregation on their side, and decryption of the whole global direction obtained from master can be done in
\mathcal{O}(d)
independent of
n
. We aim to find an approximate
\gamma
for
DCGD/PermK
. We generated
5
problems with matrices
\mathbf{A}_{i}\sim U[0\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}1)^{n_{i}\times d}
, projected
\mathbf{A}=[\mathbf{A}_{1}\mathchar 59\relax\dots\mathchar 59\relax\mathbf{A}_{n}]^{\top}
to have
L_{f}=10
, and computed
b_{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\mathbf{A}_{i}x_{\mathrm{fixed}}
. We tested various step sizes demonstrated in Figure
4.5
. We found that using a step size
\gamma\geq\dfrac{1}{2L_{f}}=0.05
led to divergence, as shown in Figure
4.5
(a, b). From Figure
4.5
(a), we see that the method exhibits linear convergence without oscillation near the solution, similar to
DCGD
with
RandK
(Here
\nabla f_{i}(x^{*})=0\mathchar 59\relax\forall i\in[n]
, because
d>n_{i}\cdot n
). From Figure
4.5
(c), we see that the variance of the optimization path using fixed step size
\gamma=0.007
and fixed
d\mathchar 59\relax n_{i}\mathchar 59\relax n\mathchar 59\relax L_{f}
is negligible.

:
Tuning step size
\gamma
without decay for
DCGD/PermK
. Synthesized
linear regression
in interpolation,
5
launches, FP64.

Comparison of DCGD/PermK/AES and GD/CKKS.

Figure
4.6
compares
GD
,
GD/CKKS
,
GD/PermK
with
GD/AES
,
GD/PermK/AES
. We see that the
CKKS
schema does not leverage the sparsity of vectors, making sparsification-based compression ineffective in reducing communication during privacy-preserving training. Therefore, there is no benefit from using
RandK
for
DCGD/CKKS
, and it is better to use vanilla
GD
. For
GD
, we used theoretical step size, which in practice is extremely tight. If communication isn’t free, Figure
4.6
(a) and Figure
4.6
(d) suggest that
CKKS
is impractical in settings where client-master communication is a bottleneck. Figure
4.6
(b) shows that
GD/AES
does not increase client-to-master traffic but does significantly increase master-to-client traffic, as seen in Figure
4.6
(d). Figure
4.6
(c) shows that the best convergence in terms of rounds is attained for
GD
or
GD/AES
with preserving security. If communication is free,
GD/CKKS
remains suboptimal due to the approximate nature of floating-point operations in
CKKS
. Next, suppose the key metric is the convergence of
\|{\nabla f(x^{k})}\|
relative to the number of bits transmitted from client to master. Figure
4.6
(b) shows that
GD/CKKS
uses approximately
0.6\cdot 10^{7}
bits per client after the first round of optimization.
DCGD/PermK/AES
with this transfers can attain
\|{\nabla f(x)}\|^{2}\approx 10^{-20}
.

,

.

4.4.2
Image classification application

We evaluated the applicability of using
DCGD/PermK/AES
on Deep Neural Networks training. We used the
ResNet-18
architecture
(
He et al. 2016
)
and trained it on
CIFAR-10
dataset
(
Krizhevsky and Hinton 2009
)
, which consists of
60\,000
images across
10
classes with a resolution of
32\times 32
pixels. We used
ResNet-18
implementation from TorchVision library, part of

PyTorch

(
Paszke et al. 2019
)
. The model size
d=11\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}181\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}642
. For Optimization Problem (
4.1
), we used a standard cross-entropy loss for
\mathcal{L}_{ij}
terms in Equation (
4.2
). Next, we distributed the dataset uniformly across
n=10
clients, with each client participating in every round. Global learning rate
0.1
, local learning rate
0.1
, local weight decay
5\cdot 10^{-4}
, number of rounds
5\,000
. During each round, clients evaluate
\nabla f_{i}(x)
using a fixed 10% subset of each client’s local data points. In FL, there are cases where clients cannot store a large number of data points (for example, high-resolution images, recorded voice in
IoT
devices) due to storage limitations. Our experiment simulates this scenario.
We compared our implementation with
FedAVG

(
McMahan et al. 2017
)
, where no compression or encryption is performed. We tracked during training communicated message size, accuracy, and convergence. Our reported metrics in Figure
4.7
are computation framework and communication topology independent. In these experiments, we did not carry out an analysis of the spent time for communication, compression, and
AES
encryption/decryption, because the actual numbers are highly implementation-dependent quantities.

In
DCGD/PermK/AES
, the message size from the client to the master contains
\left\lceil\nicefrac{{d}}{{10}}\right\rceil=1\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}118\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}165
parameters with
4
bytes each one. The overhead from
AES-128/EAX
Operation Mode is
32
bytes per single message, which is negligible. Figure
4.7
shows that the message size was reduced from
42.65
(for
FedAVG
) MBytes to
4.26
MBytes (for
DCGD/PermK
). We cannot launch
CKKS
with security guarantees as
AES-128
in our environment due to memory overhead. In Appendix
4.4.1
, we demonstrated that problems start appearing in
CKKS
with
AES-128
security guarantees already for
d=10^{6}
. For a discussion about extra flexibility in Training DL models see Appendix
I4
.

4.5
Deployment Flexibility

The
physical network topologies
describe the arrangement of the computation and routing devices. In a mesh topology, every pair of nodes is connected with a dedicated link. It has high bandwidth and fault tolerance but requires a lot of cables. In this setting,
DCGD/PermK
is the natural choice. The Algorithm
10
can be observed as a series of broadcast operations to reconstitute the optimization step. See Appendix
J4
for the benefits in other topologies.

4.6
Exploring Computation-Communication Overlap

In Appendix
H4.2
, we present the refined scheduled communication and computation plan that utilizes the possibility of computation communication overlap and handling computation heterogeneity during training
linear regression
. The modeled situation that we studied contained
5
clients (one of them is a straggler) which all are connected to the master with a shared channel. We showed that the execution plan can be refined, achieving a speedup of
\times 1.31
for
GD
and
\times 3.33
for
DCGD/PermK
.
DCGD/PermK
offers the flexibility to start multiple compute operations while waiting for the straggler, a feature not possible in
GD
. Therefore,
DCGD/PermK
, with knowledge of timings, can be rescheduled more effectively.

4.7
Conclusions

We proposed a novel secure FL framework that employs symmetric-key encryption
AES

(
Daemen and Rijmen 1999
)
with permutation compressors
PermK

(
Szlendak et al. 2022
)
to protect and compress gradients during training. We conducted experiments on both real and synthetic data. Our work introduces a new path for applying classical cryptography to FL and challenges claims about its limitations.

The appendices for this work contain the following:

•

Appendices
A4
,
D4
,
E4
,
F4
, and
G4
. Because our work is multidisciplinary, we provide a series of appendices to support readers from various backgrounds.

•

Appendix
H4
. Experiments studying the effect of problem dimension, overlapping communication, and computation.

•

Appendix
I4
. Flexibility in training DL models and auxiliary research opportunities for parallelization within Backpropagation.

•

Appendix
J4
. Deployment flexibility across various network topologies.

4

Glossary

Table 4.1
:
General terminology in the context of Federated Learning.

Term

Meaning

The term is overloaded. In the context of Federated Learning literature, typically means Multi-Party Computation.

Table 4.2
:
Terminology from Mathematical Optimization.

Term

Meaning

d

Dimension of optimization variable.

n

Number of clients/agents/devices.

f_{i}

Local loss function on client number
i
.

f

Objective function with we want to minimize with image
\mathbb{R}
and domain
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

\gamma

Step size of learning rate.

x

Trainable or Optimization variable from
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

x^{k}

Trainable or Optimization variable at most outer loop of optimization algorithm number
k
.

round

The iteration in the outermost loop of the optimization algorithm.

e_{i}

The vector from
\mathbb{R}^{d}
whose i-th component is equal
1
while all the others are zeros.

u.a.r.

uniformly at random.

r.v.

random variable.

Table 4.3
:
Terminology from Discrete Mathematics.

Term

Meaning

Algebra

Set of elements and defined operations on set which lead to elements of the same set.

Monoid

Any algebra with the binary, associative operation, which also has a neutral
element. Example - concatenation.

Group

Any algebra with the binary, associative operation, with a neutral element and each element has an inverse.

Ring

Algebra with summation, and multiplication. A summation is a commutative group, multiplication is a monoid. Multiplication is distributive with respect to summation. In a Ring without extra assumptions, it can have a situation such that
a\neq 0
,
b\neq 0
, but
ab=0
.

Polynomial

p(x)=p_{0}+p_{1}\cdot x+p_{2}\cdot x^{2}+\dots
.

K[x]

Let K be some field. The
K[x]
is the set of all polynomials with coefficients in some field
K
. In the Ring of polynomials
K[X]
there is no division in general. However, it is possible to perform division with residual. One basis for
K[x]
is
\{1\mathchar 59\relax x\mathchar 59\relax x^{2}\mathchar 59\relax\dots\}
.

Z[x]

Integer polynomial rings over commutative ring
Z
is denoted as
Z[x]
. This is the set of polynomials whose coefficients are integers and polynomials depend only on one variable.

P[x]/(x^{2}+1)

This notation means polynomials which are obtained in the following way. We take the polynomial ring
P[x]
and perform modulus division by the polynomial
x^{2}+1
. This restricts obtained polynomials to have a power less than
2
. Such a ring is an example of a quotient ring.

Unity Roots

Roots of unity are roots of the following equation
Z^{n}=1
. If
Z\in\mathbb{C}
the roots are:
z_{k}=\exp(2\pi ki/n)
for
k=0\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}1\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\dots\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}n-1
.

GCD

The greatest common divisor (GCD) of two or more integers, that are not zero, is the largest positive integer that divides each of the integers. For example
\gcd(8\mathchar 59\relax 12)=4
.

Homomorphism

Homomorphism of two groups
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
is a mapping
f:G_{1}\to G_{2}
between two groups, such that
\forall x\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}y\in G_{1}
the following holds:
f(x*y)=f(x)*f(y)

Isomomorphism

Isomomorphism of groups
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
is a mapping
f:G_{1}\to G_{2}
between two groups, such
\forall x\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}y\in G_{1}
the following holds:
f(x*y)=f(x)*f(y)
and
f
is bijection mapping. From the point of view of Algebraic structures, two isomorphic groups are the same, even if they have different nature.

The Learning With Errors (LWE) search problem to find a solution to a noisy system of linear equations.

RLWE

The Ring Learning With Errors (RLWE) problem involves finding a solution to a noisy system of linear equations, where the underlying algebra is a polynomial over a ring.

Integer Lattice

It is a set of points that correspond to all possible linear combinations with integer coefficients of the
n
vectors
b_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
:
\{x:x=\sum_{i=1}^{n}{\alpha}_{i}b_{i}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}{\alpha}_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}\}
.

B4
Compared Optimization Algorithms

In our work, we made a comparison of several optimization algorithms with different compression and security methods. In Table
4.6
below we summarize their qualitative and qualitative features.

Table 4.6
:
Compared optimization algorithms with compression and privacy mechanisms.

Name

Description

1.
GD [FP16|FP32|FP64]

Algorithm
8
, Baseline (
B
),
\mathcal{C}_{i}(x)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}x
.

Gradient Descent. In this algorithm, clients in a distributed way compute the gradient for the function
f_{i}
defined in Equation (
4.2
) in the current iterate. Master obtains gradients from the clients and averages them. The master broadcasts the result directly to all clients. The specified Floating-Point format is used to represent both data and trainable variables in all clients and during aggregation in the master.

Quantitative and qualitative characteristics:

1.

It’s completely insecure (see Appendix
E4.1
).

2.

Volume information from clients to master is
\mathcal{O}(dn)
bytes per round.

3.

Broadcasted information from master to clients is
\mathcal{O}(d)
bytes per round.

For details on why information about
\nabla f_{i}(x;D_{i})
can potentially reveal information about dataset
D_{i}
see discussion in Appendix
E4.1
.

2.
GD [FP16|FP32|FP64] /AES-128

Algorithm
8
, Naive Usage of
AES
(
A
),
\mathcal{C}_{i}(x)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}x
.

Gradient Descent with symmetric
AES
encryption which uses a key size of

128
bits (
16
bytes). In this algorithm, clients in a distributed way compute the gradient for the function
f_{i}
defined in Equation (
4.2
) in the current iterate. Then clients encrypt computed
\nabla f_{i}(x)
in parallel. After that, the Master collects the encoded gradients as a communication hub. Unfortunately, the master can not perform any algebraic operations, because
\nabla f_{i}(x)
is encrypted, and the master needs to broadcast all directions to the clients. The specified Floating-Point format is used to represent both data and trainable variables in all clients and during aggregation in the master.

During using
AES/EAX
there exists a fixed negligible overhead from using EAX Mode Operation Mode of
32
bytes in each client to master communication information transfer. Specifically, this Mode Operation includes the following overheads:
Nonce
- a random
16
-byte value, and the
Tag
-
16
-byte message authentication code.

Quantitative and qualitative characteristics:

1.

Master cannot average obtained encrypted clients’ local gradient.

2.

Volume information from clients to master is
\mathcal{O}(dn+32n)
bytes per round.

3.

Broadcasted information from master to clients is
\mathcal{O}(dn+32n)
bytes per round.

For details about
AES
see Appendix
F4
.

3.
DCGD [FP16|FP32|FP64] /AES-128/RandK

Algorithm
8
, Naive Usage of
AES
(
A
),

\mathcal{C}_{i}(x)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\dfrac{d}{k}\sum_{i\in S}x_{i}\cdot e_{i}\mathchar 59\relax S\sim_{u.a.r}\{s:s\in 2^{[d]}\mathchar 59\relax|s|=k\}.

Distributed Compressed Gradient Descent with symmetric
AES
encryption which uses a key size of

128
bits (
16
bytes), and with using
RandK
sparsifier.

In this algorithm, clients in a distributed way compute the gradient for the function
f_{i}
defined in Equation (
4.2
) in the current iterate. After this, the clients compress them by selecting
k
components from
d
u.a.r. Then clients use a sparse bitwise representation of the sparsified gradient and encode non-zero values with
AES/EAX
mode of operation. Then clients encrypt computed
\mathcal{C}(\nabla f_{i}(x))
in parallel. After that, the Master collects the encoded gradients as a communication hub. Unfortunately, the master can not perform any algebraic operations, because
\nabla f_{i}(x)
is encrypted, and the master needs to broadcast all directions to the clients. The specified Floating-Point format is used to represent both data and trainable variables in all clients and during aggregation in the master.

During using
AES/EAX
there exists a fixed negligible overhead from using EAX Mode Operation Mode of
32
bytes in each client to master communication information transfer. Specifically, this Mode Operation includes the following overheads:
Nonce
- a random
16
-byte value, and the
Tag
-
16
-byte message authentication code. The implementation of
RandK
sparsifier in the case of using a pseudo-random generator can be implemented in a way that no indices should be transferred during training because they can be reconstituted.

Quantitative and qualitative characteristics:

1.

Master cannot average obtained encrypted and compressed clients’ local gradient.

2.

Volume information from clients to master is
\mathcal{O}(kn+32n)
bytes per round.

3.

Broadcasted information from master to clients is
\mathcal{O}(kn+32n)
bytes per round.

4.

Fundamentally, there is some redundancy induced by the inability to perform aggregation in the master.

4.
GD [FP16|FP32|FP64] /CKKS

Algorithm
8
, Baseline (
B
),
\mathcal{C}_{i}(x)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}x
.
Extra encryption is carried via employing
CKKS
.

In this algorithm, clients in a distributed way compute the gradient for the function
f_{i}
defined in Equation (
4.2
) in the current iterate. After this, each client using the public key in
CKKS
schema performs encryption of gradient vectors. Master obtains the encoded gradients from the clients. Then the master using a public key performs aggregation and relinearization to reduce the size of a ciphertext after arithmetic operations. After this master broadcast aggregated gradient estimator. Clients using private (secret) keys perform the decryption of the encrypted direction from the master and then make a step.

Quantitative and qualitative characteristics:

1.

CKKS
encryption considerably increases ciphertext space. The volume of information from clients to master
\mathcal{O}(C\cdot dn)
bytes, where
C
is a constant which depends on
d
, security level, degree of the used polynomial, and cardinality of space
\mathbb{Z}_{q}
to represent integer coefficient of two transferred polynomials after encoding.

2.

Size of public and private key to guarantee
AES-128
for
CKKS
is approximately
420\,000
bytes, while for
AES-128
the key size is
16
bytes. In some tasks, this key size is negligible, in some tasks, it is not. For details see Appendix
G4
.

3.

CKKS
does not operate on the level of bits only and can operate only with FP64 floating-point format in

TenSeal
implementation. Therefore, there is no possibility of using less precision in combination with
CKKS
.

For details about
CKKS
see Appendix
G4
.

5.
DCGD [FP16|FP32|FP64] /RandK/CKKS

Algorithm
8
, Baseline (
B
),

\mathcal{C}_{i}(x)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\dfrac{d}{k}\sum_{i\in S}x_{i}\cdot e_{i}\mathchar 59\relax S\sim_{u.a.r}\{s:s\in 2^{[d]}\mathchar 59\relax|s|=k\}.

Extra encryption is carried via employing
CKKS
.

Distributed Compressed Gradient Descent with
CKKS
. In this algorithm, clients in a distributed way compute the gradient for the function
f_{i}
defined in Equation (
4.2
) in the current iterate. After this, the clients compress them by selecting
k
components from
d
u.a.r. After this, each client using the public key in
CKKS
schema performs encryption of sparsified gradient vectors. Master obtains the encoded gradients from the clients. Then the master using a public key performs aggregation and relinearization to reduce the size of a ciphertext after arithmetic operations. After this master broadcast aggregated gradient estimator. Clients using private (secret) keys perform the decryption of the encrypted direction from the master and then make a step.

Quantitative and qualitative characteristics:

1.

CKKS
encryption considerably increases ciphertext space. The volume of information from clients to master
\mathcal{O}(C\cdot dn)
, where
C
is a big constant which depends on
d
, security level, degree of the used polynomial, and cardinality of space
\mathbb{Z}_{q}
to represent integer coefficient of two transferred polynomials after encoding. For details see Appendix
G4
.

2.

Size of public and private key to guarantee
AES-128
for
CKKS
is approximately
420\,000
bytes, while for
AES-128
the key size is
16
bytes. In some tasks, this key size is negligible, in some tasks, it is not. For details see Appendix
G4
.

3.

CKKS
does not operate on the level of bits only and can operate only with FP64 floating-point format in

TenSeal
implementation. Therefore, there is no possibility of using less precision in combination with
CKKS
.

4.

The
CKKS
does not support linear operations over sparse vectors from
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. For
HE
schemas, encoding of any two vectors (for example, sparse and dense) should be indistinguishable due to semantic security requirements. However, from a computational perspective, ignoring sparsity is sometimes highly impractical. This gap presents an open research question for
HE
.

6.
DCGD [FP16|FP32|FP64] /PermK/AES

Algorithm
10
.

Distributed Compressed Gradient Descent with using
PermK
sparsifier. In this algorithm, clients in a distributed way compute gradients in the current iterate. After this, the clients compress them by selecting
k
components from
d
jointly via using Algorithm
9
.

Then clients send a sparse bitwise representation of the sparsified gradient. Master collects the encoded gradients as a communication hub.

The
PermK
compressor guarantees that what is needed to perform at master is only concatenation. Therefore, the algorithm can be implemented in practice in situations when the master can store, but cannot compute.

The specified floating-point format is used to represent both data and trainable variables. The implementation of
PermK
in the case of using a pseudo-random generator can be implemented in a way, that no indices should be transferred during training because they can be reconstituted, and negotiation between clients should be carried in runtime if clients have negotiated an initial seed.

Quantitative and qualitative characteristics:

1.

There is
32
byte overhead during message transfers compared to
DCGD/RandK
.

2.

The current implementation works only in case
d\geq n
, when
DCGD/RandK
and
GD/CKKS
do not require this

3.

Volume information from clients to master is
\mathcal{O}(d+32n)
bytes per round.

4.

Broadcasted information from master to clients is
\mathcal{O}(d+32n)
bytes per round.

7.
DCGD [FP16|FP32|FP64] /PermK

Algorithm
10
in which
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}Encrypt}(x)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}x
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}Decrypt}(x)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}x
.

Distributed Compressed Gradient Descent with using
PermK
sparsifier. In this algorithm, clients in a distributed way compute gradients in the current iterate. After this, the clients compress them by selecting
k
components from
d
jointly via using Algorithm
9
. Then clients send a sparse bitwise representation of the sparsified gradient. Master collects the encoded gradients as a communication hub. The
PermK
compressor guarantees that what is needed to perform at master is only concatenation. Therefore, the algorithm can be implemented in practice in situations when the master can store, but cannot compute. The selected floating point format is used to represent both data and trainable variables. The implementation of
PermK
in case of using a pseudo-random generator can be implemented in a way, that no indices should be transferred during training because they can be reconstituted and negotiation between clients should be carried in runtime if clients have negotiatedan initial seed.

Quantitative and qualitative characteristics:

1.

The current implementation works only in case
d\geq n
only, when
DCGD/RandK
and
GD/CKKS
does not require this.

2.

This algorithm ignores privacy and security aspects.

3.

Volume information from clients to master is
\mathcal{O}(d+32n)
bytes per round.

4.

Broadcasted information from master to clients is
\mathcal{O}(d+32n)
bytes per round.

and it does not support FP16 arithmetic. The machine also has an NVIDIA GeForce RTX 3090 GPU built on Ampere microarchitecture with 24 GBytes of DRAM
