Results of refined scheduling for GD and DCGD/PermK/AES

Scheduling results are presented in Figure
4.10
. In our experiment, all clients have the same compute power equal to
238.41
GFLOPS. Communication bandwidth and latency from all clients to the master and vice versa is the same, namely
B_{i}=41.54
Mbps, and latency (or delay) for transmission is
28\cdot 10^{-3}
seconds. The assumption for computation is that each memory operation is carried out with an L2 cache. In case there is a need to perform read and write, we treat it as two memory operations. We assume all clients during communication to the master share the same bottleneck communication link. Also, we assume that the target CPU supports
AES
. The detailed generated execution plans can not be represented in this work due to their size. However, they can be found in the source code attached to the work.

As illustrated in Figure
4.10
, overlapping communication, computation, and the refined scheduling due to time-varying into shared communication bus and time-varying load into computing devices in clients leads to different speedups in execution plans (Figure
4.10
, (b, d)) for
GD
and
DCGD/PermK
. The total computation speedup per round when compared to refined
GD
is
6.578
. We hope this simulation will prove valuable for those seeking to adopt
DCGD/PermK/AES
to align with hardware requirements closely.

I4
Flexibility in Training Deep Learning Models

I4.1
Introduction to backpropagation

In Appendix
H4.2
we have explored that
DCGD/PermK
exhibits practical flexibility for training linear models in detail. Described communication and computation overlap can be important not only for linear models but also for DL models.

The evaluation of the gradient of the score function in modern computational frameworks such as

TensorFlow

(
Abadi et al. 2016
)
or

PyTorch

(
Paszke et al. 2019
)
for complex computational graph is automated via leveraging algorithms for the numeric evaluation of derivatives. In most cases, this is achieved using
Automatic Differentiation (AD) in Reverse Accumulation mode

(
Linnainmaa 1970
)
named as a
Backpropagation Algorithm

(
Rumelhart et al. 1986
)
in ML literature. A composed loss function for DL models typically has the following structure:

\displaystyle f_{i}(x)=\sum_{j=1}^{n_{i}}\mathcal{L}(b_{real\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}\mathchar 59\relax{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}g_{1}}([x]_{Q_{1}}\mathchar 59\relax a_{j}\mathchar 59\relax{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}g_{2}}([x]_{Q_{2}}\mathchar 59\relax a_{j}\mathchar 59\relax\dots)))+R(x)

(4.3)

\displaystyle Q_{1}\cup Q_{2}\dots=[d]\mathchar 59\relax Q_{i}\cap Q_{j}\dots=0\mathchar 59\relax\forall i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j.

The score function
f_{i}(x)
is represented by a computation graph and has a nested structure where prediction is driven by a nested composition of functions
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}g_{i}}
and the predicted label is scored with a true label for training input-output pair
(a_{j}\mathchar 59\relax b_{real\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j})
with a score loss function
\mathcal{L}
. Each function
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}g_{i}}
has the following source of inputs: output from the prevision function (or layer)
g_{i-1}
, and input (trainable) scalar parameters
x_{p}
where
p\in\{Q_{1}\mathchar 59\relax Q_{2}\mathchar 59\relax\dots\}
, and
Q_{i}\subseteq[d]
.

Assume that
f_{i}(x)
is differentiable in the points where derivatives according to the chain rule are evaluated. In this case, the needed partial derivatives

[\nabla f_{i}(x)]_{k}=\dfrac{\partial f_{i}}{\partial x_{k}}

can be computed with chaining Jacobians calculated with intermediate variables. The
Backpropagation Algorithm
allows for fixed input samples
D_{i}={(a_{1}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}b_{1})\mathchar 59\relax\dots\mathchar 59\relax(a_{n_{i}}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}b_{n_{i}})}
stored in client number
i
and for fixed computational graph

f_{i}(x;D_{i})

compute the required gradient
\nabla f_{i}(x)
in two
passes
or two
phases
:

1.

Forward Pass
. Compute all intermediate variables, namely values of
g_{i}()
for every training sample, for the whole compute graph. All these variables are stored in memory for the next pass.

2.

Backward Pass
. Compute the intermediate Jacobians and produce final partial derivatives of a full gradient.

There is no need to store all intermediate Jacobians simultaneously. They are stored implicitly in special variables typically denoted as
\delta
in the implementation of backpropagation. However, there is a need to store all intermediate outputs explicitly (named as activation in ML literature) after
Forward Pass
.

I4.2
Examples of parallelization inside backpropagation

Computationally backpropagation is often a preferred strategy when the entire gradient needs to be computed. The compute scheduling and parallelism strategies for effective computation of
\nabla f_{i}(x)
is an active line of research in DL system literature
(
Jia et al. 2019b
;
Krizhevsky 2014
)
. The standard strategies for performing parallelism inside computation of
\nabla f_{i}(x)
involve the following strategies:

1.

Data parallelism.
Assign train samples
(a_{j}\mathchar 59\relax b_{j})
to different computation devices in client
i
.

2.

Model parallelism.
Assign different functions
g_{i}
to different computation devices available in client
i
.

3.

Attributes parallelism.
Split the image of functions
g_{i-1}
into parts and process it by parts if function
g_{i}
allows to do it.

4.

Parameters parallelism.
Partition trainable variables for function
g_{i}(x)
, into smaller chunks
[x]_{Q_{i}}
. After partitioning
[x]_{Q_{i}}
if
g_{i}(x)
can be computed in parallel, then compute it in parallel in different devices.

Remark on terminology.

Some researchers adopt this terminology, while others use alternative terms. In fact, the object from Equation (
4.3
) is already sufficiently complex and remains an active area of research. Therefore, constructing a universal terminology is likely to be challenging.

I4.3
Research opportunities for parallelization in backpropagation from DCGD/PermK

Assume that clients know the current iterate
x^{k}
, however, if there is even one straggler that still did not send
{g_{s}}^{k}
to the Master, then no clients can proceed in training. Master has to wait for gradient estimator
{g_{s}}^{k}
from straggler, and what can be done for
DCGD/RandK
is at least challenging in these circumstances.

However,
DCGD/PermK
exhibits useful properties that can be partially helpful in dealing with this situation, which we previously discussed in Appendix
H4.2
:

•

Clients can obtain (partial) results from the master
\left[\nabla f(x^{k})\right]_{part}
.

•

Clients can apply partial update for
x^{k}
and obtain partially new model
x_{part}^{k+1}
.

It depends on the specific situation, but during the waiting of a straggler, the forward potentially can be started using the available
x_{part}^{k+1}
(
forward pass
in practice takes at most
50\%
of
\nabla f_{i}(x)
computation). Assume that we have statistical information about stragglers, then potentially this information can be provided to DL schedulers and parallelization strategies described in the previous section Appendix
I4.2
to optimize
\nabla f_{i}(x^{k+1})
.

This opens new opportunities to refine parallelization strategies for training in general, and
parameter parallelism
in particular, due to the relaxed synchronization requirements of
DCGD/PermK
.

J4
Flexibility of DCGD/PermK Across Communication Topologies

In High-Performance Computation and Network Communication, the
physical network topologies
describe the physical arrangement of the devices and routing devices for organizing communication. Next, we will explore what benefits and flexibility
DCGD/PermK
can bring if this algorithm runs in the mentioned popular physical network topologies.

Potential benefits in point-to-point topology

In this type of topology, some clients are directly connected with a single link pairwise. It is possible to instantiate
DCGD/PermK
so that clients do not coordinate during runtime for aggregation computation. It is possible because there is no need to perform reduction in the sense of averaging
\nabla f_{i}(x)
. If some pair clients are connected with the fast link, this pair of clients can utilize this channel and deliver
\left[\nabla f(x_{part}^{k+1})\right]_{part}
for his neighbor with a fast point-to-point link without obtaining it from a master.

Potential benefits in bus topology

A single cable or bus connects all the nodes in this type of topology. In this type of topology, if some clients have slow computing, it removes them from competition for a shared bus and decreases communication contention. In a bus topology, the benefit of
DCGD/PermK
is that messages from each client can be effectively broadcast. In fact,
DCGD/PermK
can be implemented without a centralized server, and a shared bus is enough for organized communication. Employing
AES
based encryption guarantees protection from eavesdropping on the shared bus.

Potential benefits in star topology

In this type, all the nodes are connected to a central master device with individual links. In the case of using
DCGD/PermK
, the master in this topology only plays the role of communication hub. Interestingly, while obtaining parts of the gradients from clients, computation is unnecessary, and synchronization is far more relaxed, as we have observed in Appendix
H4.2
. While using
DCGD/PermK
, the server can potentially utilize communication links with Full Duplex mode. Examples of Full Duplex communication buses include PCI-Express, InfiniBand
7
7

7

https://network.nvidia.com/pdf/whitepapers/HPC_Clustering_130.pdf
, and some forms of Ethernet (see
(
Spurgeon 2000
)
Table 4.1 in Chapter 4). In our setting, we consider the situation when clients are connected to the Internet, and as it has been described in Appendix
H4.2
, PCI-Express is not a bottleneck. However, in data centers, PCI-Express can also represent a bottleneck
(
Li et al. 2019
)
. With
DCGD/PermK
, during some period, a single link can be used simultaneously to obtain information from clients and deliver information to the clients. This is not the case for
DCGD/RandK
or
GD
because they require explicit synchronization. But it is the case for
DCGD/PermK
. This doubles the maximum bandwidth during master client communication and decreases latency by a factor of two.

Potential benefits in ring topology

This type of topology connects all the nodes circularly. The ring topology is sometimes preferable because it reduces the number of
NIC
and cables required for connecting multiple devices. Specifically, each device only needs one
NIC
to participate in all-reduce. Also, ring topology avoids congestion and collisions at the central hub as messages are distributed evenly along the ring. The time delay during performing aggregation for
GD
or
DCGD/RandK
in a ring topology represents a sum of delays along the circle and is equal to

\tau_{\mathrm{ring\,delay}}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\dfrac{RTT_{i}}{2}+\dfrac{d}{B_{i}}\cdot bpp.

Fundamentally, this is because when using
GD
or
DCGD/RandK
, clients cannot begin computation for the next iteration until they have computed the entire gradient. For
DCGD/PermK
clients, after obtaining
\left[\nabla f(x_{part}^{k})\right]_{part}
from the neighbor, can partially start computation for the next iteration.

Potential benefits in mesh topology

This topology connects every node to every other node with dedicated links. It has high bandwidth and fault tolerance, but requires a lot of cables and ports and is complex to install. In this form of topology,
DCGD/PermK
is the most natural choice because underlying communication topology naturally maps to communication pattern in
DCGD/PermK
, which can observed if view Algorithm
10
as a series of broadcast operations to reconstitute the global direction for optimization step.

Potential benefits in tree topology

This topology combines multiple star topologies into a hierarchical structure with a root node. In this configuration, broadcasting can be implemented very efficiently. One potential benefit of
DCGD/PermK
is that it can operate without a central server; instead, each client broadcasts information to other clients in Line 6 of Algorithm
10
.

K4
Reproducibility

To ensure reproducibility, we use the following

FL_PyTorch
simulator
(
Burlachenko et al. 2021
)
features: random seeds were fixed for data reshuffling, and random seeds were fixed for the runtime pseudo-random generators involved in variants of
DCGD
for randomized compressors.

The source code of our experiments is a part of our submission to the
4th International Workshop on Distributed Machine Learning, co-located with CoNEXT 2023
. The source code, along with the implementation and documentation, is publicly available at the following link:

https://github.com/burlachenkok/fl-with-nonhe

Chapter 5
PAGE Extensions: Refining the Optimal Single-Node SGD Optimizer

The goals and summaries of this chapter are outlined in Table
1.1
and Section
1.4.4
.

5.1
Introduction

In this work, we consider the minimization of the average of
n
smooth functions (
5.1
) in the non-convex setting of the regime when the number of functions
n
is very large. In this regime, calculation of the exact gradient can be infeasible and the classical gradient descent method (
GD
)
(
Nesterov 2018
)
can not be applied. The structure of the problem is generic, and such problems arise in many applications, including Machine Learning
(
Bishop and Nasrabadi 2006
)
and Computer Vision
(
Goodfellow et al. 2016
)
. Problems of this form are the basis of empirical risk minimization (ERM), which is the prevalent paradigm for training supervised machine learning models.

5.1.1
Finite-sum optimization in the smooth non-convex regime

We consider the finite-sum optimization problem

\displaystyle\min\limits_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\{f(x)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\dfrac{1}{n}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}f_{i}(x)\right\}\mathchar 59\relax

(5.1)

where
f_{i}\,:\,\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is a smooth (and possibly non-convex) function for all
i\in[n]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\{1\mathchar 59\relax\dots\mathchar 59\relax n\}.
We are interested in randomized algorithms that find an
\varepsilon
-stationary point of (
5.1
) by returning a random point
\widehat{x}
such that

\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\nabla f(\widehat{x})\right\|^{2}\right]\leq\varepsilon.

The main efficiency metric of gradient-based algorithms for finding such a point is the (expected) number of gradient computations
\nabla f_{i}
; we will refer to it as the
complexity
of an algorithm.

5.1.2
Related work

The area of algorithmic research devoted to designing methods for solving the Problem (
5.1
) in the smooth non-convex regime is one of the most highly developed and most competitive in optimization.

The path to optimality.
Let us provide a lightning-speed overview of recent progress.
The complexity of
GD
for solving (
5.1
) is
\mathcal{O}\left(n\varepsilon^{-1}\right)
, but this was subsequently improved by more elaborate stochastic methods, including
SAGA
,
SVRG
and
SCSG

(
Defazio et al. 2014
;
Johnson and Zhang 2013
;
Lei et al. 2017
;
Horváth and Richtárik 2019
)
, which enjoy the better complexity
\mathcal{O}\left(n+n^{2/3}\varepsilon^{-1}\right)
. Further progress was obtained by methods such as
SNVRG
and
Geom-SARAH

(
Zhou et al. 2020
;
Horváth et al. 2022
)
, improving the complexity to
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(n+n^{1/2}\varepsilon^{-1}\right)
. Finally, the methods
SPIDER
,
SpiderBoost
,
SARAH
and
PAGE

(
Fang et al. 2018
;
Wang et al. 2019
;
Nguyen et al. 2017
;
Li et al. 2021a
)
, among others, shaved-off certain logarithmic factors and obtained the
optimal
complexity
\mathcal{O}\left(n+n^{1/2}\varepsilon^{-1}\right)
, matching lower bounds
(
Li et al. 2021a
)
.

Optimal, but hiding a secret.
While it may look like this is the end of the road, the starting point of our work is the following observation.

The big-
\mathcal{O}
notation in the above results hides important and typically very large data-dependent constants.

For instance, it is rarely noted that the more precise complexity of
GD
is
\mathcal{O}\left(L_{-}n\varepsilon^{-1}\right)
and where
L_{-}
is L-smooth constant of function
f(x)
(see Assumption
9
), while the complexity of the optimal methods, for instance
PAGE
, is
\mathcal{O}\left(n+(L_{-}+L_{+}n^{1/2})\varepsilon^{-1}\right)\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}
where
L_{-}\leq L_{+}
are
different
and often
very large
smoothness constants. Moreover, it is easy to generate examples of problems (see Example
4
) in which the ratio
L_{+}/L_{-}
is
as large as one desires
.

Client and data sampling in Federated Learning.
Furthermore, several modern applications, notably Federated Learning
(
Konečný et al. 2016b
;
McMahan et al. 2017
)
, depend on elaborate
client
and
data sampling
mechanisms, which are not properly understood. However, optimal
SGD
methods were considered in combination with very simple mechanisms only, such as sampling a random function
f_{i}
several times independently with replacement
(
Li et al. 2021a
)
. We thus believe that an in-depth study of sampling mechanisms for optimal methods will be of interest to the Federated Learning community.

There exists prior work on analyzing non-optimal
SGD
variants with flexible mechanisms.
For example, using the “arbitrary sampling” paradigm, originally proposed by
Richtárik and Takác 2016
in the study of randomized coordinate descent methods,
Horváth and Richtárik 2019
and
Qian et al. 2021
analyzed
SVRG
,
SAGA
, and
SARAH
methods, and showed that it is possible to improve the dependence of these methods on the smoothness constants via carefully crafted sampling strategies. Further,
Zhao and Zhang 2014
investigated the stratified sampling, but only provided the analysis for vanilla
SGD
, and in the convex case.

Our goal.
Motivated by the above considerations, in this chapter, we propose a
general framework
for the study of a broad family of sampling mechanisms for and provide a
refined analysis
of the optimal method
PAGE
for solving problems (
5.1
) in the smooth non-convex regime.

5.1.3
Assumptions

In our analysis, we rely on the following weak assumptions on the functions
f_{i}.

Assumption 8
.

There exists
f^{*}\in\mathbb{R}
such that
f(x)\geq f^{*}
for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

Assumption 9
.

There exists
L_{-}\geq 0
such that
\left\|\nabla f(x)-\nabla f(y)\right\|\leq L_{-}\left\|x-y\right\|
for all
x\mathchar 59\relax y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}.

Assumption 10
.

For all
i\in[n]\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}
there exists constants
L_{i}>0
such that
\left\|\nabla f_{i}(x)-\nabla f_{i}(y)\right\|\leq L_{i}\left\|x-y\right\|
for all
x\mathchar 59\relax y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}.

5.1.4
Contributions

The summary of our contributions is as follows.

1.

In the original paper
Li et al. 2021a
, the optimal (with respect to
n\mathchar 59\relax\varepsilon
) optimization method
PAGE
was analyzed with a simple uniform mini-batch sampling with replacement. We analyze
PAGE
with virtually any (unbiased) sampling mechanism using a novel Assumption
11
. Moreover, we show that some samplings can improve the convergence of
PAGE
(see Table
5.2
).

2.

We improve the analysis of
PAGE
using a new quantity, the weighted Hessian Variance
L_{\pm}
(or
L_{\pm\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}w}
), that is well-defined if the functions
f_{i}
are
L_{i}
–smooth. We show that, when the functions
f_{i}
are “similar” in the sense of the weighted Hessian Variance,
PAGE
enjoys faster convergence rates (see Table
5.2
). Also, unlike
(
Szlendak et al. 2022
)
, we introduce weights
w_{i}
that can play a crucial role in some samplings. Moreover, the experiments in Section
5.6
agree with our theoretical results.

3.

Our framework is flexible and can be generalized to
the composition of samplings
. These samplings naturally emerge in Federated Learning
(
Konečný et al. 2016b
;
McMahan et al. 2017
)
, and we show that our framework can be helpful in the analysis of problems from Federated Learning.

5.2
Tight Variance Control of General Sampling Estimators

In Algorithm
11
(a generalization of
PAGE
), we form an estimator of the gradient
\nabla f
via subsampling. In our search for achieving the combined goal of providing a
general
(in terms of the range of sampling techniques we cater for) and
refined
(in terms of the sharpness of our results, even when compared to known results using the
same
sampling technique) analysis of
PAGE
, we have identified several powerful tools, the first of which is Assumption
11
.

Let
\mathcal{S}^{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\left\{(w_{1}\mathchar 59\relax\dots\mathchar 59\relax w_{n})\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\,|\,w_{1}\mathchar 59\relax\dots\mathchar 59\relax w_{n}\geq 0\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\,\sum_{i=1}^{n}w_{i}=1\right\}
be the
standard simplex
and tuple
(\Omega\mathchar 59\relax\mathcal{F}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\mathbf{P})
be a probability space.

Assumption 11

(Weighted
AB
Inequality)
.

Consider the random mapping

{\bf S}\,:\,\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\dots\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\,\,\Omega\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}

which we will call “sampling”, such that for all
a_{1}\mathchar 59\relax\dots\mathchar 59\relax a_{n}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

\mathbb{E}\left[{\bf S}(a_{1}\mathchar 59\relax\dots\mathchar 59\relax a_{n};\omega)\right]=\dfrac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}.

Assume that there exist
A\mathchar 59\relax B\geq 0
and weights
(w_{1}\mathchar 59\relax\dots\mathchar 59\relax w_{n})\in\mathcal{S}^{n}
such that for all
a_{1}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\dots\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}a_{n}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
the following holds

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|{\bf S}(a_{1}\mathchar 59\relax\dots\mathchar 59\relax a_{n};\omega)-\dfrac{1}{n}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\right\|^{2}\right]\leq\dfrac{A}{n}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}\dfrac{1}{nw_{i}}\left\|a_{i}\right\|^{2}-B\left\|\dfrac{1}{n}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\right\|^{2}.

(5.2)

For simplicity, we denote

{\bf S}\left(\{a_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}\right)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}{\bf S}(a_{1}\mathchar 59\relax\dots\mathchar 59\relax a_{n})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}{\bf S}(a_{1}\mathchar 59\relax\dots\mathchar 59\relax a_{n};\omega).

Further, the collection of samplings satisfying Assumption
11
will be denotes as
\mathbb{S}(A\mathchar 59\relax B\mathchar 59\relax\{w_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}).
The main purpose of a sampling
{\bf S}\in\mathbb{S}(A\mathchar 59\relax B\mathchar 59\relax\{w_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n})
is to estimate the mean
\dfrac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}
using some random subsets (possibly containing some elements more than once) of the set
\{a_{1}\mathchar 59\relax\dots\mathchar 59\relax a_{n}\}.

We now define the cardinality
|{\bf S}|
of a sampling
{\bf S}\in\mathbb{S}(A\mathchar 59\relax B\mathchar 59\relax\{w_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n})
.

Definition 18

(Cardinality of a Sampling)
.

Let us take
{\bf S}\in\mathbb{S}(A\mathchar 59\relax B\mathchar 59\relax\{w_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n})\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}
and define the function
{\bf S}_{\omega}(a_{1}\mathchar 59\relax\dots\mathchar 59\relax a_{n})\,:\,\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\dots\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that

{\bf S}_{\omega}(a_{1}\mathchar 59\relax\dots\mathchar 59\relax a_{n})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}{\bf S}(a_{1}\mathchar 59\relax\dots\mathchar 59\relax a_{n};\omega).

If the function
{\bf S}_{\omega}(a_{1}\mathchar 59\relax\dots\mathchar 59\relax a_{n})
depends only on a subset
\mathcal{A}(\omega)
of the arguments
(a_{1}\mathchar 59\relax\dots\mathchar 59\relax a_{n})\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}
where
\mathcal{A}(\omega)\,:\,\Omega\rightarrow 2^{\{a_{1}\mathchar 59\relax\dots\mathchar 59\relax a_{n}\}}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}
we define
|{\bf S}|\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\mathbb{E}\left[|\mathcal{A}(\omega)|\right].

Assumption
11
is most closely related to two independent works:
(
Horváth and Richtárik 2019
)
and
(
Szlendak et al. 2022
)
.
Horváth and Richtárik 2019
analyzed several non-optimal
SGD
methods for “arbitrary samplings”; these are random set-valued mappings
S
with values being the subsets of
[n]
. The distribution of such a sampling is uniquely determined by assigning probabilities to all
2^{n}
subsets of
[n]
. In particular, they show that Assumption
11
holds with

\displaystyle{\bf S}(a_{1}\mathchar 59\relax\dots\mathchar 59\relax a_{n})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\dfrac{1}{n}\sum_{i\in S}\dfrac{a_{i}}{p_{i}}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}

\displaystyle p_{i}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\mathbf{Prob}(i\in S)\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}

\displaystyle|{\bf S}|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle|S|\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}

\displaystyle\mathrm{some\,}A

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle 0\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}

\displaystyle w_{1}\mathchar 59\relax\dots\mathchar 59\relax w_{n}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle 0\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}

\displaystyle B

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0.

Recently,
Szlendak et al. 2022
studied a similar inequality, but in the context of communication-efficient distributed training with randomized gradient compression operators. They explicitly set out to study
correlated
compressors, and for this reason, introduced the second term in the right-hand side; in other words, they considered the possibility of
B
being nonzero, as in this way they obtain a tighter inequality, which they can use in their analysis. However, their inequality only involves uniform weights
\{w_{i}\}
.
Our Assumption
11
offers the tightest known way to control the variance of the sampling estimator, and our analysis can take advantage of it. See Table
5.1
for an overview of several samplings and the values
A\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}B
and
\{w_{i}\}
for which Assumption
11
is satisfied.

Table 5.2
:
The complexity of methods and samplings from Table
5.1
and Section
5.5
.

Sampling Scheme

Complexity

Comment

Independent

(
Horváth and Richtárik 2019
)

\Theta\left(n+\dfrac{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}n^{2/3}}\left(\dfrac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}L_{i}\right)}{\varepsilon}\right)

SVRG
method

p_{i}\propto L_{i}

Uniform-With-Replacement

(
Li et al. 2021a
)

\Theta\left(n+\dfrac{\sqrt{n}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}L_{+}}}{\varepsilon}\right)

—

Uniform-With-Replacement

(new)

\Theta\left(n+\dfrac{\max\{\sqrt{n}L_{\pm}\mathchar 59\relax L_{-}\}}{\varepsilon}\right)

—

Importance

\Theta\left(n+\dfrac{\sqrt{n}\left(\dfrac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}L_{i}\right)}{\varepsilon}\right)

q_{i}=\dfrac{L_{i}}{\sum_{i=1}L_{i}}

Stratified

\Theta\left(n+\dfrac{\max\left\{\sqrt{n}\sqrt{\dfrac{1}{g}\sum_{i=1}^{g}L_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\pm}^{2}}\mathchar 59\relax gL_{-}\right\}}{\varepsilon}\right)

The functions
f_{i}

are split into
g
groups

Notation:
n=\#
of data points;
\varepsilon=
error tolerance;
L_{-}\mathchar 59\relax L_{i}\mathchar 59\relax L_{\pm}\mathchar 59\relax L_{+}
and
L_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\pm}
are smoothness constants such that
L_{-}\leq\dfrac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}L_{i}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}

L_{-}\leq L_{+}
and
L_{\pm}\leq L_{+};

g=\#
of groups in the
Stratified
sampling.

5.3
Sampling-dependent Smoothness Constants

We now define two smoothness constants that depend on the weights
\{w_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
of a sampling
{\bf S}
and on the functions
f_{i}.

Definition 19
.

Given a sampling
{\bf S}\in\mathbb{S}(A\mathchar 59\relax B\mathchar 59\relax\{w_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n})
, let
L_{+\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}w}
be a constant such that for all
x\mathchar 59\relax y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
the following holds:

\dfrac{1}{n}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}\dfrac{1}{nw_{i}}\left\|\nabla f_{i}(x)-\nabla f_{i}(y)\right\|^{2}\leq L_{+\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}w}^{2}\left\|x-y\right\|^{2}.

For
(w_{1}\mathchar 59\relax\dots\mathchar 59\relax w_{n})=(\nicefrac{{1}}{{n}}\mathchar 59\relax\dots\mathchar 59\relax\nicefrac{{1}}{{n}})\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}
we define
L_{+}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}L_{+\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}w}.

Definition 20
.

Given a sampling
{\bf S}\in\mathbb{S}(A\mathchar 59\relax B\mathchar 59\relax\{w_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n})
, let
L_{\pm\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}w}
be a constant such that for all
x\mathchar 59\relax y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
the following holds:

\dfrac{1}{n}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}\dfrac{1}{nw_{i}}\left\|\nabla f_{i}(x)-\nabla f_{i}(y)\right\|^{2}-\left\|\nabla f(x)-\nabla f(y)\right\|^{2}\leq L_{\pm\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}w}^{2}\left\|x-y\right\|^{2}.

For
(w_{1}\mathchar 59\relax\dots\mathchar 59\relax w_{n})=(\nicefrac{{1}}{{n}}\mathchar 59\relax\dots\mathchar 59\relax\nicefrac{{1}}{{n}})\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}
we define
L_{\pm}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}L_{\pm\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}w}.

One can interpret Definition
19
as
weighted
mean-squared smoothness property
(
Arjevani et al. 2023
)
, and Definition
20
as
weighted
Hessian variance
(
Szlendak et al. 2022
)
that captures the similarity between the functions
f_{i}
.
The constants
L_{+\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}w}
and
L_{\pm\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}w}
help us better to understand the structure of the optimization Problem (
5.1
)
in connection
with a particular choice of a sampling scheme. The next result states that
L_{+\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}w}^{2}
and
L_{\pm\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}w}^{2}
are finite provided the functions
f_{i}
are
L_{i}
smooth for all
i\in[n].

Theorem 21
.

If Assumption
10
holds, then

L_{+\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}w}^{2}=L_{\pm\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}w}^{2}=\dfrac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\dfrac{1}{nw_{i}}L_{i}^{2}

satisfy Definition
20
and Definition
19
.

Indeed, from Assumption
10
and the inequality
\left\|\nabla f(x)-\nabla f(y)\right\|^{2}\geq 0
we get

\dfrac{1}{n}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}\dfrac{1}{nw_{i}}\left\|\nabla f_{i}(x)-\nabla f_{i}(y)\right\|^{2}-\left\|\nabla f(x)-\nabla f(y)\right\|^{2}\leq\left(\dfrac{1}{n}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}\dfrac{1}{nw_{i}}L_{i}^{2}\right)\left\|x-y\right\|^{2}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}

thus we can take for Definition
20
the value

L_{\pm\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}w}^{2}=\dfrac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\dfrac{1}{nw_{i}}L_{i}^{2}.

The proof for
L_{+\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}w}^{2}
for Definition
19
is the same.∎

From the proof, one can see that we ignore
\left\|\nabla f(x)-\nabla f(y)\right\|^{2}
when estimating
L_{\pm\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}w}^{2}.
However, by doing that, the obtained result is not tight.

5.4
A General and Refined Theoretical Analysis of PAGE

In the Algorithm
11
, we provide the description of
PAGE
. The choice of
PAGE
as the base method is driven by the simplicity of the proof in the original paper. However, we believe that other methods, including
SPIDER
and
SARAH
, can also admit samplings from Assumption
11
.

Algorithm 11

PAGE
.

1:

Input and Initialization:
initial point
x^{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, step size
\gamma>0
, probability
{p}\in(0\mathchar 59\relax 1]

2:

g^{0}=\nabla f(x^{0})

3:

for

t=0\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}1\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\dots

do

4:

x^{t+1}=x^{t}-\gamma g^{t}

5:

Generate a random sampling function
{\bf S}^{t}

6:

g^{t+1}=\begin{cases}\nabla f(x^{t+1})&\text{with probability}\ p\\
g^{t}+{\bf S}^{t}\left(\{\nabla f_{i}(x^{t+1})-\nabla f_{i}(x^{t})\}_{i=1}^{n}\right)&\text{with probability}\ 1-p\\
\end{cases}

7:

end

for

In this section, we provide theoretical results for Algorithm
11
. Let us define

\Delta_{0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}f(x^{0})-f^{*}.

Theorem 22
.

Suppose that Assumptions
8
,
9
,
10
hold and the samplings

{\bf S}^{t}\in\mathbb{S}(A\mathchar 59\relax B\mathchar 59\relax\{w_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}).

Then Algorithm
11

(PAGE)
has the convergence rate

\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\nabla f(\widehat{x}^{T})\right\|^{2}\right]\leq\dfrac{2\Delta_{0}}{\gamma T}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}

where

\gamma\leq\left(L_{-}+\sqrt{\dfrac{1-p}{p}\left(\left(A-B\right)L_{+\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}w}^{2}+BL_{\pm\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}w}^{2}\right)}\right)^{-1}.

To reach an
\varepsilon
-stationary point, it is enough to do

\displaystyle T\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\dfrac{2\Delta_{0}}{\varepsilon}\left(L_{-}+\sqrt{\dfrac{1-p}{p}\left(\left(A-B\right)L_{+\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}w}^{2}+BL_{\pm\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}w}^{2}\right)}\right)

(5.3)

iterations of Algorithm
11
. To deduce the gradient complexity, we provide the following corollary.

Corollary 1
.

Suppose that the assumptions of Theorem
22
hold. Let us take

p=\dfrac{|{\bf S}|}{|{\bf S}|+n}.

Then the complexity (the expected number of gradient calculations
\nabla f_{i}
) of Algorithm
11
equals

\displaystyle N\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\Theta\left(n+|{\bf S}|T\right)=\Theta\left(n+\dfrac{\Delta_{0}}{\varepsilon}|{\bf S}|\left(L_{-}+\sqrt{\dfrac{n}{|{\bf S}|}\left(\left(A-B\right)L_{+\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}w}^{2}+BL_{\pm\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}w}^{2}\right)}\right)\right).

Proof.

At each iteration, the expected # gradient calculations equals

pn+(1-p)|{\bf S}|\leq 2|{\bf S}|.

Thus the total expected number of gradient calculations of Algorithm
11
is bounded above by
n+2|{\bf S}|T
to get an
\varepsilon
-stationary point.
∎

The original result from
(
Li et al. 2021a
)
states that the complexity of
PAGE
with batch size
\tau
is

\displaystyle N_{\textnormal{orig}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\Theta\left(n+\dfrac{\Delta_{0}}{\varepsilon}\tau\left(L_{-}+\dfrac{\sqrt{n}}{\tau}L_{+}\right)\right).

(5.4)

5.4.1
Uniform-with-replacement sampling

Let us do a sanity check and substitute the parameters of the sampling that the original paper uses. We take the
Uniform-With-Replacement
sampling (see Section
E5.3
) with batch size
\tau
(note that
\tau\geq|{\bf S}|
),
A=B=\nicefrac{{1}}{{\tau}}
and
w_{i}=\nicefrac{{1}}{{n}}
for all
i\in[n]
(see Table
5.1
) and get the complexity
N_{\textnormal{uniform}}=\Theta\left(n+\dfrac{\Delta_{0}}{\varepsilon}\tau\left(L_{-}+\dfrac{\sqrt{n}}{\tau}L_{\pm}\right)\right).
Next, let us fix

\tau\leq\max\left\{\dfrac{\sqrt{n}L_{\pm}}{L_{-}}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}1\right\}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}

and, finally, obtain that

N_{\textnormal{uniform}}=\Theta\left(n+\dfrac{\Delta_{0}\max\{\sqrt{n}L_{\pm}\mathchar 59\relax L_{-}\}}{\varepsilon}\right).

Let us compare it with (
5.4
). With the
same sampling
, our analysis provides better complexity; indeed, note that
\max\{\sqrt{n}L_{\pm}\mathchar 59\relax L_{-}\}\leq\sqrt{n}L_{+}
(see Lemma 2 in
Szlendak et al. 2022
). Moreover,
Szlendak et al. 2022
provides examples of the optimization problems when
L_{\pm}
is small and
L_{+}
is large, so the difference can be arbitrarily large.

5.4.2
Nice sampling

Next, we consider the
Nice
sampling (see Section
E5.1
) and get that the complexity

N_{\textnormal{nice}}=\Theta\left(n+\dfrac{\Delta_{0}}{\varepsilon}\tau\left(L_{-}+\dfrac{1}{\tau}\sqrt{\dfrac{n(n-\tau)}{(n-1)}}L_{\pm}\right)\right).

Unlike the
Uniform-With-Replacement
sampling, the
Nice
sampling recovers the complexity of
GD
for
\tau=n.

5.4.3
Importance sampling

Let us consider the
Importance
sampling (see Section
E5.3
) that justifies the introduction of the weights
w_{i}.
For

\tau\leq\max\left\{\dfrac{\sqrt{n}L_{\pm\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}w}}{L_{-}}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}1\right\}

we can get the complexity

N_{\textnormal{importance}}=\Theta\left(n+\dfrac{\Delta_{0}}{\varepsilon}\tau\left(L_{-}+\dfrac{\sqrt{n}}{\tau}L_{\pm\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}w}\right)\right)\leq\Theta\left(n+\dfrac{\Delta_{0}\max\{\sqrt{n}L_{\pm\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}w}\mathchar 59\relax L_{-}\}}{\varepsilon}\right).

Now, we take

q_{i}=w_{i}=\dfrac{L_{i}}{\sum_{i=1}L_{i}}

and use the results from Section
F5
to obtain

N_{\textnormal{importance}}=\Theta\left(n+\dfrac{\Delta_{0}\sqrt{n}\left(\dfrac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}L_{i}\right)}{\varepsilon}\right)\leq N_{\textnormal{orig}}.

In Example
5
, we consider the optimization task where
\dfrac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}L_{i}
is
\sqrt{n}
times smaller than
L_{+}.
Thus the complexity
N_{\textnormal{importance}}
can be at least
\sqrt{n}
times smaller that the complexity
N_{\textnormal{orig}}.

5.4.4
The power of
B>0

In all previous examples, the constant
A=B>0.
If
A=B\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}
then the complexity from Corollary
1
is simplified to:

N=\Theta\left(n+\dfrac{\Delta_{0}}{\varepsilon}|{\bf S}|\left(L_{-}+\sqrt{\dfrac{n}{|{\bf S}|}BL_{\pm\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}w}^{2}}\right)\right).

Thus the complexity
N
does not depend on
L_{+\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}w}^{2}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}
which greater of equal to
L_{\pm\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}w}^{2}.
This is the first analysis of optimal SGD, which uses
B>0.

5.4.5
Analysis under Polyak-Łojasiewicz condition

The previous results can be extended to the optimization problems that satisfy the Polyak-Łojasiewicz condition. Under this assumption, Algorithm
11
enjoys a linear convergence rate.

Assumption 12
.

There exists
\mu>0
such that the function
f
satisfy (Polyak-Łojasiewicz) PŁ-condition:

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla f(x)\right\|^{2}\geq 2\mu(f(x)-f^{*})\quad\forall x\in\mathbb{R}\mathchar 59\relax

where
f^{*}=\inf_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}f(x)>-\infty.

Using Assumption
12
, we can improve the convergence rate of
PAGE
.

Theorem 23
.

Suppose that Assumptions
8
,
9
,
10
,
12
and the samplings

{\bf S}^{t}\in\mathbb{S}(A\mathchar 59\relax B\mathchar 59\relax\{w_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}).

Then Algorithm
11

(PAGE)
has the convergence rate

\mathbb{E}\left[f(x^{T})\right]-f^{*}\leq(1-\gamma\mu)^{T}\Delta_{0}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}

where

\gamma\leq\min\left\{\left(L_{-}+\sqrt{\dfrac{2(1-p)}{p}\left(\left(A-B\right)L_{+\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}w}^{2}+BL_{\pm\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}w}^{2}\right)}\right)^{-1}\mathchar 59\relax\dfrac{p}{2\mu}\right\}.

5.5
Composition of Samplings: Application to Federated Learning

In Section
5.4
, we analyze
PAGE
method with samplings that satisfy Assumption
11
. Now, let us assume that the functions
f_{i}
have the finite-sum form, in other words,

f_{i}(x)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\dfrac{1}{m_{i}}\sum_{j=1}^{m_{i}}f_{ij}(x)\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}

thus we an optimization problem

\displaystyle\min\limits\limits_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\{f(x)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\dfrac{1}{n}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}\left(\dfrac{1}{m_{i}}\sum\limits_{j=1}^{m_{i}}f_{ij}(x)\right)\right\}\mathchar 59\relax

(5.5)

Another way to get the problem is to assume that we split the functions
f_{i}
into groups of sizes
m_{i}.
In summary, let us consider (
5.5
) instead of (
5.1
).

The Problem (
5.5
) occurs in many applications, including distributed optimization and Federated Learning
(
Konečný et al. 2016b
;
McMahan et al. 2017
)
. In Federated Learning, many devices and machines (nodes) store local datasets that they do not share with other nodes. The local datasets are represented by functions
f_{i}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}
and all nodes solve the common optimization Problem (
5.5
). Due to privacy reasons and communication bottlenecks
(
Kairouz et al. 2021
)
,
it is infeasible to store and compute the functions
f_{i}
locally in one machine.

In general, when we solve (
5.1
) in one machine, we have the freedom of choosing a sampling
{\bf S}
for the functions
f_{i}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}
which we have shown in Section
5.4
. However, in Federated Learning, a sampling of nodes or the functions
f_{i}
is dictated by hardware limits or network quality
(
Kairouz et al. 2021
)
. Still, each
i
th
node can choose sampling
{\bf S}_{i}
to sample the functions
f_{ij}.
As a result, we have a composition of the sampling
{\bf S}
and the samplings
{\bf S}_{i}
(see Algorithm
12
).

Assumption 13
.

For all
j\in[m_{i}]\mathchar 59\relax i\in[n]\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}
there exists a Lipschitz constant
L_{ij}
such that
\left\|\nabla f_{ij}(x)-\nabla f_{ij}(y)\right\|\leq L_{ij}\left\|x-y\right\|
for all
x\mathchar 59\relax y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}.

We now introduce the counterpart of Definitions
19
and
20
.

Definition 24
.

For all
i\in[n]
and any sampling
{\bf S}_{i}\in\mathbb{S}(A_{i}\mathchar 59\relax B_{i}\mathchar 59\relax\{w_{ij}\}_{j=1}^{m_{i}})
, define constant
L_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}+\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}w_{i}}
such that

\dfrac{1}{m_{i}}\sum\limits_{j=1}^{m_{i}}\dfrac{1}{m_{i}w_{ij}}\left\|\nabla f_{ij}(x)-\nabla f_{ij}(y)\right\|^{2}\leq L_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}+\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}w_{i}}^{2}\left\|x-y\right\|^{2}\quad\forall x\mathchar 59\relax y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}.

Definition 25
.

For all
i\in[n]
and any sampling
{\bf S}_{i}\in\mathbb{S}(A_{i}\mathchar 59\relax B_{i}\mathchar 59\relax\{w_{ij}\}_{j=1}^{m_{i}})
, define constant
L_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\pm\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}w_{i}}
such that
\forall x\mathchar 59\relax y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
:

\dfrac{1}{m_{i}}\sum\limits_{j=1}^{m_{i}}\dfrac{1}{m_{i}w_{ij}}\left\|\nabla f_{ij}(x)-\nabla f_{ij}(y)\right\|^{2}-\left\|\nabla f_{i}(x)-\nabla f_{i}(y)\right\|^{2}\leq L_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\pm\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}w_{i}}^{2}\left\|x-y\right\|^{2}.

Algorithm 12

PAGE
with Composition of Samplings.

1:

Input and Initialization:
initial point
x^{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, step size
\gamma>0
, probability
{p}\in(0\mathchar 59\relax 1]
,
g^{0}=\nabla f(x^{0})

2:

for

t=0\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}1\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\dots

do

3:

x^{t+1}=x^{t}-\gamma g^{t}

4:

c^{t+1}=\begin{cases}1&\text{with probability}\ p\\
0&\text{with probability}\ 1-p\\
\end{cases}

5:

if

c^{t+1}=1

then

6:

g^{t+1}=\nabla f(x^{t+1})

7:

else

8:

Generate samplings
{\bf S}^{t}_{i}
for all
i\in[n]

9:

h^{t+1}_{i}={\bf S}^{t}_{i}\left(\{\nabla f_{ij}(x^{t+1})-\nabla f_{ij}(x^{t})\}_{j=1}^{m_{i}}\right)
for all
i\in[n]

10:

Generate a sampling
{\bf S}^{t}
and set
g^{t+1}=g^{t}+{\bf S}^{t}\left(\{h^{t+1}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}\right)

11:

end

if

12:

end

for

Let us provide the counterpart of Theorem
22
for Algorithm
12
.

Theorem 26
.

Suppose that Assumptions
8
,
9
,
10
,
13
hold and the samplings

{\bf S}^{t}\in\mathbb{S}(A\mathchar 59\relax B\mathchar 59\relax\{w_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n})

and the samplings

{\bf S}^{t}_{i}\in\mathbb{S}(A_{i}\mathchar 59\relax B_{i}\mathchar 59\relax\{w_{ij}\}_{j=1}^{m_{i}})\mathchar 59\relax\forall i\in[n].

Moreover,
B\leq 1.
Then Algorithm
12
has the convergence rate

\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\nabla f(\widehat{x}^{T})\right\|^{2}\right]\leq\dfrac{2\Delta_{0}}{\gamma T}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}

where

\displaystyle\gamma\leq\left(L_{-}+\sqrt{\dfrac{1-p}{p}\left(\dfrac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(\dfrac{A}{nw_{i}}+\dfrac{(1-B)}{n}\right)\left((A_{i}-B_{i})L_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}+\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}w_{i}}^{2}+B_{i}L_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\pm\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}w_{i}}^{2}\right)+(A-B)L_{+\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}w}^{2}+BL_{\pm\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}w}^{2}\right)}\right)^{-1}.

The obtained theorem provides a general framework that helps analyze the convergence rates of the composition of samplings that satisfy Assumption
11
. We discuss the obtained result in different contexts.

5.5.1
Federated learning

For simplicity, let us assume that the samplings
{\bf S}^{t}
and
{\bf S}_{i}^{t}
are
Uniform-With-Replacement
samplings with batch sizes
\tau
and
\tau_{i}
for all
i\in n
, accordingly, then to get
\varepsilon
-stationary point, it is enough to do

T\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\Theta\left(\dfrac{\Delta_{0}}{\varepsilon}\left(L_{-}+\sqrt{\dfrac{1-p}{p\tau}\left(\dfrac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\dfrac{1}{\tau_{i}}L_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\pm}^{2}+L_{\pm}^{2}\right)}\right)\right)

iterations. Note that for all
\tau_{i}\geq 1
for all
i\in[n]
the following holds:

T\geq\Theta\left(\dfrac{\Delta_{0}}{\varepsilon}\left(L_{-}+\sqrt{\dfrac{1-p}{p\tau}L_{\pm}^{2}}\right)\right).

It means that after some point, there is no benefit in increasing batch sizes
\tau_{i}.
In order to balance

\dfrac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\dfrac{1}{\tau_{i}}L_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\pm}^{2}\qquad\mathrm{and}\qquad L_{\pm}^{2}\mathchar 59\relax

one can take

\tau_{i}=\Theta\left(\nicefrac{{L_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\pm}^{2}}}{{L_{\pm}^{2}}}\right).

The constant
L_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\pm}^{2}
captures the
intra-variance
inside
i
th
node, while
L_{\pm}^{2}
captures the
inter-variance
between nodes.

If the
intra-variance
is small with respect to the
inter-variance
, then our theory suggests taking small batch sizes and vice versa.

5.5.2
Stratified sampling

Let us provide another example that is closely related to
(
Zhao and Zhang 2014
)
. Let us consider (
5.1
) and use a variation of the
Stratified
sampling
(
Zhao and Zhang 2014
)
: we split the functions
f_{i}
into
g=\nicefrac{{n}}{{m}}
groups, where
m
is the number of functions in each group. Thus we get the Problem (
5.5
) with

f(x)=\dfrac{1}{g}\sum_{i=1}^{g}\dfrac{1}{m}\sum_{j=1}^{m}f_{ij}(x).

Let us assume that we always sample
all
groups, thus
A=B=0\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}
and the sampling
{\bf S}_{i}^{t}
are
Nice
samplings with batch sizes
\tau_{1}
for all
i\in[n].
Applying Theorem
26
, we get the convergence rate

T_{\textnormal{group}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\Theta\left(\dfrac{\Delta_{0}}{\varepsilon}\left(L_{-}+\sqrt{\dfrac{1-p}{pg\tau_{1}}\left(\dfrac{1}{g}\sum_{i=1}^{g}L_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\pm}^{2}\right)}\right)\right).

At each iteration, the algorithm calculates
g\tau_{1}
gradients, thus we should take

p=\dfrac{g\tau_{1}}{g\tau_{1}+n}

to get the complexity

N_{\textnormal{group}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\Theta\left(n+g\tau_{1}T\right)=\Theta\left(n+\dfrac{\Delta_{0}}{\varepsilon}\left(g\tau_{1}L_{-}+\sqrt{n}\sqrt{\dfrac{1}{g}\sum_{i=1}^{g}L_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\pm}^{2}}\right)\right).

Let us take
\tau_{1}\leq\max\left\{\dfrac{\sqrt{n}\sqrt{\dfrac{1}{g}\sum_{i=1}^{g}L_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\pm}^{2}}}{gL_{-}}\mathchar 59\relax 1\right\}
to obtain the complexity

N_{\textnormal{group}}=\Theta\left(n+\dfrac{\Delta_{0}\max\left\{\sqrt{n}\sqrt{\dfrac{1}{g}\sum_{i=1}^{g}L_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\pm}^{2}}\mathchar 59\relax gL_{-}\right\}}{\varepsilon}\right).

Comparing the complexity
N_{\textnormal{group}}
with the complexity
N_{\textnormal{uniform}}
from Section
5.4
, one can see that if split the functions
f_{i}
in a “right way”, such that
L_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\pm}
is small for
i\in[n]
(see Example
6
), then we can get at least
\nicefrac{{\sqrt{n}}}{{\sqrt{g}}}
times improvement with the
Stratified
sampling.

5.6
Experiments

We now provide experiments with synthetic quadratic optimization tasks, where the functions
f_{i}
, in general, are non-convex quadratic functions. Note that our goal here is to check whether the dependencies that our theory predicts are correct for the Problem (
5.1
). The procedures that generate synthetic quadratic optimization tasks give us control over the choice of smoothness constants.

All parameters of Algorithm
11
are chosen as suggested in Theorem
22
and Cor
1
. In the plots, we represent the relation between the norm of gradients and the number of gradient calculations
\nabla f_{i}
.

Quadratic optimization tasks with various Hessian variances
L_{\pm}

Using Algorithm
13
(see Appendix), we generated various quadratic optimization tasks with different smoothness constants
L_{\pm}\in[0\mathchar 59\relax 1.0]
and fixed
L_{-}\approx 1.0
(see Figure
5.1
). We choose
d=10\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}

n=1000\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}
regularization
\lambda=0.001\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}
and the noise scale
s\in\{0\mathchar 59\relax 0.1\mathchar 59\relax 0.5\mathchar 59\relax 1\}.
According to Section
5.4
and Table
5.2
, the gradient complexity of original
PAGE
method (“Vanilla PAGE” in Figure
5.1
) is proportional to
L_{+}.
While the gradient complexity of the new analysis with the
Uniform-With-Replacement

sampling (“Uniform-With-Replacement” in Figure
5.1
) is proportional to
L_{\pm}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}
which is always less or equal
L_{+}.
In Figure
5.1
, one can see that the smaller
L_{\pm}
with respect to
L_{+}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}
the better the performance of “Uniform-With-Replacement.” Moreover, we provide experiments with the
Importance
sampling (“Importance” in Figure
5.1
) with
q_{i}=\nicefrac{{L_{i}}}{{\sum_{i=1}^{n}L_{i}}}
for all
i\in[n].
This sampling has the best performance in all regimes.

Non-convex classification problem with LIBSVM datasets

We now solve non-convex machine learning tasks and compare samplings on
LIBSVM
datasets
(
Chang and Lin 2011a
)
(see details in Section
A5.2
). As in previous sections,
PAGE
with the
Importance
sampling performs better, especially in
AUSTRALIAN
dataset where the variation of
L_{i}
is large.

dataset.

dataset.

5.7
Conclusions

In this work, we revisit the classical problem of finding an approximately stationary point of the average of
n
smooth and possibly non-convex functions.
The optimal complexity of stochastic first-order methods in terms of the number of gradient computations of individual functions is
\mathcal{O}\left(n+n^{1/2}\varepsilon^{-1}\right)
, attained by the optimal
SGD
methods
SPIDER

(
Fang et al. 2018
)
and
PAGE

(
Li et al. 2021a
)
, for example, where
\varepsilon
is the error tolerance. However, i) the big-
\mathcal{O}
notation hides crucial dependencies on the smoothness constants associated with the functions, and ii) the rates and theory in these methods assume simplistic sampling mechanisms that do not offer any flexibility. In this work, we remedy the situation. First, we generalize
PAGE
algorithm so that it can provably work with virtually any (unbiased) sampling mechanism. This is particularly useful in Federated Learning, as it allows us to construct and better understand the impact of various combinations of client and data sampling strategies. Second, our analysis is sharper as we make explicit use of certain novel inequalities that capture the intricate interplay between the smoothness constants and the sampling procedure. Indeed, our analysis is better even for the simple sampling procedure analyzed in
PAGE
paper. However, this already improved bound can be further sharpened by a different sampling scheme that we propose. In summary, we provide the most general and most accurate analysis of optimal
SGD
in the smooth non-convex regime. Our theoretical findings are supposed with carefully designed experiments.

B5
Auxiliary Facts

We use the following auxiliary fact in our proofs.
Let us take a
random vector

\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, then

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\xi\right\|^{2}\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\xi-\mathbb{E}\left[\xi\right]\right\|^{2}\right]+\left\|\mathbb{E}\left[\xi\right]\right\|^{2}.

(5.6)

C5
Examples of Optimization Problems

Example 4
.

For simplicity, let us assume that
n
is even. Let us consider the optimization Problem (
5.1
) with
f_{i}(x):\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
:

•

f_{i}(x)=\dfrac{a}{2}x^{2}+\dfrac{b}{2}x^{2}
for
i\in\{1\mathchar 59\relax\cdots\mathchar 59\relax n/2\}
.

•

f_{i}(x)=-\dfrac{a}{2}x^{2}+\dfrac{b}{2}x^{2}
for
i\in\{n/2+1\mathchar 59\relax\cdots\mathchar 59\relax n\}
.

•

The
b\geq 0
.

Then
f(x)=\dfrac{b}{2}x^{2}
and

L_{-}^{2}=\sup_{x\neq y}\dfrac{\left\|\nabla f(x)-\nabla f(y)\right\|^{2}}{\left\|x-y\right\|^{2}}=b^{2}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}

L_{+}^{2}=\sup_{x\neq y}\dfrac{\dfrac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left\|\nabla f_{i}(x)-\nabla f_{i}(y)\right\|^{2}}{\left\|x-y\right\|^{2}}=\dfrac{1}{2}\left((a+b)^{2}+(a-b)^{2}\right)\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}

and we can take
a
arbitrary large.

Example 5
.

Let us assume that
n\geq 2
and consider the optimization Problem (
5.1
) with
f_{1}(x)=\dfrac{b}{2}x^{2}
and
f_{i}(x)=0
for
i\in\{2\mathchar 59\relax\cdots\mathchar 59\relax n\}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}
where
x\in\mathbb{R}
and
b\geq 0.
Then
f(x)=\dfrac{b}{2n}x^{2}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}

L_{-}=\sup_{x\neq y}\dfrac{\left\|\nabla f(x)-\nabla f(y)\right\|}{\left\|x-y\right\|}=\dfrac{b}{n}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}

\dfrac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}L_{i}=\dfrac{1}{n}\sup_{x\neq y}\dfrac{\left\|\nabla f_{1}(x)-\nabla f_{1}(y)\right\|^{2}}{\left\|x-y\right\|^{2}}=\dfrac{b}{n}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}

and

L_{+}=\sqrt{\sup_{x\neq y}\dfrac{\dfrac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left\|\nabla f_{i}(x)-\nabla f_{i}(y)\right\|^{2}}{\left\|x-y\right\|^{2}}}=\dfrac{b}{\sqrt{n}}.

Example 6
.

Let us consider the optimization Problem (
5.5
) with

f(x)=\dfrac{1}{g}\sum_{i=1}^{g}\dfrac{1}{m}\sum_{j=1}^{m}f_{ij}(x)

and
f_{ij}(x)=\dfrac{b_{i}}{2}x^{2}
for all
i\in[g]
and
j\in[m]
, where
x\in\mathbb{R}
and
b_{1}\geq 0
and
b_{i}=0
for all
i\in\{2\mathchar 59\relax\dots\mathchar 59\relax g\}.

Then
f(x)=\dfrac{b_{1}}{2g}x^{2}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}

L_{-}=\sup_{x\neq y}\dfrac{\left\|\nabla f(x)-\nabla f(y)\right\|}{\left\|x-y\right\|}=\dfrac{b_{1}}{g}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}

\displaystyle L_{\pm}^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sup_{x\neq y}\dfrac{\dfrac{1}{gm}\sum_{i=1}^{g}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\left\|\nabla f_{ij}(x)-\nabla f_{ij}(y)\right\|^{2}-\left\|\nabla f(x)-\nabla f(y)\right\|^{2}}{\left\|x-y\right\|^{2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\dfrac{1}{g}-\dfrac{1}{g^{2}}\right)b_{1}^{2}\mathchar 59\relax

and

L_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\pm}^{2}=\sup_{x\neq y}\dfrac{\dfrac{1}{m}\sum_{j=m}^{n}\left\|\nabla f_{ij}(x)-\nabla f_{ij}(y)\right\|^{2}-\left\|\nabla f_{i}(x)-\nabla f_{i}(y)\right\|^{2}}{\left\|x-y\right\|^{2}}=0\quad\forall i\in[n].

Substituting the smoothness constants to the complexity
N_{\textnormal{uniform}}
from Section
5.4
and
N_{\textnormal{uniform}}
from Section
5.5
, one can show that

N_{\textnormal{uniform}}=\Theta\left(n+\dfrac{\Delta_{0}\max\{\sqrt{n}L_{\pm}\mathchar 59\relax L_{-}\}}{\varepsilon}\right)=\Theta\left(n+\dfrac{\Delta_{0}\sqrt{n}b_{1}}{\varepsilon\sqrt{g}}\right)

and

N_{\textnormal{group}}=\Theta\left(n+\dfrac{\Delta_{0}\max\left\{\sqrt{n}\sqrt{\dfrac{1}{g}\sum_{i=1}^{g}L_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\pm}^{2}}\mathchar 59\relax gL_{-}\right\}}{\varepsilon}\right)=\Theta\left(n+\dfrac{\Delta_{0}b_{1}}{\varepsilon}\right).

The complexity
N_{\textnormal{group}}
is
\dfrac{\sqrt{n}}{\sqrt{g}}
times better than the complexity
N_{\textnormal{uniform}}.

Lemma 19
.

Suppose that Assumption
9
holds and let
x^{t+1}=x^{t}-\gamma g^{t}.
Then for any
g^{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
\gamma>0
, we have

\displaystyle f(x^{t+1})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle f(x^{t})-\dfrac{\gamma}{2}\left\|\nabla f(x^{t})\right\|^{2}-\left(\dfrac{1}{2\gamma}-\dfrac{L_{-}}{2}\right)\left\|x^{t+1}-x^{t}\right\|^{2}

(5.7)

\displaystyle\qquad+\dfrac{\gamma}{2}\left\|g^{t}-\nabla f(x^{t})\right\|^{2}.

Proof.

Using Assumption
9
, we have

\displaystyle f(x^{t+1})

\displaystyle\leq f(x^{t})+\left\langle\nabla f(x^{t})\mathchar 59\relax x^{t+1}-x^{t}\right\rangle+\dfrac{L_{-}}{2}\left\|x^{t+1}-x^{t}\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle=f(x^{t})-\gamma\left\langle\nabla f(x^{t})\mathchar 59\relax g^{t}\right\rangle+\dfrac{L_{-}}{2}\left\|x^{t+1}-x^{t}\right\|^{2}.

Next, due to
-\left\langle x\mathchar 59\relax y\right\rangle=\dfrac{1}{2}\left\|x-y\right\|^{2}-\dfrac{1}{2}\left\|x\right\|^{2}-\dfrac{1}{2}\left\|y\right\|^{2}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}
we obtain

\displaystyle f(x^{t+1})\leq f(x^{t})-\dfrac{\gamma}{2}\left\|\nabla f(x^{t})\right\|^{2}-\left(\dfrac{1}{2\gamma}-\dfrac{L_{-}}{2}\right)\left\|x^{t+1}-x^{t}\right\|^{2}+\dfrac{\gamma}{2}\left\|g^{t}-\nabla f(x^{t})\right\|^{2}.

∎

22

Proof.

We start with the estimation of the variance of the noise:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|g^{t+1}-\nabla f(x^{t+1})\right\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=(1-p)\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|g^{t}+{\bf S}^{t}\left(\{\nabla f_{i}(x^{t+1})-\nabla f_{i}(x^{t})\}_{i=1}^{n}\right)-\nabla f(x^{t+1})\right\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=(1-p)\left\|{\bf S}^{t}\left(\{\nabla f_{i}(x^{t+1})-\nabla f_{i}(x^{t})\}_{i=1}^{n}\right)-\left(\nabla f(x^{t+1})-\nabla f(x^{t})\right)\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad+(1-p)\left\|g^{t}-\nabla f(x^{t})\right\|^{2}\mathchar 59\relax

where we used the unbiasedness of the sampling.
Using Assumption

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|g^{t+1}-\nabla f(x^{t+1})\right\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\quad+(1-p)\left\|g^{t}-\nabla f(x^{t})\right\|^{2}.

Using the definition of
L_{+\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}w}
and

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|g^{t+1}-\nabla f(x^{t+1})\right\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq(1-p)\left(A\sum_{i=1}^{n}\dfrac{1}{n^{2}w_{i}}\left\|\nabla f_{i}(x^{t+1})-\nabla f_{i}(x^{t})\right\|^{2}-B\left\|\nabla f(x^{t+1})-\nabla f(x^{t})\right\|^{2}\right)

\displaystyle+(1-p)\left\|g^{t}-\nabla f(x^{t})\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle=(1-p)\Bigg(\left(A-B\right)\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\dfrac{1}{n^{2}w_{i}}\left\|\nabla f_{i}(x^{t+1})-\nabla f_{i}(x^{t})\right\|^{2}\right)

\displaystyle+B\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\dfrac{1}{n^{2}w_{i}}\left\|\nabla f_{i}(x^{t+1})-\nabla f_{i}(x^{t})\right\|^{2}-\left\|\nabla f(x^{t+1})-\nabla f(x^{t})\right\|^{2}\right)\Bigg)

\displaystyle+(1-p)\left\|g^{t}-\nabla f(x^{t})\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq(1-p)\left(\left(A-B\right)L_{+\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}w}^{2}+BL_{\pm\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}w}^{2}\right)\left\|x^{t+1}-x^{t}\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle+(1-p)\left\|g^{t}-\nabla f(x^{t})\right\|^{2}.

We now continue the proof using Lemma
19
.

\dfrac{\gamma}{2p}\times
(

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[f(x^{t+1})-f^{*}+\dfrac{\gamma}{2p}\left\|g^{t+1}-\nabla f(x^{t+1})\right\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\left[f\left(x^{t}\right)-f^{*}-\dfrac{\gamma}{2}\left\|\nabla f\left(x^{t}\right)\right\|^{2}-\left(\dfrac{1}{2\gamma}-\dfrac{L_{-}}{2}\right)\left\|x^{t+1}-x^{t}\right\|^{2}+\dfrac{\gamma}{2}\left\|g^{t}-\nabla f\left(x^{t}\right)\right\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle+\dfrac{\gamma}{2p}\mathbb{E}\left[(1-p)\left\|g^{t}-\nabla f\left(x^{t}\right)\right\|^{2}+(1-p)\left(\left(A-B\right)L_{+\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}w}^{2}+BL_{\pm\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}w}^{2}\right)\left\|x^{t+1}-x^{t}\right\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[f\left(x^{t}\right)-f^{*}+\dfrac{\gamma}{2p}\left\|g^{t}-\nabla f\left(x^{t}\right)\right\|^{2}-\dfrac{\gamma}{2}\left\|\nabla f\left(x^{t}\right)\right\|^{2}\right.

\displaystyle\left.\quad-\left(\dfrac{1}{2\gamma}-\dfrac{L_{-}}{2}-\dfrac{(1-p)\gamma}{2p}\left(\left(A-B\right)L_{+\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}w}^{2}+BL_{\pm\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}w}^{2}\right)\right)\left\|x^{t+1}-x^{t}\right\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\left[f\left(x^{t}\right)-f^{*}+\dfrac{\gamma}{2p}\left\|g^{t}-\nabla f\left(x^{t}\right)\right\|^{2}-\dfrac{\gamma}{2}\left\|\nabla f\left(x^{t}\right)\right\|^{2}\right]\mathchar 59\relax

where the last inequality holds due to

\dfrac{1}{2\gamma}-\dfrac{L_{-}}{2}-\dfrac{(1-p)\gamma}{2p}\left(\left(A-B\right)L_{+\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}w}^{2}+BL_{\pm\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}w}^{2}\right)\geq 0

by choosing step size

\gamma\leq\left(L_{-}+\sqrt{\dfrac{1-p}{p}\left(\left(A-B\right)L_{+\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}w}^{2}+BL_{\pm\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}w}^{2}\right)}\right)^{-1}.

Now, if we define

\Phi_{t}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}f\left(x^{t}\right)-f^{*}+\dfrac{\gamma}{2p}\left\|g^{t}-\nabla f\left(x^{t}\right)\right\|^{2}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}

then (
5.9
) can be written in the form

\mathbb{E}\left[\Phi_{t+1}\right]\leq\mathbb{E}\left[\Phi_{t}\right]-\dfrac{\gamma}{2}\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\nabla f\left(x^{t}\right)\right\|^{2}\right].

Summing up from
t=0
to
T-1
, we get

\mathbb{E}\left[\Phi_{T}\right]\leq\mathbb{E}\left[\Phi_{0}\right]-\dfrac{\gamma}{2}\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\nabla f\left(x^{t}\right)\right\|^{2}\right].

Then according to the output of the algorithm,
\widehat{x}_{T}
is randomly chosen from
\left\{x^{t}\right\}_{t\in[T]}
and

\Phi_{0}=f\left(x^{0}\right)-f^{*}+\dfrac{\gamma}{2p}\|g^{0}-\nabla f\left(x^{0}\right)\|^{2}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}

\Delta_{0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{ def }}}{{=}}f\left(x^{0}\right)-f^{*}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}

we have

\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\nabla f\left(\widehat{x}_{T}\right)\right\|^{2}\right]\leq\dfrac{2\Delta_{0}}{\gamma T}.

Proof.

From the proof of Theorem

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|g^{t+1}-\nabla f(x^{t+1})\right\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq(1-p)\left(\left(A-B\right)L_{+\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}w}^{2}+BL_{\pm\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}w}^{2}\right)\left\|x^{t+1}-x^{t}\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad+(1-p)\left\|g^{t}-\nabla f(x^{t})\right\|^{2}.

(5.10)

Using Lemma
19
, we add (
5.7
) with
\dfrac{\gamma}{p}\times
(
5.10
), and take expectation to get

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[f(x^{t+1})-f^{*}+\dfrac{\gamma}{p}\left\|g^{t+1}-\nabla f(x^{t+1})\right\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\left[f\left(x^{t}\right)-f^{*}-\dfrac{\gamma}{2}\left\|\nabla f\left(x^{t}\right)\right\|^{2}-\left(\dfrac{1}{2\gamma}-\dfrac{L_{-}}{2}\right)\left\|x^{t+1}-x^{t}\right\|^{2}+\dfrac{\gamma}{2}\left\|g^{t}-\nabla f\left(x^{t}\right)\right\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle+\dfrac{\gamma}{p}\mathbb{E}\left[(1-p)\left\|g^{t}-\nabla f\left(x^{t}\right)\right\|^{2}+(1-p)\left(\left(A-B\right)L_{+\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}w}^{2}+BL_{\pm\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}w}^{2}\right)\left\|x^{t+1}-x^{t}\right\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[f\left(x^{t}\right)-f^{*}+\left(1-\dfrac{p}{2}\right)\dfrac{\gamma}{p}\left\|g^{t}-\nabla f\left(x^{t}\right)\right\|^{2}-\dfrac{\gamma}{2}\left\|\nabla f\left(x^{t}\right)\right\|^{2}\right.

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\left[f\left(x^{t}\right)-f^{*}+\left(1-\dfrac{p}{2}\right)\dfrac{\gamma}{p}\left\|g^{t}-\nabla f\left(x^{t}\right)\right\|^{2}-\dfrac{\gamma}{2}\left\|\nabla f\left(x^{t}\right)\right\|^{2}\right]\mathchar 59\relax

where the last inequality holds due to

\dfrac{1}{2\gamma}-\dfrac{L_{-}}{2}-\dfrac{(1-p)\gamma}{p}\left(\left(A-B\right)L_{+\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}w}^{2}+BL_{\pm\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}w}^{2}\right)\geq 0

we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[f(x^{t+1})-f^{*}+\dfrac{\gamma}{p}\left\|g^{t+1}-\nabla f(x^{t+1})\right\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\left(1-\gamma\mu\right)\mathbb{E}\left[f\left(x^{t}\right)-f^{*}+\dfrac{\gamma}{p}\left\|g^{t}-\nabla f\left(x^{t}\right)\right\|^{2}\right].

Unrolling the recursion and considering that
g^{0}=\nabla f(x^{0})\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}
we can complete the proof of the theorem.
