E5
Derivations of the Parameters for the Samplings

E5.1
Nice sampling

Let
S
be a random subset uniformly chosen from
[n]
with a fixed cardinality
\tau
.
Let us fix
a_{1}\mathchar 59\relax\dots\mathchar 59\relax a_{n}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}.
A sampling

{\bf S}(a_{1}\mathchar 59\relax\dots\mathchar 59\relax a_{n})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\dfrac{1}{n}\sum_{i\in S}\dfrac{a_{i}}{p_{i}}

is called the
Nice
sampling, where
p_{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\mathbf{Prob}(i\in S).

Let us bound
\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|{\bf S}(a_{1}\mathchar 59\relax\dots\mathchar 59\relax a_{n})-\dfrac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\right\|^{2}\right]
and find parameters from Assumption
11
. Note that
|{\bf S}|=|S|=\tau.

We introduce auxiliary random variables

\chi_{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\begin{cases}1&i\in S\\
0&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}.

Due to
p_{i}=\mathbf{Prob}\left(i\in S\right)=\dfrac{\tau}{n}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}
we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\dfrac{1}{n}\sum_{i\in S}\dfrac{a_{i}}{p_{i}}\right\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\dfrac{1}{\tau}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\chi_{i}a_{i}\right\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\dfrac{1}{\tau^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\chi_{i}a_{i}\right\|^{2}\right]+\dfrac{1}{\tau^{2}}\sum_{i\neq j}\mathbb{E}\left[\left<\chi_{i}a_{i}\mathchar 59\relax\chi_{j}a_{j}\right>\right]

\displaystyle=\dfrac{1}{\tau^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}\left[\chi_{i}\right]\left\|a_{i}\right\|^{2}+\dfrac{1}{\tau^{2}}\sum_{i\neq j}\mathbb{E}\left[\left<\chi_{i}\mathchar 59\relax\chi_{j}\right>\right]\left<a_{i}\mathchar 59\relax a_{j}\right>

\displaystyle=\dfrac{1}{n\tau}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left\|a_{i}\right\|^{2}+\dfrac{\tau-1}{n(n-1)\tau}\sum_{i\neq j}\left<a_{i}\mathchar 59\relax a_{j}\right>

\displaystyle=\dfrac{1}{n\tau}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left\|a_{i}\right\|^{2}+\dfrac{\tau-1}{n(n-1)\tau}\left(\left\|\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\right\|^{2}-\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left\|a_{i}\right\|^{2}\right)

\displaystyle=\dfrac{n-\tau}{\tau(n-1)}\dfrac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left\|a_{i}\right\|^{2}+\dfrac{\tau-1}{n(n-1)\tau}\left\|\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\right\|^{2}\mathchar 59\relax

where we use
\mathbb{E}\left[\chi_{i}^{2}\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[\chi_{i}\right]=\dfrac{\tau}{n}
and
\mathbb{E}\left[\chi_{i}\chi_{j}\right]=\dfrac{\tau(\tau-1)}{n(n-1)}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}
when
i\neq j
.

Finally, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\dfrac{1}{n}\sum_{i\in S}\dfrac{a_{i}}{p_{i}}-\dfrac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\right\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\dfrac{1}{n}\sum_{i\in S}\dfrac{a_{i}}{p_{i}}\right\|^{2}\right]-\left\|\dfrac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle=\dfrac{n-\tau}{\tau(n-1)}\dfrac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left\|a_{i}\right\|^{2}+\dfrac{\tau-1}{n(n-1)\tau}\left\|\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle-\left\|\dfrac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle=\dfrac{n-\tau}{\tau(n-1)}\left(\dfrac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left\|a_{i}\right\|^{2}-\left\|\dfrac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\right\|^{2}\right).

Thus we have
A=B=\dfrac{n-\tau}{\tau(n-1)}
and
w_{i}=\dfrac{1}{n}
for all
i\in[n].

E5.2
Independent sampling

Let us define independent and identically distributed (i.i.d.) random variables

\chi_{i}=\begin{cases}1&\text{with probability}\ p_{i}\\
0&\text{with probability}\ 1-p_{i}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\\
\end{cases}.

for all
i\in[n]
and take
S\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\{i\in[n]\,|\,\chi_{i}=1\}.
We now fix
a_{1}\mathchar 59\relax\dots\mathchar 59\relax a_{n}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}.
A sampling

{\bf S}(a_{1}\mathchar 59\relax\dots\mathchar 59\relax a_{n})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\dfrac{1}{n}\sum_{i\in S}\dfrac{a_{i}}{p_{i}}

is called the
Independent
sampling, where
p_{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\mathbf{Prob}(i\in S).
We get

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\dfrac{1}{n}\sum_{i\in S}\dfrac{a_{i}}{p_{i}}-\dfrac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\right\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\dfrac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\dfrac{1}{p_{i}}\chi_{i}a_{i}\right\|^{2}\right]-\left\|\dfrac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\dfrac{\mathbb{E}\left[\chi_{i}\right]}{n^{2}p_{i}^{2}}\left\|a_{i}\right\|^{2}+\sum_{i\neq j}\dfrac{\mathbb{E}\left[\chi_{i}\right]\mathbb{E}\left[\chi_{j}\right]}{n^{2}p_{i}p_{j}}\left\langle a_{i}\mathchar 59\relax a_{j}\right\rangle

\displaystyle-\left\|\dfrac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\dfrac{1}{n^{2}p_{i}}\left\|a_{i}\right\|^{2}+\dfrac{1}{n^{2}}\left(\left\|\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\right\|^{2}-\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left\|a_{i}\right\|^{2}\right)

\displaystyle-\left\|\dfrac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle=\dfrac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(\dfrac{1}{p_{i}}-1\right)\left\|a_{i}\right\|^{2}.

Thus we have
A=\dfrac{1}{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\dfrac{p_{i}}{1-p_{i}}}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}

B=0
and
w_{i}=\dfrac{\dfrac{p_{i}}{1-p_{i}}}{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\dfrac{p_{i}}{1-p_{i}}}
for all
i\in[n].

E5.3
Importance and uniform-with-replacement sampling

Let us fix
\tau>0.
For all
k\in[\tau]\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}
we define i.i.d. random variables

\chi_{k}=\begin{cases}1&\text{with probability}\ q_{1}\\
2&\text{with probability}\ q_{2}\\
\quad&\vdots\\
n&\text{with probability}\ q_{n}\mathchar 59\relax\end{cases}

where
(q_{1}\mathchar 59\relax\dots\mathchar 59\relax q_{n})\in\mathcal{S}^{n}
(simple simplex).
A sampling

{\bf S}(a_{1}\mathchar 59\relax\dots\mathchar 59\relax a_{n})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\dfrac{1}{\tau}\sum_{k=1}^{\tau}\dfrac{a_{\chi_{k}}}{nq_{\chi_{k}}}

is called the
Importance
sampling.
The
Importance
sampling reduces to the
Uniform-With-Replacement
sampling when
q_{i}=\nicefrac{{1}}{{n}}
for all
i\in[n].
Note that
|{\bf S}|\leq\tau.

Let us bound the variance

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\dfrac{1}{\tau}\sum_{k=1}^{\tau}\dfrac{a_{\chi_{k}}}{nq_{\chi_{k}}}-\dfrac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\right\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\dfrac{1}{\tau^{2}}\sum_{k=1}^{\tau}\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\dfrac{a_{\chi_{k}}}{nq_{\chi_{k}}}-\dfrac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\right\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\quad+\dfrac{1}{\tau^{2}}\sum_{k\neq k^{\prime}}\mathbb{E}\left[\left\langle\dfrac{a_{\chi_{k}}}{nq_{\chi_{k}}}-\dfrac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\mathchar 59\relax\dfrac{a_{\chi_{k^{\prime}}}}{nq_{\chi_{k^{\prime}}}}-\dfrac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\right\rangle\right].

Using the independence and unbiasedness of the random variables, the last term vanishes, and we get

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\dfrac{1}{\tau}\sum_{k=1}^{\tau}\dfrac{a_{\chi_{k}}}{nq_{\chi_{k}}}-\dfrac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\right\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\dfrac{1}{\tau^{2}}\sum_{k=1}^{\tau}\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\dfrac{a_{\chi_{k}}}{nq_{\chi_{k}}}-\dfrac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\right\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\overset{\eqref{ch5:auxiliary:variance_decomposition}}{=}\dfrac{1}{\tau^{2}}\sum_{k=1}^{\tau}\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\dfrac{a_{\chi_{k}}}{nq_{\chi_{k}}}\right\|^{2}\right]-\dfrac{1}{\tau}\left\|\dfrac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle=\dfrac{1}{\tau}\sum_{i=1}^{n}q_{i}\left\|\dfrac{a_{i}}{nq_{i}}\right\|^{2}-\dfrac{1}{\tau}\left\|\dfrac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle=\dfrac{1}{\tau}\left(\dfrac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\dfrac{1}{nq_{i}}\left\|a_{i}\right\|^{2}-\left\|\dfrac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\right\|^{2}\right).

Thus we have
A=B=\dfrac{1}{\tau}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}
and
w_{i}=q_{i}
for all
i\in[n].

E5.4
Extended nice sampling

In this section, we analyze the extension of
Nice
sampling. First, we
l_{i}
times repeat each vector
a_{i}
; then, we use the
Nice
sampling. We define

\tilde{a}_{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\begin{cases}\dfrac{\sum_{j=1}^{n}l_{j}}{nl_{1}}a_{1}&1\leq i\leq l_{1}\\
\dfrac{\sum_{j=1}^{n}l_{j}}{nl_{2}}a_{2}&l_{1}+1\leq i\leq l_{1}+l_{2}\\
\quad&\vdots\\
\dfrac{\sum_{j=1}^{n}l_{j}}{nl_{n}}a_{n}&\sum_{j=1}^{n-1}l_{j}\leq i\leq\sum_{j=1}^{n}l_{j}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\\
\end{cases}\mathchar 59\relax

where
a_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
l_{i}\geq 1
for all
i\in[n].

Then we have

\dfrac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}(x)=\dfrac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\tilde{a}_{i}(x)\mathchar 59\relax

where
N\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\sum_{j=1}^{n}l_{j}
. Also, we denote
N_{k}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\sum_{j=1}^{k}l_{j}.

For some
\tau>0\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}
we apply the
Nice
sampling method:

{\bf S}(a_{1}\mathchar 59\relax\dots\mathchar 59\relax a_{n})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\dfrac{1}{N}\sum_{i\in S}\dfrac{\tilde{a}_{i}}{p_{i}}=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\dfrac{1}{\tau}\chi_{i}\tilde{a}_{i}\mathchar 59\relax

where

\chi_{i}=\begin{cases}1&i\in S\\
0&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\quad p_{i}=\mathbf{Prob}\left(i\in S\right)\mathchar 59\relax

and
S
is a random set with cardinality
\tau
from
[N]
. The sampling
{\bf S}(a_{1}\mathchar 59\relax\dots\mathchar 59\relax a_{n})
is called the
Extended Nice
sampling.

We are now ready to bound the variance. Using the results for the
Nice
sampling, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|{\bf S}(a_{1}\mathchar 59\relax\dots\mathchar 59\relax a_{n})-\dfrac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}(x)\right\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|{\bf S}(a_{1}\mathchar 59\relax\dots\mathchar 59\relax a_{n})-\dfrac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\tilde{a}_{i}(x)\right\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\dfrac{n-\tau}{\tau(n-1)}\dfrac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\left\|\tilde{a}_{i}\right\|^{2}-\dfrac{n-\tau}{\tau(n-1)}\left\|\dfrac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\tilde{a}_{i}\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle=\dfrac{n-\tau}{\tau(n-1)}\left(\dfrac{1}{N}\left(\dfrac{N}{nl_{1}}\right)^{2}\sum_{i=1}^{N_{1}}\left\|{a}_{1}\right\|^{2}+\dfrac{1}{N}\left(\dfrac{N}{nl_{2}}\right)^{2}\sum_{i=N_{1}+1}^{N_{2}}\left\|{a}_{2}\right\|^{2}\right.

\displaystyle\left.\quad+\cdots+\dfrac{1}{N}\left(\dfrac{N}{nl_{n}}\right)^{2}\sum_{i=N_{n-1}+1}^{N}\left\|{a}_{n}\right\|^{2}\right)-\dfrac{n-\tau}{\tau(n-1)}\left\|\dfrac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle={\dfrac{n-\tau}{\tau(n-1)}{\left(\dfrac{N}{nl_{1}}\dfrac{1}{n}\left\|a_{1}\right\|^{2}+\dfrac{N}{nl_{2}}\dfrac{1}{n}\left\|a_{2}\right\|^{2}+\cdots+\dfrac{N}{nl_{n}}\dfrac{1}{n}\left\|a_{n}\right\|^{2}\right)}-\dfrac{n-\tau}{\tau(n-1)}\left\|\dfrac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\right\|^{2}}

\displaystyle{=\dfrac{n-\tau}{\tau(n-1)}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\dfrac{1}{n^{2}w_{i}}\left\|a_{i}\right\|^{2}\right)-\dfrac{n-\tau}{\tau(n-1)}\left\|\dfrac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\right\|^{2}}

where
w_{i}=\dfrac{l_{i}}{N}
. Thus we have
A=B=\dfrac{n-\tau}{\tau(n-1)}
and
w_{i}=\dfrac{l_{i}}{N}
for
i\in[n].

F5
The Optimal Choice of
w_{i}

Let us consider
L_{+\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}w}^{2}
and
L_{\pm\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}w}^{2}.
In Section
5.1.3
, we show that one can take

L_{+\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}w}^{2}=L_{\pm\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}w}^{2}=\dfrac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\dfrac{1}{nw_{i}}L_{i}^{2}.

Let us minimize
\dfrac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\dfrac{1}{nw_{i}}L_{i}^{2}
with respect to the weights
w_{i}
such that

w_{1}\mathchar 59\relax\dots\mathchar 59\relax w_{n}\geq 0\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\quad\sum_{i=1}^{n}w_{i}=1.

Using the method of Lagrange multipliers, we can construct a Lagrangian

\mathcal{L}(w\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\lambda)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\dfrac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\dfrac{1}{nw_{i}}L_{i}^{2}-\lambda\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}w_{i}-1\right).

Next, we calculate partial derivatives

\displaystyle\dfrac{\partial\mathcal{L}}{\partial w_{i}}=-\dfrac{1}{n^{2}w_{i}^{2}}L_{i}^{2}-\lambda=0\forall i\in[n]

and get

w_{i}^{2}=-\dfrac{L_{i}^{2}}{n^{2}\lambda}.

Using
\sum_{i=1}^{n}w_{i}=1\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}
we can show that the weights
w_{i}^{*}=\dfrac{L_{i}}{\sum_{i=1}^{n}L_{i}}
are the solutions of the minimization problem. Moreover,

\dfrac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\dfrac{1}{nw_{i}^{*}}L_{i}^{2}=\left(\dfrac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}L_{i}\right)^{2}.

G5
Missing Proofs: The Composition of Samplings

Lemma 20
.

Let us assume that a random sampling function
{\bf S}
satisfies Assumption
11
with some
A\mathchar 59\relax B
and weights
w_{i}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}
and a random sampling function
{\bf S}_{i}
satisfy Assumption
11
with some
A_{i}\mathchar 59\relax B_{i}
and weights
w_{ij}
for all
i\in[n].
Moreover,
B\leq 1.
Then

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|{\bf S}\left({\bf S}_{1}\left(a_{11}\mathchar 59\relax\dots\mathchar 59\relax a_{1m_{1}}\right)\mathchar 59\relax\dots\mathchar 59\relax{\bf S}_{n}\left(a_{n1}\mathchar 59\relax\dots\mathchar 59\relax a_{nm_{n}}\right)\right)-\dfrac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(\dfrac{1}{m_{i}}\sum_{j=1}^{m_{i}}a_{ij}\right)\right\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\dfrac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(\dfrac{A}{nw_{i}}+\dfrac{(1-B)}{n}\right)\left(\dfrac{A_{i}}{m_{i}}\sum_{j=1}^{m_{i}}\dfrac{1}{m_{i}w_{ij}}\left\|a_{ij}\right\|^{2}-B_{i}\left\|\dfrac{1}{m_{i}}\sum_{j=1}^{m_{i}}a_{ij}\right\|^{2}\right)

\displaystyle\quad+\dfrac{A}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\dfrac{1}{nw_{i}}\left\|\dfrac{1}{m_{i}}\sum_{j=1}^{m_{i}}a_{ij}\right\|^{2}-B\left\|\dfrac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(\dfrac{1}{m_{i}}\sum_{j=1}^{m_{i}}a_{ij}\right)\right\|^{2}\mathchar 59\relax

where
a_{ij}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
for all
j\in[m_{i}]
and
i\in[n].

Proof.

We denote
\widehat{a}_{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}{\bf S}_{i}\left(a_{i1}\mathchar 59\relax\dots\mathchar 59\relax a_{im_{i}}\right)
and
a_{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\dfrac{1}{m_{i}}\sum_{j=1}^{m_{i}}a_{ij}.
Using (
5.6
), we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|{\bf S}\left(\widehat{a}_{1}\mathchar 59\relax\dots\mathchar 59\relax\widehat{a}_{n}\right)-\dfrac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\right\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[{\rm E}_{S}\left[\left\|{\bf S}\left(\widehat{a}_{1}\mathchar 59\relax\dots\mathchar 59\relax\widehat{a}_{n}\right)-\dfrac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\right\|^{2}\right]\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[{\rm E}_{S}\left[\left\|{\bf S}\left(\widehat{a}_{1}\mathchar 59\relax\dots\mathchar 59\relax\widehat{a}_{n}\right)-\dfrac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\widehat{a}_{i}\right\|^{2}\right]\right]+\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\dfrac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\widehat{a}_{i}-\dfrac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\right\|^{2}\right].

Next, using Assumption
11
for the sampling
{\bf S}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}
we get

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|{\bf S}\left(\widehat{a}_{1}\mathchar 59\relax\dots\mathchar 59\relax\widehat{a}_{n}\right)-\dfrac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\right\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq A\dfrac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\dfrac{1}{nw_{i}}\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\widehat{a}_{i}\right\|^{2}\right]-B\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\dfrac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\widehat{a}_{i}\right\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\quad+\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\dfrac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\widehat{a}_{i}-\dfrac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\right\|^{2}\right].

Due to (
5.6
), we obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|{\bf S}\left(\widehat{a}_{1}\mathchar 59\relax\dots\mathchar 59\relax\widehat{a}_{n}\right)-\dfrac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\right\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq A\dfrac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\dfrac{1}{nw_{i}}\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\widehat{a}_{i}-a_{i}\right\|^{2}\right]+A\dfrac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\dfrac{1}{nw_{i}}\left\|a_{i}\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\quad-B\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\dfrac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\widehat{a}_{i}-\dfrac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\right\|^{2}\right]-B\left\|\dfrac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\quad+\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\dfrac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\widehat{a}_{i}-\dfrac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\right\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=A\dfrac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\dfrac{1}{nw_{i}}\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\widehat{a}_{i}-a_{i}\right\|^{2}\right]+A\dfrac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\dfrac{1}{nw_{i}}\left\|a_{i}\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\quad+(1-B)\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\dfrac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\widehat{a}_{i}-\dfrac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\right\|^{2}\right]-B\left\|\dfrac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle=A\dfrac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\dfrac{1}{nw_{i}}\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\widehat{a}_{i}-a_{i}\right\|^{2}\right]+A\dfrac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\dfrac{1}{nw_{i}}\left\|a_{i}\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\quad+\dfrac{(1-B)}{n^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\widehat{a}_{i}-a_{i}\right\|^{2}\right]-B\left\|\dfrac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle=\dfrac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(\dfrac{A}{nw_{i}}+\dfrac{(1-B)}{n}\right)\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\widehat{a}_{i}-a_{i}\right\|^{2}\right]+A\dfrac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\dfrac{1}{nw_{i}}\left\|a_{i}\right\|^{2}-B\left\|\dfrac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\right\|^{2}.

Using Assumption
11
for the samplings
{\bf S}_{i}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}
we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|{\bf S}\left(\widehat{a}_{1}\mathchar 59\relax\dots\mathchar 59\relax\widehat{a}_{n}\right)-\dfrac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\right\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\dfrac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(\dfrac{A}{nw_{i}}+\dfrac{(1-B)}{n}\right)\left(A_{i}\dfrac{1}{m_{i}}\sum_{j=1}^{m_{i}}\dfrac{1}{m_{i}w_{ij}}\left\|a_{ij}\right\|^{2}-B_{i}\left\|a_{i}\right\|^{2}\right)

\displaystyle\quad+A\dfrac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\dfrac{1}{nw_{i}}\left\|a_{i}\right\|^{2}-B\left\|\dfrac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\right\|^{2}.

∎

See
26

Proof.

We start with the estimation of the variance of the noise:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|g^{t+1}-\nabla f(x^{t+1})\right\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=(1-p)\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|g^{t}+{\bf S}\left(\left\{{\bf S}_{i}\left(\{\nabla f_{ij}(x^{t+1})-\nabla f_{ij}(x^{t})\}_{j=1}^{m_{i}}\right)\right\}_{i=1}^{n}\right)-\nabla f(x^{t+1})\right\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=(1-p)\left\|{\bf S}\left(\left\{{\bf S}_{i}\left(\{\nabla f_{ij}(x^{t+1})-\nabla f_{ij}(x^{t})\}_{j=1}^{m_{i}}\right)\right\}_{i=1}^{n}\right)-\left(\nabla f(x^{t+1})-\nabla f(x^{t})\right)\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad+(1-p)\left\|g^{t}-\nabla f(x^{t})\right\|^{2}\mathchar 59\relax

where we used the unbiasedness of the composition of samplings.
Using Lemma
20
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|g^{t+1}-\nabla f(x^{t+1})\right\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq(1-p)\left(\dfrac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(\dfrac{A}{nw_{i}}+\dfrac{(1-B)}{n}\right)\right.

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\left.\left(\dfrac{A_{i}}{m_{i}}\sum_{j=1}^{m_{i}}\dfrac{1}{m_{i}w_{ij}}\left\|\nabla f_{ij}(x^{t+1})-\nabla f_{ij}(x^{t})\right\|^{2}-B_{i}\left\|\nabla f_{i}(x^{t+1})-\nabla f_{i}(x^{t})\right\|^{2}\right)\right.

\displaystyle\left.\qquad\qquad+\dfrac{A}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\dfrac{1}{nw_{i}}\left\|\nabla f_{i}(x^{t+1})-\nabla f_{i}(x^{t})\right\|^{2}-B\left\|\nabla f(x^{t+1})-\nabla f(x^{t})\right\|^{2}\right)

\displaystyle\quad+(1-p)\left\|g^{t}-\nabla f(x^{t})\right\|^{2}.

Using Definitions
19
,
20
,
24
and
25
, we get

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|g^{t+1}-\nabla f(x^{t+1})\right\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq(1-p)\left(\dfrac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(\dfrac{A}{nw_{i}}+\dfrac{(1-B)}{n}\right)\left(\dfrac{A_{i}-B_{i}}{m_{i}}\sum_{j=1}^{m_{i}}\dfrac{1}{m_{i}w_{ij}}\left\|\nabla f_{ij}(x^{t+1})-\nabla f_{ij}(x^{t})\right\|^{2}\right.\right.

\displaystyle\left.\left.+\quad B_{i}\left(\dfrac{1}{m_{i}}\sum_{j=1}^{m_{i}}\dfrac{1}{m_{i}w_{ij}}\left\|\nabla f_{ij}(x^{t+1})-\nabla f_{ij}(x^{t})\right\|^{2}-\left\|\nabla f_{i}(x^{t+1})-\nabla f_{i}(x^{t})\right\|^{2}\right)\right)\right.

\displaystyle\left.\quad+\dfrac{A-B}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\dfrac{1}{nw_{i}}\left\|\nabla f_{i}(x^{t+1})-\nabla f_{i}(x^{t})\right\|^{2}\right.

\displaystyle\left.\quad+B\left(\dfrac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\dfrac{1}{nw_{i}}\left\|\nabla f_{i}(x^{t+1})-\nabla f_{i}(x^{t})\right\|^{2}-\left\|\nabla f(x^{t+1})-\nabla f(x^{t})\right\|^{2}\right)\right)

\displaystyle\quad+(1-p)\left\|g^{t}-\nabla f(x^{t})\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq(1-p)\left(\dfrac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(\dfrac{A}{nw_{i}}+\dfrac{(1-B)}{n}\right)\left((A_{i}-B_{i})L_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}+\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}w_{i}}^{2}+B_{i}L_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\pm\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}w_{i}}^{2}\right)\right.

\displaystyle\left.\qquad\qquad\qquad+(A-B)L_{+\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}w}^{2}+BL_{\pm\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}w}^{2}\right)\left\|x^{t+1}-x^{t}\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\quad+(1-p)\left\|g^{t}-\nabla f(x^{t})\right\|^{2}.

From this point the proof of theorem repeats the proof of Theorem
22
with

\dfrac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(\dfrac{A}{nw_{i}}+\dfrac{(1-B)}{n}\right)\left((A_{i}-B_{i})L_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}+\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}w_{i}}^{2}+B_{i}L_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\pm\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}w_{i}}^{2}\right)+(A-B)L_{+\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}w}^{2}+BL_{\pm\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}w}^{2}

instead of

\left(A-B\right)L_{+\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}w}^{2}+BL_{\pm\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}w}^{2}.

∎

H5
Artificial Quadratic Optimization Tasks

In this section, we provide algorithms that we use to generate artificial optimization tasks for experiments. Algorithm
13
and Algorithm

allow us to control the smoothness constants
L_{\pm}
and
L_{i}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}
accordingly, via the noise scales.

Algorithm 13

Generate Quadratic Optimization Task with Controlled
L_{\pm}
(homogeneity).

1:

Input and Initialization:
number nodes
n
, dimension
d
, regularizer
\lambda
, and noise scale
s
.

2:

for

i=1\mathchar 59\relax\dots\mathchar 59\relax n

do

3:

Generate random noises
\nu_{i}^{s}=1+s\xi_{i}^{s}
and
\nu_{i}^{b}=s\xi_{i}^{b}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}
i.i.d.
\xi_{i}^{s}\mathchar 59\relax\xi_{i}^{b}\sim\textnormal{NormalDistribution}(0\mathchar 59\relax 1)

4:

Take vector
b_{i}=\dfrac{\nu_{i}^{s}}{4}(-1+\nu_{i}^{b}\mathchar 59\relax 0\mathchar 59\relax\cdots\mathchar 59\relax 0)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

5:

Take the initial tridiagonal matrix

\mathbf{A}_{i}=\dfrac{\nu_{i}^{s}}{4}\left(\begin{array}[]{cccc}2&-1&&0\\
-1&\ddots&\ddots&\\
&\ddots&\ddots&-1\\
0&&-1&2\end{array}\right)\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}

6:

end

for

7:

Take the mean of matrices
\mathbf{A}=\dfrac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{A}_{i}

8:

Find the minimum eigenvalue
\lambda_{\min}(\mathbf{A})

9:

for

i=1\mathchar 59\relax\dots\mathchar 59\relax n

do

10:

Update matrix
\mathbf{A}_{i}=\mathbf{A}_{i}+(\lambda-\lambda_{\min}(\mathbf{A}))\mathbf{I}

11:

end

for

12:

Take starting point
x^{0}=(\sqrt{d}\mathchar 59\relax 0\mathchar 59\relax\cdots\mathchar 59\relax 0)

13:

Output:
matrices
\mathbf{A}_{1}\mathchar 59\relax\cdots\mathchar 59\relax\mathbf{A}_{n}
, vectors
b_{1}\mathchar 59\relax\cdots\mathchar 59\relax b_{n}
, starting point
x^{0}

Chapter 6
Compressed L2GD: Personalized FL with Compression

The goals and summaries of this chapter are outlined in Table
1.1
and Section
1.4.5
.

6.1
Introduction

We live in the era of big data, and mobile devices have become a part of our daily lives. While the training of ML models using the diverse data stored on these devices is becoming increasingly popular, the traditional data center-based approach to training them faces serious
privacy issues
and has to deal with
high communication and energy cost
associated with the transfer of data from users to the data center
(
Dean et al. 2012
)
.

Federated Learning
(FL) provides an attractive alternative to the traditional approach as it aims to train the models directly on
resource constrained
heterogeneous devices without any need for the data to leave them
(
Konečný et al. 2016b
;
Kairouz et al. 2021
)
.

The prevalent paradigm for training FL models is empirical risk minimization, where the aim is to train a
single global model
using the aggregate of all the training data stored across all participating devices. Among the popular algorithms for training FL models for this formulation belong
FedAVG

(
McMahan et al. 2017
)
,
Local GD

(
Khaled et al. 2019
;
Khaled et al. 2020
)
,
Local SGD

(
Stich 2019
;
Khaled et al. 2020
;
Gorbunov et al. 2020a
)
and
Shifted Local SVRG

(
Gorbunov et al. 2020a
)
. All these methods require the participating devices to perform a local training procedure (for example, by taking multiple steps of some optimization algorithm) and subsequently communicate the resulting model to an orchestrating server infrastructure for aggregation; see Figure
6.2
(a). This process is repeated until a model of suitable qualities is found. For more variants of local methods and further pointers to the literature, we refer the reader to
Gorbunov et al. 2020a
.

Figure 6.1
:
Training
n
local devices,
\{W_{i}\}
on the loss,
f_{i}
of their local model,
x_{i}
with a central server/master node, where
h_{i}
penalizes for dissimilarity between the local model,
x_{i}
and the average of all local models,
\bar{x}.

(a)

FedAVG

(b)

L2GD

Figure 6.2
:
The
FedAVG

(
McMahan et al. 2017
)
and
L2GD

(
Hanzely and Richtárik 2020
)
algorithm on 2 devices. Unlike
FedAVG
,
L2GD
does not communicate after a fixed
T
local steps, it communicates based on a probabilistic protocol.

6.1.1
Personalized FL

In contrast,
Hanzely and Richtárik 2020
recently introduced a new formulation of FL as an alternative to the existing “single-model-suits-all” approach embodied by empirical risk minimization. Their formulation explicitly aims to find a
personalized
model for every device; see Figure
6.1
. In particular,
Hanzely and Richtárik 2020
considered the formulation
1
1

1

Zhang et al. 2015
considered a similar model in a different context and with different motivations.

\min\limits_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{nd}}\left[F(x)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}f(x)+h(x)\right]

(6.1)

for simultaneous training of
n
personalized FL models
x_{1}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\dots\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}x_{n}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
for
n
participating devices. They chose

{f(x)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\dfrac{1}{n}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}f_{i}(x_{i})\mathchar 59\relax\quad\text{and}\quad h(x)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\dfrac{1}{n}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}h_{i}(x)\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}}

where
f_{i}
represents the loss of model
x_{i}
over the local data stored on device
i
. Function
h_{i}
penalizes for dissimilarity between the local model
x_{i}
and the average of all local models

\bar{x}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\dfrac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}

and is defined to be

h_{i}(x)=\dfrac{\lambda}{2}\|x_{i}-\bar{x}\|_{2}^{2}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}

where
{\lambda>0}
controls for the strength of this penalization. At one extreme,
{\lambda\rightarrow\infty}
forces the local models to be equal to their average and hence mutually identical. Therefore, (
6.1
) reduces to the classical empirical risk minimization formulation

{\min\limits_{z\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\dfrac{1}{n}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}f_{i}(z).}

On the other hand, for
\lambda=0
Problem (
6.1
) is equivalent to each client (node) training independently using their own data only. And the
i^{\rm th}
client solves

\min_{x_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}f_{i}(x_{i}).

By choosing
\lambda
to a value in between these two extremes,

{0<\lambda<\infty}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}

we control the level of similarity among the personalized models
\{x_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
.

We remark that, local methods such as
FedAVG
by
McMahan et al. 2017
(also see similar methods in
Haddadpour et al. 2019a
;
Stich 2019
;
Wang and Joshi 2019
;
Zhou and Cong 2018
;
Lin et al. 2020
), are popular for training FL models. Their main drawback in the heterogeneous setting with data and device heterogeneity is inefficient communication.
Hanzely and Richtárik 2020
solved this, and we are using their model to build our compressed, personalized FL.

To solve Problem (
6.1
),
Hanzely and Richtárik 2020
proposed a
probabilistic
gradient descent algorithm for which they coined the name loopless local gradient descent (
L2GD
).
Hanzely and Richtárik 2020
shows how
L2GD
can be interpreted as a simple variant of
FedAVG
, typically presented as a method for solving the standard empirical risk minimization (ERM) formulation of FL. However, alongside
Hanzely and Richtárik 2020
argue,
L2GD
is better seen as an algorithm for solving the personalized FL formulation (
6.1
). By doing so, they interpret the nature of local steps in classical FL: the role of local steps in classical FL methods is to provide personalization and not communication efficiency as was widely believed—
FedAVG
can diverge on highly non-identical data partitions
(
McMahan et al. 2017
)
. Instead, communication efficiency in local methods comes from their tendency to gear towards personalization, and personalized models are easier to train.

Communication compression.
We observe that the
L2GD
algorithm does not support any compression mechanism
for the master-worker and worker-master communication that needs to happen. This is the starting point of our work.

We believe that equipping personalized FL with fast and theoretically tractable communication compression mechanisms is an important open problem.

In distributed training of deep neural network (DNN) models, synchronous data-parallelism
(
Dean et al. 2012
)
is most widely used and adopted by mainstream Deep Learning toolkits (such as

PyTorch
,

TensorFlow
). However, exchanging the stochastic gradients for aggregation creates a communication bottleneck, and this results in slower training
(
Xu et al. 2021a
)
. One way to save on communication costs is to use compression operators
(
Alistarh et al. 2017
;
Horváth et al. 2023
;
Xu et al. 2021a
)
. Gradient compression techniques, such as quantization
(
Alistarh et al. 2017
;
Bernstein et al. 2018
;
Horváth et al. 2019
;
Beznosikov et al. 2023
;
Safaryan et al. 2020
)
, sparsification
(
Suresh et al. 2017
;
Konečný and Richtárik 2018
;
Aji and Heafield 2017
;
Sahu et al. 2021
;
Stich et al. 2018
;
Dutta et al. 2020
;
Safaryan et al. 2020
)
, hybrid compressors
(
Strom 2015
)
, and low-rank methods
(
Vogels et al. 2019
)
have been proposed to overcome this issue
2
2

2

Model compression
(
Guo 2018
;
Chraibi et al. 2019
)
is orthogonal to gradient compression and not in the scope of this work.
.

Although recent works have introduced compression in traditional FL formulation
(
Konečný et al. 2016b
;
Reisizadeh et al. 2020
;
Philippenko and Dieuleveut 2020
;
Shlezinger et al. 2020
;
Amiri et al. 2020
;
Xu et al. 2021b
)
; except
(
Horváth et al. 2019
;
Philippenko and Dieuleveut 2020
;
Gorbunov et al. 2020c
;
Amiri et al. 2020
)
, others use compression only for the
throughput limited uplink
channel, that is, to upload the local models from the devices to the central server. But limited bandwidth in the downlink channel may pose a communication latency between the server and the devices and consequently, slow down the training; see detailed discussion in Section
6.1.2
. As of now, no study combines
bidirectional
compression techniques with a probabilistic communication protocol in the FL set-up by using a mixture of a local and global model as in Problem (
6.1
). In this work, we combine these aspects and make subsequent contributions.

6.1.2
Related work

Numerous studies are proposed to reduce communication but not all of them are in the FL setting. In this scope, for completeness, we quote a few representatives from each class of communication-efficient
SGD
.

Smith et al. 2017
proposed a communication-efficient primal-dual optimization that learns separate but related models for each participating device. The
FedAVG
by
McMahan et al. 2017
performs local steps on a subset of participating devices in an FL setting. Similar to
FedAVG
, but without data and device heterogeneity,
Haddadpour et al. 2019a
;
Stich 2019
;
Wang and Joshi 2019
;
Zhou and Cong 2018
;
Lin et al. 2020
independently proposed local SGD, where several local steps are taken on the participating devices before periodic communication and averaging the local models. While
FedProx

(
Li et al. 2020d
)
generalizes
FedAVG
,
SCAFFOLD
employs variance reduction to correct local updates arising from non-independent and identically distributed (non-i.i.d.) data in
FedAVG
. From the system’s perspective, on

TensorFlow
,
Bonawitz et al. 2019
built a FL system on mobile devices.

Compression has also been introduced in the FL.
Shlezinger et al. 2020
combined universal vector quantization with FL for throughput limited uplink channel. In
FedPAQ

(
Reisizadeh et al. 2020
)
, each local device sends a compressed difference between its input and output model to the central server, after computing the local updates for a fixed number of iterations. While
Amiri et al. 2020
used a bidirectional compression in FL,
Philippenko and Dieuleveut 2020
combined it with a memory mechanism or error feedback
(
Stich et al. 2018
)
.

Among other communication-efficient
SGDs
, parallel restarted
SGD

(
Yu et al. 2019a
)
reduces the number of communication rounds compared to the baseline
SGD
.
Haddadpour et al. 2019b
showed that redundancy reduces residual error as compared with the baseline
SGD
where all nodes can sample from the complete data and this leads to lower communication overheads.
CoCoA

(
Jaggi et al. 2014
)
,
Dane

(
Shamir et al. 2014
)
perform several local steps and hence fewer communication rounds before communicating with the other workers. Lazily Aggregated Gradient (
LAG
)
(
Chen et al. 2018a
)
, selects a subgroup of workers and uses their gradients, rather than obtaining a fresh gradient from each one.

In decentralized training, where the nodes only communicate with their neighbors,
Koloskova et al. 2019
implemented an
average consensus
where the nodes can communicate to their neighbors via a fixed communication graph.
Li et al. 2018
proposed
Pipe-SGD
—a framework with decentralized pipelined training.

Personalization in FL is a growing research area.
Arivazhagan et al. 2019
proposed
FedPer
to mitigate statistical heterogeneity of data; also see adaptive personalized FL algorithm in
Deng et al. 2020
.
Mei et al. 2021
proposed to obtain personalization in FL by using layer-wise parameters, and two-stage training; also see
Ma et al. 2022
and model personalization in
Shen et al. 2022
.
Shamsian et al. 2021
trained a central hypernetwork model to generate a set of personalized models for the local devices.
Li et al. 2021b
proposed
Hermes
—a communication-efficient personalized FL, where each local device identifies a small subnetwork by applying the structured pruning, communicates these subnetworks to the server and the devices, the server performs the aggregation on only overlapped parameters across each subnetwork; also see
Pillutla et al. 2022
for partial model personalization in FL.
DispFL
is another communication-efficient personalized FL algorithm proposed by
Dai et al. 2022
. In recent work,
Zhang et al. 2021
introduce personalization by calculating optimal weighted model combinations for each client without assuming any data distribution. For a connection between personalization in FL and model-agnostic-meta-learning (
MAML
), see
Fallah et al. 2020
. Additionally, we refer to the surveys
Kulkarni et al. 2020
;
Tan et al. 2023
for an overview of personalization in FL.

6.1.3
Contributions

1.

L2GD algorithm with bidirectional compression.
Communication compression is prevalent in recent local FL training algorithms, but these algorithms are not robust to data and device heterogeneity.
L2GD
by
Hanzely and Richtárik 2020
remedies this issue by introducing personalization in FL. Integrating compression with
L2GD
algorithm is a nontrivial task—unlike other FL algorithms,
L2GD
does not communicate after fixed local steps, it communicates based on a probabilistic protocol; see Section
6.2
and Figure
6.2
. Additionally, due to this probabilistic protocol, the communication involves local model updates, as well as gradients; see Section
6.2
. To reduce the communication bottleneck in
L2GD
, we use compression techniques on top of its probabilistic communication at both master and the participating local devices; see Section
6.3
. To the best of our knowledge, we are the first to integrate
bidirectional compression techniques
with a probabilistic communication in the FL set-up, and we call our algorithm
Compressed L2GD
; see Algorithm
15
.

2.

Convergence analysis.
In Section
6.4
, we prove the convergence of our
Compressed L2GD
algorithm based on the most recent theoretical development, such as expected smoothness as in
Gower et al. 2019
. Admittedly, convergence analysis of first-order optimization algorithms with bidirectional compression exists in the literature, see works
Tang et al. 2019
;
Horváth et al. 2019
;
Amiri et al. 2020
;
Dutta et al. 2020
, integrating arbitrary unbiased compressors with a probabilistic communication protocol into personalized FL, and showing convergence are nontrivial and the first one in its class. Our compressed
L2GD
algorithm maintains a similar asymptotic convergence rate as the baseline vanilla
SGD
without compression in both strongly convex and smooth non-convex cases; see Theorem
28
and Theorem
29
in Section
6.4
.

3.

Optimal rate and communication.
We optimized the complexity bounds of our algorithm as a function of the parameters involved in the algorithm. This leads us to the “optimal" setting of our algorithm. Mainly, we derived the optimal expected number of local steps to get the optimal iteration complexity and communication rounds; see Section
6.5
. Although our analysis is based on some hard-to-compute constants in real life, for example, the Lipchitz constant, this may help the practitioners to get an insight into the iteration complexity and communication trade-off; see Theorem
31
and Theorem
33
in Section
6.5
.

4.

Empirical study.
We perform diverse numerical experiments on synthetic and real datasets by using both convex and non-convex problems (using 4 DNN models) and invoking various compression techniques; see details in Section
6.6
, Table
6.1
. In training larger DNN models, to obtain the same global
Top1
test accuracy,
Compressed L2GD
reduces the communicated data volume (bits normalized by the number of local devices or clients,
\mathrm{\#bits/n}
), from
10^{15}
to
10^{11}
, rendering approximately
10^{4}
times improvement compared to
FedAVG
; see Section
6.6.2
. Moreover,
L2GD
with
Natural
compressor (that by design has smaller variance) empirically behaves the best and converges approximately
5
times faster, and reaches the best accuracy on both train and the test sets compared to no-compression
FedOpt

(
Reddi et al. 2020
)
baseline; see Section
6.6.2
and Section
D6
. These experiments validate the effect of the parameters used, and the effect of compressors, and show the efficiency of our algorithm in practice.

6.2
Background and Preliminaries

Notation.
For a given vector,

{x\in\mathbb{R}^{nd}}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}

by
x_{i}
we denote the
i^{\rm th}
subvector of
x
, and write

{x=\left(x_{1}^{\top}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\ldots\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}x_{n}^{\top}\right)^{\top}}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{where\,}{x_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}.

We denote the
i^{\rm th}
component of
x
by
x_{(i)}
and
\|x\|
represents its Euclidean norm. By
{[n]}
we denote the set of indexes,
{\{1\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\ldots\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}n\}}.

By
\mathbf{E}_{\xi}(\cdot)
we define the expectation over the randomness of
\xi
conditional to all the other potential random variables. The operator,

{\mathcal{C}(\cdot)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\left(\mathcal{C}_{1}(\cdot)^{\top}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\ldots\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\mathcal{C}_{n}(\cdot)^{\top}\right)^{\top}:\mathbb{R}^{nd}\to\mathbb{R}^{nd}}

denotes a compression operator with each
\mathcal{C}_{i}(\cdot)
being compatible with
x_{i}
. Denote
{\mathbf{Q}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}[\mathbf{I}\mathchar 59\relax\mathbf{I}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\ldots\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\mathbf{I}]^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{nd\times d}}
, where
\mathbf{I}
denotes the identity matrix of
\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
. With our Assumptions that we will introduce later in the chapter, the Problem (
6.1
) has a
unique solution, which we denote by
x^{*}
and we define
\bar{x}^{*}
as

{\bar{x}^{*}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\dfrac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}^{*}.}

Finally, we denote the cardinality of a set
S
by
|S|
.

Loopless local gradient descent (
L2GD
).
We give a brief overview of the loopless local gradient descent (
L2GD
) algorithm by
Hanzely and Richtárik 2020
to solve (
6.1
) as a two sum problem. At each iteration, to estimate the gradient of
F
,
L2GD
samples either the gradient of
f
or the gradient of
h
and updates the local models via:

{x_{i}^{k+1}=x_{i}^{k}-\alpha G_{i}(x^{k})\mathchar 59\relax~i=1\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\ldots\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}n\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}}

where
G_{i}(x^{k})
for
~i=1\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\ldots\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}n\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}
is the
i^{\rm th}
block of the vector

{G(x^{k})=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\dfrac{\nabla f(x^{k})}{1-p}&\text{ with probability }1-p\mathchar 59\relax\\
&\textbf{(Local gradient step)}\\
\dfrac{\nabla h(x^{k})}{p}&\text{ with probability }p\mathchar 59\relax\\
&\textbf{(Aggregation step)}\end{array}\right.}

where

{0<p<1}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}

and
\nabla f(x^{k})
is the gradient of
f
at
x^{k}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}
and

{\nabla_{i}h(x^{k})=\dfrac{\lambda}{n}\left(x_{i}^{k}-\bar{x}^{k}\right)}

is the
i^{\rm th}
block of the gradient of
h
at
x^{k}
.

In this approach, there is a hidden communication between the local devices because in aggregation steps they need the average of the local models. That is, the communication occurs when the devices switch from a local gradient step to an aggregation step. Note that there is no need for communication between the local devices when they switch from an aggregation step to a local gradient step. There is also no need for communication after two consecutive aggregation steps since the average of the local models does not change in this case. If
k
and
k+1
are both aggregation steps, we have

\bar{x}^{k+1}=\dfrac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}^{k+1}=\dfrac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}^{k}-\dfrac{\alpha\lambda}{n}\dfrac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(x_{i}^{k}-\bar{x}^{k}\right)=\bar{x}^{k}-\dfrac{\alpha\lambda}{n}\cdot 0=\bar{x}^{k}.

6.3
Compressed L2GD

Algorithm 15

Compressed L2GD
.

Input:
step size
\eta>0
, probability
p

Initialize:

\{x_{i}^{0}\}_{i=1\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\ldots\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}n}
,
\xi_{-1}=1
,
\bar{x}^{-1}=\dfrac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}^{0}

for

k=0\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}1\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}2\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\ldots

do

Draw:

\xi_{k}=1
with probability
p

if

\xi_{k}=0

then

on all devices:

x_{i}^{k+1}=x_{i}^{k}-\dfrac{\eta}{n(1-p)}\nabla f_{i}(x_{i}^{k})
for
i\in[n]

else

if

\xi_{k-1}=0

then

on all devices:
Compress
x_{i}^{k}
to
\mathcal{C}_{i}(x_{i}^{k})
and communicate
\mathcal{C}_{i}(x_{i}^{k})
to the master

on master:

1. Receive
\mathcal{C}_{i}(x_{i}^{k})
from all devices
i\in[n]

2. Compute
\bar{y}^{k}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\dfrac{1}{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\mathcal{C}_{j}(x_{j}^{k})

3. Compress
\bar{y}^{k}
to
\mathcal{C}_{M}(\bar{y}^{k})

4. Communicate
\mathcal{C}_{M}(\bar{y}^{k})
to all devices

on all devices:
Perform aggregation step
x_{i}^{k+1}=x_{i}^{k}-\dfrac{\eta\lambda}{np}\left(x_{i}^{k}-\mathcal{C}_{M}(\bar{y}^{k})\right)

else

on all devices:

a.
\bar{x}^{k}=\bar{x}^{k-1}

b. Perform aggregation step
x_{i}^{k+1}=x_{i}^{k}-\dfrac{\eta\lambda}{np}\left(x_{i}^{k}-\bar{x}^{k}\right)

end

if

end

if

end

for

Now, we are all set to describe
Compressed L2GD
Algorithm
15
for solving Problem (
6.1
). We start by defining how the compression operates in our set-up.

6.3.1
Compressed communication

Recall that the original
L2GD
algorithm has a probabilistic communication protocol—the devices do not communicate after every fixed number of local steps. The communication occurs when the devices switch from a local gradient step to an aggregation step. Therefore, instead of using the compressors in a fixed time stamp (after every
{T>0}
iteration, say), each device
i
, requires to compress its local model
x_{i}
when it needs to communicate it to the master, based on the probabilistic protocol. We assume that device
i
uses the compression operator,

{\mathcal{C}_{i}(\cdot):\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}}.

Moreover, another compression happens when the master needs to communicate with the devices. We assume that the master uses the compression operator,

{\mathcal{C}_{M}(\cdot):\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}}.

Therefore, the compression is used in uplink and downlink channels similar to
Dutta et al. 2020
;
Horváth et al. 2019
, but occurs in a probabilistic fashion. There exists another subtlety—although the model parameters (either from the local devices or the global aggregated model) are communicated in the network for training the FL model via
Compressed L2GD
, the compressors that we use in this work are the compressors used for gradient compression in distributed DNN training; see
Xu et al. 2021a
.

6.3.2
The algorithm

Note that, in each iteration
{k\geq 0}
, there exists a random variable,
{\xi_{k}\in\{0\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}1\}}
with

{\mathbf{Prob}(\xi_{k}=1)=p}\mathchar 59\relax\quad{\mathbf{Prob}(\xi_{k}=0)=1-p}.

If
{\xi_{k}=0}
, all local devices at iteration
k
, perform one local gradient step. Otherwise (if
{\xi_{k}=1}
), all local devices perform an aggregation step. However, to perform an aggregation step, the local devices need to know the average of the local models. If the previous iteration (i.e,
{{k-1}^{\rm th}}
iteration) was an aggregation step (i.e,
{\xi_{k-1}=1}
), then at the current iteration the local devices can use the same average as the one at iteration
{k-1}
(recall, the average of the local models does not change after two consecutive aggregation steps). Otherwise, a communication happens with the master to compute the average. In this case, each local device
i
compresses its local model
x_{i}^{k}
to
\mathcal{C}_{i}(x_{i}^{k})
and communicates the result to the master. The master computes the average based on the compressed values of local models:

{\bar{y}^{k}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\dfrac{1}{n}\sum\limits_{j=1}^{n}\mathcal{C}_{j}(x_{j}^{k})\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}}

then it compresses
\bar{y}^{k}
to
\mathcal{C}_{M}(\bar{y}^{k})
by using a compression operator at the master’s end and communicates it back to the local devices. The local devices further perform an aggregation step by using
\mathcal{C}_{M}(\bar{y}^{k})
instead of the exact average. This process continues until convergence.
From Algorithm
15
, we have, for
i=1\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\ldots\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}n:

x_{i}^{k+1}=x_{i}^{k}-\eta G_{i}(x^{k})\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}

where

{G_{i}(x^{k})=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lll}\dfrac{\nabla f_{i}\left(x_{i}^{k}\right)}{n(1-p)}&\text{ if }\xi_{k}=0\\
\dfrac{\lambda}{np}\left(x_{i}^{k}-\mathcal{C}_{M}(\bar{y}^{k})\right)&\text{ if }\xi_{k}=1~\&~\xi_{k-1}=0\mathchar 59\relax\\
\dfrac{\lambda}{np}\left(x_{i}^{k}-\bar{x}^{k}\right)&\text{ if }\xi_{k}=1~\&~\xi_{k-1}=1.\\
\end{array}\right.}

For the pseudocode of
Compressed L2GD
, see Algorithm
15
.

6.4
Convergence Analysis

With the above setup, we now present the convergence results of Algorithm
15
. Detailed proofs can be found in Section
A6
.

6.4.1
Assumptions

We make the following general assumptions in this chapter.

Assumption 14
.

For
i=1\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\ldots\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}n
:

•

The compression operator,
{\mathcal{C}_{i}(\cdot):\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}}
is unbiased,

\mathbf{E}_{\mathcal{C}_{i}}\left[\mathcal{C}_{i}(x)\right]=x\mathchar 59\relax\quad\forall x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}.

•

There exists constant,
\omega_{i}>0
such that the variance of
\mathcal{C}_{i}
is bounded as follows:

{\mathbf{E}_{\mathcal{C}_{i}}\left[\|\mathcal{C}_{i}(x)-x\|^{2}\right]\leq\omega_{i}\|x\|^{2}\mathchar 59\relax\forall x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}.}

•

The operators,
\{\mathcal{C}_{i}(\cdot)\}_{i=1}^{n}
are independent from each other, and independent from
\xi_{k}
, for all
k\geq 0
.

•

The compression operator,
\mathcal{C}_{M}(\cdot)
is unbiased, independent from
\{\mathcal{C}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
and has compression factor,
\omega_{M}
.

From the above assumption we conclude that for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we have

{\mathbf{E}_{\mathcal{C}_{i}}\left[\|\mathcal{C}_{i}(x)\|^{2}\right]\leq(1+\omega_{i})\|x\|^{2}.}

The following Lemma characterizes the compression factor,
\omega
of the joint compression operator,
{\mathcal{C}(\cdot)=\left(\mathcal{C}_{1}(\cdot)^{\top}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\ldots\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\mathcal{C}_{n}(\cdot)^{\top}\right)^{\top}}
as a function of
\omega_{1}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\ldots\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\omega_{n}
.

Lemma 21
.

Let
x\in R^{nd}
, then

{\mathbf{E}_{\mathcal{C}}\left[\|\mathcal{C}(x)\|^{2}\right]\leq(1+\omega)\|x\|^{2}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}}

where
{\omega=\max_{i=1\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\ldots\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}n}\{\omega_{i}\}.}

Assumption 15
.

We assume that
f
is
L_{f}
-smooth and
\mu
-strongly convex.

6.4.2
Auxiliary results

Before we state our main convergence theorem, we state several intermediate results needed for the convergence. In the following two Lemmas, we show that based on the randomness of the compression operators, in expectation, we recover the exact average of the local models and the exact gradients for all iterations.

Lemma 22
.

Let Assumption
14
hold, then for all
{k\geq 0}
,

{\mathbf{E}_{\mathcal{C}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\mathcal{C}_{M}}\left[\mathcal{C}_{M}(\bar{y}^{k})\right]=\bar{x}^{k}.}

Lemma 23
.

Let Assumptions
14
hold. Then for all
k\geq 0
, knowing
x^{k}
,

G(x^{k})
is an unbiased estimator of the gradient of function
F
at
x^{k}
.

Our next Lemma
24
gives an upper bound on the iterate at each iteration. This bound is composed of two terms—the optimality gap,
F(x^{k})-F(x^{*})
, and the norm at the optimal point,
x^{*}
.

Lemma 24
.

Let Assumption
15
hold, then

{\left\|x^{k}\right\|^{2}\leq\dfrac{4}{\mu}\left(F(x^{k})-F(x^{*})\right)+2\left\|x^{*}\right\|^{2}.}

Our next Lemma
25
helps us to prove the expected smoothness property
(
Gower et al. 2019
)
. The bound in Lemma
25
is composed of—the optimality gap, the difference between the gradients of
h
at
x^{k}
and
x^{*}
, and an extra constant,
\beta
that depends on the used compressors.

(a)

(b)

(c)

(d)

Figure 6.3
:
Uncompressed
L2GD
on
n=5
workers. We show the loss,
f
as a function of
p
and
\lambda
obtained after
K=100
iterations of Algorithm
15
with
\mathcal{C}
an identity compressor. (a)
A1A
dataset,
d=124\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\lambda=10
, (b)
A2A
dataset,
d=124\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\lambda=10
, (c)
A1A
dataset,
d=124\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}p=0.65
(d)
A2A
dataset,
d=124\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}p=0.65
.

Lemma 25
.

Let Assumptions
14
and
15
hold. Then

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}_{\mathcal{C}_{M}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\mathcal{C}}\left\|x^{k}-\mathbf{Q}\mathcal{C}_{M}(\bar{y}^{k})-x^{*}+\mathbf{Q}\mathcal{C}_{M}(\bar{y}^{*})\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\dfrac{4n^{2}}{\lambda^{2}}\left\|\nabla h(x^{k})-\nabla h(x^{*})\right\|^{2}+\alpha\left(F(x^{k})-F(x^{*})\right)+\beta\mathchar 59\relax

where and

\displaystyle\bar{y}^{*}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\dfrac{1}{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\mathcal{C}_{j}(x_{j}^{*})\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}

\displaystyle\alpha

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\dfrac{4\left(4\omega+4\omega_{M}(1+\omega)\right)}{\mu}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}

\displaystyle\beta

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}

\displaystyle 2\left(4\omega+4\omega_{M}(1+\omega)\right)\left\|{x}^{*}\right\|^{2}+4\mathbf{E}_{\mathcal{C}_{M}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\mathcal{C}}\left\|\mathbf{Q}\mathcal{C}_{M}(\bar{y}^{*})-\mathbf{Q}\bar{x}^{*}\right\|^{2}.

Next Lemma
26
is the final result that (together with Lemma
25
) shows the expected smoothness property and gives an upper bound on the stochastic gradient. This bound is composed of three terms:

1.

The optimality gap,
F(x^{k})-F(x^{*})
.

2.

The expected norm of the stochastic gradient at the optimal point,
\mathbf{E}\|G(x^{*})\|^{2}
.

3.

Some other quantity that involves interplay between the parameters used in Algorithm
15
and the used compressor.

Lemma 26

(Expected Smoothness)
.

Let Assumptions
14
and
15
hold, then

\mathbf{E}\left[\|G(x^{k})\|^{2}|x^{k}\right]\leq 4\gamma\left(F(x^{k})-F(x^{*})\right)+\delta\mathchar 59\relax

(6.2)

where

\displaystyle\gamma

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\dfrac{\alpha\lambda^{2}(1-p)}{2n^{2}p}+\max\left\{\dfrac{L_{f}}{(1-p)}\mathchar 59\relax\dfrac{\lambda}{n}\left(1+\dfrac{4(1-p)}{p}\right)\right\}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}

\displaystyle\delta

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\dfrac{2\beta\lambda^{2}(1-p)}{n^{2}p}+2\mathbf{E}\|G(x^{*})\|^{2}.

Remark 27
.

If there is no compression, the operators,
\mathcal{C}_{i}(\cdot)
, for
i\in[n]
, and
\mathcal{C}_{M}(\cdot)
are equal to identity. The compression constants,
\omega_{i}
, for
i\in[n]
, and
\omega_{M}
are equal to zero. Therefore,
{\alpha=\beta=0}
(Lemma
25
), and the factor
4
in the formula of
\gamma
(Lemma
26
) can be replaced by
1
and thus

\displaystyle\gamma

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\max\left\{\dfrac{L_{f}}{(1-p)}\mathchar 59\relax\dfrac{\lambda}{n}\left(1+\dfrac{(1-p)}{p}\right)\right\}=\max\left\{\dfrac{L}{n(1-p)}\mathchar 59\relax\dfrac{\lambda}{np}\right\}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}

\displaystyle\delta

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\dfrac{2\beta\lambda^{2}(1-p)}{n^{2}p}+2\mathbf{E}\|G(x^{*})\|^{2}=2\mathbf{E}\|G(x^{*})\|^{2}.

where
L=nL_{f}
. Same constants arise in the expected smoothness property in
Hanzely and Richtárik 2020
.

For nonconvex convergence of our algorithm, we cannot use the expected smoothness of Lemma
26
, as it requires
\mu
-strong convexity of the loss function. Therefore, similar to
(
Sahu et al. 2021
)
we require a new assumption to bound the stochastic gradient,
G(x^{k})
. Let
G(x^{k})
is of the form:

G(x^{k})=\nabla F(x^{k})+\zeta_{k}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}

where
\zeta_{k}
is the stochastic noise on the gradient.

Assumption 16
.

There exist
M\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\sigma^{2}\geq 0
, such that

\mathbf{E}[\|G(x^{k})\|^{2}\mid x^{k}]\leq M\|\nabla F(x^{k})\|^{2}+\sigma^{2}.

6.4.3
Main result

We now state the convergence result for Algorithm
15
for both strongly convex and non-convex cases.

Theorem 28
.

(Strongly convex case) Let Assumptions
14
and
15
hold.
If
{\eta\leq\dfrac{1}{2\gamma}}
, then

{\mathbf{E}\left\|x^{k}-x^{*}\right\|^{2}\leq\left(1-\dfrac{\eta\mu}{n}\right)^{k}\left\|x^{0}-x^{*}\right\|^{2}+\dfrac{n\eta\delta}{\mu}.}

Proof.

The proof follows directly from Lemma
23
,
26
, and Theorem 3.1 from
Gower et al. 2019
.
∎

Theorem 29
.

(Non convex case) Let Assumptions
14
and
16
hold. Assume also that
F
is
L_{f}
-smooth, bounded from below by
F(x^{*})
. Then to reach a precision,
\textstyle{\epsilon>0}
, set the step size,

\textstyle{\eta=\min\{\frac{1}{{L_{f}M}}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\frac{\epsilon^{2}}{2L_{f}\sigma^{2}}\}}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}

such that for

\textstyle{K\geq\frac{4L_{f}M(F(x^{0})-F(x^{*}))}{\epsilon^{2}}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}}

we have

\min_{k=0\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}1\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\dots\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}K}\mathbf{E}\|\nabla F(x^{k})\|_{2}\leq\epsilon.

Remark 30
.

For smooth non-convex problems, we recover the optimal
O(\epsilon^{4})
classical rate as vanilla
SGD
in a non-convex smooth case.

6.5
Optimal Rate and Communication

In this section, we provide the “optimal" setting of our algorithm that is obtained by optimizing the complexity bounds of our algorithm as a function of the parameters involved. The analysis in this section is based on the following upper bound of
\gamma
. We recall from Lemma
26
that

\displaystyle\gamma

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\dfrac{\alpha\lambda^{2}(1-p)}{2n^{2}p}+\max\left\{\dfrac{L_{f}}{(1-p)}\mathchar 59\relax\dfrac{\lambda}{n}\left(1+\dfrac{4(1-p)}{p}\right)\right\}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\dfrac{\alpha\lambda^{2}(1-p)}{2n^{2}p}+\max\left\{\dfrac{L_{f}}{(1-p)}\mathchar 59\relax\dfrac{4\lambda}{np}\right\}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\gamma_{u}.

Note that the number of iterations is linearly dependent on
\gamma
. Therefore, to minimize the total number of iterations, it suffices to minimize
\gamma
. Define
L\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}nL_{f}
.

Theorem 31

(Optimal rate)
.

The probability
p^{*}
minimizing
\gamma
is equal to

\max\{p_{e}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}p_{A}\}
, where
p_{e}=\dfrac{7\lambda+L-\sqrt{\lambda^{2}+14\lambda L+L^{2}}}{6\lambda}
and
p_{A}
is the optimizer of the function
A(p)=\dfrac{\alpha\lambda^{2}}{2n^{2}p}+\dfrac{L}{n(1-p)}
in
(0\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}1)
.

Remark 32
.

If we maximize the upper bound,
\gamma_{u}
instead of
\gamma
then
p_{e}
simplifies to
\dfrac{4\lambda}{L+4\lambda}
.

Lemma 27
.

The optimizer probability
p_{A}
of the function
A(p)=\dfrac{\alpha\lambda^{2}}{2n^{2}p}+\dfrac{L}{n(1-p)}
in
(0\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}1)
is equal to

p_{A}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lll}\dfrac{1}{2}&\text{ if }2nL=\alpha\lambda^{2}\\
\dfrac{-2\alpha\lambda^{2}+2\lambda\sqrt{2\alpha nL}}{2(2nL-\alpha\lambda^{2})}&\text{ if }2nL>\alpha\lambda^{2}\\
\dfrac{-2\alpha\lambda^{2}-2\lambda\sqrt{2\alpha nL}}{2(2nL-\alpha\lambda^{2})}&\text{otherwise.}\end{array}\right.

Note that the number of communication rounds is linearly proportional to
C\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}p(1-p)\gamma
. Therefore, minimizing the total number of communication rounds suffices to minimize
C
or
nC
.

Theorem 33

(Optimal communication)
.

The probability
p^{*}
optimizing
C
is equal to

\max\{p_{e}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}p_{A}\}
, where
p_{e}=\dfrac{7\lambda+L-\sqrt{\lambda^{2}+14\lambda L+L^{2}}}{6\lambda}
and
p_{A}=1-\dfrac{Ln}{\alpha\lambda^{2}}
.

Remark 34
.

As in Remark
32
, we note that, if we use the upper bound,
\gamma_{u}
instead of
\gamma
then
p_{e}
simplifies to
\dfrac{4\lambda}{L+4\lambda}
.

We note that
\lambda\to 0
implies
p^{*}\to 0
. This means that the optimal strategy, in this case, is
no communication at all
. This result is intuitive since for
\lambda=0
, we deal with pure local models which can be computed without any communication. As
\lambda\to\infty
implies
p^{*}\to 1
denoting that the optimal strategy is to communicate often to find the global model.

6.6
Experiments

We conducted diverse numerical experiments with
L2GD
algorithm that includes: (
i
) Analysis of algorithm meta-parameters for
logistic regression
in strongly convex setting; see Section
6.6.1
; (
ii
) analysis of
Compressed L2GD
algorithm on image classification with DNNs; see Section
6.6.2
.

Computing environment.
We performed experiments on server-grade machines running Ubuntu 18.04 and Linux Kernel v5.4.0, equipped with 8-cores
3.3
GHz Intel Xeon and a single NVIDIA GeForce RTX 2080 Ti.Tesla-V100-SXM2 GPU with 32GB of GPU memory.

The computation backend for
logistic regression
experiments was

NumPy
library with leveraging

MPI4PY
for inter-node communication. For DNNs, we used a recent version of

FedML

(
He et al. 2020
)
benchmark
3
3

3

FedML.AI
and patched it with: (
i
) distributed and standalone version of Algorithm
15
; (
ii
) serializing and plotting mechanism; (
iii
) modifications in standalone, distributed version of
FedAVG

(
McMahan et al. 2017
)
and
FedOpt

(
Reddi et al. 2020
)
to be consistent with (
6.1
); (
iv
) not to drop the last batch while processing the dataset.

6.6.1
Meta-parameter study

The purpose of these experiments is to study the meta-parameters involved in the uncompressed
L2GD
algorithm. We used
L2GD
algorithm without compression for solving
\ell_{2}
regularized
logistic regression
on LIBSVM
A1A
and
A2A
datasets
(
Chang and Lin 2011b
)
. Both datasets contain shuffled examples in the train set, and we did not perform any extra shuffling. To simulate the FL settings, we divided both datasets into
5
parts. After splitting, each worker has
321
and
453
records for
A1A
, and
A2A
, respectively.

Setup and results.

We define
f_{i}(x)
to be local empirical risk minimization for logistic loss with additive regularization term for local data
D_{i}
and of the form:

f_{i}(x)=\dfrac{1}{n_{i}}\sum_{j=1}^{n_{i}}\log(1+\exp(-b^{(j)}x^{\top}a^{(j)}))+\dfrac{L_{2}}{2}\|x\|^{2}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}

where
a^{(j)}\in\mathbb{R}^{124}\mathchar 59\relax b^{(j)}\in\{+1\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}-1\}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}n_{i}=|D_{i}|
.
We set
L_{2}=0.01
, and varied meta-parameters
p
and
\lambda
. For each parameter, we performed
100
iterations of Algorithm
15
. Note that, as meta-parameter
\lambda
decreases the models will fit more to its local data, while
p
provides a stochastic balance between local gradient steps with probability,
1-p
and aggregation with probability,
p
.

Table 6.2:
Summary of the benchmarks. The measured quantity is
{\rm bits}/n
to achieve
0.7

Top1
test accuracy, with
n=10
clients. For
DenseNet-121
,
MobileNet
,
ResNet-18
the baseline is
FedAVG
with
Natural
compressor with
1
local epoch;
L2GD
also uses
Natural
compressor.
