:
The
FedAVG
as a particular case of
L2GD
: Test and train accuracy for
ResNet-56
on
CIFAR-10
.

Figure 6.8
:
The
FedAVG
as a particular case of
L2GD
: Test and train loss for
ResNet-56
on
CIFAR-10
.

Takeaway message.
The results in Figure
6.3
support the theoretical finding—there exists an optimal choice of
(p\mathchar 59\relax\lambda
), where the loss function,
f
achieves the least value. Nevertheless, this choice is problem-dependent. Additionally, we find small
p
is not good due to the lack of samples in a single node compared to samples available at other nodes. There is a trade-off for each node in learning from the other nodes’ data and spending time to learn from its own data. In the experiments, the “optimal" setting of our algorithm is attained for
p=0.4
and
\lambda
in
[0\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}25]
.
Finally, we observe that
to get the smallest errors on the training and validation sets, it is better not to perform the averaging step too often
.

6.6.2
Training DNN models

We choose three practically important DNN models used for image classification, and other down-streaming tasks, such as feature extractions for image segmentation, object detection, image embedding, and image captioning.

•

ResNet-18

(
He et al. 2016
)
. The overwhelmingly popular
ResNet
architecture exploits residual connections to remedy vanishing gradients. The network supported the trend toward smaller filters and deeper architectures. Additionally, we use
ResNet-56

(
He et al. 2016
)
.

•

DenseNet

(
Huang et al. 2017
)
contains a short connection between layers via connecting each layer to every other layer in a feed-forward fashion. A dense connection allows propagating information to the final classifier via concatenating all feature maps. Each layer in
DenseNet
is narrow and contains only 12 filters—another practical model for FL training.

•

MobileNet

(
Howard et al. 2017
)
. DNN architecture has a trade-off between computational complexity and accuracy
(
Bianco et al. 2018
, p.3, Figure 1)
. For mobile devices that appear in cross-device FL, the computation cost and energy consumption are both important. The energy consumption is mostly driven by memory movement
(
Chen et al. 2018b
;
Horowitz 2014
)
. In
MobileNet
architecture standard convolution blocks perform depth-wise convolution followed by
1\times 1
convolution. This is computationally less expensive in flops during inference time (see
(
Bianco et al. 2018
, Figure 1, p.3)
) and is
\sim 3.5\times
more power efficient compared to
DenseNet

(
García-Martín et al. 2019
, p.85, Table 7)
. This makes
MobileNet
an attractive model for FL training.

Dataset and Setup.

We consider
CIFAR-10
dataset
(
Krizhevsky and Hinton 2009
)
for image classification. It contains color images of resolution
28\times 28
from 10 classes. The training and the test set are of size,
5\times 10^{4}
and
10^{4}
, respectively. The training set is partitioned heterogeneously across
10
clients. The proportion of samples of each class stored at each local node is drawn by using the Dirichlet distribution (
\alpha=0.5
). In our experiments, all clients are involved in each communication round. Additionally, we added a linear head in all CNN models for
CIFAR-10
, as they were originally designed for classification tasks with
1000
output classes.

Loss function.
Denote

{f_{i}(x)=w_{i}\cdot\dfrac{1}{|D_{i}|}\sum_{(a_{i}\mathchar 59\relax b_{i})\in D_{i}}l(a_{i}\mathchar 59\relax b_{i}\mathchar 59\relax x)}

to be a weighted local empirical risk associated with the local data,
\mathcal{D}_{i}
stored in node,
i
. We note that
l(a_{i}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}b_{i}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}x)
is a standard unweighted cross-entropy loss,
a_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{28\times 28\times 3}
,
b_{i}\in\{0\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}1\}^{10}
with only one component equal to
1
, the ground truth value, and the weight is set to
w_{i}={|D_{i}|}/{|D_{1}\cup\dots\cup D_{n}|}
.

Metrics.
To measure the performance, we examine the loss function value,
f(x)
, and the Top-1 accuracy of the global model on both the train and the test set. Additionally, we measure the number of rounds and
{\rm bits}/n
—communicated bits normalized by the number of local clients,
n
. The intuition behind using the last metric is to measure the communicated data volume; it is widely
hypothesized
that the reduced data volume translates to faster training in a constant speed network in distributed setup
(
Gajjala et al. 2020
;
Xu et al. 2021a
)
.

Compressors used.
The theoretical results of compressed
L2GD
are attributed to unbiased compressors. We used 4 different unbiased compressors at the clients:
Bernoulli

(
Khirirat et al. 2018a
)
,
Natural
compressor
(
Horváth et al. 2019
)
, random dithering a.k.a.
QSGD

(
Alistarh et al. 2017
)
, and
Terngrad

(
Wen et al. 2017
)
; see Table
6.1
for details. Additionally, we note that biased compressors (mostly sparsifiers) are popular in DNN training.
Therefore, out of scientific curiosity, we used a popular sparsifier:
TopK

(
Aji and Heafield 2017
;
Sahu et al. 2021
)
as a proof of concept. We note that extending
Compressed L2GD
theory for biased compressors (with or without error-feedback
(
Xu et al. 2021a
)
) is nontrivial and mathematically involved, and left for future work.

Algorithms used for comparison.
We used state-of-the-art FL training algorithms,
FedAVG

(
McMahan et al. 2017
)
and
FedOpt

(
Reddi et al. 2020
)
as no compression baseline to compare against our
Compressed L2GD
. However, the performance of
FedAVG
is not stable but improves with the compression mechanism. The original
FedAVG
algorithm does not contain any compression mechanism, but for comparison, we incorporated compressors into
FedAVG
via the following schema which is similar to the classic error feedback
Xu et al. 2021a
: (
i
) After local steps, client estimates change of current iterate from the previous round and formulates direction,
{g_{c\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\mathrm{computed}}}^{i}
; (
ii
) client sends compressed difference between previous gradient estimator from previous round and currently computed gradient estimator,
\mathcal{C}({g_{c\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\mathrm{computed}}}^{i}-{g_{c}}^{i-1})
to the master; (
iii
) both master and client updating
{g_{c}}^{i}
via the following schema:
{g_{c}}^{i}={g_{c}}^{i-1}+\mathcal{C}({g_{c\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\mathrm{computed}}}^{i}-{g_{c}}^{i-1})
. We provide the details about step size and batch size selection in the Appendix
D6
.

Results

We show the results for training
ResNet-18
,
DenseNet-121
, and
MobileNet
with
Compressed L2GD
and other state-of-the-art FL algorithms in Figures
6.4
,
6.5
,
6.6
. For these experiments, the communication rounds are set to
12\times 10^{3}
,
25\times 10^{3}
, and
20\times 10^{3}
, respectively. For
FedAVG
algorithm, each client performs one epoch over the local data. We empirically tried
1\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}2\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}3\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}
and
4
epochs over the local data as local steps, but one epoch is empirically provided the best results.

For training
ResNet-18
, from Figure
6.4
we observe that
FedAVG
with compression has albeit better convergence than no compression
FedAVG

4
4

4

We have observed that batch normalization
(
Ioffe and Szegedy 2015
)
in
ResNet
is sensitive for aggregation; see our discussion in Section
D6
.
. At the same time, compressed
FedAVG
affects the convergence as a function of communicated rounds only negligibly (see Figure
6.4
(b,d)). Therefore, for training other DNN models we use
FedAVG
with compression and
FedOpt
without any compressors to enjoy the best of both baselines.

Take away message.
The
Compressed L2GD
with
Natural
compressor sends the least data and drives the loss down the most in these experiments. At the same time,
L2GD
with
Natural
compressor (by design it has a smaller variance) reaches the best accuracy for both train and test sets.
Compressed L2GD
outperforms
FedAVG
by a huge margin—For all DNN experiments, to reach the desired
Top1
test accuracy,
Compressed L2GD
reduces the communicated data-volume,
\mathrm{\#bits/n}
, from
10^{15}
to
10^{11}
, rendering approximately a
10^{4}
times improvement compared to
FedAVG
; see Table
6.2
.

Interestingly, in training
MobileNet
, the performance of biased
TopK
compressor degrades only about 10% compared to
Natural
compressor, while approximately degrades 35% in training
DenseNet
. See discussion in Section
D6
, Figures
6.9
,
6.10
,
6.11
. These phenomena may lead the researchers to design unbiased compressors with smaller variance to empirically harvest the best behavior of
Compressed L2GD
.

Nevertheless, we also observe that
Compressed L2GD
converges slower compared to other FL algorithms without compression in all cases. What follows, it can be argued, is that when we compare the communicated data volume for all DNN models, the convergence of
Compressed L2GD
is much better. Additionally, the gain in terms of lowering the loss function value is significant.

By sending the same amount of data,
L2GD
lowers the loss the most compared to the other no-compression FL baseline algorithms.

These experiments also demonstrate that when communication is a bottleneck,
FedAVG
is not comparable with
L2GD
. The only comparable baseline for
L2GD
is
FedOpt
. Also, see discussion in Section
D6
, and Figures
6.9
,
6.10
,
6.11
. A similar observation holds for the
Top1
test and train accuracy. Taken together, these indicate that for training larger DNN models in a personalized FL setting, with resource-constrained and geographically remote devices,
Compressed L2GD
could be the preferred algorithm because its probabilistic communication protocol sends less data but obtains better test accuracy than no compression
FedAVG
and
FedOpt
.

Additionally, we observe that when
\nicefrac{{\eta\lambda}}{{np}}\in[0.5\mathchar 59\relax 0.95]\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}

Compressed L2GD
incurs a significant variance in objective function during training. Empirically, the best behavior was observed for
\nicefrac{{\eta\lambda}}{{np}}\approx 1
or
\nicefrac{{\eta\lambda}}{{np}}\in(0\mathchar 59\relax 0.17]
.

FedAVG as a particular case of L2GD.

We note that if

\dfrac{\eta\lambda}{np}=1\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}

then the aggregation step of Algorithm
15
reduces to
x_{i}^{k+1}=\bar{x}^{k}
, for all devices. Thus, in this regime
L2GD
works similarly as
FedAVG
with a random number of local steps. E.g., if
p=0.5
, then Algorithm
15
reduces to the randomized version of
FedAVG
with an average of
3
local steps. Figures
6.7
and
6.8
, confirm this observation numerically, where we see that both algorithms exhibit similar performance. In that experiment we trained
ResNet-56
on
CIFAR-10
with
n=100
workers, and
600
rounds. For
L2GD
in this experiment, we set
\nicefrac{{\eta\lambda}}{{pn}}=1
.

6.7
Conclusions

In this work, we equipped the loopless gradient descent (
L2GD
) algorithm with a compression mechanism to reduce the communication bottleneck between local devices and the server in an FL context. We showed that the new algorithm enjoys similar convergence properties as the uncompressed
L2GD
with a natural increase in the stochastic gradient variance due to compression. This phenomenon is similar to classical convergence bounds for compressed
SGD
algorithms. We also show that in a personalized FL setting, there is a trade-off that must be considered by devices between learning from other devices’ data and spending time learning from their own data. However, a particular parameterization of our algorithm recovers the well-known
FedAVG
Algorithm. We assessed the performance of the new algorithm compared to the state-of-the-art and validated our theoretical insights through a large set of experiments.

Appendices to Chapter
6

A6
Overview of Convergence Analysis Results

In the Appendix, we provide the proofs of both the convex and non-convex convergence results for
Compressed L2GD
algorithm. In Section
B6
, we provide the technical Lemmas necessary for the analyses. The Section
C6
contains the auxiliary results pertaining to both convex and non-convex convergence. In Section
C6.1
we provide the non-convex convergence results, and Section
C6.2
provides the proofs for optimal rate and communication.

B6
Technical Results Used for Convergence

The following two Lemmas are instrumental in proving other compression-related results.

Lemma
.

Let
x\in R^{nd}
, then

{\mathbf{E}_{\mathcal{C}}\left[\|\mathcal{C}(x)\|^{2}\right]\leq(1+\omega)\|x\|^{2}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}}

where
{\omega=\max_{i=1\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\ldots\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}n}\{\omega_{i}\}.}

Proof.

By using Assumption 1, we have

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}_{\mathcal{C}}\left[\|\mathcal{C}(x)\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}_{\mathcal{C}}\left[\sum_{i=1}^{n}\|\mathcal{C}_{i}(x_{i})\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{E}_{\mathcal{C}_{i}}\|\mathcal{C}_{i}(x_{i})\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}(1+\omega_{i})\|x_{i}\|^{2}\leq(1+\omega)\|x\|^{2}.

Hence the result.
∎

Lemma
.

Let Assumption
14
hold, then for all
{k\geq 0}
,

{\mathbf{E}_{\mathcal{C}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\mathcal{C}_{M}}\left[\mathcal{C}_{M}(\bar{y}^{k})\right]=\bar{x}^{k}.}

Proof.

We have

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}_{\mathcal{C}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\mathcal{C}_{M}}\left[\mathcal{C}_{M}(\bar{y}^{k})\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}_{\mathcal{C}}\left[\mathbf{E}_{\mathcal{C}_{M}}\left[\mathcal{C}_{M}(\bar{y}^{k})\right]\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}_{\mathcal{C}}\left[\dfrac{1}{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\mathcal{C}_{j}(x_{j}^{k})\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\dfrac{1}{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\mathbf{E}_{\mathcal{C}_{j}}\left[\mathcal{C}_{j}(x_{j}^{k})\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bar{x}^{k}.

Hence the result.

∎

In the following Lemma, we show that based on the randomness of the compression operators, in expectation, we recover the exact average of the local models and the exact gradient for all iterations.

Lemma
.

Let Assumptions
14
hold. Then for all
k\geq 0
, knowing
x^{k}
,

G(x^{k})
is an unbiased estimator of the gradient of function
F
at
x^{k}
.

Proof.

We have

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}_{\mathcal{C}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\mathcal{C}_{M}}\left[G_{i}(x^{k})\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{lll}\dfrac{\nabla f_{i}\left(x_{i}^{k}\right)}{n(1-p)}&\text{ if }\xi_{k}=0\\
\dfrac{\lambda}{np}\left(x_{i}^{k}-\mathbf{E}_{C\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\mathcal{C}_{M}}\left[\mathcal{C}_{M}(\bar{y}^{k})\right]\right)&\text{ if }\xi_{k}=1~\&~\xi_{k-1}=0\mathchar 59\relax\\
\dfrac{\lambda}{np}\left(x_{i}^{k}-\bar{x}^{k}\right)&\text{ if }\xi_{k}=1~\&~\xi_{k-1}=1\mathchar 59\relax\\
\end{array}\right.

\displaystyle\overset{{\rm By~Lemma}~\ref{ch6:lem:compmean}}{=}

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\dfrac{\nabla f_{i}(x_{i}^{k})}{n(1-p)}&\text{ if }\xi_{k}=0\mathchar 59\relax\\
\dfrac{\lambda}{np}\left(x_{i}^{k}-\bar{x}^{k}\right)&\text{ if }\xi_{k}=1.\end{array}\right.

Therefore,

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}[G_{i}(x^{k})|x^{k}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}_{\xi_{k}}\left[\mathbf{E}_{C\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\mathcal{C}_{M}}\left[G_{i}(x^{k})\right]\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(1-p)\dfrac{\nabla f_{i}(x_{i}^{k})}{n(1-p)}+p\dfrac{\lambda}{np}\left(x_{i}^{k}-\bar{x}^{k}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\nabla_{x_{i}}f(x^{k})+\nabla_{x_{i}}h\left(x^{k}\right)=\nabla_{x_{i}}F(x^{k}).

Hence the result.
∎

C6
Main Convergence Results

Based on the results given in the previous section, we are now set to quote our key convergence results. Our next Lemma gives an upper bound on the iterate at each iteration. This bound is composed of two terms—the optimality gap,
F(x^{k})-F(x^{*})
, and the norm of the optimal point,
\|x^{*}\|
.

Lemma
.

Let Assumption
15
hold, then

{\left\|x^{k}\right\|^{2}\leq\dfrac{4}{\mu}\left(F(x^{k})-F(x^{*})\right)+2\left\|x^{*}\right\|^{2}.}

Proof.

We have

\displaystyle\left\|x^{k}\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\overset{\|a+b\|^{2}\leq 2\|a\|^{2}+2\|b\|^{2}}{\leq}

\displaystyle 2\left\|x^{k}-x^{*}\right\|^{2}+2\left\|x^{*}\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\dfrac{4}{\mu}\left(F(x^{k})-F(x^{*})\right)+2\left\|x^{*}\right\|^{2}.

Hence the result.
∎

Recall that, inspired by the expected smoothness property
Gower et al. 2019
, we use a similar idea in our convergence proofs. The next Lemma is a technical Lemma that helps us to prove the expected smoothness property
(
Gower et al. 2019
)
. The bound in Lemma
25
is composed of the:

1.

Optimality gap,
F(x^{k})-F(x^{*})
.

2.

The difference between the gradients of
h
at
x^{k}
and
x^{*}
,
\left\|\nabla h(x^{k})-\nabla h(x^{*})\right\|
.

3.

An extra constant,
\beta
, which depends on the used compressors.

Lemma
.

Let Assumptions
14
and
15
hold, then

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}_{\mathcal{C}_{M}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\mathcal{C}}\left\|x^{k}-\mathbf{Q}\mathcal{C}_{M}(\bar{y}^{k})-x^{*}+\mathbf{Q}\mathcal{C}_{M}(\bar{y}^{*})\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\dfrac{4n^{2}}{\lambda^{2}}\left\|\nabla h(x^{k})-\nabla h(x^{*})\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad+\alpha\left(F(x^{k})-F(x^{*})\right)

\displaystyle\qquad+\beta\mathchar 59\relax

where

\displaystyle\bar{y}^{*}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\dfrac{1}{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\mathcal{C}_{j}(x_{j}^{*})\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}

\displaystyle\alpha

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\dfrac{4\left(4\omega+4\omega_{M}(1+\omega)\right)}{\mu}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}

\displaystyle\beta

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}

\displaystyle 2\left(4\omega+4\omega_{M}(1+\omega)\right)\left\|{x}^{*}\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad+4\mathbf{E}_{\mathcal{C}_{M}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\mathcal{C}}\left\|\mathbf{Q}\mathcal{C}_{M}(\bar{y}^{*})-\mathbf{Q}\bar{x}^{*}\right\|^{2}.

Proof.

We have

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}_{\mathcal{C}_{M}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\mathcal{C}}\left\|x^{k}-\mathbf{Q}\bar{x}^{k}+\mathbf{Q}\bar{x}^{k}-\mathbf{Q}\mathcal{C}_{M}(\bar{y}^{k})-x^{*}+\mathbf{Q}\bar{x}^{*}-\mathbf{Q}\bar{x}^{*}+\mathbf{Q}\mathcal{C}_{M}(\bar{y}^{*})\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}_{\mathcal{C}_{M}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\mathcal{C}}\left\|\left(x^{k}-\mathbf{Q}\bar{x}^{k}-x^{*}+\mathbf{Q}\bar{x}^{*}\right)\right.

\displaystyle\left.\qquad\qquad+\left(\mathbf{Q}\bar{x}^{k}-\mathbf{Q}\bar{y}^{k}\right)+\left(\mathbf{Q}\bar{y}^{k}-\mathbf{Q}\mathcal{C}_{M}(\bar{y}^{k})\right)\right.

\displaystyle\left.\qquad\qquad+\left(\mathbf{Q}\mathcal{C}_{M}(\bar{y}^{*})-\mathbf{Q}\bar{x}^{*}\right)\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 4\left\|x^{k}-\mathbf{Q}\bar{x}^{k}-x^{*}+\mathbf{Q}\bar{x}^{*}\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad+4\mathbf{E}_{\mathcal{C}}\left\|\mathbf{Q}\bar{x}^{k}-\mathbf{Q}\bar{y}^{k}\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad+4\mathbf{E}_{\mathcal{C}_{M}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\mathcal{C}}\left\|\mathbf{Q}\bar{y}^{k}-\mathbf{Q}\mathcal{C}_{M}(\bar{y}^{k})\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad+4\mathbf{E}_{\mathcal{C}_{M}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\mathcal{C}}\left\|\mathbf{Q}\mathcal{C}_{M}(\bar{y}^{*})-\mathbf{Q}\bar{x}^{*}\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 4\left\|x^{k}-\mathbf{Q}\bar{x}^{k}-x^{*}+\mathbf{Q}\bar{x}^{*}\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad+4n\mathbf{E}_{\mathcal{C}}\left\|\bar{x}^{k}-\bar{y}^{k}\right\|^{2}+4n\mathbf{E}_{\mathcal{C}_{M}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\mathcal{C}}\left\|\bar{y}^{k}-\mathcal{C}_{M}(\bar{y}^{k})\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad+4\mathbf{E}_{\mathcal{C}_{M}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\mathcal{C}}\left\|\mathbf{Q}\mathcal{C}_{M}(\bar{y}^{*})-\mathbf{Q}\bar{x}^{*}\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 4\left\|x^{k}-\mathbf{Q}\bar{x}^{k}-x^{*}+\mathbf{Q}\bar{x}^{*}\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad+4\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{E}_{\mathcal{C}}\left\|x_{i}^{k}-\mathcal{C}_{i}(x_{i}^{k})\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad+4n\omega_{M}\mathbf{E}_{\mathcal{C}_{M}}\left\|\bar{y}^{k}\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad+4\mathbf{E}_{\mathcal{C}_{M}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\mathcal{C}}\left\|\mathbf{Q}\mathcal{C}_{M}(\bar{y}^{*})-\mathbf{Q}\bar{x}^{*}\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 4\left\|x^{k}-\mathbf{Q}\bar{x}^{k}-x^{*}+\mathbf{Q}\bar{x}^{*}\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad+4\sum_{i=1}^{n}\omega_{i}\left\|x_{i}^{k}\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad+4\omega_{M}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(1+\omega_{i})\left\|x_{i}^{k}\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad+4\mathbf{E}_{\mathcal{C}_{M}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\mathcal{C}}\left\|\mathbf{Q}\mathcal{C}_{M}(\bar{y}^{*})-\mathbf{Q}\bar{x}^{*}\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 4\dfrac{n^{2}}{\lambda^{2}}\left\|\nabla h(x^{k})-\nabla h(x^{*})\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad+\left(4\omega+4\omega_{M}(1+\omega)\right)\left\|x^{k}\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad+4\mathbf{E}_{\mathcal{C}_{M}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\mathcal{C}}\left\|\mathbf{Q}\mathcal{C}_{M}(\bar{y}^{*})-\mathbf{Q}\bar{x}^{*}\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\overset{{\rm By~Lemma}~\ref{ch6:lem:xrelF}}{\leq}

\displaystyle 4\dfrac{n^{2}}{\lambda^{2}}\left\|\nabla h(x^{k})-\nabla h(x^{*})\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad+\left(4\omega+4\omega_{M}(1+\omega)\right)\left(\dfrac{4}{\mu}\left(F(x^{k})-F(x^{*})\right)+2\left\|x^{*}\right\|^{2}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad+4\mathbf{E}_{\mathcal{C}_{M}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\mathcal{C}}\left\|\mathbf{Q}\mathcal{C}_{M}(\bar{y}^{*})-\mathbf{Q}\bar{x}^{*}\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 4\dfrac{n^{2}}{\lambda^{2}}\left\|\nabla h(x^{k})-\nabla h(x^{*})\right\|^{2}+\alpha\left(F(x^{k})-F(x^{*})\right)+\beta.

Hence the result.
∎

Now we are all set to prove the expected smoothness property in our setup.

Lemma

(Expected Smoothness)
.

Let Assumptions
14
and
15
hold, then

\mathbf{E}\left[\|G(x^{k})\|^{2}|x^{k}\right]\leq 4\gamma\left(F(x^{k})-F(x^{*})\right)+\delta\mathchar 59\relax

(6.5)

where

{\gamma\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\dfrac{\alpha\lambda^{2}(1-p)}{2n^{2}p}+\max\left\{\dfrac{L_{f}}{(1-p)}\mathchar 59\relax\dfrac{\lambda}{n}\left(1+\dfrac{4(1-p)}{p}\right)\right\}}

and

\delta\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\dfrac{2\beta\lambda^{2}(1-p)}{n^{2}p}+2\mathbf{E}\|G(x^{*})\|^{2}.

Proof.

We have

\|G(x^{k})-G(x^{*})\|^{2}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lll}\dfrac{\|\nabla f\left(x^{k}\right)-\nabla f\left(x^{*}\right)\|^{2}}{(1-p)^{2}}&\text{ if }\xi_{k}=0\\
\dfrac{\lambda^{2}}{n^{2}p^{2}}\left\|x^{k}-\mathbf{Q}\mathcal{C}_{M}(\bar{y}^{k})-x^{*}+\mathbf{Q}\mathcal{C}_{M}(\bar{y}^{*})\right\|^{2}&\text{ if }\xi_{k}=1~\&~\xi_{k-1}=0\mathchar 59\relax\\
\dfrac{1}{p^{2}}\|\nabla h(x^{k})-\nabla h(x^{*})\|^{2}&\text{ if }\xi_{k}=1~\&~\xi_{k-1}=1.\\
\end{array}\right.

Finally,

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}_{\xi_{k}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\xi_{k-1}}\|G(x^{k})-G(x^{*})\|^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(1-p)\dfrac{\|\nabla f\left(x^{k}\right)-\nabla f\left(x^{*}\right)\|^{2}}{(1-p)^{2}}

\displaystyle\quad+p^{2}\dfrac{1}{p^{2}}\|\nabla h(x^{k})-\nabla h(x^{*})\|^{2}

\displaystyle\quad+p(1-p)\dfrac{\lambda^{2}}{n^{2}p^{2}}\left\|x^{k}-\mathbf{Q}\mathcal{C}_{M}(\bar{y}^{k})-x^{*}+\mathbf{Q}\mathcal{C}_{M}(\bar{y}^{*})\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\dfrac{\|\nabla f\left(x^{k}\right)-\nabla f\left(x^{*}\right)\|^{2}}{(1-p)}

\displaystyle\quad+\|\nabla h(x^{k})-\nabla h(x^{*})\|^{2}

\displaystyle\quad+\dfrac{\lambda^{2}(1-p)}{n^{2}p}\left\|x^{k}-\mathbf{Q}\mathcal{C}_{M}(\bar{y}^{k})-x^{*}+\mathbf{Q}\mathcal{C}_{M}(\bar{y}^{*})\right\|^{2}.

Therefore, by using Lemma
25
we get

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}\|G(x^{k})-G(x^{*})|x^{k}\|^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\dfrac{\|\nabla f\left(x^{k}\right)-\nabla f\left(x^{*}\right)\|^{2}}{(1-p)}

\displaystyle\quad+\|\nabla h(x^{k})-\nabla h(x^{*})\|^{2}

\displaystyle\quad+\dfrac{\lambda^{2}(1-p)}{n^{2}p}\mathcal{A}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\dfrac{\|\nabla f\left(x^{k}\right)-\nabla f\left(x^{*}\right)\|^{2}}{(1-p)}

\displaystyle\quad+\|\nabla h(x^{k})-\nabla h(x^{*})\|^{2}

\displaystyle\quad+\dfrac{\lambda^{2}(1-p)}{n^{2}p}\left(4\dfrac{n^{2}}{\lambda^{2}}\left\|\nabla h(x^{k})-\nabla h(x^{*})\right\|^{2}+\alpha\left(F(x^{k})-F(x^{*})\right)+\beta\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\dfrac{\|\nabla f\left(x^{k}\right)-\nabla f\left(x^{*}\right)\|^{2}}{(1-p)}

\displaystyle\quad+\left(1+\dfrac{4(1-p)}{p}\right)\|\nabla h(x^{k})-\nabla h(x^{*})\|^{2}

\displaystyle\quad+\dfrac{\alpha\lambda^{2}(1-p)}{n^{2}p}\left(F(x^{k})-F(x^{*})\right)+\dfrac{\beta\lambda^{2}(1-p)}{n^{2}p}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\dfrac{2L_{f}}{(1-p)}\left(f(x^{k})-f(x^{*})\right)+\dfrac{2\lambda}{n}\left(1+\dfrac{4(1-p)}{p}\right)\left(h(x^{k})-h(x^{*})\right)

\displaystyle\quad+\dfrac{\alpha\lambda^{2}(1-p)}{n^{2}p}\left(F(x^{k})-F(x^{*})\right)+\dfrac{\beta\lambda^{2}(1-p)}{n^{2}p}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 2\gamma\left(F(x^{k})-F(x^{*})\right)+\dfrac{\beta\lambda^{2}(1-p)}{n^{2}p}.

Finally, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}\|G(x^{k})|x^{k}\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 2\mathbf{E}\|G(x^{k})-G(x^{*})|x^{k}\|^{2}+2\mathbf{E}\|G(x^{*})\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 4\gamma\left(F(x^{k})-F(x^{*})\right)+\dfrac{2\beta\lambda^{2}(1-p)}{n^{2}p}+2\mathbf{E}\|G(x^{*})\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 4\gamma\left(F(x^{k})-F(x^{*})\right)+\delta.

Hence the result.
∎

Based on the above results, the convergence of Algorithm
15
for strongly convex functions follows directly from Lemmas
23
,
26
and Theorem 3.1 from
Gower et al. 2019
.

C6.1
Nonconvex convergence

Theorem 35

(Non convex case)
.

Let Assumptions
14
and
16
hold. Assume also that
F
is
L_{f}
-smooth, bounded from below by
F(x^{*})
. Then to reach a precision,
\textstyle{\epsilon>0}
, set the stepsize,

\textstyle{\eta=\min\{\frac{1}{{L_{f}M}}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\frac{\epsilon^{2}}{2L_{f}\sigma^{2}}\}}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}

such that for

\textstyle{K\geq\frac{4L_{f}M(F(x^{0})-F(x^{*}))}{\epsilon^{2}}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}}

we have

\min_{k=0\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}1\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\dots\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}K}\mathbf{E}\|\nabla F(x^{k})\|_{2}\leq\epsilon.

Proof.

From
L_{f}
-smoothness of
F
we have

\displaystyle F(x^{k+1})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle F(x^{k})-\eta_{k}{\nabla F(x^{k})}^{\top}{G(x^{k})}+\frac{L_{f}}{2}\eta_{k}^{2}\|G(x_{k})\|^{2}.

By taking the expectation in the above inequality, conditional on
x^{k}
, we get

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}{\left[F(x^{k+1})\;|\;x^{k}\right]}

\displaystyle\overset{{\rm By\;Lemma\;\ref{ch6:lem:unbiasGrad}}}{\leq}

\displaystyle F(x^{k})-\eta_{k}\|\nabla F(x^{k})\|_{2}^{2}+\frac{L_{f}\eta_{k}^{2}}{2}\mathbf{E}\left(\|G(x_{k})\|^{2}|x_{k}\right)\mathchar 59\relax

which by using Assumption
16
reduces to

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}{\left[F(x^{k+1})\;|\;x^{k}\right]}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle F(x^{k})-\eta_{k}\|\nabla F(x^{k})\|_{2}^{2}+\frac{L_{f}\eta_{k}^{2}}{2}\left(M\|\nabla F(x^{k})\|^{2}+\sigma^{2}\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle F(x^{k})-\eta_{k}\left(1-\frac{L_{f}M\eta_{k}}{2}\right)\|\nabla F(x^{k})\|_{2}^{2}+\frac{L_{f}\eta_{k}^{2}\sigma^{2}}{2}.

After rearranging, we have

\displaystyle\eta_{k}\left(1-\frac{L_{f}M\eta_{k}}{2}\right)\|\nabla F(x^{k})\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle F(x^{k})-\mathbf{E}{\left[F(x^{k+1})\;|\;x^{k}\right]}+\frac{L_{f}\eta_{k}^{2}\sigma^{2}}{2}.

Setting
\eta_{k}=\eta>0
in the above, taking expectation, using the tower property of expectation, and finally summing over the iterates
k=0\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}1\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\cdots K-1
we have

\displaystyle\eta\left(1-\frac{L_{f}M\eta}{2}\right)\sum_{k=0}^{K-1}\mathbf{E}\left[\|\nabla F(x^{k})\|_{2}^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\left(F(x^{0})-F(x^{*})\right)+\frac{KL_{f}\eta\sigma^{2}}{2}.

If
\eta\leq\frac{1}{L_{f}M}
then

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{K-1}\mathbf{E}\left[\|\nabla F(x^{k})\|_{2}^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{2}{\eta}\left(F(x^{0})-F(x^{*})\right)+{L_{f}K\eta\sigma^{2}}.

Dividing throughout by
K
, we get

\displaystyle\frac{1}{K}\sum_{k=0}^{K-1}\mathbf{E}\left[\|\nabla F(x^{k})\|_{2}^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{2}{\eta K}\left(F(x^{0})-F(x^{*})\right)+{L_{f}\eta\sigma^{2}}.

Finally, setting
\eta=\frac{1}{L_{f}M}
we have

\displaystyle\min_{k=0\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}1\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\cdots K-1}\mathbf{E}\|\nabla F(x^{k})\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{2L_{f}M}{K}\left(F(x^{0})-F(x^{*})\right)+\frac{\sigma^{2}}{M}.

(6.6)

For a given precision,
\epsilon>0
, to make
\min_{k=0\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}1\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\cdots K-1}\mathbf{E}\|\nabla F(x^{k})\|^{2}\leq\epsilon^{2}
, we require
\frac{2L_{f}M\left(F(x^{0})-F(x^{*})\right)}{K}\leq\frac{\epsilon^{2}}{2}
and
L_{f}\eta\sigma^{2}\leq\frac{\epsilon^{2}}{2}
, resulting in

K\geq\frac{4L_{f}M(F(x^{0})-F(x^{*}))}{\epsilon^{2}}\;{\rm and\;}\eta\leq\frac{\epsilon^{2}}{2L_{f}\sigma^{2}}.

Hence the result.
∎

C6.2
Optimal rate and communication

The following proofs are related to optimal rate and communication as given in Section
6.5
.

Theorem

(Optimal rate)
.

The probability
p^{*}
minimizing
\gamma
is equal to

\max\{p_{e}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}p_{A}\}
, where

p_{e}=\dfrac{7\lambda+L-\sqrt{\lambda^{2}+14\lambda L+L^{2}}}{6\lambda}

and
p_{A}
is the optimizer of the function
A(p)=\dfrac{\alpha\lambda^{2}}{2n^{2}p}+\dfrac{L}{n(1-p)}
in
(0\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}1)
.

Proof.

We can rewrite
\gamma
as follows

\displaystyle\gamma

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\dfrac{\alpha\lambda^{2}}{2n^{2}}+\max\left\{A(p)\mathchar 59\relax B(p)\right\}\mathchar 59\relax

where

A(p)=\dfrac{\alpha\lambda^{2}}{2n^{2}p}+\dfrac{L}{n(1-p)}

and

B(p)=\dfrac{\alpha\lambda^{2}}{2n^{2}p}+\dfrac{4\lambda}{np}-\dfrac{3\lambda}{n}.

The function
B
is monotonically decreasing as a function of
p
. The function
A
goes to
\infty
as
p
goes to zero or one, and it has one stationary point between zero and one hence it is convex in the interval
(0\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}1)
. Thus it admits an optimizer
p_{A}
in
(0\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}1)
. Note that

p_{e}=\dfrac{7\lambda+L-\sqrt{\lambda^{2}+14\lambda L+L^{2}}}{6\lambda}

is the point for which
A(p)
is equal to
B(p)
. Note also that near to zero

B(p)\geq A(p).

Therefore if
p_{e}\leq p_{A}
then the optimizer of
\gamma
is
p_{A}
otherwise it is equal to
p_{e}
.
Thus the probability
p^{*}
optimizing
\gamma
is equal to
\max\{p_{e}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}p_{A}\}
.
∎

Lemma
.

The optimizer probability
p_{A}
of the function

A(p)=\dfrac{\alpha\lambda^{2}}{2n^{2}p}+\dfrac{L}{n(1-p)}

in
(0\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}1)
is equal to

p_{A}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lll}\dfrac{1}{2}&\text{ if }2nL=\alpha\lambda^{2}\\
\dfrac{-2\alpha\lambda^{2}+2\lambda\sqrt{2\alpha nL}}{2(2nL-\alpha\lambda^{2})}&\text{ if }2nL>\alpha\lambda^{2}\\
\dfrac{-2\alpha\lambda^{2}-2\lambda\sqrt{2\alpha nL}}{2(2nL-\alpha\lambda^{2})}&\text{ otherwise }\end{array}\right.

Proof.

If
2nL\neq\alpha\lambda^{2}
, then the function
A
has the following two stationary points

\dfrac{-2\alpha\lambda^{2}+2\lambda\sqrt{2\alpha nL}}{2(2nL-\alpha\lambda^{2})}

and

\dfrac{-2\alpha\lambda^{2}-2\lambda\sqrt{2\alpha nL}}{2(2nL-\alpha\lambda^{2})}.

If
2nL=\alpha\lambda^{2}
, then the function
A
has one stationary point equal to
\dfrac{1}{2}
.
∎

Theorem

(Optimal communication)
.

The probability
p^{*}
optimizing
C
is equal to

\max\{p_{e}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}p_{A}\}
, where
p_{e}=\dfrac{7\lambda+L-\sqrt{\lambda^{2}+14\lambda L+L^{2}}}{6\lambda}
and
p_{A}=1-\dfrac{Ln}{\alpha\lambda^{2}}
.

Proof.

We can rewrite
nC
as follows

\displaystyle nC

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\max\left\{A(p)\mathchar 59\relax B(p)\right\}\mathchar 59\relax

where

A(p)=\dfrac{\alpha\lambda^{2}p(1-p)}{2n}+\dfrac{\alpha\lambda^{2}(1-p)}{2n}+Lp

and

B(p)=\dfrac{\alpha\lambda^{2}p(1-p)}{2n}+\dfrac{\alpha\lambda^{2}(1-p)}{2n}+4\lambda(1-p)-3\lambda p(1-p).

The function
B
is monotonically decreasing as a function of
p
in
[0\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}1]
. Note that

B(0)=\dfrac{\alpha\lambda^{2}}{2n}+4\lambda\quad\mathrm{and}\quad B(1)=0.

The function
A
admits a minimizer equal to

p_{A}=1-\dfrac{Ln}{\alpha\lambda^{2}}.

Of course
p_{A}
is a probability under the condition that
Ln\leq\alpha\lambda^{2}
. Thus we consider the following 2 scenarios

1.

If
Ln>\alpha\lambda^{2}
(
p_{A}<0
) then
p^{*}=p_{e}
.

2.

Otherwise,
p^{*}=\max\{p_{e}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}p_{A}\}
.

We conclude in both cases that
p^{*}=\max\{p_{e}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}p_{A}\}.

∎

D6
Addendum to the Experimental Results

Batch Normalization.
Beside the trainable parameters, the
ResNet
models contain batch normalization
(
Ioffe and Szegedy 2015
)
layers that are crucial for training. The logic of batch normalization depends on the estimation of running mean and variance, and these statistics can be pretty personalized for each client in a heterogeneous data regime. The implementation of
FedAVG
and
FedOpt
in

FedML
considers the averaging of these statistics during the aggregation phase. In our implementation, the batch normalization statistics are included in aggregation.

Step size.
The step sizes for
FedAVG
and
FedOpt
tuned via selecting step sizes from the following set

\{0.01\mathchar 59\relax 0.1\mathchar 59\relax 0.2\mathchar 59\relax 0.5\mathchar 59\relax 1.0\mathchar 59\relax 2.0\mathchar 59\relax 4.0\}.

We consider the step size for both algorithms to be
0.1
. Starting with step size
0.2
algorithms diverge; we also did not use step size schedulers. Additionally, we have tuned a number of local epochs for
FedAVG
from the following set
\{1\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}2\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}3\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}4\}
. The used batch size is set to
256
.

Compressed L2GD versus FedOpt.
From the experiments in Section
6.6.2
, Figures
6.4
,
6.5
,
6.6
we realized that
FedAVG
is not a competitive no-compression baseline for
L2GD
; see Table
6.2
.
FedOpt
, on the other hand, remains a competitive no-compression baseline comparable to
Compressed L2GD
. Therefore, we separately measure the performance of
Compressed L2GD
and non-compression
FedOpt
for training
ResNet-18
,
DenseNet-121
, and
MobileNet
. Figures
6.9
,
6.10
,
6.11
demonstrate that
L2GD
with
Natural
compressor (that by design has small variance) empirically behaves the best and converges approximately
5
times faster compare to
FedOpt
. They also show that
Compressed L2GD
with
Natural
compressor sends the least data and drives the loss down the most. At the same time,
L2GD
with
Natural
compressor reaches the best accuracy for both train and test sets.

Chapter 7
Unlocking FedNL: Practical Implementation of FedNL

The goals and summaries of this chapter are outlined in Table
1.1
and Section
1.4.6
.

7.1
Introduction

Convex optimization finds applications in science and engineering and additionally serves as a tool for tackling non-tractable global optimization and combinatorial optimization problems. Examples can be found in
Bertsekas et al. 2003
,
Boyd and Vandenberghe 2014
. The second-order methods represent a category of continuous optimization techniques that go beyond utilizing gradient and function value information by incorporating details about the Hessians of the minimization objective. These methods offer advantages, such as invariance to affine changes in optimization variable coordinates and rapid local convergence. For instance, the pure Newton method exhibits a quadratic convergence rate near the solution. Existing ready-to-use systems within Mathematical Optimization and Machine Learning lack good support for large-scale distributed second-order optimization.
Wytock et al. 2016
attribute this issue to the following factors: (a) Distributing optimization using Newton and Quasi-Newton methods demands high bandwidth to transmit Hessian-like quantities during communication; (b) The memory requirement for forming and storing second-order information is substantial; (c) Practical low-level linear algebra libraries, have limited interfaces. However, these concerns are highly correlated with questions under study in Federated Learning.

Federated Learning (FL), introduced by
Konečný et al. 2016b
, is a specialized multidisciplinary subfield within ML. It facilitates intelligent clients to collaboratively train ML models in a distributed way, eliminating the need for centralized data collection. The FL optimization algorithms have internal mechanisms to balance memory transfers and computation. Importantly in FL, they are an integral part of the optimization algorithms. One of them is
communication compression
. Examples of first-order optimization algorithms that was co-designed with communication compression includes
MARINA

(
Gorbunov et al. 2021
)
,
COFIG/FRECON

(
Zhao et al. 2024b
)
,
EF-21

(
Richtárik et al. 2021b
;
Richtárik et al. 2023
)
. These algorithms and also recently started to appear algorithms for second-order optimization methods with mechanisms for
communication compression
are still not spread enough, despite the robust theory. The first reason is that despite the rapid growth, FL is still a highly specialized subfield within ML. The second reason is that effectively applying methods in a multidisciplinary field demands proficiency in several disciplines. Ready-to-use implementations play a crucial role in simplifying this challenge.

The recent work by
Safaryan et al. 2022
introduces the family of Federated Newton Learn (
FedNL
) algorithms, marking a significant step in incorporating second-order methods into FL and large-scale optimization. The optimization problem addressed by
FedNL

(
Safaryan et al. 2022
)
has a finite sum structure:

\min\limits_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\{f(x)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\dfrac{1}{n}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}f_{i}(x)\right\}.

(7.1)

In Equation (
7.1
), functions
f(x)\mathchar 59\relax f_{1}(x)\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\dots\mathchar 59\relax f_{n}(x)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
satisfy Assumptions
17
and
18
from the following section.

7.1.1
Assumptions

Assumption 17
.

The
f(x)
is
\mu_{f}
strongly convex. If
f(x)
is twice continuously differentiable, it is equivalent to the condition that
\nabla^{2}f(x)-\mu I
be positive semi-definite
\forall x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

Assumption 18
.

There exist Lipschitz constants
L_{*}
,
L_{\rm F}
, and
L_{\infty}
such that
\forall i\in[n]
,
\forall x\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
:

\displaystyle\|\nabla^{2}f_{i}(x)-\nabla^{2}f_{i}(y)\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle L_{*}\|x-y\|\mathchar 59\relax

\displaystyle\|\nabla^{2}f_{i}(x)-\nabla^{2}f_{i}(y)\|_{\rm F}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle L_{\rm F}\|x-y\|\mathchar 59\relax

\displaystyle\max_{j\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}l}|(\nabla^{2}f_{i}(x)-\nabla^{2}f_{i}(y))_{jl}|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle L_{\infty}\|x-y\|.

To the best of our knowledge,
FedNL
is the state-of-the-art second-order optimization method in terms of
theoretical
convergence guarantees in a class of algorithms striving to solve Problem (
7.1
) under Assumptions
17
,
18
. We will not reiterate the comparisons of
FedNL
with prior methods, as this has been comprehensively covered in Appendix A
Safaryan et al. 2022
.

FedNL
supports communication compression for transferring Hessian information from clients. Its extensions include
FedNL-PP
, designed for partial participation among clients, and
FedNL-LS
, which ensures global convergence. The algorithm exhibits local superlinear convergence rates in terms of the squared Euclidean distance to the solution, independent of the Hessian’s condition number. Practically, this is indistinguishable from the local quadratic rate achieved by the Newton method. Notably, executing
FedNL
does not require knowledge of any problem-specific constants. These factors make the
FedNL
algorithm family a promising choice for a range of practical applications in which machine learning problems fulfill Equation (
7.1
) under Assumptions
17
,
18
.

7.1.2
Practical considerations to avoid catastrophic outcomes

In 1943, Kurt Lewin, a pioneer in social science, stated that
"There is nothing so practical as a good theory"
1
1

1

The phrase is commonly linked to Kurt Lewin rather than his specific publication.
, which sets one connection to how theory influences practice. It is well recognized that practical applications validate theories, and prompt theorists to refine their models. However, there are
two lesser-known facts
that highlight the need to balance theory and practice, discovered in communities adjacent to but not strictly within Machine Learning.

The first insight comes from the realm of Data-intensive Computing and Operating Systems. The constructive criticism by
McSherry et al. 2015
, argues that an excessive focus on scalability in big-data systems hides an essential evaluation of absolute performance. The concern of the authors of this work was about whether (a) state-of-the-art distributed processing systems genuinely enhance overall performance or (b) the overheads introduced by them lead in fact to performance degradation. The authors demonstrated that in experiments with popular graph algorithms, unfortunately, the latter is true. This phenomenon has to be considered in the context of any multidisciplinary field.

The second is from the Computer Architecture. Before
2004
, there was no motivation to focus on focused performance optimization of algorithms. Up to this year, the transistor density experienced exponential growth
(
Danowitz et al. 2012
)
, and
“just wait”
for new hardware was a valid strategy to improve wall clock time. From
2004
, the clock rate plateaued at
1.7-3.4
GHz, and microprocessor vendors began incorporating multiple cores, cache hierarchies, and specialized units. After the paradigm shift, the compute architecture research discovered that there are two ways to progress:

Design of domain-specific compute architectures.

The key lies in adapting algorithms to the architecture’s features. This process involves closely intertwining algorithms with the presented functional compute units and the intricate rules governing the utilization.

Refining software implementation.

Modern scripting languages offer the advantage of democratizing algorithm implementations. But their eco-system clashes too much with the principles of the real hardware and systems (see arguments in Appendix
L7.3
).
Leiserson et al. 2020
underscore this fundamental conflict by demonstrating a staggering
\times 62\,806
speedup for dense matrix multiply.

7.1.3
Contributions

Inspired by
Safaryan et al. 2022
, we have explored the landscape of enhancements to make
FedNL
more practical. One line of possible research involves modifying optimization algorithms where step size computation explicitly avoids dependence on problem-specific constants. Notably,
FedNL
already exhibits this property. Next, during our experimentation with a reference implementation of
FedNL
, a significant challenge arose in launching numerical experiments. Launching
FedNL
experiments using provided prototypes took
4.8
hours for a single optimization process. Next, we noticed that the referenced prototype only simulates a distributed environment. The
FedNL
has a super-linear local convergence rate. With this level of theory development, the gain from further theoretical improvements might not be as substantial as those derived from a highly optimized implementation. These aspects motivated us to create a more well-developed
FedNL
implementation on top of the original work. We acknowledge the considerable challenges of simultaneously developing both a comprehensive theoretical framework and a proficient implementation. Therefore in the present work, we focused our efforts on enhancing its
practical implementation
and
practical applicability
of
FedNL
algorithm family. The practical implementation also serves other needs.

First, the theoretical cost model may not be entirely accurate and needs to be adjusted (for examples of nuances in modern compute systems see Section
7.5.3
, Appendix
J7.1
). Second, without real implementation, scientific methods often remain confined to theory. As S. Boyd noted in
Doe 2023
, this is one reason why Control Theory has experienced limited adoption in the broad sense.

Summary of our contributions:

1.

Compute effectiveness.
We addressed the challenge of executing computationally intensive multi-node simulations for
FedNL
on a single workstation, achieving a remarkable
\times 1000
improvement in wall-clock time.

2.

Self-contained design.
We introduced a self-contained single-node and multi-node implementation of
FedNL
(Algorithm
16
),
FedNL-PP
(Algorithm
18
),
FedNL-LS
(Algorithm
17
). Our design facilitates seamless integration into resource-constrained systems and eliminates the need for library dependency management. Our solution relies only on OS interfaces. It is compatible with several OS, Compilers (Appendix
I7.1
),
CPUs (Appendix
I7.2
). We provide native OS executable applications, dynamic libraries, and static libraries. In addition, we provide an easy way to generate extension modules for other programming languages (Appendix
L7.4
).

3.

Adaptive TopLEK compressor.
We introduced an extension of the
TopK
compression mechanism, termed
TopLEK
. The core idea is to perform compression for
TopK
adaptively and to compress as much as theory allows, but not more (for details see Appendix
D7
).

4.

Cache-aware RandSeqK compressor.
We proposed a cache-aware version of
RandK
compressor, named as
RandSeqK
. The theory of
RandK
provides the need
”null space”
, which we have exploited to make the algorithm
cache-aware
(for details see Appendix
C7
).

5.

Logistic regression with FedNL outperforms best-practice solutions.
In a single-node setup, our implementation practically outperforms solvers encapsulated within

CVXPY

(
Diamond and Boyd 2016
)
, including the commercial

MOSEK

(
ApS 2022
)
for solving
logistic regression
. In a multi-node setup, our implementation surpasses the performance of

Apache Spark MLlib

(
Meng et al. 2016
)
and

Ray/Scikit-Learn

(
Moritz et al. 2018
)
. Our implementation has an initialization time smaller by
\times 25-\times 50
, and exhibits faster solving by a factor
\times 7-\times 82
in wall clock time (see Section
7.9
).

6.

First robust practical implementation.
To the best of our knowledge, our implementation is the first
robust
,
practical
implementation for training (strongly) convex objectives Equation (
7.1
) in FL settings (see Section
7.9.4
).

Contributions (3)--(6) are more focused toward FL. The principles from contributions (1)--(2) are valuable in scenarios when a theoretical compelling
2
2

2

A robust theory is necessary. The good practical implementation, especially in C++, demands a significant amount of time.
ML algorithm requires a strong realization. We believe some of our findings and improvements raise interesting questions for designing training algorithms because the achievement of improvements on the order of
\times 1000
(if the underlying compute and storage hardware is fixed) indicates underlying fundamental issues. The broader impact of our work is elaborated in Appendix
K7
.

7.2
Background on FedNL

Algorithm 16

FedNL
: Baseline from
Safaryan et al. 2022

[Existing]
.

1:

Input:
Hessian learning rate
\alpha\geq 0
; compression operators
\{\mathcal{C}_{1}^{k}\mathchar 59\relax\dots\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\mathcal{C}_{n}^{k}\}

2:

Initialize:

x^{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
;
\mathbf{H}_{1}^{0}\mathchar 59\relax\dots\mathchar 59\relax\mathbf{H}_{n}^{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
and
\mathbf{H}^{0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\dfrac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{H}_{i}^{0}

3:

for
each device
i=1\mathchar 59\relax\dots\mathchar 59\relax n
in parallel
do

4:

Get
x^{k}
from the server and compute local gradient
\nabla f_{i}(x^{k})
and local Hessian
\nabla^{2}f_{i}(x^{k})

5:

Send
\nabla f_{i}(x^{k})
,
\mathbf{S}_{i}^{k}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\mathcal{C}_{i}^{k}(\nabla^{2}f_{i}(x^{k})-\mathbf{H}_{i}^{k})
and
l_{i}^{k}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\|\mathbf{H}_{i}^{k}-\nabla^{2}f_{i}(x^{k})\|_{\rm F}
to the server

6:

Update local Hessian shift to
\mathbf{H}_{i}^{k+1}=\mathbf{H}_{i}^{k}+\alpha\mathbf{S}_{i}^{k}

7:

end

for

8:

On Server:

9:

Get
\nabla f_{i}(x^{k})\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\;\mathbf{S}_{i}^{k}
and
l_{i}^{k}
from each node
i\in[n]

10:

\mathbf{S}^{k}=\dfrac{1}{n}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{S}_{i}^{k}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\;l^{k}=\dfrac{1}{n}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}l_{i}^{k}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\;\mathbf{H}^{k+1}=\mathbf{H}^{k}+\alpha\mathbf{S}^{k}

11:

Option 1 (a)
:
x^{k+1}=x^{k}-[\mathbf{H}^{k}]_{\mu}^{-1}\nabla f(x^{k})

Option 2 (b)
:
x^{k+1}=x^{k}-[\mathbf{H}^{k}+l^{k}\mathbf{I}]^{-1}\nabla f(x^{k})

The optimization problem which
FedNL
solves has a finite sum structure described by (
7.1
). Here,
n\in\mathbb{N}
is the number of clients in a distributed system,
d\in\mathbb{N}
denotes the dimension of the problem, and
x^{k}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
represents the model parameters at iteration
k
. When using
FedNL
for FL problems in ML,
f_{i}(x)
provides the score criteria for using the model
x
on client
i
data. The optimization formulation in Problem (
7.1
) encodes the final goal of the training process as a selection of a function from a parameterized function class
\mathcal{F}
indexed by
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. In this context,
f_{i}(x)
often takes the form

f_{i}(x)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\dfrac{w_{i}}{n_{i}}\sum_{1\leq j\leq n_{i}}(\mathcal{L}_{ij}(b_{ij}\mathchar 59\relax\hat{F}(a_{ij};x))+R_{i}(x)).

Here
f_{i}(x)
assesses a predictive model
\hat{F}(\cdot;x)\in\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{Y}
common to all
n
clients,
n_{i}\in\mathbb{N}
denotes the number of input-output pairs at client
i\in[n]
, and
(a_{ij}\mathchar 59\relax b_{ij})\in\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{B}
represent the single input-output pair with the number
j
at client
i
. The function
\mathcal{L}_{ij}(y_{\mathrm{real}}\mathchar 59\relax y_{\mathrm{pred}}):\mathcal{B}\times\mathcal{B}\to\mathbb{R}
is a loss function that scores prediction, and
R_{i}(x):\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
is the regularization function. The weight
w_{i}\geq 0
encodes the role of client
i
.

The convergence guarantees of
FedNL
hold if
f(x)
satisfies Assumption
17
and
f_{i}(x)
satisfies Assumption
18
. While
FedNL
does not require strong convexity of functions
f_{i}
, it is required for
f(x)
. In
FedNL
(Algorithm
16
from Section 3.4
Safaryan et al. 2022
), the only quantity not evaluated in runtime is
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
. It is derived from the global characteristics of the used compressor.

To integrate user-defined optimization problems, users must explicitly define oracles
\nabla^{2}f_{i}(x)
,
\nabla f_{i}(x)
, and
f_{i}(x)
. To facilitate this process, we provide a comprehensive collection of mathematical primitives for computations on both CPU and NVIDIA GPUs, as well as system primitives for efficient input/output management and memory utilization. Also, we offer tools for numerically verifying the correctness of the
\nabla^{2}f_{i}(x)
and
\nabla f_{i}(x)
oracles (see Appendix
L7.4
,
L7.5
). Our implementation follows a modular design. Due to the principles of the selected language (C++ ISO/IEC 14882:2020), modern compilation tools, and static linkage, there is no performance penalty from the modularity.

7.3
Compute and Storage Demands of a Single Worker

Algorithm
16
encapsulates the essential procedures employed when multiple
clients
collaboratively solve the optimization problem (
7.1
). The algorithm’s versatility is independent of specific computing hardware, but in realistic scenarios, computing devices should be fixed. In our implementation, we target modern general-purpose central processing units.

This type of computing device is ubiquitous, spanning from server-grade machines to portable devices. Even when electronic components are integrated into a single System-on-Chip, the Central Processing Unit (CPU) remains a fundamental element. The architectural details at the assembly language level, have evolved sustainably over the decades, facilitating effective decoupling between Electrical Engineering and Computer Science and Engineering. In contrast, the situation with creating low-level implementations for Graphics Processing Units (GPUs) is entirely different. The Application Programming Interfaces
3
3

3

OpenCL for ARM Mali GPU, CUDA for NVIDIA GPU, Metal API for Apple GPU, and ROCm for AMD GPU
, memory management rules, and computational organization principles evolve rapidly, even within a single vendor.

To evaluate our implementation of the
FedNL
a specific class of optimization problems must be chosen and we chose
L_{2}
regularized
logistic regression
. This selection is guided by the existence of experiments with this objective in the original
FedNL
paper, and the fact that it ensures strong convexity.

The
logistic regression
can be obtained from Problem (
7.1
) through the following specialization
f_{i}(x)
for client
i\in\{1\dots\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}n\}
which contains
n_{i}
samples:

f_{i}(x)=\dfrac{1}{n_{i}}\sum_{j=1}^{n_{i}}\log(1+\exp(-b_{ij}\cdot a_{ij}^{\top}x))+\dfrac{\lambda}{2}\|x\|_{2}^{2}\mathchar 59\relax\quad b\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n_{i}}\mathchar 59\relax a\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n_{i}\times d}

(7.2)

From Equation (
7.2
) we can analytically compute
\nabla f_{i}(x)
,
\nabla^{2}f_{i}(x)
by:

\displaystyle\nabla f_{i}(x)=\mathbf{A}_{i}\times\begin{bmatrix}\dfrac{-1/n_{i}}{\exp(x^{\top}(b_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}1}a_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}1}))+1}\\
\dots\\
\dfrac{-1/n_{i}}{\exp(x^{\top}(b_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}n_{i}}a_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}n_{i}}))+1}\end{bmatrix}+\lambda\cdot x\mathchar 59\relax

(7.3)

\displaystyle\nabla^{2}f_{i}(x)=\mathbf{A}_{i}\times{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}H_{i}}}\times\mathbf{A}_{i}^{\top}+\lambda\cdot\mathbf{I}_{d\times d}.

(7.4)

Where
\mathbf{I}_{d\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}d}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
is the identity matrix,
A_{i}
matrix contains samples by columns and
H_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{n_{i}\times n_{i}}
is diagonal matrix consisting of:

\displaystyle[{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}H_{i}}}]_{j\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\dfrac{1}{n_{i}}\dfrac{\exp(x^{\top}\cdot b_{ij}a_{ij})}{\left(1+\exp(x^{\top}\cdot b_{ij}a_{ij})\right)^{2}}\mathchar 59\relax

(7.5)

\displaystyle\mathbf{A}_{i}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}b_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}1}\cdot a_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}1}\mathchar 59\relax b_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}2}\cdot a_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}1}\mathchar 59\relax\dots\mathchar 59\relax b_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}n_{i}}\cdot a_{i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}n_{i}}\end{bmatrix}^{d\times n_{i}}.

These analytical formulas have been verified numerically. Regarding memory, our implementation allocates virtual memory in the executing processes without utilizing specific mechanisms in the operating system to force dedicated physical memory (via
page-locking
, also known as
pinning
mechanisms).

The virtual memory is used to store
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
\nabla f_{i}(x)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
\nabla^{2}f_{i}(x)\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
, design matrix
\mathbf{A}_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times n_{i}}
and other intermediate buffers. The selected dataset in LIBSVM
(
Chang and Lin 2011a
)
format is read from disk storage twice, following a schema similar to Scikit-Learn
(
Pedregosa et al. 2011
)
and

Apache Spark MLlib

(
Meng et al. 2016
)
.

7.4
Compute Issues in Original Implementation

The reference implementation of
FedNL
was developed in Python
(
Rossum et al. 1995
)
with

NumPy

(
van der Walt et al. 2011
)
employed as the computational backbone. A single execution of the provided implementation for
logistic regression
utilized parameters
d=301
,
n=142
, derived by splitting the LIBSVM
W8A
dataset into chunks
n_{i}=348
, takes
19\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}770
seconds for
TopK[
k=8d
]
and
17\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}510
seconds for
RandK[
K=8d
]
, with
r=1000
rounds of repeating Lines 3-11 of Algorithm
16
. This measurements were taken on a machine equipped with an
Intel(R) Xeon(R) Gold 6246 CPU

4
4

4

Intel Xeon Gold 6246 Processor Technical Specification
.
with 12 physical computation cores. The CPU clock frequency was set to
\mu=3.3
GHz (For preparation details see Appendix
G7.3
).

Back-of-the-Envelope calculation.

To estimate the lower bound on the execution time of the simulation for a fixed algorithm, we need to examine the internal organization of the
Intel(R) Xeon(R) Gold 6246 CPU
. This organization is determined by the micro-architecture of
Cascade Lake
processors, which encompasses information about the components inside the CPU. The adders and multipliers are electrical circuits that perform corresponding operations at the rising edge of the clock that synchronize the operations inside the CPU. In the
Cascade Lake
microarchitecture, each physical CPU core contains 3 Float Functional Units (FPUs)
5
5

5

Reference information about Cascade Lake Microarchitecture
.
. These functional units, responsible for float add, subtract, and multiply typically have a throughput of
1
operation per clock cycle in modern CPU (See
Fog 2011
). Each FPU is a pipelined device. If the pipeline needs to be restarted, typically additional
4
clocks are incurred per operation. The computation demands of Algorithm
16
require each of
n
client compute Hessian with
\mathcal{O}(d^{2}\cdot n_{i})
arithmetic operations, full gradient with
\mathcal{O}(d\cdot n_{i})
arithmetic operations, function values
\mathcal{O}(d\cdot n_{i})
per round. Hessian compression takes
\mathcal{O}(d^{2})
, update the Hessian shift
\mathcal{O}(d^{2})
arithmetic operations.

Clients’ compute logic happened during
r
rounds requires:

\mathcal{O}\left((d^{2}\cdot n_{i}+dn_{i}+2d^{2})\cdot r\right)/(\mu\cdot\rm{cores}\cdot\rm{fpu})\propto 0.26\,\rm{sec.}

The master has to perform
n
additions of Hessian with
d\cdot k
elements, and
n
additions of gradients with dimension
d
. The master’s compute time for processing:

\mathcal{O}\left((d\cdot k+d)\cdot r\cdot n\right)/(\mu\cdot\rm{cores}\cdot\rm{fpu})\propto 0.0032\,\rm{sec.}

The master also needs to solve a linear system in each round. In the reference implementation, it was done using Gaussian elimination, which demands
(2/3)d^{3}
arithmetic operations. Time for it:

\mathcal{O}({3}/{2}\cdot d^{3}\cdot r)/(\mu\cdot\rm{fpu})\propto 4.1316\,\rm{sec.}

With optimal CPU core utilization, the time for
float arithmetic
should be a small multiple of
4.394
sec. However,
float operations
are also required to get the actual operands. For doing this, the CPU control unit sends requests to the Load and Store Units (LS), which is also in charge of memory access to CPU registers and caches. The number of LS units in
Cascade Lake
is
3
. If we assume that LS units work most of the time with L1 cache, then extra penalty
\times 3
(See Table
7.8
) should be paid per single access. If we assume that each float operation requires
3
memory accesses, then the memory access penalty for
float operations
will be
(4.394\cdot\rm{fpu})/\rm{ls}\cdot 3=13.182
sec.

Insights from rough estimates.

The rough lower bound of the estimated target execution time is
17.576
sec. This estimate does not include memory cost for transfers in client-master communication, controlling logic, and
possible improvements
from utilizing Single Instruction Multiple Data (SIMD) instructions available in this CPU. The observed time for launching the
FedNL
baseline is
19770\,\rm{sec.}
(
5.5
hours), indicating a notable discrepancy:

5.5\,\rm{hours}\ggg 17.576\,\rm{sec.}

We addressed the challenges related to creating a practical single-node simulation of
FedNL
, ensuring its compatibility with diverse OS/CPU configurations (see Appendix
I7.1
). After providing an optimized single-node simulation, we created a practical multi-node implementation.

7.4.1
Computation problems of reference implementation

After examining Python/

NumPy
reference implementations, we found a deeper issue. Major ML frameworks, burdened by extensive auxiliary management code, are suboptimal for complex system and algorithm creation with high-performance requirements.

To tackle this, we have shifted away from general-purpose ML and FL middleware and the prevalent Python-centric design philosophy. For further discussions on the performance limitations of the Python ecosystems, see Appendix
L7.3
.

7.5
Structure of x1000 Time Improvement

We present more notable improvements from the
FedNL
simulation in one machine, focusing on the wall clock time improvement for training
logistic regression
based on Equations (
7.1
), (
7.2
), using
LIBSVM

W8A
dataset. For finer granularity of improvements see Appendix
B7
. We augmented each sample in a dataset with an artificial feature equal to
1
to have an intercept term. After augmentation,
W8A
dataset contains
d=301
features. Number of rounds
r=1000
. The dataset is reshuffled u.a.r and was split across
n=142
clients with
n_{i}=350
. The
x^{0}=0
and regularization coefficient
\lambda=0.001
, for this problem
\lambda\left(\nabla^{2}f\right)\in[0.001\mathchar 59\relax 0.0058]
.

The overall time encompasses: (i) loading and parsing the dataset; (ii) distributing the dataset and preparing runtime; (iii) saving the outcome of the experiment to the disk; (iv) training with Algorithm
16
. The (i)-(iii) takes
4.7\%
, and (iv)
95.3\%
of the simulation time.

7.5.1
Naive C++ implementation: x20

Targeting the CPU requires the
FedNL
to be implemented in a formal programming language. Defining a
programming language
lacks universal consensus. The definition advocated by authors of compiler-based languages suggested that a language must directly translate logic into computation devices. Scripting languages lack this feature due to the requirement of an interpreter. Studies
Pereira et al. 2017
,
Leiserson et al. 2020
further highlight the superior speed and energy efficiency of compiled languages. Transitioning from Python/

NumPy
to C++
(
Stroustrup 2013
)
yielded a
\times 22
time improvement for
FedNL/TopK
and
\times 16
for
FedNL/RandK
. Each client is represented as a user-space thread in the simulation.

7.5.2
Data processing optimization: x1.077

We optimized input data processing in
LIBSVM
format by shifting from sequential I/O to memory-mapped files, along with custom string
\to
FP64 conversion, resulting in a speedup of
\times 1.077
. For details on memory-mapped files, see Appendix
L7.2
.

7.5.3
Eliminating some integer division: x1.225

Combination circuits handling integer and floating-point operations exhibit latency from
1
to
4
clocks. The division poses a challenge due to microarchitectural complexities, often requiring approximately
56
clocks
(
Fog 2011
)
. In our dense linear algebra implementation, we optimized indexing operations by eliminating a division during indexing. These optimizations yielded a
\times 1.2
gain. In our dense matrix/vector implementation, we leverage SIMD CPU instructions (AVX-512). To chunk dense arrays into 512-bit packs and the rest we utilized bit tricks. Our implementation requires
7
x86-64 instructions, and GCC 11.4 emits
9
(from C++ code). It gave extra
\times 1.0212
speedup.

7.5.4
Utilizing AVX512 CPU extension: x1.379

Data alignment and utilization of the AVX-512 instruction set resulted in an additional gain
\times 1.379
.

7.5.5
Compiler and linker optimization: x1.128

By explicitly disabling exceptions and runtime type information in C++ compilers, we achieved a gain of
\times 1.07
. Leveraging whole program optimization, where code-emitting decisions are deferred to the final stages, adds an extra gain of
\times 1.047
. Employing force inlining at specific locations results in an improvement of
\times 1.007
. The combined effect of compile-time optimization is
\times 1.128
.

7.5.6
Use sparsity from FedNL compressors: x1.44

The
FedNL
Lines 5,6,10 contain sparse updates. Exploiting this for the dense matrices has a drawback in that SIMD cannot be used, but the gain from not performing useless arithmetics outweighs it.

7.5.7
Reuse computations from oracles structure: x1.50

There exists a significant redundancy in the structure of
f_{i}(x)
,
\nabla f_{i}(x)
,
\nabla^{2}f_{i}(x)
as presented in Equation (
7.2
), (
7.3
), (
7.4
), (
7.5
). The classification margins
b_{ij}\langle x^{\top}\mathchar 59\relax a_{ij}\rangle
are reused within a single client three times. The computational cost in clock cycles is approximately
\mathcal{O}(d)
for elementary add/multiply operations. Not only is the classification margin reused in all oracles but also the sigmoid function values
g(z)=(1+\exp(-z))^{-1}
and quantities derived from it, such as
g(-z)=1-g(z)
and
g(-z)\cdot g(z)=\exp(z)/(1+\exp(z))^{2}
are implicitly reused in all three oracles. We eliminated this by reusing common values, incurring the cost of storing two vectors of dimension
\mathcal{O}(n_{i})
for margins and sigmoid values.

7.5.8
Basic linear algebra improvements: x1.338

A careful implementation of adding the same scalar to the diagonal in Line 11 resulted in a gain of
\times 1.06
. Exploiting the symmetry of the input matrix when computing the Frobenius norm in Line 5 provided a gain of
\times 1.00751
. Explicitly storing information about the number of columns in a dense matrix yielded a
\times 1.0421
gain. Implementing an efficient memory
move
and
copy
for matrix gave
\times 1.019
. Improving the initialization of matrices gave
\times 1.057
. To enhance instruction-level parallelism, the manual loop unrolling for vector-vector and vector-scalar operations gave
1.034
. Eliminate the aliasing effect problem in C++ code gain
\times 1.08
. Cumulative gain is
\times 1.3386
.

7.5.9
Linear system solve improvements: x1.31

We transitioned from dense Gaussian elimination to the more numerically stable Cholesky-Banachiewicz decomposition for dense matrices
(
Golub and Van Loan 2013
)
. We didn’t explore the connections between
FedNL
and
indirect solvers
like Krylov methods
(
Kelley 1999
)
and Multi-grid methods
(
Hackbusch 2013
)
.
Dense direct
methods are nonheuristic, independent of problem instances. The switch to Cholesky, with optimized forward-backward substitution, yielded a
\times 1.196
gain. Further enhancements, with in-place vector arithmetic and cache-aware computation organization, contributed to an addition
\times 1.096
gain.

7.5.10
Hessian and gradients oracles: x3.072

We began with the computation of
\nabla^{2}f_{i}(x)
using a naive implementation that employed straightforward matrix multiplication with three nested loops. We progressed to cache-aware matrix tiled multiplication, incorporating
9
nested loops. The optimal tile size can be estimated based on the CPU’s L1 and L2 data cache sizes, which are
32
and
1024
KBytes in our CPU. The optimal tile size to operate on three matrices (two inputs and output) are
\sqrt{L1/8}/3
,
\sqrt{L2/8}/3

(
Hong and Kung 1981
)
for FP64 matrix items. Theory recommends tile sizes of
21
and
120
, but practical experimentation favored the smaller sizes as
4
and
32
. The gap arises from the difference between cache-aware and cache-oblivious (
CO
) algorithms. The
CO
algorithms adapt to the multilevel structure of caches, available cache size in a specific moment implicitly
(
Demaine 2002
)
and do not require explicit knowledge of caches in advance. We tried
CO
schema
6
6

6

Cache-Oblivious Matrix Multiplication
by
Steven G. Johnson
:
https://math.mit.edu/˜stevenj/18.335/oblivious-matmul-handout.pdf
for matrix multiplication with 8-way splitting which is to best of our knowledge is standard schema for
CO
matrix multiplication to date. It resulted in a
\times 3
speedup compared to the tuned tiled version! The final possible gain is
\times 1.2
.

Better strategy.

Alternatively, we can compute
\nabla^{2}f_{i}(x^{k})
from Equation (
7.4
) as the sum of symmetric rank-1 matrices. We can focus on the upper diagonal part and symmetrize the result matrix afterward (gain
\times 1.85
overall). We did switch to processing
4
samples with instruction-level parallelism inside the Hessian oracle (gain
\times 1.6303
). We fused operations for matrix-vector operations and added multiples of vectors required from Equation (
7.3
) (gain
\times 1.0203
). The cumulative gain is
\times 3.077
.

7.5.11
Better compressors implementation: x1.14

Optimized
SIMD
generation of integer sequences
(s\mathchar 59\relax s+1\mathchar 59\relax\dots)
, coupled with a known at compile-time
s
value, yielded a
\times 1.006
gain because this operation was used in data shuffling and in
RandK
. Next, we computed and stored indices for the upper triangular part of
\nabla^{2}f_{i}
once without recomputing (gain
\times 1.0165
). For sparsification compressors, spending an additional
\mathcal{O}(k\log_{2}(k))
time per client on sorting by index improved memory access in master, resulting in a
\times 1.0182
gain.

In implementing
TopK
, diverse approaches were explored, including quicksort, merge sort, radix sort, randomized order statistics,
CO
Multi-way merge sort
(
Cormen et al. 2022
)
,
CO
Funnelsort
(
Frigo et al. 1999
)
. The most efficient implementation involved a 4-way Min-Heap
(
Tarjan 1983
)
for supporting
K
smallest items found so far, resulting in a
\times 1.0412
gain. In the
RandK
compressor, adopting in-place memory shuffling (instead of using two separate arrays of size
\mathcal{O}(d^{2})
) gained
\times 1.073
. Finally, during waiting for
x^{k+1}
(Line 4, Algorithm
16
), clients start precomputation of indices for the next round (gain is
\times 1.0211
).

7.5.12
Subtleties with multi-threading: x1.412

To minimize client wait times we utilized busy loops on atomic variables instead of semaphore OS primitives, resulting in a gain of
\times 1.0057
.

To minimize system contention we established a thread pool with a size matching the number of physical cores. Clients were statically dispatched to this pool, avoiding unnecessary congestion for compute cores. Next, we processed messages from clients as they became available. The last two techniques result in a combined gain of
\times 1.404
. This is a common technique in parallel and concurrent programming. These optimizations aim to minimize contention and enhance overall system efficiency.

7.5.13
Subtle memory optimization: x1.278

In C++ runtime, threads allocate memory from a global heap with OS locking mechanism. To enhance efficiency, we introduced thread-custom memory pools for vectors and matrices, resulting in a gain of
\times 1.0109
. Additionally, using aligned load/store for dense vector/matrix operations in
\nabla^{2}f_{i}(x)
,
\nabla f(x)
oracles provided a gain of
\times 1.11
. The client-master communication was organized via a memory buffer. Next, we observed that labels
b_{ij}
is not needed in Equations (
7.2
), (
7.3
), (
7.4
) explicitly and can absorbed into
\mathbf{A}_{i}
explicitly. It gave a gain of
\times 1.009
. Next, in the naive implementation, clients stored both
\mathbf{A}_{i}
and
{\mathbf{A}_{i}}^{\top}
. By eliminating the storage of
\mathbf{A}_{i}
and adding the functionality for matrix operation with transposed argument, we gained
\times 1.129
. Cumulative gain is
\times 1.278
.

7.6
Intermediate Conclusion on Single-Node Simulation

The total speedup gained from the previous steps is
\times 1000
. Extra improvements are more technical and obtained with profiling tools such as Valgrind
(
Nethercote and Seward 2007
)
, and LLVM remarks
(
Lattner and Adve 2004
)
. For details, see Appendix
B7
.

7.7
Network Improvements: x1.29

Baseline.

We focused on a setting where communication happens via
TCP/IP
for the reasons in Appendix
L7.1
. We took the setting
n=10
,
TopK[
k=8d
]
,
r=300
,
W8A
dataset. Each client
\to
master stream of information was organized as a separate
TCP
connection. The baseline took
14.92
seconds.

Improvements.

We observed that it is more effective to have a single communication channel from client to master. Next, we interleaved connection establishment with the master and dataset loading in the clients. For
RandK
and
RandSeqK
compressors, we enable index reconstruction using a pseudo-random generator. For
TopK
and
TopLEK
, it is necessary to transfer indices. We found that employing a fixed-width
32
-bit integer format surpassed the performance of a strategy involving varying sizes. Next, we intentionally disabled the Nagle algorithm
(
Nagle 1984
)
due to the explicit formulation of small buffers, as detailed in Appendix
J7.2
.

7.8
Introduced Compressors

In our pursuit of bridging the gap between
theory
and
practice
we asked ourselves:

"Is any nullspace of the introduced compressors that can be exploited for practical purposes?"

We observed that the
TopK
compressor in the original work is used solely through its contractive definition proposed in
Safaryan et al. 2022
. This absence of an unbiasedness requirement allows for the construction of
"any algorithm"
satisfying this property. We introduced
TopLEK[k’]
described in Appendix
D7
. It adapts the
TopK[
k
]
compressor, transforming it into a search process for
TopK[
k^{\prime}\leq k
]
. The selection of
k^{\prime}\in[0\mathchar 59\relax k]
ensures that contractive inequality becomes a tight equality.

Next, the
RandK
compressor exhibits an intriguing degree of freedom. While
RandK
selects a subset of coordinates of cardinality
k
uniformly at random (u.a.r.) from a total of
d
coordinates, zeroing out the rest and scaling the output to preserve unbiasedness, an alternative realization of item selection is possible. In
RandSeqK
schema, the group of coordinates is sequentially chosen from the start index
s\sim_{u.a.r.}[d]
, but next
k-1
are sequential deterministic indices
\mathbb{Z}_{d}
. This
RandSeqK
schema is unbiased and satisfies
Safaryan et al. 2022
and has the same variance as
RandK
. But it is more appealing for practice because (a) it is cache-aware; (b) the number of invocations of the used pseudo-random generator is
1
. For a more elaborate presentation of
RandSeqK
see Appendix
C7
.

7.9
Experiments

We trained
L_{2}
regularized
logistic regression
as defined in Equation (
7.2
) with
\lambda=0.001
. The initial iterate
x^{0}=0
. When utilizing
RandK
and
RandSeqK
, we leveraged our implementation’s ability to (optionally) reconstruct indices of sparsified information. For
TopK
and
TopLEK
, the indices are transferred as 32-bit integers. See Appendix
H7
,
G7
for details on the software and hardware environment. Employed steps for reliable time measurements are described in Appendix
G7.3
. We augmented implementation with
Natural
compressor
(
Horváth et al. 2019
)
, which behaves remarkably well for
FedNL
. The selected datasets are example datasets used in the original
FedNL
paper by
Safaryan et al. 2022
.

7.9.1
Single-node: baseline vanilla FedNL improvement

In a single-node simulation, we tackled
logistic regression
with a dimensionality of
d=301
. The experiment involved a total of
n=142
simulated clients and the number of rounds
r=1000
. The
W8A
dataset reshuffled u.a.r. and was partitioned into equal
n_{i}
chunks. We utilized
FedNL
without Line Search, Option-B,
\alpha
using option-2. Results are presented in Table
7.1
. It can be observed that our
FedNL/RandK[K=8d]
implementation achieves a significant total speedup of
\times 929.4
in a single-node setup compared to the baseline. Similarly, for
FedNL/TopK[K=8d]
, the total speedup from our implementation is
\times 1053.9
. The master aggregates the following data from all clients:
(i)
2\,937.0
MBytes for
RandK
and
RandSeqK
compressors;
(ii)
49\,568.7
MBytes for
Identical
mapping
\mathbb{C}(x)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}x
compressor;
(iii)
4\,241.4
MBytes for
TopK
;
(iv)
358.8
MBytes for
TopLEK
compressor.

We employed a two-level schema for
\nabla f_{i}(x)
aggregation in our implementation. After obtaining
\nabla f_{i}(x)
, the master dispatches updates to one of the
4
helper threads (configurable) in a round-robin fashion, incurring an extra
\mathcal{O}(d)
memory storage per helper. Once all workers finish work, the master performs the final aggregation. For Hessian updates, the master utilizes another pool of configurable
4
(configurable) helpers responsible for decompression and atomic updates to
H^{k}
.

Table 7.1
:
Single-node setting,
n=142
,
FedNL
(B),
r=1000
,
\lambda=0.001
,
\alpha
- option 2, FP64,
24
cores at
3.3
GHz.
