7.9.2
Single-node: extension FedNL-LS and CVXPY

We implemented
FedNL-LS
which represents the
FedNL
with global convergence guarantees (See pseudocode in Appendix
A7.1
or
Safaryan et al. 2022
) both for simulation and practical distributed usage. In experiments, the line search procedure requires almost always a
1
step. We have compared
FedNL-LS
against solvers from

CVXPY

Diamond and Boyd 2016
which can solve
logistic regression
. The summary is presented in Table
7.2
. Plots are available in Appendix
E7.1
.

We have fine-tuned tolerance for

CVXPY
solvers so that
\left\|\nabla f(x^{k})\right\|
is the same. Experiment shows that our implementation already outperforms solvers from

CVXPY

Diamond and Boyd 2016
:

CLARABEL

Goulart and Chen 2024
,

MOSEK

ApS 2022
,

SCS

O’Donoghue et al. 2016
,

ECOS

Domahidi et al. 2013
by
\times 20
. The solving times in Table
7.2
were obtained from

CVXPY
’s internal functions. These times reflect the actual duration used by the corresponding solver, excluding any overhead introduced by

CVXPY
. The initialization time refers to the period required to load all necessary Python extensions into the interpreter and to parse the data using

sklearn.datasets
. As shown for
A9A
and
PHISHING
datasets, the initialization time alone is
\times 7
longer than both the initialization and solving times combined for
FedNL-LS
with any compressor. We did not perform a comparison with

GUROBI

(
Gurobi Optimization, LLC 2023
)
. Despite its strength in solving linear and mixed-integer programming problems and accessibility to be used from

CVXPY
and ability to obtain an academic license,

GUROBI
solver has inherent limitations when applied to
logistic regression
or more complex convex models (that includes intermediate transformation into exponential cone).

Additionally, our experiments indicate that the benefits introduced by
RandSeqK
are already noticeable when
d
and
n_{i}
are small. In this low-dimensional regime, the internal logic of the compressor and its memory access patterns become significant.

7.9.3
Multi-node: FedNL, Apache Spark, and Ray

To the best of our knowledge, the best-known open-source ready-to-use solutions that can be used for distributing training
logistic regression
are

Apache Spark

(
Meng et al. 2016
)
and

Ray

(
Moritz et al. 2018
)
. We provide the time required for
FedNL
,

Ray
, and

Apache Spark
to achieve a tolerance of
|\nabla f(x^{k})|\approx 10^{-9}
via configuring final tolerance for solvers. For details about the cluster configuration see Appendix
H7
.

From Table
7.3
, it is evident that

Ray
and

Apache Spark
necessitate more initialization time.
FedNL
surpasses alternative approaches in this benchmark not only in initialization time but in solving time as well. For extra multi-node experiments with
FedNL
,
FedNL-PP
,
FedNL-LS
see Appendix
E7.2
.

7.9.4
Practical limitations of existing FL systems

In cross-device Federated Learning (FL), clients typically consist of edge devices such as IoT and mobile devices. The simplest IoT devices may lack an Operating System (OS), operating in a bare-metal environment where feasible implementations can be carried out only in Assembly, C, or C++. Mobile applications, on the other hand, function in a highly competitive and performance-sensitive market. Given the time and energy constraints of many mobile applications, Python is often the least favorable choice for mobile development. For competitive analysis of programming languages, see
Pereira et al. 2021
.

Consequently, FL systems that rely solely on Python runtimes, such as

OpenFL

(
Reina et al. 2021
)
and

Flute

(
Dimitriadis et al. 2022
)
, can face significant limitations in real-world applications. Some frameworks, like

FL_PyTorch

(
Burlachenko et al. 2021
)
and

Apple PFL-Research

(
Granqvist et al. 2024
)
, explicitly state that their focus is on simulation rather than practical implementation. Interestingly, in response to real-world challenges, frameworks like

FedML

(
He et al. 2020
)
and

Flower

(
Beutel et al. 2020
)
, initially designed in Python, are also considering support for non-scripting languages like Java and C++ to enhance their applicability and performance, despite the strong preference for Python in the AI community.

Without additional effort, existing FL frameworks from
(
He et al. 2020
)
,
(
Beutel et al. 2020
)
,
(
Granqvist et al. 2024
)
,
(
Roth et al. 2023
)
,
(
Dimitriadis et al. 2022
)
, and
(
Ro et al. 2021
)
lack mechanisms for robustly and autonomously training
logistic regression
models on arbitrary datasets without human intervention. As a result, our work does not compare with these frameworks.

For baselines comparison, we have selected to compete against solvers accessible via

CVXPY

(
Diamond and Boyd 2016
)
, and solutions such as

Apache Spark

(
Meng et al. 2016
)
, and

Ray/Scikit-Learn

(
Moritz et al. 2018
)
. Although these frameworks provide effective and robust industrial solutions, they do not fully adhere to FL principles, particularly regarding partial client participation or communication compression. Despite this limitation, we have conducted comparisons with them as they represent well-developed tools.

7.10
Conclusions

Our work represents a significant contribution to advancing the field of FL by addressing a crucial gap between theoretical advancements in
FedNL
optimization algorithm family and their practical implementation. Drawing inspiration from
Safaryan et al. 2022
, our work is rooted in cutting-edge optimization theory, demonstrating the way of implementing theory into resource-constrained FL settings. Our work serves as a guiding beacon for researchers navigating the intricate path of translating theoretical algorithms into efficient implementations across diverse domains of Machine Learning. Our work emphasizes the multifaceted considerations involved in aiming to improve the actual wall clock time.

Also, our work challenges the predominant Python-centric design philosophy in Machine Learning. It underscores the significance of considering alternative languages when prioritizing computational and memory efficiency.

7

Practically Implemented FedNL Extensions

Algorithm 17

FedNL-LS
: FedNL with Line Search. Baseline from
Safaryan et al. 2022

[Existing]
.

1:

Input:
Hessian learning rate
\alpha\geq 0
; compression operators
\{\mathcal{C}_{1}^{k}\mathchar 59\relax\dots\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\mathcal{C}_{n}^{k}\}
; line search parameters
c\in(0\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}]
and
\gamma\in(0\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}1)

2:

Initialize:

x^{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
;
\mathbf{H}_{1}^{0}\mathchar 59\relax\dots\mathchar 59\relax\mathbf{H}_{n}^{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
and
\mathbf{H}^{0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\dfrac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{H}_{i}^{0}

3:

for
each device
i=1\mathchar 59\relax\dots\mathchar 59\relax n
in parallel
do

4:

Get
x^{k}
from the server; compute
f_{i}(x^{k})
,
\nabla f_{i}(x^{k})
and
\nabla^{2}f_{i}(x^{k})

5:

Send
f_{i}(x^{k})
,
\nabla f_{i}(x^{k})
and
\mathbf{S}_{i}^{k}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\mathcal{C}_{i}^{k}(\nabla^{2}f_{i}(x^{k})-\mathbf{H}_{i}^{k})
to the server

6:

Update local Hessian shifts
\mathbf{H}_{i}^{k+1}=\mathbf{H}_{i}^{k}+\alpha\mathbf{S}_{i}^{k}

7:

end

for

8:

On Server

9:

Get
f_{i}(x^{k})
,
\nabla f_{i}(x^{k})
and
\mathbf{S}_{i}^{k}
from all devices
i\in[n]

10:

f(x^{k})=\dfrac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}f_{i}(x^{k})\mathchar 59\relax\;\nabla f(x^{k})=\dfrac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\nabla f_{i}(x^{k})\mathchar 59\relax\;\mathbf{S}^{k}=\dfrac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{S}_{i}^{k}

11:

Compute search direction
d^{k}=[\mathbf{H}^{k}]_{\mu}^{-1}\nabla f(x^{k})

12:

Find the smallest integer
s\geq 0
satisfying
f(x^{k}+\gamma^{s}d^{k})\leq f(x^{k})+c\gamma^{s}\langle\nabla f(x^{k})\mathchar 59\relax d^{k}\rangle

13:

Update global model to
x^{k+1}=x^{k}+\gamma^{s}d^{k}

14:

Update global Hessian shift to
\mathbf{H}^{k+1}=\mathbf{H}^{k}+\alpha\mathbf{S}^{k}

A7.1
FedNL-LS extension: globalization via line search

The first extension of
FedNL
that we implemented is
FedNL-LS
. We selected an algorithm in which the globalization technique remains independent of any problem-specific parameters. The pseudocode for this algorithm, presented as Algorithm
17
, has been sourced from
Safaryan et al. 2022
.

The
FedNL
family includes an extension incorporating a globalization strategy through cubic regularization
FedNL-CR
. However, its practical implementation requires knowledge of problem-dependent
L_{*}
.

Algorithm 18

FedNL-PP
: FedNL with Partial Participation. Baseline from
Safaryan et al. 2022

[Existing]
.

1:

Input:
Hessian learning rate
\alpha>0
; compression operators
\{\mathcal{C}_{1}^{k}\mathchar 59\relax\dots\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\mathcal{C}_{n}^{k}\}
; number of participating devices
\tau\in\{1\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}2\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\dots\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}n\}

2:

Initialize:

For all
i\in[n]
:
w^{0}_{i}=x^{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
;
\mathbf{H}_{i}^{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
;
l_{i}^{0}=\|\mathbf{H}_{i}^{0}-\nabla^{2}f_{i}(w_{i}^{0})\|_{\rm F}
;
g_{i}^{0}=(\mathbf{H}_{i}^{0}+l_{i}^{0}\mathbf{I})w_{i}^{0}-\nabla f_{i}(w_{i}^{0})
; Moreover:
\mathbf{H}^{0}=\dfrac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{H}_{i}^{0}
;
l^{0}=\dfrac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}l_{i}^{0}
;
g^{0}=\dfrac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}g_{i}^{0}

3:

on
server

4:

x^{k+1}=\left(\mathbf{H}^{k}+l^{k}\mathbf{I}\right)^{-1}g^{k}

Main step: Update the global model

5:

Choose a subset
S^{k}\subseteq\{1\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\dots\mathchar 59\relax n\}
of devices of cardinality
\tau
, uniformly at random

6:

Send
x^{k+1}
to the selected devices
i\in S^{k}

Communicate to selected clients

7:

for
each device
i=1\mathchar 59\relax\dots\mathchar 59\relax n
in parallel
do

8:

for participating devices

i\in S^{k}

do

9:

w_{i}^{k+1}=x^{k+1}

Update local model

10:

\mathbf{H}_{i}^{k+1}=\mathbf{H}_{i}^{k}+\alpha\mathcal{C}_{i}^{k}(\nabla^{2}f_{i}(w_{i}^{k+1})-\mathbf{H}_{i}^{k})

Update local Hessian estimate

11:

l_{i}^{k+1}=\|\mathbf{H}_{i}^{k+1}-\nabla^{2}f_{i}(w_{i}^{k+1})\|_{\rm F}

Compute local Hessian error

12:

g_{i}^{k+1}=(\mathbf{H}_{i}^{k+1}+l_{i}^{k+1}\mathbf{I})w_{i}^{k+1}-\nabla f_{i}(w_{i}^{k+1})

Compute Hessian-corrected local gradient

13:

Send
\mathcal{C}_{i}^{k}(\nabla^{2}f_{i}(w_{i}^{k+1})-\mathbf{H}_{i}^{k})
,
l_{i}^{k+1}-l_{i}^{k}
and
g_{i}^{k+1}-g_{i}^{k}
to server

Communicate to server

14:

for non-participating devices

i\notin S^{k}

do

15:

w_{i}^{k+1}=w_{i}^{k}
,
\mathbf{H}_{i}^{k+1}=\mathbf{H}_{i}^{k}
,
l_{i}^{k+1}=l_{i}^{k}
,
g_{i}^{k+1}=g_{i}^{k}

Do nothing

16:

end

for

17:

On Server

g^{k+1}=g^{k}+\dfrac{1}{n}\sum_{i\in S^{k}}\left(g_{i}^{k+1}-g_{i}^{k}\right)

Maintain the relationship
g^{k}=\dfrac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}g_{i}^{k}

19:

\mathbf{H}^{k+1}=\mathbf{H}^{k}+\dfrac{\alpha}{n}\sum_{i\in S^{k}}\mathcal{C}_{i}^{k}(\nabla^{2}f_{i}(w_{i}^{k+1})-\mathbf{H}_{i}^{k})

Update the Hessian estimate on the server

20:

l^{k+1}=l^{k}+\dfrac{1}{n}\sum_{i\in S^{k}}\left(l_{i}^{k+1}-l_{i}^{k}\right)

Maintain the relationship
l^{k}=\dfrac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}l_{i}^{k}

A7.2
FedNL-PP extension: FedNL with clients partial participation

The subsequent extension to
FedNL
, which we incorporate into our practical implementation, is
FedNL-PP
from the original
FedNL
paper by
Safaryan et al. 2022
(Algorithm
18
). This extension enables the handling of the situation, where only randomly selected clients participate in each iteration.

B7
Progression of Practical Enhancements: Chronological Overview

There is an inherent complexity involved in concurrently addressing three cornerstones:

1.

Theoretical algorithm.
Serves as a foundational framework.

2.

Modern computation systems.
Serving for efficient and reliable computation.

3.

Best engineering practices.
Serves as a bridge between the previous two.

In our work, we transition a well-developed theoretical algorithm analyzed with worst-case guarantees, exploring possible practical improvement gains. We adhered to a set of decisions in creating a practical implementation, taking steps that do not violate theory at all on one hand, but on the other side, they target to make
FedNL
practical.

In refining
FedNL
, our focus was on addressing
L_{2}
regularized
logistic regression
as a proxy, which is used for measuring improvements. The LIBSVM
W8A
dataset was selected for experimentation as a proxy to drive progress. Each sample in the
W8A
dataset was augmented with an artificial feature set to
1
to introduce an intercept term. Post-augmentation, our dataset
W8A
has
d=301
features, and encompasses
49\,749
samples. Uniform shuffling and equitable distribution across
n=142
clients were ensured, with each receiving a share of
350
sample data points, while the remaining
49
samples were excluded. The initial value of the optimization variable
x
is initialized at zero. The regularization coefficient is
\lambda=0.001
. In our instance, the strong convexity parameter is
\mu_{f}\geq 0.001
and smoothness contact is
L_{f}\leq 0.0058
. This lead to condition number
\nicefrac{{L_{f}}}{{\mu_{f}}}\leq 5.8
. We executed
1000
rounds.

Experiments setup.

All our experiments have been carried out under the Assumption that the preliminary step from Appendix
G7.3
was done, and next, the launching has been done
4
times with a minimum time from
4
launches. The employed hardware for single-node multi-core simulation is described in Appendix
G7.1
. The start iterate is
x^{0}=0
. Our
FedNL
implementation contains function value evaluation
f(x)
that is optionally tracked and computed via obtaining this information from clients. Norm of a full gradient in last iterate
\|\nabla f(x^{k})\|\approx 3\cdot 10^{-18}
for
FedNL
with
RandK[
k=8d
]
compressor, and
\|\nabla f(x^{k})\|\approx 3.88\cdot 10^{-18}
for
FedNL
with
TopK[
k=8d
]
compressor.

Improvements history.

The detailed history of improvements presented in the Table
7.4
below. The total speedup of our implementation in a single-node multi-core machine v63 compared to baseline v0 is
\times 951.88
for
RandK[
k=8d
]
and
\times 1069
for
TopK[
k=8d
]
.

C7
RandSeqK: A Practical Cache-Aware Improvement of RandK

C7.1
Background about RandK compressor

The sparsification of the input matrix
\mathbf{M}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
with
RandK
happens by selecting
k
items from possible
n=d(d+1)/2
elements from the upper triangular part of the input matrix
\mathbf{M}
and scaling the result in a way to preserve unbiasedness. The distribution employed for selecting this subset is uniform across all possible subsets of cardinality
k
. The specific subset is selected with probability
\left(\dfrac{n!}{k!(n-k)!}\right)^{-1}
. The probability that a specific single element from
\mathbf{M}
will be included in the selection equals to:

\displaystyle p

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\dfrac{(n-1)!}{(k-1)!((n-1)-(k-1))!}\right)/\left(\dfrac{n!}{k!(n-k)!}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\dfrac{(n-1)!}{(k-1)!(n-k)!}\right)/\left(\dfrac{n!}{k!(n-k)!}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\dfrac{(n-1)!k}{k!(n-k)!}\right)/\left(\dfrac{n!}{k!(n-k)!}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\dfrac{(n-1)!k}{n!}=\dfrac{k}{n}

This implies that specific items will be selected during sparsification can be described as indicator random variables which take
1
with probability
\nicefrac{{k}}{{n}}
, and
0
with probability
1-\nicefrac{{k}}{{n}}
.

RandK as a collection of Bernoulli Random Variables.

The
RandK
(
\mathbf{M}
) compressor applied for symmetric matrices from
\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
can be observed as an operator that selects elements from the upper triangular part and scales the results by scalar constant
C
. Let’s use notation whereby with
e_{ij}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
we denoted the matrix from
\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
with the only non-zero element in positions
(i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j)
and
(j\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}i)
equal to
1
.
Next we consider the collection of indicator random variables
Z_{ij}\in\{0\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}1\};i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j\in[d]
. If
\delta\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\mathbb{E}\left[Z_{mn}\right]\mathchar 59\relax\forall m\mathchar 59\relax n\in[d]
we proceed as follows:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\mathrm{RandK}(\mathbf{M})\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[C\sum_{i=1}^{d}\sum_{j=i}^{d}e_{ij}Z_{ij}m_{ij}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{d}\sum_{j=i}^{d}C\mathbb{E}\left[Z_{ij}\right]e_{ij}m_{ij}=C\cdot\delta\sum_{i=1}^{d}\sum_{j=i}^{d}e_{ij}m_{ij}=C\cdot\delta\cdot\mathbf{M}.

To ensure unbiased compression, there is only one choice for
C=\dfrac{1}{\delta}
.

Observation 1:

Although we utilized the fact that

\mathbb{E}\left[Z_{ij}\right]
does not vary across matrix elements, during the derivation, we did not explicitly rely on any specific dependence between
Z_{ij}
random variables.

Next, the derivations for the theory of
RandK
for bounding variance can be simplified by observing that
{Z_{ij}^{2}}={Z_{ij}}
. We proceed as follows:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\|\mathrm{RandK}(\mathbf{M})-\mathbf{M}\|_{F}^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{i=1}^{d}\sum_{j=1}^{d}\left([\mathrm{RandK}(\mathbf{M})]_{ij}-m_{ij}\right)^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{i=1}^{d}\left(C\cdot Z_{ii}m_{ii}-m_{ii}\right)^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\qquad+2\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{i=1}^{d}\sum_{j=i+1}^{d}\left(C\cdot Z_{ij}m_{ij}-m_{ij}\right)^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{i=1}^{d}\left(C\cdot Z_{ii}-1\right)^{2}m_{ii}^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\qquad+2\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{i=1}^{d}\sum_{j=i+1}^{d}\left(C\cdot Z_{ij}-1\right)^{2}m_{ij}^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\sum_{i=1}^{d}\mathbb{E}\left[(C^{2}\cdot Z_{ii}^{2}-2CZ_{ii}+1)\right]m_{ii}^{2}}

\displaystyle\qquad+2{\sum_{i=1}^{d}\sum_{j=1}^{d}\mathbb{E}\left[(C^{2}\cdot Z_{ij}^{2}-2CZ_{ij}+1)\right]m_{ij}^{2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\sum_{i=1}^{d}\mathbb{E}\left[(C^{2}\cdot Z_{ii}^{2}-1)\right]m_{ii}^{2}}

\displaystyle\qquad+2{\sum_{i=1}^{d}\sum_{j=i+1}^{d}\mathbb{E}\left[(C^{2}\cdot Z_{ij}^{2}-1)\right]m_{ij}^{2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\sum_{i=1}^{d}\mathbb{E}\left[(C^{2}\cdot Z_{ii}-1)\right]m_{ii}^{2}}

\displaystyle\qquad+2{\sum_{i=1}^{d}\sum_{j=i+1}^{d}\mathbb{E}\left[(C^{2}\cdot Z_{ij}-1)\right]m_{ij}^{2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(C-1)\|\mathbf{M}\|_{F}^{2}.

Therefore for
RandK
compressor
w=\dfrac{1}{\delta}-1=C-1
.

Observation 2:

During derivation to bound variance
we utilized the fact that
\mathbb{E}\left[Z_{ij}\right]
does not vary across matrix elements, however, we did not explicitly rely on any specific dependence between
Z_{ij}
random variables.

C7.2
Looking for degrees of freedom in RandK compressor

The theoretical derivation for
RandK
does not prescribe the joint distribution of
Z_{ij}\mathchar 59\relax i\leq j
, therefore analysis of
RandK
is more general and can be applied to other compression algorithms. The only requirement to maintain previous mathematical analysis is that
\mathbb{E}\left[Z_{ij}\right]
is constant equal to
\delta
. This will imply automatically that
w=1/\delta-1
for a compressor that specifies input leaving non-zero (and scale by
C
) items from the upper triangular part of input matrix
\mathbf{M}
specified by
Z_{ij}
.

C7.3
Compression schema: cache-aware RandSeqK

The proposed next modification of the
RandK
compressor introduces a sampling strategy that ensures a cache-aware memory access pattern during the compression of a matrix
M\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
arranged densely in column-wise order. If we order all elements from the upper triangular part into the sequence
E
, its size becomes
w={d(d+1)}/{2}
. The sampling strategy begins by randomly selecting a start index
s\sim_{u.a.r.}E
. The subsequent set of indices is created deterministically:

I=\{s\mathchar 59\relax(s+1)\mod w\mathchar 59\relax\dots\mathchar 59\relax(s+k-1)\mod w\}

The
RandSeqK
compressor is then applied and utilizes the same logic of
RandK
, with a difference that the selection of
Z_{ij}
is modified in the following sense
Z_{ij}=1\iff(i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j)\in I\subseteq E
.

The number of indices in set
I
is
k
, implying that exactly
k
indicator random variables
Z_{ij}
will be equal to
1
. These random variables are sampled in a dependent manner. The total number of groups from which sampling occurs is
w
, and each group consists of:

\{i\mod w\mathchar 59\relax(i+1)\mod w\mathchar 59\relax\dots\mathchar 59\relax(i+k-1)\mod w\}.

For each element in the original sequence
e
, there are
k
groups with sequential indices that cover this element. The probability of selecting a specific element (in other words,
Z_{ij}=1
) from the sampling schema where the start index
s
is initially sampled, and then the next
k-1
are committed to, is equal to
k/n
. It does not depend on the indices
(i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j)
, allowing to reuse theory from Appendix
C7.1
.

C7.4
Advantages of RandSeqK over RandK

First, the
RandSeqK
requires only
1
pseudo-random generator (PRG) call, while
RandK
demands
k
calls.

Second, let’s consider the scenarios where the input matrix is located in DRAM memory. The
RandK
compressors necessitate fetching
k
items, each comprising
b
bytes (
b=8
for FP64). For
RandK
, this results in a worst-case scenario of
k
DRAM memory transactions. The
RandSeqK
faces a worst-case scenario of
{kb}/{L}+2
memory transactions, where
L
denotes the cache line size. In contemporary systems, the cache line size is typically
64
bytes. For FP64,
RandSeqK
markedly amplifies memory access efficiency in both clients (Algorithm
16
, Lines
5
and
6
) and the master (Algorithm
16
, Line
10
) by a factor of
L/b=8
. This improvement may seem negligible, but if considering data fetching from DRAM memory based on Table
7.8
from Appendix
J7.1
, the scaled improvement becomes
\times 2640
if making a comparison with floating-point arithmetics. Finally, contemporary ARM and x86-64 processors often integrate hardware components for data prefetching, enhancing memory access latency from DRAM. Specific details about the number and types of prefetchers in CPUs are under Non-Disclosure Agreements.

Therefore, while
RandK
selects items for compression uniformly at random,
RandSeqK
exhibits a more structured and predictable access pattern, which is more favorable for prefetchers. This structured access pattern contributes to improved efficiency in memory operations, as it helps reduce cache misses and enhances data locality, ultimately leading to better performance during compression.

D7
TopLEK: A Randomized and Adaptive Improvement over TopK

D7.1
Background about TopK compressor

To simplify exposition, let’s initially focus on the class of contractive compressors to vectors:

\{\mathcal{C}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}:\mathbb{E}\left[\|\mathcal{C}(x)-x\|^{2}\right]\}\leq(1-\alpha)\|x\|^{2}\mathchar 59\relax\forall x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\mathchar 59\relax\alpha\in(0\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}1]\}.

(7.6)

An important example of a compress operator that satisfies this property is the
TopK
compress operator. This operator preserves the
k
largest (in absolute value) entries of the input, zeroing out the rest. In this case, the compression algorithm is deterministic (if breaks ties in the same way).

The deterministic
TopK
compressor satisfies condition in Equation (
7.6
) deterministically:

\|\mathrm{TopK}(x)-x\|^{2}\ \leq(1-\alpha)\|x\|^{2}\mathchar 59\relax\forall x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}.

Firstly, we observe that
TopK
is homogeneous in
\beta\in\mathbb{R}\mathchar 59\relax\beta\neq 0
. For
\forall x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
:

\displaystyle\|\mathrm{TopK}(x)-x\|^{2}\ \leq(1-\alpha)\|x\|^{2}

\displaystyle\iff

\displaystyle\|\mathrm{TopK}(\beta x)-\beta x\|^{2}\ \leq(1-\alpha)\|\beta x\|^{2}

\displaystyle\iff

\displaystyle\|\mathrm{TopK}(\beta x)/\beta-x\|^{2}\ \leq(1-\alpha)\|x\|^{2}

\displaystyle\iff

\displaystyle\|\mathrm{TopK}(x)-x\|^{2}\ \leq(1-\alpha)\|x\|^{2}

Comments.

The
TopK
compressor returns the
k
largest values in absolute value. Scaling by any nonzero number does not change the result of the compress operator (if ties are broken in the same way). After returning the result,
\mathrm{TopK}(\beta x)/\beta
correctly reconstitutes the sign. Thus, we can conclude that
TopK
is a homogeneous.

D7.2
Pessimism of TopK analysis

If
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\backslash\{0\}^{d}
and selected indices for
TopK
compressor is the set
S(x)\in 2^{[d]}
then we have:

\displaystyle\|\mathrm{TopK}(x)-x\|^{2}=\|x-\mathrm{TopK}(x)\|^{2}\leq(1-\alpha)\|x\|^{2}\mathchar 59\relax\quad\forall x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

\displaystyle\iff

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{d}x_{i}^{2}-\sum_{j\in S(x)}x_{j}^{2}\leq(1-\alpha)\sum_{i=1}^{d}x_{i}^{2}\mathchar 59\relax\quad\forall x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

\displaystyle\iff

\displaystyle 1-\dfrac{\sum_{j\in S(x)}x_{j}^{2}}{\sum_{i=1}^{d}x_{i}^{2}}\leq(1-\alpha)\mathchar 59\relax\quad\forall x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\mathchar 59\relax x\neq 0

\displaystyle\iff

\displaystyle\alpha\leq\dfrac{\sum_{j\in S(x)}x_{j}^{2}}{\sum_{i=1}^{d}x_{i}^{2}}\mathchar 59\relax\quad\forall x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\mathchar 59\relax x\neq 0

With utilizing the set
S(x)
and
S^{\prime}(x)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}[d]\setminus S(x)
the optimal
\alpha_{\rm{opt}}
can be expressed as:

\displaystyle\alpha_{\rm{opt}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\min}_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left(\sum_{j\in S(x)}x_{j}^{2}\right)\mathchar 59\relax\,\text{subject to:}\,\|x\|_{2}^{2}=1

(7.7)

Claim.

The minimizer of this optimization problem is attained when:

\forall i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j\in[d]:x_{i}=x_{j}=c

Proof of the claim.

First, observe that in Problem (
7.7
), the value of
\alpha(x)=\sum_{j\in S(x)}x_{j}^{2}
is invariant under the permutation of the elements
x_{i}
in the input vector
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Therefore, without loss of generality, we can assume that the components of
x
are indexed such that their magnitudes follow a non-increasing order:
|x_{1}|\geq|x_{2}|\geq\dots\geq|x_{d}|
. This indexing allows us to represent
S(x)=\{1\mathchar 59\relax 2\mathchar 59\relax\dots\mathchar 59\relax k\}
. Furthermore, for simplicity, we will consider only the case where
x_{i}\geq 0
, since
\alpha(x)
is not sensitive to the signs of
x_{i}
.

Assume, for the sake of contradiction, that
\alpha_{\rm{opt}}
is attained at a point
y
where the elements of
y
are not equal (and
\|y\|^{2}=1
). Thus, by the ordering of components, it means
y_{1}>y_{d}
. In this case,
y_{1}
contributes significantly to
\alpha(y)
, while
y_{d}
for
k\neq d
does not. However, we can show that
\alpha(y)
can be further reduced by perturbing the components of
y
to form a new vector
z\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

Let us define
z
with using two free parameters
\tau_{1}\mathchar 59\relax\tau_{d}\in\mathbb{R}
as follows:

\displaystyle z_{1}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}

\displaystyle y_{1}+\tau_{1}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}

\displaystyle z_{d}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}

\displaystyle y_{d}+\tau_{d}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}

\displaystyle z_{i}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}

\displaystyle y_{i}\mathchar 59\relax\forall i\in[d]\mathchar 59\relax i\neq 1\mathchar 59\relax i\neq d.

To maintain the constraint
\sum_{i=1}^{d}z_{i}^{2}=\sum_{i=1}^{d}y_{i}^{2}=1
, we need to ensure that the new vector
z
satisfies this condition. This leads to the equation:

\sum_{i=1}^{d}z_{i}^{2}=\sum_{i=1}^{d}y_{i}^{2}

Expanding both sides and simplifies:

\displaystyle(y_{1}+\tau_{1})^{2}+(y_{d}+\tau_{d})^{2}=y_{1}^{2}+y_{d}^{2}

\displaystyle\iff

\displaystyle 2y_{1}\tau_{1}+\tau_{1}^{2}+2y_{d}\tau_{d}+\tau_{d}^{2}=0

\displaystyle\iff

\displaystyle\tau_{1}^{2}+2y_{1}\tau_{1}+(2y_{d}\tau_{d}+\tau_{d}^{2})=0

We now solve for
\tau_{1}
and select one root from the two:

\tau_{1}=\frac{-2y_{1}+\sqrt{4y_{1}^{2}-4(2y_{d}\tau_{d}+\tau_{d}^{2})}}{2}=-y_{1}+y_{1}\sqrt{1-(2y_{d}\tau_{d}+\tau_{d}^{2})/y_{1}^{2}}

Therefore, for sufficiently small
\tau_{d}>0
and any
y_{1}>0
, we can select a sufficiently small
\tau_{1}<0
, because
\tau_{1}\in(-y_{1}\mathchar 59\relax 0)
, and
\tau_{1}\to 0
as
\tau_{d}\to 0
. Choosing
\tau_{1}
small enough such that
y_{1}+\tau_{1}\geq y_{k+1}
ensures that
y_{1}
remains among the top
k
components. Similarly, choosing
\tau_{d}
small enough such that
y_{d}+\tau_{d}\leq y_{k}
ensures that
y_{d}
remains in the smallest
d-k
components.

However, this implies that
\alpha(z)<\alpha(y)
, which contradicts the assumption that
y
was the minimizer. Therefore, the assumption that
y
is the minimizer must be false. Consequently, the optimal minimizer
\alpha_{\rm{opt}}
is achieved when all components of
x
are equal, i.e.,
x_{1}=x_{2}=\dots=x_{d}=c
. ∎

Consequences of optimal solution property.

The fact that optimal solution for Problem (
7.7
) implies that for the entire space
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, the minimum value of
\alpha_{\rm{opt}}
is attained only along the diagonal of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, where
x_{1}=x_{2}=\dots=x_{d}=c
. In practical computer implementations,
\mathbb{R}^{d}
is represented as a set of finite values. If the set is discretized to
M
values, the diagonal elements in this finite representation are
M
, and the number of non-diagonal elements is
M^{d}-M
. The diagonal is only a small subset of the implementable elements of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

Therefore, while the worst-case
\alpha
, as defined in Equation
7.6
, for the
TopK
compressor is
\alpha_{\rm{worst\,case}}=\nicefrac{{k}}{{d}}
, this estimate is overly pessimistic and excessively conservative when the compressor’s input approximately follows
\nicefrac{{x}}{{\|x\|}}\sim_{\rm u.a.r.}S^{d-1}
. This discrepancy is illustrated in Figure
7.1
.

(a)

d
=50,
TopK
with
k=10
.

(b)

d
=2000,
TopK
with
k=10
.

Figure 7.1
:
Discrepancy between worst-case
\alpha
and
\alpha(x)
when
x\sim_{\rm u.a.r.}S^{d-1}
. Number of trials
20\,000
.

D7.3
Compression schema: adaptive TopLEK compressor

The worst-case contraction factor from Definition
7.6
for
TopK
compressor may be too pessimistic. One possibility is to generalize the definition of
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
to
\alpha(x)
where
x
is input to the compressor. If followed this way, then
\alpha(x)
will vary in each client in each round. Serious reworking is required to analyze the convergence of the Lyapunov function for
FedNL
in this case.

We propose an alternative and practical approach to address this situation. Instead of introducing
adaptivity
from
\alpha(x)
directly into Optimization Algorithms, we incorporate
adaptivity
into the compression. Clients during employing
FedNL
obtain information about value
k
at the beginning of the training. Then they compute
1-\alpha=1-k/d
also at the beginning of the optimization process to define contraction value
1-\alpha
from Definition
7.6
.

Next in the compression phase clients return not
k
values, but
k^{\prime}
items
0\leq k^{\prime}\leq k
with the strategy described in Algorithm
19
.

1:

Input and Initialization:
Input for compressor
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
; Integer value
0<k\leq d
;

2:

Compute
1-\alpha=1-\dfrac{k}{d}

3:

Step
1
: Compute
\dfrac{\|\mathrm{TopK}[K=k](x)-x\|^{2}}{\|x\|^{2}}=(1-\alpha_{k}(x))

4:

\dots

5:

Step
K+1
: Compute
\dfrac{\|\mathrm{TopK}[K=0](x)-x\|^{2}}{\|x\|^{2}}=(1-\alpha_{0}(x))=1

6:

Find step
i\in[K]
such that
(1-\alpha_{i}(x))\geq 1-\alpha\geq(1-\alpha_{j}(x))
where
j=i-1
. Condition is equivalent to
\alpha_{i}(x)\leq\alpha\leq\alpha_{j}(x)
.

7:

Sample a Bernoulli random variable and:

1.

Compress
x
with
TopK
and
K
defined from step
i
with probability
{p}
.

2.

Compress
x
with
TopK
and
K
defined from step
j
with probability
1-p
.

Algorithm 19

TopLEK
: Top Less Equal K Compressor
[NEW]
.

In Algorithm
19
step
i
and step
j\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}i-1
corresponds to
TopK
with
K_{j}>K_{i}
. The running value of
k
is decreasing during the execution of Algorithm
19
. As steps progress leads to
1-\alpha_{i}\to 1
. In particular for step
K+1
the value
1-\alpha(K=0)=1
. Therefore we will find steps
i\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}j
such that:

\displaystyle(1-\alpha_{i}(x))\geq 1-\alpha\geq(1-\alpha_{j}(x))\mathchar 59\relax j\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}i-1.

To make Equation (
7.6
) as tight as possible we compress in a randomized way with
TopK[
k=K_{i}
]
compressor employed with probability
{p}
, and with
TopK[
k=K_{j}
]
compressor with probability
1-{p}
, we
{p}
derived as:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\|\mathcal{C}(x)-x\|^{2}\right]\leq(1-\alpha)\|x\|^{2}

\displaystyle\iff

\displaystyle p\left[\|\mathcal{C}_{i}(x)-x\|^{2}\right]+(1-p)\left[\|\mathcal{C}_{j}(x)-x\|^{2}\right]\leq(1-\alpha)\|x\|^{2}

\displaystyle\iff

\displaystyle(p(1-\alpha_{i})+(1-p)(1-\alpha_{j}))\|x\|^{2}\leq(1-\alpha)\|x\|^{2}

\displaystyle\iff

\displaystyle p-p\alpha_{i}+1-\alpha_{j}-p(1-\alpha_{j})=1-\alpha

\displaystyle\iff

\displaystyle-p\alpha_{i}-\alpha_{j}+p\alpha_{j}=-\alpha\iff p(\alpha_{j}-\alpha_{i})=\alpha_{j}-\alpha\iff p=\dfrac{\alpha_{j}-\alpha}{\alpha_{j}-\alpha_{i}}

The last equation which defines
{p}
has non-negative values
in the numerator and denominator. Due to construction of
\alpha_{j}\mathchar 59\relax\alpha\mathchar 59\relax\alpha_{i}

namely
\alpha_{i}(x)\leq\alpha\leq\alpha_{j}(x)
the value of
{p}\leq 1
. Therefore
p\in[0\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}1]
, represents a valid probability. The constructed
TopLEK[K]
compressor contractive inequality (
7.6
) attains tight equality duing using
FedNL
, that is

\mathbb{E}\left[\|\mathcal{C}(x)-x\|^{2}\right]=(1-\alpha)\|x\|^{2}.

The contraction coefficient
1-\alpha
for clients during the execution of
FedNL
remains constant and the mathematical framework of
FedNL
can be seamlessly applied without any modifications. A notable advantage of
TopLEK[k]
over
TopK[k]
is that clients can transmit not only
k
components but at most
k
. In fortuitous scenarios, clients may only need to send
0
components.

D7.4
TopLEK applied for matrices

The
FedNL
operates on a family of contractive compressors applied for matrices which is defined as:

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}(\delta)=\{\mathcal{C}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}:

\displaystyle(i)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\|\mathcal{C}(\mathbf{X})-\mathbf{X}\|_{F}^{2}\right]\leq(1-\delta)\|\mathbf{X}\|_{F}^{2}\mathchar 59\relax\forall\mathbf{X}\mathchar 59\relax\delta\in(0\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}1]\mathchar 59\relax

\displaystyle(ii)

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{C}(\mathbf{X})\|_{F}\leq\|\mathbf{X}\|_{F}\}.

Requirements (i) can be seen as a generalization of the class of contractive operators for vectors, defined in Equation (
7.6
). Because both
TopK
and
TopLEK
only replace some matrix elements by
0
value, and do not perform any scaling. Therefore both compressors satisfy the requirement (ii) automatically.

We have carried out experiments with
FedNL
,
FedNL-LS
, and with
FedNL-PP

FedNL-PP
. The results for
W8A
are presented in Figures
7.5
,
7.6
,
7.7
, for
A9A
in Figures
7.8
,
7.9
,
7.10
, for
PHISHING
in Figures
7.11
,
7.12
,
7.13
.

In this experiment, we conducted the training of a
logistic regression
model, formally defined in Equation (
7.2
), incorporating
L_{2}
regularization on three
LISVM

(
Chang and Lin 2011a
)
benchmark datasets:
W8A
,
A9A
,
PHISHING
. The training was done within a distributed data center, utilizing the hardware detailed in Appendix
G7.2
. Fifty clients were connected to a single master node, facilitating communication through a single
TCP/IPv4
connection between client and master.

The
FedNL
Algorithm
16
, particularly in Line 10, necessitated compute reduction for
s^{k}\mathchar 59\relax l^{k}
. This reduction was implemented using various communication patterns, conceptualizing client-master communication in a star topology. The master collected information from clients in a centralized manner. Both clients and masters within the experimental setup operated with a single CPU core. Refer to Appendix
G7.2
for a more comprehensive description of machine capabilities. In this scenario, we disabled the dedicated pool of workers for handling hierarchically gradient reduction and a pool of workers to parallelize the in-place update of the master Hessian.

Firstly, it is observed that the backtracking line search in
FedNL-LS
slowed down the entire training process by a factor of
\times 1.14
, but it ensures global convergence.

The proposed
RandSeqK
outperformed
RandK
, showcasing better convergence as wall-clock time progressed, communication volume increased, and external iterations (rounds) progressed. This is due to two contributing factors. The
RandSeqK
is cache-aware and consistently reaches the super-linear convergence neighborhood empirically better. The proposed
TopLEK
compressor emerged as the most economical method for sending information from client to master. While it did not enhance
TopK
when measuring progress in terms of communicated bits, it demonstrated improvement when measuring actual time in
W8A
and
A9A
datasets. In the
PHISHING
, it is less effective compared to
TopK
due to an unfortunate situation with catching the neighborhood slower.

Regarding
FedNL-PP
, we caution readers against extrapolating wall-clock time beyond this experiment. The
FedNL-PP
Algorithm
18
lacks explicit support for the computation of
\nabla f(x^{k})
as part of the training process, and computation of full gradient brought the measured time overhead.

Although our primary goal initially centered on enhancing approaches from the paper
Safaryan et al. 2022
, scientific curiosity prompted us to explore the application of the
Natural
compressor. Designed to be unbiased and adapted to a real representation of
\mathbb{R}
in FP32 and FP64
(
Markstein 2008
)
with a small
w=1/8
constant, this compressor was originally proposed within the realm of first-order compressed gradient descent methods. Remarkably, it exhibited favorable behavior for
FedNL
as well. It is important to note that the
Natural
compressor introduces technical challenges in effective implementation due to operating at the granularity of bits. Despite these, our implementation effectively supports it.

Peak kernel handles.

Stacking layers of scientific software on top of each other leads to a sufficiently big amount of OS kernel objects
7
7

7

Examples of kernel objects include files, memory-mapped file views, processes, threads, semaphores, and mutexes. These objects belong to the operating system, but they are created and managed in response to process activity.
. Each kernel handle (in any OS) consumes system resources, such as kernel memory and system table entries. As we see from Table
7.5

FedNL
simulation imposes a smaller number of kernel handles (responsible for file objects, thread objects, and others). Every kernel object occupies memory allocated within the kernel space of the OS and is managed by it. Regardless of whether the memory is paged (potentially swappable to disk) or pinned (never writable to disk under any circumstances), the object still consumes resources within the kernel.

G7.3
System preparation for reliable time measurements and experimental reproducibility

Our experiments were conducted on a system equipped with
Intel Xeon Gold 6246 CPU

8
8

8

Intel Xeon Gold 6246 Processor Technical Specification
. The CPU has a cache line size of
64
bytes,
12
physical cores, and
24
logical cores, with a core frequency from
3
GHz to
4.4
GHz
9
9

9

The
frequency
is the inverse of the clock
period
, where the clock
period
is the time required to complete one full cycle of the synchronization signal. The typical physical device that generates this signal for electrical circuits is a
crystal oscillator

(
Harris and Harris 2021
)
.
. To ensure accurate time measurements, we did the following steps:

1.

Disabled Intel Turbo Boost.
This technology dynamically increases the CPU clock speed above the base frequency based on workload demands. Disabling Turbo Boost ensures a more predictable and consistent measurement.

2.

Disabled Simultaneous Multithreading (SMT).
SMT enables multiple execution threads to run on a single physical CPU core. We disabled SMT to ensure consistent performance measurement and minimize related fluctuations.

3.

Disabled Intel CPU Frequency scaling (CPU power management).
This technology allows the operating system to adjust the CPU frequency dynamically.

4.

Fixed CPU frequency.
The CPU frequency during the experiments was set at a stable
3.3
GHz clock rate using the
cpupower frequency-set
command-line utility in Linux.

- supported by Intel (Knights Landing), AMD (Zen 4).

- explicitly turn off usage of any computing vectorization.

List of tested processor architectures for implementation.

2.

3.

FedNL
on MacBooks with the following CPU architectures:

2.

This demonstrates that the
FedNL
implementation is fully cross-platform, capable of running across multiple operating systems, CPU architectures, integer word sizes, and endianness of CPU configurations.

A Comparative analysis of x86-64 and ARM Instruction Sets.

The x86-64 Instruction Set Architecture (ISA) is widely used in desktops, laptops, and servers. Processors from vendors such as Intel and AMD, including the Intel Core and AMD Ryzen series, are commonly integrated into systems based on the x86-64 architecture. The ARM AArch64 Instruction Set Architecture (also known as A64) is primarily used in mobile and embedded devices. Additionally, ARM’s energy-efficient design has contributed to the growing adoption of ARM-based servers.

Some practical differences between ARM and x86 assembly languages include the following:

1.

AArch64 has a dedicated register for storing return addresses (the link register
x30
), whereas x86-64 does not.

2.

AArch64 allows arithmetic instructions to avoid modifying CPU flags, whereas x86-64 does not.

3.

AArch64 includes a dedicated zero register.

4.

Both x86-64 and AArch64 support memory load/store instructions that incorporate indexing directly.

5.

Both x86-64 and AArch64 support built-in conditional execution within some instructions.

The increasing adoption of the ARM ISA is primarily driven by its energy efficiency and simpler hardware implementation compared to x86-64, which follows a complex instruction set architecture (CISC). While x86-64 complex instructions can simplify high-level operations, they often come at the cost of increased power consumption.

Right now, both x86-64 and ARM remain widely used processor architectures in modern computing, and our implementation supports both of them. We also tested our implementation on several other CPU architectures, as described earlier.

I7.3
Supported and tested GPU architectures

Our current GPU support is limited. We primarily focused on the minimal support of creating oracles for optimization problems using NVIDIA CUDA
11
11

11

https://developer.nvidia.com/cuda-toolkit
- NVIDIA CUDA Toolkit provides an environment for creating high-performance, GPU-accelerated applications for NVIDIA GPUs.
, providing minimal support for expressing computation in dense and non-structured linear algebra primitives. The used version of the NVIDIA driver is 560.35.03. Functionality has been tested on:

1.

Rocky Linux 9.1, x86-64, Kernel: Linux 5.14.0-162.23.1.el9.

2.

Windows 11, x86-64, Versio 23H2, OS Build: 22631.4169.

With GPUs featuring the following microarchitectures:

1.

2.

3.

4.

NVIDIA Ada Lovelace microarchitecture, CUDA Compute Capability: 8.9.

In addition, we provide several executable applications for system introspection to detect OpenCL and CUDA-compatible devices and their characteristics (see Table

in Appendix

).

J7.1
Memory latency variance in computing systems

We present a minimum background that is enough to motivate
RandSeqK
in Appendix
C7
. For more about Compute Architecture see
Hennessy and Patterson 2012
. Modern computing systems are equipped with various storage devices. The following table, adapted from
Gregg 2014
, illustrates typical latency scales for them. More hardware-focused comparisons can be found in digital circuits design literature
(
Harris and Harris 2021
)
.

Table 7.8
:
